## FOREWORD
The region of Malwa occupies a place 6f pride in the political
and cultural history of India. The kingdom of Avanti

(ancient

name of Malwa) was counted among the major kingdoms

{mabnjanapadas) with Ujjayini as its capital. The Vcdic-puranic

traditions and the Buddhist and Jalna works refer to the supre-
macy of Chanda Pradyota of Avanti during the time of the
Buddha.
The archaeological excavations and explorations conducted
at sites likd Maheshwar, Navadatoli. Kayatha, Nagda, Ujjain
and
Mandsaur have brought lo light the evidence of rich material
culture of western Malwa during the proto historic and the early
historical periods.
Like its western counterpart, the eastern Malwa also had a
glorious past. Field work conducted at Eran, Vidisha, Tumain
and several other sites has been very fruitful in revealing multi-
faced history of this wonderful land.
The Paramaras started their rule in western Malwa in the
middle of the tenth century A.D. Gradually the entire Malwa
and parts of Rajasthah, Gujarat and Dcccan came under their
sway. The towns of Ujjain, Dhara, Dasapura, VidiSa, Bhojpur,
Gyaraspur, Udaypur, On, Onkar-Mandhata, Chandriivatj and
several others developed during the rule of the Paramaras.
The region rose lo great heights in the reigns of Munja and
Bhoja. The latter was a versatile genius. His manifold achieve-
ments as conqueror, administrator, scholar and writer made Malwa
rise to its full glory. Some of the later rulers of the dynasty
continued the traditions of Munja and Bhoja.
The period of Bhoja was an age of artistic upsurge. Not
only do the Agamas, the Tantras and the 3ilpasastras give an
indication to this upsurge, but also the numerous extant monu-
ments and sculptural remains testify to the extensive growth of
fine arts during his- time.
The Bhumija style of temple-architecture gained ground during
the Paramara rule. Quite a large number of the extant . temples
in Malvva, Gujarat and Bajastban can be referred to in this
connection. The last example can be seen in the Udayesvara
temple at Udaipur which incorporates all the typical features of a
Bhumija temple of the t'agara style. Most of the Paramara
temples are of the Panchayatana type.
The contemporary inscriptions furnish some interesting names
of Siva and Devi in whose honour temples were set-up by kings,
merchants and other people. For Siva-Mahadeva, we get the
names Kedara, Ramesvara, Somanatha, Supdira, Kala, Rudra,
DhaneSvara, Amaresvara, Udayesvara, Ballalesvara, Samidhes-
vara, Ekalladeva, etc. Similarly, for goddesses, the Inscriptions
give the names of Bhittesvari, Charchikadevi, VagdevI, Chamunda
etc.
Quite a large number of the Brahmanical deities carved
during the Paramara period are still preserved in the temples and
elsewhere. Some are preserved in the museums at Indore, Dhara,
Ujjain, Bhopal and Gwalior. Both from the point of view of
aesthetic excellence and the iconographic details, they are worth
study side by side with their iconic texts given in the contemporary
Silpa texts.
In the Udayesvara temple the two sculptures representing
Parvati and Sarasvati are exquisitely carved. They very well
tally with the description of Sridevi given by Bhoja. Numerous

other examples of Siva (in various forms), Visnu, Surya, Gapesa,

Devi and the Tlrthankaras can be cited. There are remarkabl :
depictions of Yaksas, YaksTs and Sura-Sundarls at Hinglajgarh,
Nemawar, Jhalarapatan, Chittor, Vidisha, Badoh-Patharl, Ujjain,
Mandsaur and several other sites. Recent explorations at
Hinglajgarh in the Mandsaur district of Madhya Pradesh have
brought to light more than 6ve hundred stone sculptures of the
Paramara period. Some of them are extremely important for the
study of Indian art-history.
In the present work Dr. Mahesh Singh has utilized the
relevant literary and archaeological evidence. He has tried to
interpret several knotty problems pertaining to the history of
Bhoja’s time. I am glad that his work is being published now
for the use of scholars interested in the history of the period.

( K. D. BAJPAI )Sagar

Holikotsava, the 9th March, 1982.
## PREFACE
The importance of the political and cultural history of the

time of the Pa;amara ruler Bhoja can hardly be over emphasised.

An attempt has been made in the following pages to present a
comprehensive account of the period associated with other illustri-
ous rulers of ancient India.

This study has been divided into three sections ;

—

(i) The political system,

(ii) The Administrative system.

(iii) The Cultural history.

Part I contains four chapters (i) the Ancestors of Bhoja,

(ii> the controversy about his succession, (iii) the life sketch of Bhoja

and his political achievements, I have consulted the original cpi-
graphical and literary sources bearing on the history of the period.
Part II deals with the administrative system of Bhoja. To give
a clear picture of this aspect cn phasis has been placed in the
contemporary cpigraphic and literary sources, including the works
compsed by Bhoja himself.
For a correct appraisal of the administration of Bhoja a compa-
rative study of the administrative machinery adopted by some
contem jjorary dynasties such as the Kalachuri, the Chandclla, the
Chahaman and the Rastrakuta has been made.
Part III deals with the cultural history of the time. It includes
the social, religious and economic conditions and also the develop-
ments in the field of architecture and arts during the period. Here
again the literary contributions of Bhoja have been thoroughly
exploited.
Epigraphic and literary sources have primarily been relied
upon and an effort has been made to show how far the literary
evidence is confirmed by the evidence obtained from the inscrip-
tions, coins and architectural and sculptural remains of the
period.
This is the first attempt to present a comprehensive picture of
the cultural history of the times of Bhoja.
1 express my sincere gratitude to Professor K-D. Bajpai whose
valuable guidance has enabled me to complete the present work.
I owe my deep indebtedness to my revered father Sri Raghuraj
Singh, who althrough inspired me to work round the clock.
Thanks are due to my elder brothers Sarvasri Jaganath Singh,
Balbhadra Singh and Swayambar Singh and my younger brother
Sri Ran Bahadur Singh and my nephew Sri Bajrang Singh. My
friend, Sri Krishna Kumar Trivedi, Sri R.K. Paul and Brijendra
Nath Bajpai deserve my thankfulness for their commendable help
in preparing the Index and contents.
In conclusion, I would like to offer my hearty thanks to the
Educational Academy, Dehradun for undertaking the type-
work.
Lastly, I have to thank Shri Kishore Chand Jain of Bharatiya
Vadya Prakashan, Delhi and Varanasi for his courtesy to publish
this book.
B. D. College, MAHESH SINGH

Lalganj (RBL)

March 28, 1984
## INTRODUCTION
The present work embraces all the aspects of political and
cultural history of the time of Bhoja, the Paramara ruler of the
first half of the eleventh century A.D.. On the political history of
this period, several works by eminent scholars arc available in the
market Mention may be made of Dr. D.C. Ganguly, History of

of the Paramara dynasty; Prof. H C. Ray, History of Northern

India; Prof. C.V. Vaidya, History of Hindu Mediaeval India:

Prof. P.T.S. Ayyangcr, Bhojraja; B.N. Reu, Raja Bhoja and K.M.

Munshi, Glory that was Gurjardesa. Besides, some account on

the subject is preserved in works such as struggle for Empire;

Imperial Kanauj; History of Kanauj; The History of Dekkan; Col.

Tod’s Annals of Rajasthana and Elliot’s History of India. The

works of other scholars c.g. the History of the Solankis; the

Cholas; the Etirly History of the Chauhana-dynasty and the History

of the Chandclla dynasty also throw sufficient light on the
Comparative history of tlie time.
Several points pertaining to the political history of this period
still bristle with controversies. These include his parentage, succe-

ssion of Bhoja; the date of his succession; the story ofBIioja’s

persecution; his war against the Muslims; his death in consequence

of the joint attack of Bhima, the ChSlukya king of Gujrat and the
Kalachuri King Karpa.
The cultural history of the period, not adequately presented
by the previous scholars has been given here in detail. The per-
sonal contribution of Bhoja to Indian literature and art has been

duly emphasised. His works, such as the Yuktikalpataru; the

Samarangapa Siitradhara; the Rajamartapda and the Tattvapra-

kasa throw sufficient light on Indian life and thought. Apart from
Bhoja’s own writings, several earlier contemporary and later works

such as Desinaraamala; Yasastilaka-Champu; Sukranitisara; Nava-

sahasahkacharita; Rasamala; Rajatarahgipl: Vikramankadevacha-

rita: Manasollasa; PrabandhachihtamapI and Bhojaprabandha are

quite valuable for the purpose. The accounts of AlberunI, the
Arab traveller of the time, furnish interalia, sufficient material
concerning political and the cultural history of the period.
As regards the social and religious conditions, besides the
works of Bhoja and the epigraphic records of the time and the
later period, Alberuni’s ‘India’ is of great help. It provides us
with the details about the society of the period viz. the social orga-

nisation; caste-system with its complete functioning; the system of

marriage and education sjstem etc . Works like Sukranitisara and
Manasollasa offer useful material in this regard.
A detailed description of the economic-condition of the period
is found in the Yuktikalpataru of Bhoja. In addition to other
topics it describes in detail the methods of constructing the navi-
gating vessels, ships and boats of various types. Various kinds of
raw-materials, metals and products with their lindspots are descri-
bed in the same book. The other details of the economic condition
of this period are found in Alberuni’s ‘India’. Besides, the contem-
porary epigraphic records also provide us with the material on the
subject. Some relevant portions of the Sukranltisara have been
utilized for the purpose. Several modern works like Indian ship-

ping, Bharatiya-Vyapara-ka-Itihasa (Hindi), The Socio-Economic

condition of northern India during the 11th and 1 2th centuries and
state and government in Ancient India, by R.K. Mookerjee, K D.
Bajpai, B P. Mazumdar and A.S. Altekar respectively, throw much
light on the subject. In regard to the navigation, import and export
of various goods and raw-materials with their producing localities
and commercial relations of the then India with other countries,
several stories from Soma-deva’s Kathasaritasagara have also been
quoted.
Among all the Rajput dynasties which arose from the collapse
of Pratiharas, Paramaras for sometime, continued to be the mostpowerful rulers of the northern and central
India This dynasty
came to importance in the time of Vakpati Munja and reached its
zenith during the time of Bhoja.
Bhoja was not only the greatest ruler of the Paramara dynasty
but he can be counted among the few most powerful monarchs of
the age His acivities were not confined to the political field but
embraced almost all the spheres. Culturally his era made a remark-
able advancement in Indian History. He gave a new direetion to
the poetic flow, initiated new concepts to the political thought, laid
down different new methods for the construction of machines' and
the most important among all of his contributions was his discovery
of constructing navigating vessels such as boats and ships. The
inclusion of these inventions to the Indian mechanism would have
certainly facilitated the naval warfare and the commerce of the
country.

## CHAPTER I
### ANCESTORS OF BHOJA
Bhoja figures as the ninth ruler in the list of the Paraniara
kings of Malwii. On the basis of the contemporary and other
records, an attempt will be made here to fix up the genealogical
and chronological position of Bhoja.
The Udaipur- prasasti is the only document which gives an
apparently complete enumeration of the earlier Paramara rulers
of Malwfi. Besides, three other imperfect lists arc given in the
Navasahasahkacarita,! the Nagpur-pra^asti- and in the land-
grants of Vakpati and Bhoja.® We shall first discuss the historical
facts obtained in the above-mentioned records.
As for the Nagpur-inscription, Kiclhorn takes it merely as a
prasasti or a laudatory account of the Paramara rulers of Malwa.'*
Even then we get some historical fads from it. The first king
mentioned in this inscription is Vairisirfiha, who in the chain of
regular succession was followed by Slyaka, Mufijaraja, Sindhu-
riija and Bhojadeva. The description of these five kings, accord-
ing to Kielhorn, is purely conventional and for the historian
worthless.®

The Betma-platcs of Bhojadeva dated VS. 1078 (A.D. 1021)®

also do not give a complete list of the ancestors of Bhoja. The
plates begin with the name of Slyakadcva who may be identical

4 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
It appears that the Udaipur-Prasasti alone presents an un-
broken line of kings from Upendra to Udayaditya. Even the
Navasahasahkacarita, whieh is more explicit than the inscrip-
tions, omits two names after Upendra. It, moreover, contains a
general statement that “other kings” reigned between Upendra
and Vakpati P'*. The use of the plural number jiaturally leads
to the suggestion, that they were atlcast three in number. This
seems now impossible, as, according to the Udaipur-Prasasti, the
first four kings after Paramara followed each other in the direct
line of descent.
In cons'dering some other historical statements of the Pra-
sasti, it will be advisable to add to them the information contain-
ed in the Navasahasankacarita, in the Jaina-Prabandhas and in
other accessible inscriptions.
The legend regarding the origin of the Paramaras given here,
is the same as found in the Navasahasankacarita and the
Nagpur-Prasasti. Buhler opines that a great portion of the
following remarks is a revised reproduction of part VI of the
German paper Uber das Navasahasahkacarita. When in ancient
times great Brahmana-Vasisfha was living on Mt. Abu, Visvami-
tra forcibly abducted his famous cow. Vasistha then created
out of the firepit a hero who slew the enemies and brought the
cow back. In reward of this deed the sage gave him the name
Paramara, the slayer of foes, and blessed him that he should
become a king. The myth, which figures also in the" stories of
the bards, probably arose on Mt. Abu, where Paramara princes
for a long time held the fort of Achalgaih while their capital
was at “Candravati” a tew miles South-East of the mountain.
Somesvara's Prasasti in Tcjahpala’s Temple’® at Dibvara
enumerates an older line of Paramara princes viz. Dhumaraja,
Dhandhuka and Dhravabhata, regarding whom nothing Ts
known. The prasasti also gives a series of later kings, i!e.
Ramadeva, Yasodhavala, Dharavarsa, Prahladana, Somasiihiia
and Krsnaraja, who belonged to the twelfth and thirteenth
centuries A.D. and were vassals of the Chalukyas of Anahilavad.
14. Sarga XI, p.,80.
15. Kirtikatimudl, pp. 4-6 and 14-15.
Ancestors of Bhoja 5
As the Paramaras of Malwa believed in the origin of their
heroes eponymous from the fire-pit at Abu, it seems that they
came from the North-West and formed a branch of the rulers of
Acalagadha.
.Now we shall discuss the question as to the first historical
person and the real founder of the Paramara-dynasty.
Tn the Udaipur-Prasasti, Upendra is mentioned as the first
historical-king of the dynasty. In support of this statement the
prasasti says that he gained the high honour of ‘knighthood’ or
‘the 'honour of exalted knighthood’ by his bravery.^® It may,
therefore, be inferred that the author of the Prasasti considered
him to be the conqueror of Malwa and the founder of its Para-
mara dynasty. Although Padmagupta is not clear on this point,
he undoubtedly refers to Upendra as the first king of the dynasty.
He does agree with the Udaipur-prasasti in praising Upendra for
'the performance of numerous vedic sacrifices, on the occasion of
which he is said to have adorned the earth with golden sacrificial
posts
While the Udaipur-prasasti and the Navasahasankacarita
^ are unanimous in referring to Upendra as the first historical
ruler' and the founder of the dynasty, the land-grants of the
'family raise some difficulty. They often trace the genealogy to
one Krsoaraja, But the identification of these two princes is
generally accepted. Dr. F. E. Hall and Sir Cunningham have
identified Upendra with Krsnaraja of the land-grants.
According to them the two names are synonymous, and if the
new list is complete, there is no room for a Krsnaraja besides
an Upen'dra.i® Buhler also suggests that the latter was the poeti-
cal form of the name, and Krsna or its prakrit equivalent was
that used in every day Iife^®«. But Dikshit and Diskalkar con-
tradict the above view and suggest that Krsnaraja was another

name of Vakpali for in Ihe landgrants, Vairisiihha 11, the
fifth ruler, is said to have meditated on Krsna’s feet. Buhler
does not agree with this identification. According to him the
phrase “tatpadanudhyata” does not always necessarily imply
immediate descent^". For, though usually it refers to an immediate
predecessor, there are cases where it is used to' a remoter king^i.
For example, some of the Calukya land-grants^^ assert that
Durlabha meditated on the feet of Camunda, though 'Vallabha
was Durlabha’s immediate predecessor. Therefore, this need not
cause any difficulty.
The Flarsola-plates®^, the earliest records of the Paramara
dynasty so far discovered, hardly accept the identification of
Upendra with Krsnaraja. These plates refer to Bappairaja as
the first name in the Paramara genealogy. H.C Ray identifies
this Bappairaja with VSkpati I of other records^*. BarnetF'
also suggests that Bappal is the regular prakrit equivalent of
Vakpati, but the syllable, ‘pa’ after it is inexplicable, and seems
to be a mistake, perhaps for ‘ka’. In this way, ' Dr. Barnett too
has identified Bappairaja with Vakpatiraja. But Dikshit and
Diskalkar do not agree with this identification. In their opinion
Vakpatiraja must be identified with Krsnaraja, who is placed
immediately before Vairisiiiiha with the epithet ‘Padanudhyata’
in the grants of Vakpati 11.““' Now if we accept the identification
of Vakpati with Krsna and take Vakpati identical .with
Bappairaja as has been suggested by Dr. Barnett and Prof. Ray,
we come to the conclusion that Bappairaja and Krsnaraja were
the two names of the same person. Again, if we take the
statement of the land-grants of the family as correct, where
Krsnaraja was first ruler of the family, we naturally come to the

.' •figure 'than his two predecessors Thus, he regards him as the
, "real founder of the importance of the family, for in his opinion,

''vatamandala aft6r the extinction of the local feudatory family in

" family and not as the first king or the founder of the dynasty.
'/Moreover, there is not even a single evidence other than the
Hatsdla plates to confirm this testimony.
we shall now discuss the views put forth by some scholarsI ^1 t ^ I
.with regard to tJpendra-Krsna as the real founder and the first
" ruler of dynasty. C.V. Vaidya^” does not take Upendra as the
^ first ruler of the family. According to him, the Udaipur-prasasti
alone mentions Upendra' as the first king, and puts Vairisiriiha I,
Slyaka I and Vakpati Tbefore Vairisiihha II. This is simply the

reduplication of names and is intended probably to carry back
the genealogy to an anterior period. Then again the'Nagpiir-
prasasti begins with Vairisiihlia only and omits all' the four kings.
Thirdly, it is not possible to believe that the Paramaras' could
have founded an independent kingdom during the reign of Naga-
bhatta II, the Gurjara-Pratlhara king who ruled about 8C0-825
A.D., and who is expressly stated to have reduced Malwa to sub-
jection. Lastly, if we omit these three or four kings viz. Yairisi-
mha I, STyaka I and Vakpati I, we shall be historically sound.
This would make the epithet Krspa-padanudhyata as applied'.to
Vairisimha in Vakpati’s grants not far-fetched. These grants

form an official document and should, in Vaidya’s view; i he

strictly followed instead of the Udaipur-praSasti of a much later
date. Vaidya takes Krsnaraja as the first king of the line, who
probably distinguished himself first as a subordinate chief,

and then as an independent sovereign in about 910 A.D.2’(“).

Krsparaja took advantage of the then declining power of the
Imperial Pratiharas owing to the growing might of an ambitious
Rastrakufa King Govinda III. C.V. Vaidya thus accepts Krisna-
raja as the first ruler of the family and rejects Upendra. But as
has already been seen Upendra and Krsna were the names of the
same person. Vaidya’s assertion thus does not stand and loses its
weight. Upendra emerges as the first ruler of the family. We
may safely conclude that Upendra-Krsua was the first historical
ruler and the real founder of the Paramara family. V.A. Smith
and Ray agree with this view. Smith accepts that a dynasty was
founded by a chief named Upendra- Krsnaraja, early in the 19th
century when so many ruling families attract notice for the first
time and it lasted for centuries. Upendra appears to have come
from Chandravati, and Achalagarh, near Mt. Abu, where his
clan had settled for a long time.’" Prof. Ray takes Upendra and
and Krsnaraja as one and the same and in his genealogical table

(given below) Upendra has been mentioned as Upendraraja alias

Krsnaraja.’*

The table thus represents Upendra alias Krsnaraja as the
first historical man of the Paramara family and Bhoja as the 9th
king of the line.
A'close examination of all the records and literary evidences
confirms the historicity of Upendra alias Krsna as the real founder
of the Paramara family and the first ruler of the dynasty. 'This
fact is further supported by the grants®^ of Krsnaraja, Vairisi-
mha I, and Siyaka I, where the title P.M.P. has been assumed by
all three kings before Vakpati I. As we know this royal epithet
was applied only to an independent sovereign. Upendra-Kr§naraja
can, therefore, be safely regarded as one, enjoying the indepen-
dent sovereign status.
The reigning period of Krsna-Upendra ’may be ascertained
approximately by counting backwards from Vakpati II, who died
between A.D. 994 and 997, after reigning for about 25 years.

12 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
l
were perhaps earned by him from the Rastrakutas whom he or
his father had humbled. There is no doubt about the identity of
Vakpati with Munja as the Nagpur prasasti is the only source
which described Mufija instead of Vakpati as the son ofSIyaka.
On the basis of Munja’s earliest grant dated 974 A.D., scholars
have speculated his reign from about C 970 A.D. to 977 A.-'D.
As no other historical evidence is available regarding the exact
period of Vakpati’s reign, we are mainly to depend on specula-
tions. Future researches may throw some light on this subject.
Vakpati II was succeeded by his younger brother Sindhu-
raja**, who according to the Navasahasankacarita has the
birudas, ‘Navasahasanka’ and ‘Kumaranarayana. The half praknt
form of his name, used in the Prabandhas, is ‘Sindhula’ or
‘Sindhala’. The Udaipur-prasasti allots only verse 16 to him and
reports of him the single fact that he conquered a king of the
Hunas.*^ The Navasahasankacarita*® mentions the same victory,
and in addition, others over the prince of the Kosalas as well as
the inhabitants of vagada and Lata and the Muralas.
Stiiith*’, however, places Mufija as the 7th ruler and Bhoja
as the 8th. He does not explicitly mention the reign of Sindhu-
raja between Vakpati and Bhoja. Thus, on one hand, he places
Bhoja immediately after Mufija, while on the bther, he takes a
long gap of about 24 or 21 years between Muiija and Bhoja. It
is thus, quite natural to infer that during this long gap which fell
after Mufija and before Bhoja, the kingdom of Malwa could not
have remained kingless. Somebody must have taken over charge
of the kingdom. And as we do not find the name of any other
king except Sindhuraja during the gap, the historicity of Sindhu-
raja becomes automatically proved. We may simply take that
his reign lasted only for a short period. If this is correct, Bhoja
comes 9 th in the list of the Paramara dynasty. After a few years’
reign Sindhuraja was succeeded by his son and successor Bhoja.

• Conttoversy About the Succession of Bhoja n
that when Mufija launched his expedition over the Rastrakutas,
he left the charge of the Government in the hands of Sindhuraja
and when the former died in the battle the latter became the
king. Dr. Vaidya’s identification does not appear to be correct,
for, it was probably the war against the Chaulukyas of Anahtl-
vada and not against the Rastrakutas, where Munja lost his life.
Whatever might be the fact, it becomes atleast certain that
Sindhuraja had succeeded Munja, The same source contains
another reference in support of the above statement which
lecords “When his majesty Vakpati was about to ascend to
Heaven, he put a seal on my lip, Sindhuraja, the younger brother
of that monarch now breaks it^”. The reference throws light
on the continuation of Sindhnraja’s reign afier IVtunja, as
the poet makes us acquainted with the fact that he had been the
chier-court-poet of Munja and on his death, Sindhuraja, the
brother and successor of Munja had again conferred the same
post on him. In this way, we come to know that Munja was
succeeded by Sindhuraja. It would, therefore, not be wrong to
conclude that Bhoja ascended the throne after Sindhuraja. The
information supplied by Padmagupta is more authentic and
reliable than other sou.ces because he was not only a con-
temporary of both Munja and Sindhuraja, but was also closely
connected with their courts Besides, the epigraphic records also
agree with his statement that Munja was succeeded by Sindhu-
raja, who again in natural succession was succeeded by his son
Bhoja.2 D.C. Ganguly^ on the basis of the inscriptions of the
dynasty, states that Bhoja, the son and successor of Sindhuraja
ascended the throne of Dhara, the then capital of Malwa after
Sindhuraja. Prof Ayyanger'* accepts the testimony of Ganguly
and asserts that though the relations between Munja and his
younger brother Sindhuraja were not cordial owing to Sindh'u-
raja's unruly behaviour, Munja had placed the kingdom in charge
of Sindhuraja, when the former started for war against Tailapa
1. Nav. Sarga XI Nv 87-88.
2. E. 7. I, p. 235 V. 16.
3. Struggle for Empire Vol. V Chap. U, p. 66.
4. Bhojaraja, Chap. Ill, p. 26.
Controversy About the Succession of Bhoja 19'
unreasonable to think of Bhoja as an immediate successor of
Munja. We may, therefore, conclude that it was Sindhuraja and
and not Vakpati Munja who was the immediate predecessor of
Bhoja.
The question now arises as to why Munja should have
placed his brother Sindhuraja in charge of Government and
deprived his sons of it, giving them only small principalities like
Abu.
Several reasons may have caused this incident. Firstly, as the
contemporary, Navas ahasahkacarita and the like make no
mention of any son to Munja, it seems probable that either Munja
had no sons or his sons were minor and not in a p'osition to
shoulder the responsibilities of the State. Secondly, Munja had
given the charge of Government to his brother only for the period
but his sudden death in the wir had paved the way of Sindhuraja
to become a declared king. K. M. Munshi^ observes that when

Munja (if we accept the identification of Munja with Vakpati

Paramara, the founder of the Abu line) left for Karpataka on Iiis

last campaign, he might have placed his brother Sindhuraja in
charge of Dhara. Later, upon Munja’s death in Manyakheta,
Sindhuraja might have ascended the throne, allowing some
principalities to the sons ol Munja to be retained by them. Had
Munja been confirmed of his death in the war against Tailapa II,
he would have certainly placed his sons, instead of his brother
Sindhuraja on the xhrone.
A number of scholars^ propose the name of Dusala as the
son of Sindhuraja. According to them Vakpati-Munja had
appointed Dusala, son of Sindhuraja to govern Bhillamala or

Srimala, (modern Bhinmala, in Jodhpur) and whose jurisdiction

20 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
extended upto Balmer in the west’ Bhandarkar® read this name

as Usa (tpa) la and even suggested that he was the successor of

Sindhuraja. But as there is no recorded proof in support of this,
we cannot take it as true and it may not be probable that Sindhu
raja had any other son than Bhoja. This Sindhuraja may be
identical with Sindhuraja of the Kiradu inscription^ the first
ruler of the Paramara house of Abu and thus he may be the
younger brother of Utpalaraja. This makes it probable that the
territories including Arbuda-roandala and Marumandala were of
course under the dominarce of Sindhuraja Utpalaraja was
assigned the rule of Arbudamandala. The governance of Maru-
mandala, then naturally went into the hands of Dusala.
Now we come to the next question as to why Munja should
have preferred to appoint Bhoja as Yuvaraja while he himself
had sons. But as has already been discussed Muflja had only
one son Chandana who remained contented with the governor-
ship ot Jabalipura, though in the early days of Muftja’s re’gn the
legations between these cousins were not cordial.

## CHAPTER III
### LIFE SKETCH OF BHOJA
In spite of a detailed description of Bhoja the Udaipur
pra^asti is silent regarding his early life. Other epigraphic records
also do not throw any light on the point. For this, we are to
depend solely on Literary sources which hardly contain any
reliable material.
Date of Birth
In the absence of any positive proof, it is very difficult to
ascertain the exact date of Bhoja’s birth. N. J. Kirtane presumes
that at the time of Munja’s death Bhoja was a minor of eight
years only.^ If we accept this as correct and suppose Munja’s

death in A.D. 994 (as is generally believed) and deduct 8 years

from this, we find A.D. 986 as the date of birth of Bhoja. But this
date becomes controversial when we take into account the version
of 'Qhopprabandha that at the time of Munja’s death Bhoja was
only five years of age,® and again if we accept A. D. 994 as
Munja’s death and deduct 5 years from this, we come to A.D. 989
as Bhoja’s date of birth. At the same time if we accept the account
of Prabandhacintamain that Bhoja would rule for 55 years
7 months and 3 days® and take A.D. 1055 as the year of Bhoja’s

death, (for we find a grant of Jayasiraha,^ the successor of

1. LA. Vol. VI, p. 50.
2. Bhojaprabandha, p, 13-14,
3. Prabandhacintamani, p. 22; Bhojaprabandha V. 6,
4. Mdndhata Plates of Jayasifnha, dated A.D. 1055;
E. I. Ill, pp, 48-53.
22 Bhoja Paramdra and his Times

Bhoja dated in the same year) we arrive at the conclusion that

Bhoja sat on the throne about A. D. 1000. Again, if we suppose
that his coronation took place in his I8th year, according to the
provision of Hindu Law in such cases, we find A D. 982 as the
date of birth of Bhoja by deducting 18 years from lOCO A. D.
Thus three different dates are found for the date of Bhoja’s birth
i.e. 982, 986 and 989 A.D. . We may simply presume here that
Bhoja may have been born sometime between A.D. 982 and 989.

father was Sindhuriija. Regarding his mother’s name K M.
MunshB states that ‘RatnavatP was her name. But we ^o not
find this name in any record. The Navasahasankacarita®

defeating Varankusa, who resided in Ratnavati, fifty jarynstis
from Narmada.’a Buhler identifies this Naga princess with the
Naga Ksatriya princess whose former existence was. in central
India and Rajputana ^ It need not cause surprise, if K M. Munshi
has called SaSiprabha as Ratnavati, after the name of her tesi-
dence, as it so often happens If it is true, we may frankly’say
that Sasiprabha’ was also known as Ratnavati.'* But in the
Bhojaprabandha® Savitri is the name of Bhoja’s mother. So far

Education
### EARLY LIFE
:
The versatility of Bhoja is in itself a sufficient proof thdt
Bhoja was well educated from his very childhood. Prof. Ayyanger^
states that like a ksatriya prince Bhoja underwent an extensive
course of studies. He mastered ^astras on King’s craft, achieved
skill in the use of thirty-six weapons of warfare, and mastered the
six^yfour arts. He became an accomplished poet and scholar
who could hold his own in learned debates against the greatest
scholars of his time.
The statement of Ayyanger is also supported by the Mana-
sollasa, a contemporary work It records that the princes should
be instructed in the Vedas as well as military Science. When the
princes have completed their training and become skilled in
mounting horses and elephants and in managing chariots the
king shall test them one after another. The test, applies to their

skill in various branches of literature (the Vedas, logic, the

Dharma sastra, belles letters and grammar), in the fine arts,

in wielding the bow and other weapons, and in exhibiting their
strength as well as steadiness of body and mind.^ The high
standards of princes’ training are illustrated at their best by the
instances of the scholar kings of this period, namely, Apararka

(also called Aparaditya), Somesvara III of the western chalukya

dynasty of Kalyana, Ballalasena of the Sena dynasty of Bengal
and, the illustrious Bhoja of the Paramara dynasty of Malwa.®
From Ballala we learn that Bhoja was schooled inliis early
age. It is stated in the Bhojaprabandha • that Bhoja was not only
1. Bhojraja Ch. Ill, p. 28. •;
2. ManasolJasa, III 1283-1304.
3. The struggle for Empire, Chap. XVIII, p. 508
24 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
schooled by his guardians, but was also kept under the guidance
of learned scholars* If the story of Bhoja’s persecution is
correct, it appears Bhoja could not get sufficient education as he
was recalled from school at an early age, condemned to death and
when he escaped and returned to be appointed as Yuvaraja ® But
it does not mean that he discontinued his studies, for we know
that he was well acquainted with all branches of learning.
THE STORY OF BHOJA'S PERSECUTION
An interesting ep'sode is found in the later Prabandhas
about Bhoja’s persecution by his uncle, Munja. This story found

its way first in the Prabandhacintamani of Merutunga (1300 A D.“

It is stated that once an astrologer came to the Court of Munja,
who on seeing the Horoscope of bhoja prophesied that he would
be destined to rule for 55 years, 7 months and 3 days.* It was
shocking to Muflja as he had desired it to make his own son the
successor to the throne.® In order to prevent the suceession of
his nephew Bhoja, Munja ordered his retinue led by Vatsaraja
to assassinate Bhoja in the temple of Mahamaya situated in the
forest of Bhuvane^varl.® Vatsaraja has been identified with a
very powerful king of Vanga.’ The ruffians were smitten with
pity on beholding the beauty of Bhoja’s person and hearing the
sweetness of his cultured voice. They agreed to take to the king
a communication from Bhoja. Thereupon, the young prince-poet
wrote on the leaf of a fig tree with his blood a verse to the follow-
ing effect and sent it to his uncle Munja :

runs : V2.^7

“The birth of Bhoja troubled his relatives. They became
jealous of him and so they left him in the Jungle where he was
brought up by the travellers”.
Later on, Barruj Vararuci prepared the horoscope of -Bhoja
and prophesied that bis reign would last for ninety years. When
the king saw the horoscope he lost his temper and ordered 'the
ministers to re-examine the horoscope which was found to be
wrong. Then the king went in person and brought back Bhoja
to his kingdom. The same author mentions : \ 5 5 fS O 4
“It is said that in the 8th year of his age the innocent Munja
was ordered to be blinded by the king. But some ruffians set
him free. Munja gave the ruffians a letter to hand it over to
Bhoja. When the king read the letter, he lamented on his deed.
The ministers requested the king to set Munja free. The king
appreciated Munja and appointed him his real successor, whose
capital, later on, went into the hands of Jayacanda, the Tomara
King.’ The historicity of Ain-I-Akbari is doubted. It simply
distorts the legend given in the Prabandhacintamani. Moreover,
after Bhoja, 14 kings ruled for about 25 years. We know that
it was about the time of Jayasiraha IV, the successor of Bhoja II,
when Malwa slipped in the hands of Muslim rulers. It appears
that Abul Fazl wrote ‘Jayachand’ for ‘Jayasiraha 11’. If this be
true, it becomes clear that it was Munja who blinded Bhoja and
not Bhoja who blinded Munja Moreover, Abul Fazl, has
committed a blunder inasmuch as he regarded Bhoja I and II
and their successors Jayasiraha I and II as one. No reliance can
be put on the account found in Ain-I-Akbari. . ^ .

- We shall now consider the historicity of the story referred to
in the above-mentioned Prabandhas. Ayyanger holds the story
to be mythical.! According to him, as the story of Bhoja’s
persecution found its way into records after the death of Bhoja^
it is not based on historical facts. C.V. Vaidya- states that
.neither Vakpati alias Munja appointed Bhoja his successor, nor
.he wanted to kill him. Buhler^ and Zachariae'! disagree with the
story mentioned above. According to them, as the stoiy of the
wicked uncle Vakpati Mufija’s alleged attempt to get his nephew
assassinated, is not found in the Navasahasahkacarita or any
other contemporary works, it hardly bears any veracity. N J.
Kirtane® thinks that the story only shows the signs of bad blood
between the uncle and the nephew but he adds that the natural
aversion of the high-spirited and gifted youth towards a regent
who tried to delay his desires is not sufficient to uphold its
truthfulness.
. ‘.Therefore, it would not be unreasonable to assume that the
story that Munja wished to kill Bhoja as it was foretold that the
Tatter would eclipse him, is another unhistorical myth and has to
be treated merely as the creation of poet’s imagination
Justification of the Story
So far as the story regarding the persecution of Bhoja by his
uncle Munja is not referred to in the contemporary literature and
epigraphic records, no reliance can be placed on it. Padmagupta,
the court-poet of Munja and Sindhuraja, writes that Munja had
placed the earth, in Sindhuraja’s hands when he started for
Ambika’s town.® This shows that Munja gave the charge' of

28 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
government to his younger brother Sindhuraja when he set out on
his last fatal expedition againt Tailapa 11, where he died. The
story is further refuted when we take the statement of Dhanapala,
who is said to have been alive during Bhoja’s time. Dhanapala
writes that Mufija had a great love for his nephew and therefore,
he appointed Bhoja his successor.* It would not be reasonable
therefore, to rely on the legends of much later date against the
contemporary authentic record.
Date of Accession
In the absence of documentary proofs, it is difficult to fix up
with certainty the date of Bhoja’s accession to the throne. In
spite of the approximate date settled from the reference contained
in the contemporary record, there is not even a single evidence
to show the exact date of accession. In the light of the epigra-
phic records, the Prabandhas and the view of the scholars, we
shall endeavour to fix up the exact date of Bhoja’s accession.
The earliest known inscription of the reign of Bhoja is the
copper-plates of Banswara' of V. S. 1076 or A. D. 1020 and the
second in point of time is the Ujjain Copper-plate grant of

Sunday 24th Dec ; A. D. 1021 or V. S. 1078.^ From these, we may

conclude that Bhoja must have ascended the throne before the
issue of these grants.
Mr. Stirling* points out that according to the Orissa annals
Bhoja ruled from B C. 184 to B C. 57.^ But Kielhorn refutes it and
says that king Bhoja is antedated by about twelve centuries,
as is clear from Stirling’s account who writes that Bhoja is also

said lo have repulsed a yavana invasion from Sindh. Kielhorn,

with Mahmud of Ghazni, who in A.D. 1022 and 1023 had
penetrated as far as the territories of Kalinjara in Bundelkhand
and in A.D. 10..4 had invaded Gujarat. According to him, as
the Udaipur prasastP claims Bbojadeva conquered the Turuskas,
there is no other foreign power with which he can have
come in collision. But Kielhoin’s account cannot be taken
for granted. This simply shows that Bhoja may have come into
conflict.with Mahmud of Ghazni. Moreover, the presence of
Bhoja’s own plates dated one or two years back than the earliest
dates of Kielhorn, dearly shows that Bhoja must have ascended
the throne before the dates prescribed by Kielhorn.
A Jaina Subhasita in the Bhojaprabandha mentions that
after Vakpati-Muhja, Bhoja ascended the throne in V.S. 10/8

(A.D. 1021).® But the account does not appear to be true, for

we know that Bhoja had undertaken an expedition into the
Konkan about three years before this date either in sarhvat 1075
or in one of the years preceding it ® Moreover, we know that
Munja died sometime between A.D. 994-997 in a war against
Tailapa II. It has already been discussed that after Munja,
Sindhuraja ascended the throne. He died in a war against
Chamundaraja of Gujarat This is supported by a Jain work
written by Jayasirhhadeva Suri C V. Vaidya opines that the
date of this war cannot be later than A.D. 1010. Thus he con-
cludes that the date of Bhoja’s accession to the throne cannot
exceed A.D. 1010.^

In this way, there remains a period of 9 years in which w'e must

ground. It appears that Kirtane has overlooked the Banswaia

copper-plates of Bhoja dated V.S. 1076 or AD. 1020.^ More-

wait for about 13 years after Manja’s death. Here, if we add
13 years to 997, we get A-D. 1010 for the date of Bhoja’s acces-

of Ballala mainly for two reasons. Firstly, Bhojaprabandha
being a legendary collection hardly contains any grain of Histo-
rical fact, and secondly, it is a composition of as late as the
17th century.

Bhoja Pardmard and his Times
H. C. Ray asserts that as no inscription of the reign of
Sindhuraja has so far been discovered it is difficult to fix up the
exact date of Bhoja’s reign. He, therefore, agrees with Buhler’s
date and opines that Bhoja must have ascended the throne in
A.D. 1010 or somewhat later.'
A close examination of the views explained above tends us
to believe that Bhoja’s accession took place some time about
A.D. 1010. Moreover, we know Bhoja mounted the throne
after Sindhuraja. It is equally true that Sindhuraja began his
rule after Munja. The maximum date of Munja’s reign is given
A.D. 997. It may, therefore, be presumed that Sindhuraja sat
on the throne either in A D 937 or somewhat later. If we take
the minimum age limit for a Hindu King to be 18 years and
suppose that Bhoja was 5 years old, the time of Munja’s death
which we suppose in AD. 997, we get AD. lOiO as the exact
date for Bhoja’s accession to the throne. Whatever may be the
exact date of Sindhuraja’s death, it is atleast clear from Vadnagar
Pra§asti of Kumarapala that Vallabharaja invaded Malwa during
the reign of Sindhuraja.^ But Hemachandra tells us that he died
of small-pox before he reached Malwa.’ Hemacandra further
says that hearing about the sad and sudden death of his son,
Chamundaraja left the kingdom in favour of his younger brother
Durlabharaja and retired to Sukratirtha where he is said to have
fasted unto his death." We also know that the reign of Durlabha-
raja begins from about A D. 1009, which was the consequence
of the death of his nephew Vallabha.’ It becomes thus possible
that Vallabha died sometime in A D. 1009 or lOlO. It is also
clear that at the time of Vallabha’s death, Sindhuraja was alive.
Now, if we accept the account of the Prabandhas that Vallabha
besieged Dhara' which may be possible only after the death of

2. E. r. Vol I., p. 297.
3. DP—VU, vv. 35-46.
4. Ibid.
5. Glory dial nar Gurjaradesa, p. 246.
6. Prabandhachintsmani, pp. 41-42.
Life Skefch of Bhoja ^5
Sindhur aja, we find that Sindhuraja died sometime about A.D.
1C09 If this be true, we come to the conclusion that Bhoja
ascended the throne in A.D. 1009 tOr a little later instead of
Buhler’s assertion that Bhoja ascended the throne in A D. 1010
or somewhat later. Vaidya tells us that Sindhuraja died in a
war against Chamuiidaraja.^ This war, of course^ was fought by
Chamundaraja in consequence of his son’s death. This also
shows that the events of the death of Vallabharaja, launching of
war against Sindhuraja and the abdication of throne by Cham-
undaraja in favour of his younger brother Durlabharaja are co-
related with each other and the last incidence is the result of the
first. As we know that Durlabharaja’s reign begins from A.D.
1009,2 we find A.D. 1009 as the last date for Chamundaraja’s
reign. In this way too, it is clear that Sindhuraja must have
died in A.D. 1009 or sometime before this date. If this be true,
we come to the point that Bhoja ascended the throne either in
A.D. 1009 or nearabout.

## CHAPTER IV
### POLITICAL ACHIEVEMENTS OF BHOJA
The period of Bhoja marks an important feature in the poli-
tical history of the early Medieval India. Coloured in the
fashion of the time, each great king indulged in wars .with other
rival kings. The prime motive ol these wars was not so much to
annex some portions of the territories of the Vanquished kings
as the conquerers’ desire for the achievement of glory.
The kings loved to include in their official documents,
grandiloquent titles for them indicative of their superiority to
others. They described in eloquent terms the successes achieved
by them in battles and also the acts of their charity The
Prabandhas or chronicles a'so contain accounts of historical
events.
1 he Paramaras, who consolidated their power and position
under Munja and Sindhuraja, rose to imperial rank during the
reign of Bhoja.^
At the time of Bhoj a’s accession his homeland comprised
Malwa and the bulk of Gujarat. He added to these by the con-
quests of several territories.’'
The Paramara inscriptions are unanimous in eulogising
Bhoja’s great military talents. In the Udaipur Prasasti .Wao]^
is recorded as a king who resembled Prthu The verse 18 of
the same Prasasti praises Bhoja for defeating the lord of Chedi,

Indraratha, Togglala (?), Bhima, the king of Gurjaras, the lord of

Laja, the Karnafas and the Turuskas. Inverse 17, the boundary
1. The Struggle for Empire Vol. VI, note 19, pp. 95 ff.
2. Glory that was Gurjaradesa, part II, chapt. VIII, p. 218.
Political Achievements of Blioja 37
of Bhoja’s empire is sketched in a laudatory verscas "Bhoja ruled
the earth from Kailasa in Himalayas to Malabar and from the
mountain where the sun rises to that where it sets.”^ This is' really
an exaggerated account of the extent of his dominions
I - >
-The Arthuna inscription of the Paramara Camun^araja of
V.S. 1136 describes that the illustrious Bhoja was "as sublime as
the high peaks of the great mountain of Meru who had caused

the three worlds whitened by his fame (won by) causing fear and

destroying his enemies such as the lords of Korikana, Chedi,
Gujarat, Lata and Karnata and who had destroyed the hosts of
his fierce enemies by his great army.”^
Dhara-Prasasti of Arjunavarman does not furnish any
detailed information regarding the extent of Bhoja’s empire
but glorifies Bhoja with the title of Sarvabhauma ®
Dr. Buhler thinks that the extension of Bhoja’s dominion as
given in the above eulogies, is much hyperbolic. According to him
‘‘Bhoja’s rule never exceeded the limits of modern Malwa”.^ In
Ganguly’s opinion, "the kingdom of Bhoja extended upto Chitor,
Banswara, Durgapur, Khandesa, Konkapa and upper courses of
Godavari”. Here we concede to the assertion of H.C. Ray who
remarks that though there is much exaggeration in these state-
ments of the prasasti writers, yet epigraphic evidence shows' that
there is a substantial basis for ascribing to Bhoja many of these
conquests?® Unfortunately only fragmentary information is' availa-
ble to us from which we can reconstruct the career of this great
man. Let us have attempts to find out the facts from these
meagre sources.

Bhoja's war with the Chahikyas >
Bhoja selected Deccan first for his initial military adventure.^
Hemachandra in his Dvyasraya Kavya logically explains the
reasons for this military adventure of Bhoja. According to him
the emnity bet ween the Chalnkva s-of-CiiihLtat and the Paramaras

f)f MnUva wn»! an old one -Tn the very beginning of his reign,

Chamunda came into conflict with Sindhuraja. The Paramara
king invaded Anahilvada, but had to retreat owing to the brave
resistance offered by Chamunda. This incident revived the enmity
between the two houses. 2 '
From Abhayatilaka, the critic of Hemachandra, we learn
that Chachimdevi compelled h'm to abdicate the throne in favour
of his elder son, Vallabharaja. Chamupda thereupon started
towards KaSi for the expiation of his sin. While he was on his way
to Ka^I, he was checked at Malwa by Sindhuraja, who deprived
him of the insignia of state -the royal umbreila and other valua-
bles.^ This indicates that Chamunda was defeated by Sindhuraja.
Here Abhayatilaka differs from Hemachandra who states that
it was Sindhuraja and not Chamupda who had been defeated.
Hemachandra mentions that after his return Cfaamupdaraja
ordered his son, Vallabharaja to uproot ‘the thorn’ i.e. the
Malwa king. Vallabharaja in obedience to his father’s order
invaded Malwa by crossing the confluence of the rivers Para and
Sindhu. But he died of small-pox before he reached Dhara and
consequently his army withdrew.* Merutunga narrates the
same incidence.® On the contrary, some Prabandhas speak of
Vallabha’s occupation over Dhara. As there is no authentic

which he is said to have abdicated the throne in favour of his

Durlabharaja never invaded Malwa Hemachandia,^ how-
ever, narrates an interesting story about Bhoja’s encounter with

Durlabharaja (A.D. 1010-1022), though the cause of this fight was

not the same as that of Vallabha’s invasion of Malwa. It is stated

that Mahendraraja, the king of Nadduladesa (Nadol) had arrang-

ed a Svayaihvara for his sister, Durlabhadevl. In this Svayarhvara
among others, Durlabharaja, the king of Gujarat participated
and so also the rulers of Angade^a, KasT, Ujjayini, K-uru-
desa. Mathuradesa and Himadesa. But among all, Durlabhadevl
selected Dnilabhataja, the king of Gujatat as her fntme hushawd.
This aroused jealousy among the other chiefs. Consequently,
they formed a league and fell upon Durlabharaja. But the later
emerged victorious. The king of Malwa, who, according to
Ganguly, was a companion of the king of Angadesa,^ threw down
his weapons. Dr. Ganguly thinks that the Raja of Malwa,
referred to abo\e, was, in all probability, Bhoja. To Ganguly,
the chronological accuracy in the above story seems to be doubt-
ful.® The first part of the story appears to be correct. For we
know that Durlabharaja’s reigns began from A.D. 1010. This
shows that Durlabha must have won the Svayamvara in or after
A.D. U 10. By this time, Bhoja was above the minimum a_e limit
prescribed by the Hindu Law-givers, i.e. 18 years, as he was un-
disputed master of the Paramara realm. It may thus be well
presumed that Bhoja was mature enough to participate in the
Svayaihvara. But the latter part of the story is not based on

able ground. It is not convincible that Durlabharaja, whose father
was easi'y checked by Sindhuraja alone on his way to KasI grew
so powerful as to defeat the confederate forces in which Bhoja was
also a party. We think that Durlabharaja, throughout his reign
maintained friendly relations with Bhoja.’ Even the death of
Vallabharaja could not induce him to invade Malwa. Instead,
the friendship facilitated Durlabha to plunder Lata in A.D. 1018.®

Durlabha reigned for full 12 years In A.D 1022 (VS 1077} he

abdicated the throne in favour of his nephew, Bhima.® Meru-
tuhga, on the other hand, relates a story that Durlabharaja sat

on the throne in V, S. 1066 (A.D.1099 . After 12 years’ reign he

handed over the throne to his nephew and himself undertook a
pilgrimage to KasI. On his way to Kasi he was stopped by
Munja at Malwa. He was compelled to give up the insignia of
royally chowries and other valuables to Munja.'* But as we know
that Munja died a decade before Durlabha sat on the throne the
story seems purely mythical. Besides, the story shows that after
his 12threignal year, Durlabha left his throne in favour of his
nephew Bhima, and then he came into conflict with Munja. In
this way, if we add I years to 100', we find that this incident
took place in about A.D, 1021, which is quite off the mark as
Munja’s reigning period
A brief summary of all the views put forth makes it clear that
the relations between the two houses i.e Paramaras and the Cha-
lukyas of Gujarat were friendly rather than inimical, at least upto
A.D. 1021. During this gap of period there was no war between
the Paramara Bhoja and the Chalukya kings. Durlabharaja, all
through his reign, never attempted to break amity which sub-
sisted between him and Bhoja. The river Sabarmatl, or Sarasvatt,
remained an unbroken frontier between the Chalukyan domains
and the kingdom of Bhoja,

Bhoja's war wiih La[a >
Bhoja’s military career begins with a war against Lata, the
country extended upto Surat, in the Modern Gujarat state and
in the North its boundaries touched the kingdom of the Sllaharas
of Northern Konkana. From Merutunga,* we learn that Bhoja
previously had no intention to invade Lata. Bhoja’s tirst pro-
posed plan was to invade his western neighbours, the Chalukyas
of Gujarat. Damara, a diplomatic agent of king Bhima had
sagaciously revived the old enmity between Bhoja aad the Chalu-
kyas of Kalyani. It was for this reason that Bhoja turned his
arms against Jayasimha, the then Chalukya king of Kalyani. But
it was not possible for Bhoja to conquer his Southern enemy
without having his control over Lata. It was through this country
of Lata, that the Nasik-Bulsar passage, the gate-way to the South
could be controlled. Bhoja, therefore, may have thought it

essential to have his control over La(a first in order to Viin'quish

the Chalukyas.
The ruler Lata, contemporary ^f Bhoja was Kirtiraja, the
son of Goggiraja, a feudatory of Western Chalukyas. It is
recorded that Durlabharaja, the king of Western Chfilukyas and
an ally of Bhoja had already plundered Lata in A D. 1018. This
facilitated Bhoja to conquer Lafa. But K M. Munshi does not
agree with these findings. In his view, “the reference is to some
stray skirmish, for Lata remained independent, to be later
subdued by Bhoja Whatever might be the fact, it is clear
from the copper-plate grant of his grandson, Trilochanapala that
Kirtiraja was defeated by Bhoja, for it records that “the fame of
Kirtiraja was taken away for a time by his enemies’’.® Dr.
Ganguly states that he was also defeated by Sindhuraja.* The
Surat-grant of Kirtiraja dated A.D. 1018 records that he was

evidently no match for the powerful armies of Bhoja, and being

Bhoja's War With Kohkaija
The annexation of Lata to his Kingdom, emboldened Bhoja
to push his arms further south. This brought him into direct
conflict with the Silahara king Arikesari, also known as Kesideva.'*
The country of Kohkana at this time extended from the Tirana

district (Bombay Presidency) to the Malabar coast (of the

Madras Presidency) on the South. The ^Ilaharas held sway over

the northern part of this country.’ Dr. Buhler says, “during the
9th and 10th centuries of our era the ‘Silaharas were the feuda-
tories of Rastrakutas of Manyakheta and later of the Chalukyas
ofKalyanl”.® They held besides the Kohkan, considerable parts
of the Dekhan, between Sitara and Belgan.® Prof. Ayyanger
states that Arikesari alias Ke^ideva the then ruler of northern-

It appears that after Bhoja’s conquest of Konkana the §ila-
haras continued to rule over Konkana, probably as vassals of
the Paramaras.^
The Miraj plates of Jayasiihha II, the Chalukya king dated
AD. 1024 indicate Bhoja’s short rule over Konkana. In these
plates is recorded, Jayasiriiha took into his possession the wealth
of the lords of the Seven-Kohkanas’ encamped at Kolhapur for
the purpose of conquering the northern country,* From this
some scholars conclude that Jayasiihha wrested Konkana from
Bhoja sometime before A D- 1024. Dr. Disalkar holds this
view.^ But according to Dr R G. Bhandarkar, ‘ the passage
simply refers to the ruler of Seven Konkanas”.® In Fleet's opinion
it shows 'the lords of the Seven Kohkanas’, Prof Ray accepts the
assertion of Fleet and states, “as 'adhisvaranam’ is in the plural,
Fleet seems to be right’’.’ In the plates the word 'lords’ is used
for the rulers of ‘Seven-Konkapas’. He also thinks that the
surmise of Fleet and Ray is correct It clearly shows that the
country ‘Sevcn-Konkaiias’ was ruled over by more than one
ruler. Besides, it is a well known fact that the country of
‘Konkana’ was finally annexed by Bhoja to his own kingdom.

A close examination of the views put forth, makes it clear
that Jayasimha had only conquered the territories, south of
Kolhapur and the countries lying north of it still remained to be
conquered In other words, the northern part of Kohkana which

lies north of Kolhapur (in the Bombay Presidency), still did not

Let us sec whether Bhoja had actually occupied the territory

identifies the ruler of the ‘Scvcn-Kohkaiias’ with a Kadamba king

thana’, modern-Goa, accepted the vassalage of Jayasiriiha II,

Bhoja' s war with the Cldliikyai of Kalyani
The control over the whole of Lata and Konkana may
have prompted Bhoja to over-come his natural enemy, Jayasimha

II (A.D. 1015-1042), the Western Chalukya king of Kalyani. But

before launching an invasion over Karnata country, he deemed it
advisable to consolidate his military strength by entering into
the alliances with some powerful kings, for a successful attack on
such a far-flung country of the South. He, therefore, sought for
allies. Ultimately, he found in Uajendra Chola I, Gangaikonda

anti Kaichari Garrgeya (be common cause of boscilicy for Jaysiriiba

II,' and thus the triple alliance could be finally executed. Stren-
gthened by the forces of Rajendra Chola 1 and Kalchuri Gahgeya,
Bhoja invaded the dominions of Jayasimha II. But it appears
that in this war, the allied forces had to suffer a defeat at the hand
of Jayasimha II, for an Inscription of the reign of Jayasimha II
dated A.D 1088, glorifies him wiih the glowing success against

the allied forces. It records ‘ he (Jayasimha) routed the elephant

squadrons of the Chola King Gahgeya and Bhojaraja”.’ Jaya-
simha Il’s victory over Bhoja is also attested to by the other ins-
cription of Irivrvcdanagdeva, a lord of Banvasi and feudatory of
Jayasiihha II. It states “Bhoja became a lotus to the Moon which
was Jayasithhi, who searched out desert, pursued ground down

“the rising of the glory of Jayasimhadeva has curbed (or

Since the middle of the 7th century A.D. Mfilwa was divided

into seven small states (frequently mentioned in the inscriptions

as the sapta-Malavas). The Pratiharas and more often the

Rastrnkutas raided the ‘seven-Mrdavas’ and annexed some of the
states. 'I hus Dantidurga subjugated the Malavas," Hence this
was one of tlic great deeds attributed to the Rasirakutas and
the Chfilukyas. Nothing more is meant by the phrase. Long
before Jayasimha’s time, Upendra, the Paramara king had
conquered Malavfi, a wcll-Knit Kingdom, If this is true, the
phrase ‘Malava confederacy* becomes meaningless, for we know
that at that time there were not seven great powers near Mfilava
who could have formed a confederacy. It is, therefore, clear
that the term 'contcdcracy of Mfilava’ is used for the above-
mentioned Triple alliance.
In this defensive operation Jayasimha seems to have been
assisted by one of his vassals named Bachirfija, who is eulogised
for having put the Malavayas to shame by his victorious afr^s.'*

Whatever might be the ease, it is cer(ain.lha( in this war,

Bhoja ultimately emerged victorious. The Kmyan inscription''
and Udaipur-prasasti'’ also record Bhoja’s vietbry over the

Karnata country. The Kalvan inscription clearly mentions “Bhoja

Dr. Ganguly disagrees with Bhoja’s final victory over Jayasimha,
and asserts ‘‘though Bhoja and his allies at first obtained some
victories, they were ultimately routed by Jayasirhha”.* As the
assertion of Ganguly is not based on any authentic evidence, it
may be rejected. D.B. Diskalkar writes” in the contest for power
among the Malwa Paramaras and the Deccan Cbalukyas going on
for years together, in the first quarter of the llth C.A D , both
the parties were successful in turns. Bhoja got some 'victory first,

then he with his confederacy was defeated by Jayasimha II (who

ascended the throne in A.D. 1015) before December A D. 1019.

But finally Bhoja emerged victorious as he did not take long time
to recover”.- Prof. H.C. Ray agrees with Diskalkar’s view and
writes "though Bhoja was defeated, yet he was not completely
destroyed by Jayasiriiha II”. He further adds, ‘‘the Kalvanplates
ofYasovarman are clear proofs to show Bhoja's final victory
over Jayasiriiha. ”3 Buhler also expresses the similar view” it is
quite pos'ible that in course of his expedition to the Soth, Bhoja
gained some advantage which may have been magnified by the
Mfilwa court-poets into a great victory over the Karnats”.^
The literary evidences speak of Bhoja’s victory over Karnafs,
but it is stated therein that it was Taila or Tailapa 11, and not
Jayasiriiha II, who was finally defeated and executed By Bhoja.
Rajavallabha and Merutunga both support this view. According
to Rajavallabha”, in his youth, when Bhoja took the reins of
government a drama was played before him on the insistence of
Kusumavati, the wife of Munja and the sister of Tailpa. The
drama depicted a scene of Munja’s persecution at the hands of
Tailpa. Bhoja infuriated by the show immediately launched an
expedition against Tailapa to avenge his uncle’s death. KusUma-
vatl, clad in a male dress persuaded Bhoja who captured Tailapa
and then executed him. After this Kusumavati became a Buddhist

nun and unto her death, she resided on the banks of river Saras-

of Gujarat begins from A.D. 1022, it would be advisable to suggest
that Bhlma's contemporary Chalukya ruler of Kalyan was Jaya-
sithha U. This is also supported by a verse given in the Vikraman-

kadcvacharita. “Filling the whole of Svarga (Heaven) with the

fame of his victories, Jayasiriiha received a garland of flowers
culled from the Parijala tree, from Indra’s own hands,”’
From the above verse, G.H. Ojha concludes, “it was probably
Chfilukya Jayasiihha H, who lost his life in a war against Bhoja”*"’
But venkataraman-Ayyar disagrees with this view and nrgucs-
“since, in the Hindu mythology, it is usually the 'Apsaras’ and not
Indra, who garland the persons dying in the battlefield”. The
assertion of Ayyangcr may be ruled out, for we do not find any

record of Jayasimha II after A;D. 1028. This indicates that

Jayasiriiha could not have won any battle against Bhoja after
A.D. 1028
Moreover, while Bhoja was bent upon achieving a victory
over the enemy he could not have breathed the air of peace till
he rooted out his enemy, Jayasiriiha II. Thus it appears that

52 Bho]a Paramara and his Times
Jayasiriiha was vanquished and probably he lost his life in the
battle Little before A.D. 1042 which is the last known date of
Jayasiriiha.
Dr. Ganguly writes "as the story given in the Bhojacharita
and Prabandhachintamani represents the gross chronological
inaccuracy, it should be rejected”. He further adds “it was
Jayasiriiha and not Vikramaditya who was the contemporary of
Bhima and who lost his life against Bhoja”^ Prof Ayyanger^
holds the same view. D. B. Diskalkar on the authority of
Bhojacharita, states that Bhoja attacked the Chalukya kingdom
immediately after his accession and defeated and killed both
Vikramaditya and his successor Jayasiriiha II.“ Buhler also holds
that a war was fought between Bhoja and Jayairiiha between
A.D 1011 and 1013.^ But as such, the latest date of
Vikramaditya V, the predecessor of Jaisiriiha is A D. 1014.
Buhler’s assertion loses its ground. This fact also goes against
the presumption of Diskalkar. Moreover, there is not even a
single evidence to show that any war was fought between Bhoja
and the Chfilukyas before A. D 1014.
By the time of Som’^vara I’s accession, Bhoja had become
old. The incessant wars with his neighbours made Bhoja tired
of his military career and must have told upon his health. Some-
svara I, the son and successor of Jayyasiinha II, ascended the
throne sometime in 1043. The quarrel soon sprang up with the
accession of Sonesvara. But it is certain that he could not invade
Malwa immediately after mounting the throne. The Tflakwada-
copper-plate records, “in A. D. 1047, the illustrious Jasoraja, the
appointed governor of Bhoja was ruling over the Sangamakheta
mandala, which covered the former Baroda state’’.^ It is now
apparent that to avenge the death of his father, Somesvara I,
must have launched a formidable attack over Malwa sometime
after A.D. 1047.

In the above passage Bilhana clearly mentions that in a battle
with Ahavamalia when Dhara, the glory of Paramara lords in
Malwa, was stormed, king Bhoja fled away after which the
Chalukyas took possession of the city. This fact may also be
called from a verse in the Kuvalayananda, a work on Alamkaras
by Appaya-Dixit. In this werse the poet presents a conversation
between the ocean and the river Narmada. He writes, “the water
of the river Yamuna became black owing to the soot-mixed tears
of the widows of Malwa who wept on the death of their husbands

who died in a battle against the king of Kuntala (Kuntale, svara)”i

In the above passage although there is no mention of the
name of any king yet it clearly speaks of Kuntaleivara’s raid over
Malwa.
The Chalukyan-inscriptions ranging from A. D. 1058 to 106?
A.D. also confirm the above-mentioned literary evidences. An
inscription of the reign of Somesvara, I tells us that the king
assailed even the city of Dhara, which proved invincible to early
kings.® The Sudi-inscription of the reign of Some svara mentions

this cataclysm they lost quite a large portion of their Southern
possessions. This fact is also corroborated in the Sitabaldi-

“under Somesvara I, the Chalukyas kingdom extended north-

beyond doubts that for a while the whole of Malwa Kingdom
went into direct possession of Somesvara I. The blow was
undoubtedly a severe one to the power of the Paramaras and it
paved the way to the neighbours to take the advantage of Bhoja’s
weak position Somesvara’s rule over Malwa did not last long,
and as soon as he left the Malwa, Bhoja re-occupied it and re-
established his authority over there.
WAR WITH INDRARATHA.
Bhoja appears to have come into conflict with Indraratha
at about the same time when he was indulged in a war with
Jayasiihha II, the Chalukya king of Kalyana. Dr. Ganguly
identifies Indraratha with a powerful king of YayStinagar or
Adinagar in Orissa. Kielhorn thinks that this Indraratha was
the same prince who was defeated by Rajendra Chola I, in his
Northern expedition.=> The Tiruvavalangadu inscription of the

sixth regnal year of Rajendra Chola (A.D. 1012-1042), dated

A.D. 1018 records that the king’s general, “Conquered Indraratha
and captured the country of the jewel of that lunar race, who

met him (in battle) with powerful elephants, horses and innumera-

ble foot-soldiers.’’^ The Tirumalairoefc inscription of the 13th
regnal year of the same Chola monarch dated A.D. and 1025
reports, “the King, with the confederate forces of the dual alliance

(i.e. with the forces of Bhoja and Kalachuri Gangeya) won the

According to Dr, Ganguly this vanquished king was of no mean
importance. We <io agree with Ganguly's view, for we know
that the confederate forces could do nothing more except simply
repelling the insasion of Indraralha.^
In this battle which was fought .some time between A.D.
1012 and 1018 JMioja played the second role, for he did not like
to attack such a far off southern city of Adinagar, Thus Adin-
agar, the Kingdom of Indraraiha went into the lap of Rajendra
Chola.’ In our opinion, one of the reasons for this .seems to be,
that the rest of the allied forces may have thought that Rajendra
Chol.i, being mighty southern k»ng would have conveniently
controlled the capital of Adinagar.
Dr. Ganguly identifies the city of .Adinagar with the famous
city of Nagar, the modern Mukhalingam in the Ganjam Dist-
rict, Andhra State which was some time also the capital of the
Gangc.s of Kaliuea.'' This .suggestion finds further .support from
the fact that in the list of the northern countries, successively
conquerred by Rajendra Chola I, it precedes the odda-Visuya,
which comprised the modern Orissa. We may here offer two
suggestions I*ir.<.tly, Indraratha might have been ruling over
Adinagar tis the Vassal of the K.nlingas, Secondly, as he is des-
cribed as being captured by Rajendra Chola I, simetime before
A.D. 1018, Jiis reverses at the hands of the Paramar.i Hhoja may
have taken place prior to that catastrophe.

elephant (led away from the bcttlefield. The Hindu forces with-

drew in confusion. Consequently, Anandapala’s land was plund-
ered and property burnt to ashes and 8000 Hindu soldiers were
Killed. Anandapala fled to Kashmir and his son Trilochanapala
was taken prisoner.^

force from that which Mahmud had so easily brushed aside on
his way to Multan.'*

we know that kings of Delhi and Ajmer were not so powerful as

All the above statements, it appears, are imaginary for there

any other Muslim invader. D C. Ganguly remarks, “Bhoja never
had to fight with the Muslim in defence of his own realm.”^
Prif Ray simply presumes, “it was probably in the region of
Kulu valley walley where Bhoja met and defeated some
detachment of the Turuska prince Mahmud of Ghazni when the
latter invaded Gwalior and Baghelkhand.’"’ But as there is no
direct evidence regarding Bhoja’s conflict with Mahmud, the
assertion of Ray cannot be taken as correct.
After the fall of Somnath in the middle of January, 1025,^
Mahmud did not stay for long in India. What was the reason for
Mahmud’s hasty retreat only a month and a half after the Soma-
nath conquest 1 What caused Mahmud to leave so rich a country
and to rush back through inhospitable Kachchha 7 In this con-
nection we may cite the statements of the later Muslim
Chroniclers.
Kitab-Zain-ul-Akbar, the earliest of the sources, states, “from
that place Mahmud turned back, the reason being that Paramdeo,
who was the king of the Hindus, was on the way, and Amir
Mahmud feared lest this great victory might be spoiled He did
not come back by the direct way but took a guide and marching
by the way of Mansura and the bank of the Sihun, went towards
Multan.^
Ibn-ul-Alhir,® writing two hundred years later gives the
same reason for Mahmud’s retreat. In this reference he quotes
Al-Gardizi, “the sultan raised his standard with the intention of

In the Tabtjat-i-Akbari, it is written, “in II. 417 (10?4) when

Mahmud was returning from Somanath, he heard that a Icing
named Paramadco stood ready with his formidable army to deal
with Mahmud on his return. But Mahmud did not like to wage
a war with him and so he returned to Multan through Sindh,”’
If we put reliance on the above sources, we find that it was
owing to the terror of P.iramdco that Mahmud beat a hasty retreat.
Let us see who this P.aramdeo was.
Firishta’ identifies P.ir.imdco with Bhima of Gujarat. Gan-
guly" accepts the view of Firishia. Hut in A.D 1025 Bhima
was the king of a small principality which was not then known as
Gujarat. He was merely a Mdt.ir.ljadhir.'ija while Bhoja was
Parame. <ara, c-scrciving imperial sway over Anarta, Khejaka,

ally. Paramdeo, therefore, c.in only be Bhoja, a corruption of

62 Bho]a Paramara and his Times
by A.D. 1025 Bhoja was an unchallenged master of a vast empire.
Alberuni also writes that Bhoja was the ruler of Malwa.i
Bhoja had a regular standing army. He is said to have despat-
ched troops to the Raja of Delhi for his war against Turks.=“
Although on account of the retreat of Mahmud of Ghazni,
Bhoja had no opportunity to cross swords with him, there is no
doubt that. he was sufficiently powerful to force the ruler of
Ghazni to retreat. His contribution to the defence of north India
from the onslaughts of Turks is unmatched and deserves notice.
His deeds, in this regard were so great that they found their way
into Gahadvala records of even much late dates.^
But Mahmud’s attack on India, was possibly an irreparable
loss for the Aryavarta. It weakened the integral power of India
and accelerated the regional feelings. Kalhana’s Raja Tarahgint,
Bilhana’s Vikramankadevacharita Hemachandra’s Dvya srya-
Kevya and Nandi’s Ramacharita are regional histories in which
the author glorifies the respective kings and the regions ruled over
by them. V

TRIPURI. 1^3 30 6
Kalachuri-Gangeyadeva, the ruler of Tripuri, was contempor-
ary of Bhoja, the Parmara ruler of Malwa. The earliest known

date of Gahgeyadeva is A D. 1019 (V.S. 1067J.* He is said to

have ceased to reign shortly before A.D. 1042.° Gahgeya was the
most powerful king of the dynasty and it was during his reign
that the Kalachuris, for the first time, attained an imperial posi-
tion.
The epigraphic evidence shows the long-drawn hostility bet-
ween the paramaras of Malwa and the king Kalachuris of Tripuri,

military excursions Vakpati plundered its capital Tripuri which
was vacated by the ruling sovereign Yuvaraja^ II. But no
evidence is found regarding the enmity between Kokkala, the son
and successor of Yuvaraja II, and the Paramaras. It seems that
the former maintained peaceful relations with the latter.
Like his father, Gahgeyadeva, at least in the early part of his
reign remained the ally of Paramara Bhoja. It has already been
discussed that Gangeyadeva organised a confederacy with the
Paramara Bhoja and Rajendra Chola I and invaded the Deccan,
which was then ruled over by the Chfilukya Jayasimha 11, This
friendship between Bhoja and Gahgeyadeva lasted some years,
for the mahoba inscription records Chandclla Vidyadhara’s
victory over Bhoja and his ally the Kalachuii king." We may,
therefore, tsuggest that they remained friendly to each other at
least upto A.D. 1025.
But the peacctul relations between Bhoja and Gahgeyadeva
did not last long as is proved by the epigraphic and literary
evidences.
Prof. R. Saihianathaier remarks, '"‘it was probably after the
victory over the Somavamsis by Gahgeyadeva that he assumed the

title ‘Trikatihgadhipati’ (lord of three Kalihgas) and that the

relations between the two houses became hostile, as Gahgeyadeva
subsequently quarreled with his former ally Bhoja, who inflicted a
defeat on him”“’
The Udaipur Prasasti, records Bhoja’s victory over Gahgeya-
deva. Although the name of the vanquished ruler is now given
in the Pra.vasti, it is beyond doubt that it was Gfihgcyadc.'a who
was Bhoja’s contemporary Kalachiiri. king on the throne of Chedi-
kingdom. Thus the Chedi-king referred to in the Prasasti was, in all

In verse 3 of the Dhar Prasasti of Arj unavarmen which is an

glorious king Bhojadeva, who was able to defeat (his enemies by

leaping arrows... (and who) had desires speedily fulfilled for a

long time at the festive defeat of Gangeya”.=
The last few words of this verse imply that king Bhojadeva
defeated a prince named Gangeya, just as the epic-hero Arjuna
Killed Bhima, whose metronymic was Gangeya. As the well-
known Paramara king Bhojadeva of Dhara was reigning in the
first half of the eleventh century,’ his enemy Gaiigeya, according
to Hulfzhcha, must be ideniical with the Kalachuri kin’g Gangeya
of Tripuri '' Prof Kielhorn identifies Gangeya, of the Prasasti
with Kalachuri king Gangeyadeva, whose reign falls in the same
period ’
Merutunga also admits Bhoja’s superiority over the Chedi
king. According to him “the warrior monarch of Chedi trembled
with fear of Bhoja’s army.”® As has been discussed above, Bhoja’s
contemporary Chedi king was Gangeyadvea, so it is an indisputed
fact that he was defeated by Bhoja.
Pran Nath Shukla, on the basis of verse 3 of the Dhar Pra-

king Gangeyadeva (Vikramaditya) of Tripuri, such as God Krsna

The hostility between the Kalachuris and Bhoja did not cease
even after the death of Gangeyadeva. Soon after his accession.

Kama (1041-1072), the son and successor of Gangeyadeva, may

ave thousht it his duty to avenge his father’s defeat from Bhoja’s
and. In this way the hostility between the two houses continued
althrough Kama’s reign.
Merutuhga narrates^ a very interesting story regarding
Kama’s challenge to the might of Bhoja. According to him.
the king of Dahala was a great warrior and diplomat. He was
a suzerain over 136 petty chiefs. Once Kama dispatched an agent
m the court of Bhoja with the message "O Bhoja, you are known
or the construction of 104 great buildings, and for the writing
o same number of Prabandhas and verses. You also possess 104
titles I, therefore, desire that either by defeating me, in the war or
by conversion in the religion and charity you should assume the
105th title or I should become the sovereign over 137 kings”.
Hearing ihis news, Bhoja at first trembled but after his humble
request Kama, the king of Kail accepted Bhoja’s proposal th^t
each of .hem should begin to construct a palace 50 hands high
separately, m the same time and whosoever, completed it before-
would be victorious. Thenceafter it was agreed upon that
e ing V ho was defeated should come forward for the assistance
mounting on an elephant and giving up all theroyal insignia.
Accordingly, Karpa and Bhoja undertook thevor
o construction in Kaii and Ujjain respectively. Karpa was
able to complete the construction. Bhoja, however, went back

Prof. R. D. Banerji, on the basis of the Kalvan inscription
and other inscriptions of the Paramfiras and Haihayas of Tripuri
opines that Bhoja had to suffer a crushing defeat and, most
probably, he fell on the ground while he was trying to stem the
tide of a combined invasion by Karna, the king of Tripuri and
Bhima, the Chalukya king of Gujarat." This fact is also found in
the Nagpur inscription of the Paramfira king Laksmadeva The
inscription records, “Udayfiditya, a kinsman of Bhoja, cleansed
away the clouds of dilTicultics which spread over Dhara after the
death of Bhoja”^
Prof. H. C. Ray takes the account of the Nagpur inscription
as correct. According to him, ‘The mention of Karna’s invasion
in this inscription as one of the factors that brought about
Bhoja’s downfall, shows that the Udaipur prasasli was right in
mentioning the ChedTs’vara as one of the adversaries of the
Paramara king.* Ray, on the authority of the Jain chroniclers,
states that Bhoja died during a joint attack on Dhara by Chalukya
Bhima I and Kariia, the king of Dahala. He further adds,
“besides Bhima and Karna, the league also comprised, the
Chalukya Somesvara I and, probably. Lata Prince Trilochanapala

for whom we have the date §aka 972 (C. 1051 A. D,)”.®
