Bhoja's War With the Chandellas of Jejakbhukti or Bundelkhand
in the North East of Malwa.
After Mahmud’s departure from India, for the time being,
the countrj except Punjab became virtually free from the Muslim
incursions. This temporary lull paved the way for Bhoja to turn
his attention to the consolidation of his empire. Having
completed his expedition in the South, Bhoja diverted his
attention towards his northern neighbours. The kingdom of the
Chandellas of Jejakbhukti bordered the Paramara territory on the
worth east.
In the middle of the 10th century A. D. the Chandella
Yaiovarman was fighting with the Paramaras of Malwa. YoSo-

varman w;is followed by Dhanga (A. D. 950-999) and Ganda

{999-1024 A. D.) respectively. Gaiida was succeeded by his son

Vidyadhara (1025-1040 A. D.), who was known for his bravery,

and is saic to have^ defeated and killed the Pratihara king Rajya-
pala of Kaiauj.* The Kalachuri Gangeyadeva had also to yield
to his milit try force.'
It was rather difficult for Bhoja to carry out his military
operations freely in the north until the power of Vidyadhara had
been shattered.* This idea led Bhoja to attack the Chandella
kingdom. In this invasion, Bhoja was probably assisted by

succeeded in extending his influence in the Kulu valley by taking
advantage of the weakness of Vidyadhara’s successors ®

Bhoja's Conquest of Kanauj in the east
In spite of his failure to capture the fort of Gwalior, Bhoja
pushed his arms further north, towards Kanauj.^ According to
Dr. Ganguly, “Bhoja appears to have cherished the ambitions of
conquering Kanauj.” Since the collapse of the imperial power of
the Gujara-Pratihfiras Kanauj was passing through a trinsitional
period. In the middle of the 10th C.A.D., the Paramara
Vairisimha II wrested Malwa from Pratihara Mahcnilrapala II

(946 A D.) or from Dcvapala (949 A.D ), the successor of the

former. Since then the Government of Kanauj had ocen fast
declining in pover and its kingdom was reduced to a sntall terri-
tory around its capital. Dcvapala was followed by \'ijayapala

(960 A.D.) and Rajyapala (A.D. 1025) respectively. The last-

mentioned king, as has been noticed above, was kil ed by the
Chandella Vidyadhara. He was succeeded by Trilochanapala

(1027 A.D.) and Yasahpala (1036 A.D.), after which tncre is no

trace of the Gurjara Pratihara dynasty.'^
Both Dr. Ganguly” and K.M. Munshi" opine that Bhoja
defeated the Pratihara kihg Yasal.iapala icfcrrcd to in the Kara
inscription.® But as the name of Ya^ahapala is not found
in the genealogical table of the Pratiharas, it is inconvin:ibIe that
Bhoja defeated him. On one place, Dr. Ganguly idcntifu.s Yasalta

was assisted by Satyaraja, the Paramara ruler of Vagada and who
was Bhoja’ s feudatory
The epigraphic and literary evidences are unanimous in testi-
fying to Bhoja’s victory over the king of Gurjaras. This victory
of Bhoja is obviously recorded in the Paramara inscriptions.* The
Udaipur Prasasti® tells us that Bhoja conquered the king of the
Gurjaras. In the Kalvan inscription* Bhoja’s conquest over
Kanauj is clearly recorded. Merutufiga narrates that Bhoja
conquered the king of kanauj.’ On these grounds K.M. Munshi
asserts, “after this defeat the Pratiharas were completely extingui-
shed from the map of ruling dynasty of India and its place was
taken by the Paramaras’’.*
Undoubtedly, Bhoja, for the lime being became the master
of imperial Kanauj, but it is equally true that his rule over Kanauj
did not last for long. The Rastrakutas of Kanauj. it appears,
were probably ruling over Kanauj in A.D. 1050.® Gopala, the
4th ruler of this dynasty is referred to as the king of Gadhiphra,
i.e. Kanauj. This shows that Bhoja’s rule over Kanauj was tem-
porary and he was unable to have any material gain from there.’*

PolHical Achievtftents of Bhoja 75
Within a decade, fortune had turned against Gopala and
Kanauj first passed into the hands of Kalachuri Karna and in the
later part of the 11th century it was conquered by Chandradeva,
the Gahadvala king of Kanauja. The Basahi plate of Govind-
chandra, dated V.S. 1161 = 1104 A.D. states, “in the lineage of
Gahadvala, there was a victorious king, the son of Mahipala,
named, Candradcva, who when on the death of king Bhoja and
king Karna, the world became troubled, came to the rescue and
became the king and established his capital at Kanyakubja.i
According to Ganguly, “if this verse is read in the light of the
information supplied by the Udaipur Pra^asti’ it suggests that
Bhoja established for a time, some kind of political authority
over Kanauj. But at the close of his reign, the country passed
under the sway of Karna, after whose death anarchy and disorder
prevailed until Chandradeva of the Gahadvala dynasty assumed
the sovereignty and succeeded in restoring peace and order.”®

Bho}a's JPar with Cliamba Prince

Bhoja is said to came into conflict with the ruling dynasty
of Chamba, in the Punjab, during his northern campaign. The
Bhojacharita contains a very interesting story, which runs, “Once

a yogi (ascetic) came to Malwa from Kashmir. He Transformed

its ruler Bhoja, into a parrot and himself became the king of
Dhara. On one occasion, while the cnchautcd prince was flying
over the forest of Chandravatl, he w'as caught up by a Bhila.
The Bhila presented the parrot to Cbandrascna, the king of that
locality. Chandrasena kept the bird under the care of his young
daughter, by whose grace Bhoja regained the human form and got
back his throne. Subsequently, the yogi was killed by
Chandrasena.”®
Col. Tod remarks on the above story, “if any historical fact
is meant under this allegory, it would probably be that an invader
from the north deprived Bhojadeva of his kingdom and that he
fled in disguise to the forests wherefrom he was taken by the wild

and as the chaniba copper-plates record Sahavabana’s victory over

his enemies of Kuruk§etra (The sacred place in the Karnal district

of Punjab) and assuming of the litle ‘Karivar?a’ for him,^ it indi-

cates that Bhoj.i may have met with this prince on that historic

Suggests that his overlord had to suffer some preliminary reverses
in the battle, though finally he emerged victorious."
But the above indentification of Sahavahana of Tilakawada
plate with the Ohamba prince Salahavahandcva, docs not appear
to be correct. As we know that the Chamba copper plate came
to an end in the middle of the 10th C. A D., Sahavahana cannot
be Bhoja’s contemporary ruler of Chamba. Moreover, even if we
accept that it was Chamba princs it does not seem probable that
such a patty chief, may have ever ventured to launch a formidable
attack on Bhoja. Here, if we want to attach some importance to
the chroniclers, we may say that owing to the friendship with
Kashmirian king, Bhoja may have despatched his general
SQraditya against Chamba prince Sahavahana, at the former’s
request. But as Sahavahana was no match to the Paramfira force
he was easily defeated and killed by Siiraditya. Moreover, it is
no wonder, if Karna, who was an accepted general of the time and
who ascended the Kalachuri kingdom sometime between A. D.
1034 and 1042.^ may have despatched a force under Sahavahana
against the Paramara Bhoja, to avenge the defeat of his father
Gahgeyadeva. If this be true, wc come to know from the Tilak-
wada plate that, for the time being, Bhoja’s might was stormed
by Sahavahana, which was reinstated by his general ^uraditya
sometime before A.D. 1047.
Bhoja’’!: war with the Chahamams of ^akanibhari and Napol
In the first half of the eleventh century A.D. the main

Chahamana dynasty was divided into the two houses, namely (1)

Sakambharl and (2) Nadol. The former was ruling over ^akam-

bharl or modern Sambhar (Ajmer) and the later over Nadol or

Bhoja’s warlike activities on the north brought him first into
direct conflict with the Chahamanas of Sakambharl. The Surjana-
charita, which along with the Hammiramahakavya and the
Prabandhakosa’s genealogy, knows Vakpati 'II as the son- of
Govindaraja, the Chhahamana ruler of Sapdalaksa, knows him as
Vallabha, credits him with the defeat of Bhoja of - Malwa and a
ruler of Chedi.' K M. Munshi on the authority of Prithvirajavijaya,
thinks that Chahmana Vakpati II, was. then on the throne of
Sakambbarl and he was defeated by Bhoja. But Dr. D.C.
Ganguly disagrees with Munshi’s identification and suggests that
it was Viryaiaraa, the son and successor of Vakpati II, who was
defeated by Bhoja.® Dr. Dasharatha Sharma opines, “though the
Surjanacharita provides no chronological difficulty, the lateness of
the source and the fictitious details with which it embellishes
the story, make the information unreliable.*. It is, therefore
indisputed that it was Viryarama, the younger brother and
successor of Vakpati who was slaim in battle by Bhoja.® We agree
with this fact. Bhoja’s victory over Viryarama was complete,
for the panegyrist of his descendant writes that his glory was

destroyed by Bhoja of Avanti (C 1010-1055 A.D.)® who, if the

information supplied by the Surjanacharita be true, had already
been fighting with the Chauhaniis in the previous reign. Paramara

forces perhaps occupied ‘Sakambhari’ for a while as a result of

The victory over tlie main branch of the Chahamanas, may
very well have prompted Bhoja to attack the Chahamana king
Baliraja of Nadol holding sway over southern Marwar.
Chahamana king Baliraja of Nadol is said to have defeated
Paramara VakpitiMuhja. After this there was, for sometime a
cessation of hostilities between the two houses.-
Baliraja was succeeded by Mahindra, Asvapala. Ahila and

Anahilla, of whom (he last two boast of defeating Bhima of

Anahilavada.3 These princes, according to Dr. Ganguly, “though
ruling over a .small territory, maintained a military force strong
enough to cope successfully with their ambitious neighbours
Tlic Sundha hill inscription of Chachigadeva. dated V.S. 1319
=A.D. 1262, tells us, ‘ Anahilla, the paternal uncle of Ahila, was
one of the most successful and energetic rulers of the Nadol line.”
The verse I7 of the same inscription glorifies him with the defeat
and slaying of ‘Sadha’ a general of Bhoja, the strongest ruler of
northern India.'’ Here the slaying of Malwa commander is said
to have followed immediately after the capture of Sakambhari by
Anahilla. This clearly indicates some connection between the two
events. As Bhoja was an enemy of the ‘Sakambhari Chauhan too,
what we naturally conclude from the almost simultaneous capture
of ‘Sakambhari, and the slaying of Bhoja’s general Sadha by
Anahilla, is that the latter came to the assistance of his clansmen,
captured ‘Sakambhari from the Paramaras, and soon after killed
the Paramara, commander in the battle that followed as a result of
Anahilla’s coup.®
Dr. Ganguly explains the consequences of this battle in the
following way, “the fortune was no more favourable to Bhoja than
it had been to his uncle Muilja. Ultimately he was defeated and

the defeat was a great collapse and disgraceful blot in the shining
political career of Bhoja. It left an irreparable loss to his might
and dominion. Consequently, the formerly conquered territory of
Sakambharl again slipped into the hands of Chahamana dynasty.
Thus instead of some gain, Bhoja had to lose much.
BHOJA'S WAR WITH HjS WESTERN NEIGHBOUR CHILUKYA
BHlMA I THE RULER OF GUJARAT.
BhTma played an important role in the downfall of Paramara
Bhoja, which for a time left the Paramaras totally powerless.^ The
Vadnagar Prasasti of Kumarpala records Bhima’s victory over
Bhoja.’ The chaplain Somesvara in his KTrttikauraudi states the
defeat of Bhoja by Bhima. According to him, Bhima once defe-
ated Bhoja and captured him. But impressed by the virtues of
the latter, he immediately released him.’ The Vastupala Tejaljpala
Prasasti states, “it was proper that upon his attack, the Goddess
Bhoja’s heart, the Goddess of learning his mouthand
the swords his hands’” Arisiriiha and Balachandra also des-
cribe Bhoja’s defeat by Bhima.’ Jayasithha Suri writes, “at the
siting in the forest of his greatness, the arm of Bhoja faded away
like^the lotus and that very properly. Merutufiga also accepts
Bhoja s defeat be Bhima. He narrates, “on one accasion when
Bhoja was worshipping his family Goddess in a temple in the
suburbs of Dhara, he was surprised and nearly captured by a party
of Gujarati cavalry.’” A copper plate grant of king Trilochana-

“just as soon ns the rising of the moon, the lotus withers, so did

has conquered the lord of Dhara (ckadharapati) with a two-

bladed swords (dvidhara), what wonder is there, that he (BHiMA)

strong) = “The defeat of the great Pararaara monarch, in the

words of Dr. A K. Mazumdar, “was naturally considered the high
watermark of BhTmn’s glory
Tn the presence of so many strong evidences, we think it pro-
bable, that Bhoja may have suffered some preliminary reverses at
the hands of Chalukya Bhima I. But it is rather unbelievable
that the defeat was so disastrous that Bhoja’s arm faded away.
Had it been been the fact Hcmachandra would not have forgotten
to refer this event in his Dva^raya Kavya, which must have added
to the glory of the ancestors of his patron king.
After Bhoja’s defeat by Bhima, we find a temporary full in
the much-strained relations. This suggests that both the houses
may have compromised to maintain friendly relations with each
other. Merutuhga gives a detailed information regarding the
peaceful relations between them. He interalia states that Bhima
and Bhoja were contemporaries and there was a league of friend-
ship.* From Merutuhgs we learn that Bhima maintained an amba-
ssador at Bhoja’s court whose name was Damara.®

80 Bhoia Paramara and his Times
However, the friendly relations between Bhoja and Bhima did
not last long. According to a legend preserved in the Prabandha-
chintamani Bhoja wanted to break with Durlabha’s successor,
Bhima^ The reasons for this were many. The first and foremost
among them was the hereditary enmity. Bhoja could not forget the
idea of punishing Bhima for the sin of the latter’s father’s invasion
over Malwa and causing the death of Bhoja’s father, Sindhuraja.
Secondly, as Bhima was the greatest rival of Bhoja, he was in
search of the opportunity for taking arms against Bhoja, Thirdly,
Bhima was growing ambitious, and was. therefore, favourably
inclined towards Karri, the son of Gahgeya.® Lastly, it was Bhoja’s
policy of self expansionism which worsened the relations more. It
made the atmosphere hostile in the court of Dhara against Bhima,
the ruler of Gujarat.^ Moreover, it was essential for Bhoja to make
his position safer by crushing the growing might of Bhima, who
was challenging Bhoja in almost all the spheres e g. in skill of war,
patronage of scholars, religion and in the constructive works and
so on. Thinking so, Bhoja broke out the existing pease and sent
a gatha to Bhima through a diplomatic agent, to the following
effect. “The lion who cleaves with ease the heads of elephants,
whose might is noise abroad.
“Deigns not to fight with the dear, yet non can truly say, he
keeps with him in peace’’.
In response to the above gatha, Bhima despatched a gatha to

the following matter;

“Bhima by fate was created to destroy the sons of Andhaka.
Gan be who fought with hundred foes now shirk from three who
are but one ?’’*
The exchange of these gatha may have added fuel ^to the
flame of hostility going on between the two houses. Shortly after,
Bhoja determined to put an end to these peaceful relations by
waging a war against Bhima. In a certain year, when Gujarat

was badly hit by famine, Bhoja launched an expedition against

During Bhoja’s campaign in the south, the enmity between
Bhima and Bhoja ceased for a while, Merutuhga asserts that

during this period Bhima visited the ;court of Dhara, He states

that Damara. the diplomatic agent of Bhima at the court of
Dhara often used to praise the splendour of the court of Dhara
before Bhima, who, in consequence became anxious to sec the court
of Dhara. But it was not possible because of the enmity. Damara.
once sagaciously disguised Bhima in a form of a betel bearer and
went to the court of Dhara to offer some valuable presents to
Bhoja. At this Bhoja hinted that he should bring his lord Bhima
with him. Lateron, Bhoja questioned Damara about the age and
shape of Bhima. Damara replied that in shape and beauty his
suzerain was matching with the beiel-be.irer standing nearby But
Bhoja anyhow suspected the betel-bearer. Seeing this, Damara
ordered the betel-bearer to bring from his room the rest of the
presents to offer to Bhoja. Then Damara, to gain time, began to
describe, in a dilatory fashion, the excellences of the gift. At this
Bhoja impatiently asked Damara ‘ How much longer is your betel-
bearer going to linger ?”
Damara with a smile replied...
“At the end of every twelve yoganas from Dhara to
Anahiiavada stand horses ready littered, camels can go a yojini

in one Indian hour (of 24 minutes

This ironical reply was enough to convince Bhoja that Bhima
had Bed to his capital, Alahilaved. According to Vfjrutuhgi, it
was after this incident that Bhoja ordered his general Kulachandra

to march against the Aiiahilavads.^ But the story seems to be

about it. Moreover, being so cautious about the hostile attitude,
Bhiraa would not have played with his fate, taking a marginal
risk.
Infuriated by the mischiefs played by Bhima, Bhoja in a cold

weather, {w hen Bhtma was far away in the region of Sindhu),

despatched his general Kulachandra, with an army to revagfi
Anahilwad Kulachandra got a fair measure of success. In this
expedition, Anahilvvada was captured. After sowing the cowries
at the gate of the King's palace, the conqueror returned to Mala,

carrying wit i him a letter of victory (Jayapatra), written by Bhima’s

general, in charge of the town.'^

Knowir g about the cowries, Bhoja remarked;

“The ttxes of this country (paid in cowries by the poor) shall

go to Gajarat, why did you not sow charcoal ’’.®
Most p obably, an iron pillar of Victory was set up in Dharii
in honour O’’ the victory over Anahilawada.*' Accoiding to K.M.
Munsi it i.s not certain as to whether Kulachandra marched
against Anahilavvflda in the time of Durlabharaja or in the time
of Bhima “ But as we do not find any evidence in favour of
Kulachandra’s march during the reign of Durlabharaja, it is pro-
bable that this incident may have happened sometime, during
Bhima’s reign. It is wonder, if Bhoja had sent Kulachandra to sake
Anahilawads, the capital of Gujarat, in view of taking revenge of
his own, ar the hands of Bhima I. Prof. H C. Ray also asserts
that ihe kingdom of Gujarat was threatened by an invasion of the
Paramaras.' After his return from Sind, Bhima knew about the
insults put to his general by Kulachandra. Immediately, he

marched against the the junior branch of the Paramfiras of Abu.

Being hardpressed by Bhima, he fled to Malwa for asylum.
^
Bhima entrusted the administration of Abu to Vimal of the
Pragvata family.-
Bhima*s conquest and control over the Paramaras of Abu was
an open challenge to the might of Bhoja, who could not tolerate
this inglorious incident and strained every nerve to reinstate his
authority over Abu. Ultimately, he succeeded in ousting Bhima
from there and re-established his authority over Abu, This victory
of Bhoja over Bhima is well recorded in the Udaipur Prasasti,
which describes that the king of Gujarat was conquered by his
mercenaries.-'* Prof. H C. Ray opines, “as the Nagpur inscription
refers to the Gujarat as another enemy of Bhoja, there is little
doubt that this Gurjara-prince is to be identified with BliTma I,
who is mentioned in the same verse as one of the chief enemies of
Bhoja”.'* Buhler also agrees with Ray’s identification and asserts,

“Bhima was Bhoja’s contemporary Gurjara king (A.D. 102! , 102/,

1363-64), the Gurjara-prince referred to in Prasasti was no other

than Bhima.”** This victory is also acknowledged by one of the
later Prabandhakaras of Gujarat. Mcrutuhga also narrates the
stories of several conflicts between Bhima and Bhoja.*'* Now
Hcmachandra’s silence regarding any fight of Bhima with Bhoja is
quite understandable. Being a court-poet could nut speak of the
reverses which his masters’ grandfather had’ suficred. K. M.
Munshi is quite certain about Bhlnia’s defeat."
A summary of the views putforth, reveals the fact that in all
probability, Bhima was once defeated by Bhoja. Besides, if the
narratives of Meruntuiiga bear any authenticity, it will be advis-
able to suggest that Bhima always apprehended a Paramara

invasion But it is equally true that Bhima neither accepted the
vassalage of Bhoja, nor assisted him in his campaigns It is also

Bhima, however, became confident that he alone woiild not
be able to cope with the growing power of the Paramaras. He,
therefore, ’ooked for an ally. Fortunately for him, Bhoja also
antagoniseil his eastern neighbours, the Kalachuries of Tripuri,
by waging war against Gangeyadeva, who had to yield to bis
arms. Now Bhima found a good ally in Gangeyadeva’s son
Karna against Bhoja and jointly invaded Malwa.i Prof. Rdy
writes, “his league was an answer to the confederacy of Malwa,

for the en ;mies of the Paramaras succeeded in forming another

league. The Chalukya Bhima I, the Kalachuri LaksmI-Karna
and the Chalukya Somesvara were the chief parties. Beside them,
this league may have also included the Lata prince Trilochanapala,

for whom v'e have the date ‘Saka 972 (C. 1051 A.D.).”^ How long

it’s membeiship extended is not definitely known but it is certain
from MerituAga, account that in the struggle that followed,

attack on E'hara by the Chalukya Bhima I. But Dr. Ganguly

the war against his neighbours in the east and in the west. It is
possible that Karna may have attacked Dhara from the east and
Bhima from the west and during this campaign Bhoja lost his

forces could not occupy Dhar.=i. It was after Bhoja’s death that
Malwa fell into the hands of the Kalachuris and Chalukyas in
A.D. 1055.'’ This fact is supported by Merutunga who writes,

and older Gujarati writers should both have forgotten to notice
an event which must have rcllcclcd so much glory to the ancestors
of their patrons.’’-’ We think it probable that Bhima .ind Karna
may have come in terms with each other as the situat .on was in
their favour, firstly because of Some svara’s victory over Dhiira
which and shakened and disclosed the weak possition of Bhoja
and secondly because Bhoja grew old by this time, and there
was no one in the royal family .strong enough to control the
situation. But in the absence of any strong evidence it would be
erroneous to accept Merutunga’s story that BhTma and Karna
jointly attacked Dharfi and caused the destruction of Bhoja. In
this way, we come to know that it wai probably after Bhoja’s
death that Karna took the wealth of Bhoja,'* for Bhoja’s successor

Jayasimha was too weak to match with Karna’s force ^ MerutuAga
also tells us that after Bhoja’s death Kalachuri Karija stormed
the gates of Dhara and sacked wealth lying therein.^ He adds
that hearing this, either to have the half Share in the property or
to present Karna’s head before him, Bhima ordered his minister

for peace and war (sandhivigrahikal. Damara proceeded and

with 3?. soldiers, captured Kama while he was sleeping in a
temple. At this, Kama presented him the gold mandapika which
he had wrested from Bhoja. Hemachandra Suri also gives the
same story.^ If these stories have any basis, we arrive at the
concluston that there was no joint attack of Bhima and Kama
which caused Bhoja’s death. It was, probably, after Bhoja’s
death that the enemies rushed towards Dhara and sacked it.
This view is also supported by Vikramankadevacharita.'*
Thus ended the long military career of Bhoja, in the words
of Dr. D C. Ganguly who was admittedly a king of superior merit
and great valour. He was undoubtedly an able ruler. He enlar-
ged the kingdom he had inherited to three times it’s original size
by aDne.xing the countries such as Lata, Karnatas, Konkana and
Chedis.
The Udayapur Prasasati sums up the military activities of
Bhoja in one ‘sloke’ Seeing the Karnatas, the lord of Lata, the king
of Gurjaras, the T uruskas chief among whom were the lords of
Chedi, Indraratha, Toggal and Bhima conquered by bis merce-
naries alone, his hereditary warriors thought only of the strength
of their arms, not the numbers of the fighters. The most admirable
deed of Bhoja was to save the country from the Muslim onslaughts.

Bhoju was invincible to his foes and was unmatched in milit-
ary strength. The ‘Nagpur stone inscription’’ of tiic rulers of
Mdhva admires Bhojs’s military power in the following way.
“His army distinguished by the sharp sound of roi-ring Kettle
drums by the thunder of loud jharajharas, by a multitude of
shrill daniarus, and by tabor.s, heard above the tumult, roamed
filling and world with its noise, over the whole earth, thrown into
confusion under the foot steps of his rutty elephants.” This shows
that Bhoja had a standing army of high rank which comprised
foot-soldiers, chariots, horses and elephants. Bur we do not know
whether naval force e.sistcd or not.
Mcrutunga beautifully dcstribes the valour of Bhoja. He

narrates that when Bhoja went out for conquest, “the Choja ):mg

enters into the Besom of the sea, the AnJhraking icpairs to a

hole in a mighty mountain ;

The king of Karnata docs not wear hi.s turban, the king of
Gujarat frequents the mountain torrents. Chedi, that warlike
monarch flickers with weapons, the king of Knuyaki.bja is here
bent double.
The bards sang before the king ‘ O, Bliojn all the kings are

di<;tractcd with burden of the fear of the advance of the army

only.
On the floor, of thy prison, the angry wrangle about place on
which to lay their beds.
Has increased in the night among these mutual rivals who
thus dispute, “The king of Kankana sleeps in the corner, Late

near the door, Kalinga in the courtyard ;

You, arc new arrival my father also used to abide this level
spot.’’”
This description, according to Dr. Ganguly, though poetic
in expression, contains a great deal of truth. A brief survey of
the king’s military career will show that he came into conflict with
almost all the chiefs referred to in the above verse. His successes

88 bh'ija Paramara and Ids Times
in the early years of his reign were sweeping and' put him' into
possession of vast territories.'
In addition to these great wars, Bhoja fought petty wars also
which were conducted not by him in person, but by his generals.
This is clear fiom Kulachandra’s attack over Anahilavada in the
absence of Bhima, the Chalukya king of Gujarat and from an
inscription of the Naddula Chauhanas, which claims that a general

(dandadhisa) of Bhojadeva, called Sadha, was defeated or slain

by Anahilla “
Just as recorded in the Dhar prasasti,^ an inscription of
Dcvapala also compares Bhoja with Srikrishna.
“There was a king, great like Kamsa’s conqueror, an orna-
ment of the Paramara family, the glorious Bhojadeva, who
occupied the surface of the earth by the van of his army,. When
the lustre of the moonlight of his glory over- flowed the lap of the-
regions, there closed the day lotuses of the glory accumulated by
hostile kings.”*
A brief summery of all the sources, shows that except
Rajaraja, the Chola of Tanjoie, who was the only other empetoi
of equal importance and was in terms of cordial friendship with
him, Bhoja conquered and overcame all the hereditary enemies.
He emerged a mighty and invincible figure moving mysterious
dignity. His imperial sway extended from Chamba and Tbanes-
war in the north, to Krishna and the Tungabhadra in the
south and from Dwarka to Kanauj Among ‘Sakambhari
Naddula, Medapata, Patana with its vassals of Kachebha and
Saurashtra, Lata and Konkana, but also Gangeya and his son
Kama of Chedi, Chalukya Jayasimha II and his son Some’sware
of Kalyani. He was also the master of imperial Kanauj, no
longer the seat of an empire. In this wise, the old Gurjaradeas
was for the time being brought together.®

Bhoja, undoubtedly, was an astute general and a great empire
builder the world has ever produced. In valour and the art of
peace and war, he was no less than Chandragupta Maurya, and
Samudragupta, the emperor of Ancient India. He embodied in
himself all the qualities of a successful military leader and thus
ranks among the greatest kings of the world. The suppression of
all the intransigent elements in ihe capital, the complete reversal
of the traditional policy of making tendatories out of vanquished
enemies, and the genius with which he dominated the brilliant
court of Dhara by maintaining the power in his own hands, shows
rare perception and ability.
Bhoja was not only a blind fighter but was also a diplomat
of high rank Reestablished matrimonial alliances with different
powerful chiefs of the time. We have been able to know the
names of only four queens of Bhoja till now. Liladevi or Lilavati,^
was the chief queen and Padmavati, the daughter of the lord of
Kuntala,2 Chandramukhi, the Anga princess and Kamala were his
other queens.^
These alliances may have facilitated Bhoja in bis expeditions.
Besides, Bhoja’s able generals who were, probably, there in
number also played a significant role in building Bhoja’s success-
ful military career. These generals were Kulachandra', Sadha'^
Suraditya.®
Bhoja is blamed by some scholars for not being able to
acquire any new territory except that of Kohkana, in spite of his
innumberable battles.’ But this is not true, because he did much
more than simply acquiring territories after territories. Neither

Munja, nor his father Sindhuraja, had left for him a consolidated
empire. After Munja’s death the empire of Malwa collapsed.
His successor, Sindhuraja did nothing except mainaing a hold
over the feudatories of the outlying parts. Bhoja consolidated
the empire. The collapse of the Mahmud’s ambitions after
A.D. 1024 was due to the mighty empire which Bhoja had built
up.

‘Satvabhauma’, 'Tribhuvananat-ayana or ‘Trilokanarayana’^ and
‘Dharesvara’2 applied to him were justified in every respect.

## CHAPTER V
### ADMINISTRATION OF BHOJA
The Paramara kingdom under Bhoja was extended from
Chamba and Thanesvara in the north to Krishna and Tungbhadra
rivers in the south and from Dwaraka in the west of Kanauj, in the
north-east. Such a vast empire could not have been directly
controlled from Dhara. He, therefore, divided his empire into
several administrative units.
Division of Empire
The empire of Bhoja was divided into a number of mandalas

(provinces) each of which was sub-divided into Bhogas (Districts

or vishyas). The fact that the District (Vishya) was a unit of the

raandala is proved by the Banswara plates of Bhoja dated VS. 1076

(A.D. 1020).! li is stated therein that Vatapadraka was included

in the Ghaghradora district (Bhoga) of the Sthali province (man-

dala). D. C. Ganguly is not certain about the identification of

vishaya with bhoga but he accepts that ‘Vishya’ was a unit of
Mandala." As there is no other term except ‘bhoga’ equivalent to
‘Vishaya’ mentioned in the contemporary records, we agree with
Hultzch® and U.N. GhshaH that vishaya was synonymous with
bhoga. Each bogha in it’s turn was divided into ‘Pathakas’ which
were further sub-divided into pratijagaranakas. whom Dr. Ganguly
identifies with modern paragana.*^ Each Pratijagaranaka was split
up into several groups, each composed of certain number of

villages. The Betma plates® of Bhojadeva dated V.S. 1076 (A.D.

1020) record that Bhoja granted properly to Brahmana, including

Administration System
Monarehy was the popular form of government in the times of
Bhoja. It is proved by the literary and epigraphic evidences.
The foreign travellers of this period also confirm this,^
The administration set up of the age was the same as laid
down in the smritis and the independent works on Nitisastra.
Leaving only a few slight alterations, the administration of all the
contemporary dynasties such as that of the Kalachuris, the
Chandellas, the Paramaras and the Chalukyas was of the same
general pattern.” The theory of King’s divine origin was adhered
to by the monarchs. The canonical rule of the earlier smriti
writers, like Manu,^ Narada and Lakshmindra^ etc that the mona-
rch is the absolute head of the people was deep-rooted in the
minds of the rulers of this period. The Udaipur prasasli^ along-
with the Navasahasankacharita,* the Prabandhachintamani’ and
the Paramara inscriptions® refer to the hereditary system of king-
ship. We do not find the reference of any elective type of
monarchy.
It was realised that the divi.'ion of a Kingdom among
brothers would make it weak and lead to its eventual destruction.®
The crown, therefore, usually passed to the eldest son,^“ but his
brothers were generally appointed to responsible posts in the

Yuvarajas .
Usually, the monarch nominated his successor or an heir
apparent during his life-time. No body could elevate to Yuvaraja-
ship unless one was formally appointed as such. Kautilya lays
down that the eldest son is authorised to be the Yuvaraja, provided
he is first nominated as such by his predecessor."
Hindu law, however, did not permit the only daughter after a
king to succeed her father as the ruling queen What actually
happened therefore when the only heir to the throne was a
daughter was that her husband succeeded to the kingdom of his
father-in-law. In such cases the daughter was sometimes regarded
as the regent-queen, as is suggested by the joint coinage of
Chandragupta I and his Lichchavi queen Kumaradevi. Usually
the heiress became merely a queen consort.
If the king died without a male issue, or if the one he had
appointed as a successor was minor and the times were troublous,
the younger brother of the king was made, Yuvaraja. We may
rightly quote the case of Bhoja in this context. It is recorded that
at the time of Munja’s death Bhoja, the intended successor was a
minor.^ Munja, therefore, entrusted the charge of government to
his younger brother.^
The regency had it’s own advantages and was an obvious nece-
ssity in the c .se of a minor When the heir apparent was a minor
at the time of his succession, the administration was carried on by
a regency council.®

Puradhyal’sha and Baladhyaksha. A-ll these officers according
to Bhoja should be examined and cross-examined again and again.
The main court bearers, thus, were the Mahapradhana

(Premier)^ the warder” (Chamberlain) and his subordinate staff,

senior military officers and other high dignitaries, learned poets,
“

the astrologers, the doctors (Bhoja himself was an eminent

physician) and diplomatic agents as Damara.^ Sometimes, also

the other nrinces who were given shelter in the capital took an
active parr in the court proceedings. The example of Dhanduka,
the Paramara ruler of A-bu branch may well be cited in this
context ®
Morecver, the vassals or the feudatory chiefs not only received
a lion’s share in the state revenue, but also got the facility of
attending the royal court. Sukranitisare categorically states that
that victorious kings should grant half and a quarter of the revenues
of the subiugated kingdom to the vanquished king’s sons and wife
respectivelv “ Merutunga states that the vassals not only attended
the royal court but also received dresses from the emperor at the
time of witnessing sports and games.’
Among the other non-officials members, who graced the royal
court, were the merchants, presidents of traders associations o
guilds® and other notables of the capital.®
Bhoja preferred to hold his court in the hall of audience
cess to the court was regulated by the royal chamberlain or
the warder®' and his staff.*® The court was surrounded by regi-

other attendents of Bhoja were the beautiful maidens who held the
royal umbrella and swung chowries over him," The betel-bearers
remained always alert to serve bctaels to the king at the latter’s

Without the permission of the king, even the princes could not

Besides, Bhoja was attended by dancing girls and courtesans.
This was a common fashion of the kings of that the period. A
ITdrisi states the wide prevalence of this custom in the Chalukyan
court of Analilapattana.® Alberuni also refers to the presence of
these dancing girls.” Merulunga states that Bhoja had bestowed a
beautiful damsel, on his commandcr-in-chief, named Kulachaodra.®
Like Chandragupta the Mauryan emperor, Bhoja also had selec-
ted the most trustful women as his body guards. They were
personally interviewed by the King, for his whole care rested with
these body-guards.®

the vedas and women upon their husbands’’.^® The Arthasastra

Administraiion of Bhoja lOi
The ministry embraced the whole administration. It was to
enunciate new policies, ensure their successful working, remove
any difficulties that may crop up, supervise and direct the state
policy regarding taxation and expenditure, take steps for the
proper cdueation and training of the princes, participate in their
coronation, and to direct the foreign policy, both with reference
to the internal feudatory kings and the external independent
state.^
Ministers were the pillars of the state. ^ Normally the king
used to accept their advice, though the ultimate responsibility
rested with the crown.’ It was the first and the foremost duty of
the minister to control the king and to see that he did not follow
a wrong path.* They were the real friends of the king, says
Kamandaka, who prevented him from following a dangerous
path.®
Personal factors weighed much in determining the position
and powers of ministers. When kings were stiong and powerful,
they were the centres of power and the administration was known

as king-centred (rajyatta-tantra) ; when they w’crc weak and

II ^ ?c.||

incapable, ministers were the virtual rulers and then the adminis-

tration was known as ministry-controlled (Sachivayatta-tantra).

In normal times, however, power was shared by both and the
administration was known as Ubhayatta, dependent equally both
on the king and the ministry.^ Sukra recommends the occasional
transfer of ministers from one portfolio to another.
Normally the ministers were held in high esteem by the
kings. Their advice was generally accepted by them. Yet it was
not binding upon the kings to do so. We may quote here for
example, the case of Munja was disregarded the advice of his
minister Radraditya and marched against the Chalukyas.® Some
rulers even tried to ride rough shod over their ministers. The
Rajatarangini states that some ministers were the worthless
puppets in the hands of their tyrant masters.®
However, the power, influence and the utility of the ministry
varied with the competence, nature and temperament of the king.
Sometimes, able ministers succeeded in exercising a great
influence of the Administration as a whole. They sometimes,
appointed even their kings. The Jabalpur copper plate of
Yasahkarna records that owing to the cowardice of Yuvaraja II,
the chief ministers of that ruler replaced him by his son Kakalla
II.^ Generally speaking, however, in normal times the ultimate
responsibility of decision rested with the king.®
Capable and ambitious ministers often held more than one
portfoilios. Alamkata for example, held simultaneously the
posts of the Commander-in-chief and the chief Justice.® But
normally each minister held the charge of one department only

Administration of Bhoja i03
Strength of Ministry
We have no exact information regarding the actual number
of Bhoja’s ministers and their respective protfolios. Ai inscrip-
tion^ of Bhoja’s contemporary Silahara king records that in
A. D. 1024 the total strength of the ministry was only lve..,‘^The
ancient writers on polity give different numbers. In t ic Manu-
smriti,2 the number is given ss 7 or 8, while Kautilya i icrcascs it
from 8 to 20.® The information supplied by Sukra, an eleventh
century writer, may rather be more helpful in this regard.
According to Sukra,^ the ministry whose strength was to be 10
was to consist of Purohita, Pratinidhi, Pradhana, Sachiva,
Mantrin, Pradvivaka, Pandita, Suraantra, Ainatya and Dula.
Thus we arrive at the conclusion that the strength of the ministry
varied from time to time according to the form and structure of

the administration. Dhara had a good number.® The ;taius and

pay of each succeeding minister were lower than those of the
preceding one.®
Let us now make a survey of the scope, the status and the
working of each of the ministers.
Purohita
The royal chaplain figures in all the lists of ratnins which
include among the departmental heads, senani, suta, gramini,
sangrahita and bhagadnhuka. But the ascendancy of the Purohita
declined with the rise of Upanishadic, Jain and Budh st move-
ments. He, virtually ceased to be a member of the ministry
from century 200 A. D. Even then, he continued tc exercise
considerable moral influence upon the king. The frown of an
ideal Purohita was suflicicnt to keep the king on the path of
virtue and rectitude.’ The Samarangana Sutradhara’ and the

Samarangana Sutradhara, may be identical with the Mahapuro-

^
Pradhona of Premier
Pradhana or premier was the most important member of the
ministry According to Sukra the Premier was to be ‘Sarva-
darsin.’ i e. the general superintendent of the ministry ^ Ihe
Samarangana sutradhara and the Prabandhachintamani refer to
Mahapradhana as sutradhara.^ The Premier had the exalted
position in the state. He often held more then one portfolios.
The Premier of the Silahara king Ananta held for instance also
the portfolios of High treasury in A. D.‘ 1085. Sametimes, the
feudatory chiefs made grants at his dictation. In case of kings'
temporary absence, the Premier took over the charge of adminis-
tration also.*
Pratinidhi
Pratinidhi was the second minister according to Sukia who
gives him a status inferior only to that of heir apparent ’ 'It was
his duty to deputise for the king when he was ill or absent on an
expedition. It is very 1‘kely that this outy must have been
entrusted to the croVn prince when the king had become old
enough to assume the reins of his office. The uparaja of the
Jatakas seems to be same as pratinidhi of-Sokra. It, however,
appears that the portfolio of pratinidhi was not generally
infcluded in the ministry. Stikra states that 'the premier should
deptuse for the king and the pratinidhi.®

Administration of Bhoja 105
Pandit
Pandit was incharge of religion and morality. His duty was
to find out and discourage the practices which had become
antiquated. Me was also to advise the government about suitable
changes that could be introduced in consonance with the spirit
of dharma and culture,^
War Minister
War minister was the next important member of the ministry
In the Bhoja’s administration, he was known as Baladhyaksa.^
The Nitivakyamrta lays down that the commander of the army
should not be a member of the council.^ Sukra and Kalhana on
the other hand included the commander in chief in the ministry
and call him as Sachiva^ and ‘Kampana respectively.' We do
not know whether under Bhoja’s administration the portfolios of
the war-minister and the commander-in-chief were different from
one another or the same. But it is certain that the commander-
in-chief was assisted by generals. Digambara Kulachandra, the
commandcr-in-chicf of Bhoja was also assisted by generals such
as Sadha® and Suraditya. In the Paramara regime the commander-
in-chief was designated also as dandadhisa.’ Dandanaih of the
Saraarangana Sutradhara may be identical with general.*'
Amatya or Revenue Minister
This minister flares among other ministers in the list presen-
ted by Bhoja and the Samarangana Sutradhara includes his name

in the list of ministers. The revenue minister is called amatya
by Sukra. The Samarangana-Sutradhara includes this minister
in the ministry.^ We do not find any detailed description about
the exact nature of duties of a revenue minister under Bhoja's
administration. But his duties may have been the same sa
described by Sukra, ^ and Soinadeva,® the eleventh century writers.
According to them the main functions of this minister were to
have an accurate account of the land under cultivation, and land
lying fallow, as also of the expected produce from the different
mines. He must have been assisted by the inspection general of
records in carrying on his business.
Foreign Minister
The well being the prosperity of the whole realm depended
upon the skill and ability of the foreign minister. He used to
draft the charters of the copper-plates, which were dictated by
the king. He was designated as the Mahasandhivigrahika and
not as a duta as referred to in the works on polity such as the
Arthasastra, Rajadharama-parvan^ and Manusmriti.'
The term ‘duta’ was probably applied to the diplomatic agent
who was appointed to a rival court for collecting necessary
information thereof. Damara for example was the diplomatic
agent of the Chalukya king Bhima deputed at Dhara.' A duta
was expected to be skilful in penmanship.’
Treasurer
The treasurer is styled as ‘Samaharta’ by Kautilya and as
‘Sumantra’ by Sukra. In ihe contemporary records he is
designated as ‘Bhandagarika.’* In 1094 A. D. the Silahara king
Ahantadeva had a small ministry of 3, and yet Bhandagarika

The accountant general was probably known as ‘Lekhaka’

General Secretariat
Uuforlunatcly we have very little information about the
\Yorking of the secretariate under Bhoja's administration. Some
of the Chola records state that the royal orders when drafted by
the secretariat were countersigned by the chief secretary.''' On
the other hand, all the grants of Bhoja arc signed in his own
royal sign-manual.® This shows that Bhoja probably had no
provision for the secretary.
Judiciary
The period under review is noted for the vigorous develop-
ments in the field of juristic activities, which had characterised
the preceding epoch." Among the legal authorities of this age,

Dharesvara (whom U.N. Ghosal identifies with Paramara Bhoja ))

was a prominent'* one. He wrote certain book on the legal
institutions, the extract from which have been quoted by the later
writers. Unfortunately, it has not been preserved as such.
Justice, like legislation, was very largely a matter of local
concern. Minor disputes were settled by one or more of the
corporations to which they belonged. The distinction between
civil and criminal oficnccs was unknow. Generally speaking, all
offences including those committed by village officials, were tried

in the first instance, in village courts ; in case of dissatisfaction

108 Bhoja Faramara and his times
the matter was taken up to the officer of the king’s government
in charge of the administration.*
With regard to the ownership, Dharesvara writes that sons
have no ownership in the father’s property while the father is
alive. He has also laid down several laws regarding the rights
of succession, rights of widows, and rights of property etc. It
all shows and advanced stage of Judiciary.
Turning to law, Alberuni was struck by the mildness of the
Hindu criminal code, “In this regard,’’ he actually remarks,
‘the manners and customs of the Hindus resemble those of the
Christians, for they are. like those of the latter, based on the
principle of virtue and absence from wickedness, upon my word,
this is a noble philosophy, but the people of this world are not
all philosophers. Most of them are ignorant and erring who
can not be kept on the straight road, saved by sword and whip.’’®
Alberuni gives an elaborate description of the law suits. He
writes that the procedure of filing suits was well organised. “In
civil cases,’’ states he “written plaints were filed, oaths were taken
and witnesses heard. The Judge demanded from the suitor a
document written against the accused person in a well known
writing which was though suitable lor writs of the kind, and in the
document the well established proof of the Justice of bis suit. In
case there was no written document, the contest was settled by
means of witnesses, without a written document.®
Various kinds of oaths and ordeals were popular in the civil
courts. Alberuni states that oaths were taken in accordance with
the value of the object of the claim. If the object was no great
importance and the suitor agreed that the accused person should
swear, the latter had simply to swear before five learned Brah.
mans, in the following words..,.
“If I lie he shall have as recompenscns much of my goods as
is equal to the eight-fold of the amount of his claim.

Adminisfafion of Bhoja 109
In judgement, the main Importance was attached to the wit-
nesses. According to A Ibcruni, the witnesses were not to be
less than 4, but thes'J might be more in ease the justice of the
deposition of a witness was perfectly established and certain before
the judge. He might admit it, and decide the question alone on
the basis of the deposition of this sols witness. However, he did
not admit prying about In secret, deriving arguments from mere
signs or indications in public, concluding by analogy from one
thing which seemed established about another, and using all sorts
of tricks to elicit the truth as I yas - Ibh Mu* awiaya used to du.
If the suitor was not able to prove his claim, the defendant
was to swear, but he might also tender the oat to the suitor by
suing, "I swear thou that thy claim is true and I will give thee
what thou claimcst.”^
One of the processes was that the Judge sent both claiment
and defedant to the temple of the most venerated idol of the town
or realm. There the defendant had to fast during that day On
the following day he dressed in new garments, and posted himself
together with the claiment in that temple. Then the priests poured
water over the idol and gave it to him to drink. If he had not
spoken the truth he would vomit blood.- Several other such legal
procedures were brought into practice to investigate the real facts
of the crimes.
That the murder, theft, adultery and forgery were considered
serious olTences is clear from the contemporary evidences. But the
Brahamanas enioyed especial privileges in this regard. Instead of
killing a Brahman or a kshattriya for any of these crimes, the king
simply confiscated his property. The murderers were banished
from their country, if the murdered person belonged to their own
castes. In case the murdered person belonged to the Brahman
caste the kings inflicted upon a Brahman convict a punishment
onlyfore setting an example.
The law of theft was also severe one. The punishment to
this crims was in accordance with the stolen object. If the object
was very great, the kings blinded a Brahman and multilated him,
1. Sachau II, p. 158,
2. Ibid.
110 Bhoja Paramnra and his Times
cutting oif his left hand and right foot, or t|ie right hand and the
left foot whilst they mutilated a Kshattriya without blinding him,
and killed thieves of the other castes.^ A close examination of the
above statements thus reveals the faet that the penal code of this
period was mainly based on official ground. It was less scientific.
Even then severe punishments were administered for certain crimes.
Multilation for example was a common punishment. The capital
punishment was rarely inflicted In our opinion the punishment
for theft indicates the ascendancy of this crime.
Bhoja had a careful watch over the judiciary. Merutunga

narrates tJiat he used to attend the assembly of pandits (Pandita-

sabhyan)® and the general assembly (Sarvavasara).^ C.H. Tawney

suggests that these terras were equivalent to di-wan-e-amffl and
and darbar-i-amm respectively. Bhoja attended these courts in
the evening.^ The king was the final court of appeal as he might
reduce sentences and set free the prisoners on the days of his birth
and other great events.'
Provincial, District, Division and Town Government
Like contemporary dynasties, the whole empire of Bhoja also
was divided into several administrative divisions. According to
Bhoja,* the empire as a whole including towns and janapadas was
designated by the term ‘Rashtra’. Desa or mandala was the unit
of a Rashtra. A desa or mandala excluding towns was known as
janapada. The small towns were caled the iiigamas, whereas the
bigger ones sakhanagars. These nigams and sakhanagaraS may
have been identical with the modern municipalities and copera-

tions respectively. The village was the smallest administrative

The Provinces (Mandalas)

The actual number of provinces included in the empire of
Bhoja is now known. However, his widely spread dominion indi-
cates that this number may have been pretty high. According to
A.S. Altckar the provinces were approximately of the same size as
the modern division consisting of about 5 or 6 district.- Some of
the governors of these provinces were royal princes, while the rest
were appointed in recognition of their distinguished military
services. Usually their status was equal to the Mahasamanta or
the Mahamandalcsvara. They often bore the title of king Raja

in Canaresa).^ The governors were directly apponted By the king

himself and were solely resposiblc for the administration of the
provinces over which they ruled. A province was divided into

many districts (Bhojas) each under a ‘Vishayapati’ as has already

been mentioned before. The Vishayapati also was appointed by
the king.
As collected from the available evidences, the names of some
of the provinces which were in existence during the period, arc
Avanti, Mahadvadaska, Niigiri, Purnapathaka, Samgamakheta,
Sthali, Vindhya, Vyapurc, Upendrapura and Selluka. Each of
the these provinces, of course, comprised in it hundres of villages.
Selluka mandala, for example comprised 1500 villages.®
District or Bhoja or Vishaya
Each province was divided into several districts. The officcr-
in-charge of a Vishaya or Bhoga was known as Samanta or Vishya-
pati.® Some of these officers enjoyed the feudatory status like the

provincial governors. Kundamaraja, the governor of Knntala
Vishaya was mahamandalesvara entitled t-j the Panchamahasabdas.^
We do not know whether all the governors enjoyed the same
status. For the revenue or other matters, the vistyaptis were re-
sponsible either to the governors or to The Central Government.
Each Vishayapati was associated with a countil of Vishayama-
hamattars composed of the district notables.^
Village Administration
The village was the lowest unit of the territorial divisions.
Generally, each village was under the supervision of a headmgn.
His ofSce had been a very ancient institution both in the south and
in the north. The village headman was a hereditary officer. He
was more arepresentative of the people than a servant of the
central government.^ He was solely responsible for the welfare of
his village. For instance, a headman in Shikarpur-Taluka
committed suicide in A,D. 999 on seeing the ruins of his town.^
Every village headman was solely responsible for the defence,
security and the welfare of his village. The people of the villages
were so trained in the use of arms that even a bangle-seller could
fight to the bitter end Each village had its own militia The
headman was also connected with the revenue administration of
the village. Moreover, he used to take prominent part in the
meetings of the village council for the settlement of private
disputes. All the records of the village were kept under his
custody.*
Since very ancient times, the village headman enjoyed the
rent-free lands in reward of his services,' as is stated by Kautilya
and Manu.* The village headman enjoyed taxes in kind from

the villagers down to the recent times. From the nature of his

been a Kshaitriya. We do not find a '.y evidence regarding the

was cons’dcrably large in the south such as is c car from the
records of the Cholas.
Town Administration
The Samnrangaija-Sulradhara refers to various kinds of
towns. The king used to live in the capital ciiy, which was
known as ‘Rnjadhanl’. 'the other towns were known as ‘Sakhfi-
nagar.'* The subsidiary place ot the king was known as
‘Pattana.’- We do not find any evidence regarding the town
administration of Bhoja. The Siiahara records of some earlier
period certainly throw some light on this. •' According to ihem
the cities and towns were under the charge of prefects who were
distinguished as Purpatis’ or N'agarpalis, The city affairs weie

managed by the prefects with the help of non-o(llcial committees.

In A. D 997, the administration of Gunapura in Konk; im was

vested with a prefect assisted by a commiitce of bankers com()ri*

sing Ambusresthin and Vappaiyasrcsihin, a merchant called
Chclappaiya, a Brahman named Govaneya and some oihers.*
No clue is available to indicate the way in which these town
committees were formed. The records do not inform us as to
whom these town prelccis were responsible.
Selection of Officers
The selection of oIBctrs was governed partly by miliiary,
partly by hereditary and partly by educational consideratiims. Titc
Mt Abu Vimala inscription of V.S. 1378 tells us that theChalukya
king of the Pragvata family was the commander of the forces

114 Bhoja ParamSra and his Times

(dardapati) and the governor of the Abu province.^ This shows

that at a time an officer could hold two portfolios. Most of the
officials were appointed by the king on hereditary basis. In the
Bhojaprabandha the term ‘hereditary servant’ is used at several

places.2 The Yuktikalpataru states that a duta (diplomatic

agent) was required to be well up in politics, writing and drafting

the official documents. He was also expected to be a fluent
speaker, good writer and an efficient hand in court affairs.^
Military Administration
Bhoja’s extensive military operations go a long way to prove
that he had a formidably strong military hands by him and his
able generals, through which he could strike terror into the hearts
of every prince of the age. The ascendancy of the military was so
great that it was reflected even in the civil administration. We
have already discussed that many of the provincial governors,
district oflicers and city prefects were generals or captains. The
incessant wars with protected feudatories had infused the military

spirit in the whole population; there were local militias even in

villages.^
Usually the army headquarters were located at the imperial
capital, Dhara. There must have been provincial headquarters
as well. Al-Masudi tells us that the Gurjara-Pratiharas main-
tained large army garrisons in the south, north, east and west
in order to deal promptly with the perspective attacks on all the
fronts.® Bhoja’s army arrangements also were similar.
Bhoja had a huge standing army. In the Sundha hill

inscription of the reign of Chahamana Chachigadeva dated
V. S. 1319 (A. D. 1262), Bhoja is referred to as a possessor of
huge army.^ This is further supported by a statement where it
is stated that even at the time of its extinction, Paramara
government’s armed forces amounted to 30 or 40 thousand
cavalry and innumerable infantry."
Usual as it was, the army of Bhoja also consisted of infantry,
cavalary and elephants. But the use of chariots also as a
fighting force was not unknown. The concept of ‘ChaturahginI’
army was intact into the minds of the contemporary rulers. The
Jabalpur copper plate of Ya^ahkarnadeva, refers to the term,
‘chaturai'iga’ for Kokalla II’s armies.” Besides, Bhoja in his
Yuktikalpataru has vividly described llic characteristics and
kinds of all the four kinds of armies viz. the infantry, the
cavalry, the elephants and the chariots."*
In the ‘Yuktikalpataru’ the importance is equally attached
to all the four organs of the army. Yet the main emphasis is
laid upon the maintenance of foot-soldiers or infantry. It is
because the chariots may travel only on plain lands, the
elephants can be best employed in the hilly region, the cavalry
may serve in the jungles, but the infantry can move everywhere.
Bhoja also stresses that special attention must be paid to the foot
soldiers,'’ It goes without saying that Bhoja must have arranged
his forces accordingly.
The whole army of Bhoja was divided into several battalions.
Each of them included infantry, cavalry, elephants and chariots.

116 Bhoja Paramara and his Tiines
The Yuktikalpataru tells us that a battalion which included one
chariot, one elephant, 3 horses and 5 foot-soldiers, was known
as ‘Patti’.i Thus the battalions were generally very small.
The recruitment to the army was extended to all the classes
including Brahmapas. Bhoja had 3 generals out of which Sodha
and Rohak were Brahmanas, while general Kulachandra was
Jain.^ Prof. Habib thinks that the Brahmanas did not join the
military profession. This, however, does not appear to be true
as we find the instances of Brahmanas. choosing military profes-
sion down from the days of epics, if not much earlier. The
predecessors of Kautilya gave preference to the Brahmana army
over the Kshattriya, Vaisya and Sudra armies. But Kautilya
states that if an army ot Vaisyas and Sudras be fairly sizeable or,
if there is an army of well trained Kshattriyas, it is better than
a Brahmapa army.=> This shows that the profession of army was
not confined to the castes even in the times of Kautilya. Sukra
also holds the same view and lays down that the caste does not

determine the quality of soldiers ; they may be ‘Sudras’, Kshattri-

yas, Vaiiyas, MIechchhas or of mixed castes but must be well
disciplined and brave.'* Kamandaka writes that the hereditary
army should consist mainly, though not exclusively, of Kshattri-
yas ® Agnipurana" and Matsya-purapa’ hold that the
commander-in-chief must be either a Brahmana or a Kshattriya.
Epigraphic and literary evidences of the period conclusively
prove the adoption of military career by castes other than
Kshattriays.

Administration of Bhoja ill
Types of Troops
Since the times of Kautilya, if not much earlier, six types of
troops were known to the Indian kings. Kautilya writes that

beside the ‘maula’ (hereditary) army, the king has to depend

upon the ‘Bhrita’ or hired soldiers, who should be stationed
near at hand, and always ready to march Among the other

troops were 5rcni (or guild levies), the mitrabala (the allied

powers), ‘amitrabala’ (or the soldiers from the conquered

enemies) and the 'atavibala’ or the forces from the feudatory

chiefs.^
All the above six types of troops, it appears, were well
employed by the rulers of this period The Yuktikalpataru,
however, refers only to four of them, namely, maulabhuta,

(hereditary) Srciiibaddha (guild-levies), Samgliatantu (allied

forces) and Smrtabalarn (or feudatories,) who may be called at

the emergenej). But the rest two types /.c. the troops from the

conquered enemies and aiavibala (or the neutral ncigbbours)^ were

also not known
Maulabala or Hereditary Forces
Both '^ukra^ and Kamandaka^ hold the ‘maulabala’ in the
highest esteem. Bhoja also preferred this force. The muslim
writers seem to be referring to the hereditary forces, when they
mention the troops of Indian kings, who came out to fight for the
king though they received no pay from him •'Epigraphic records
very often refer to ‘maulabala’ as the most efficient and trust-
worthy army. It appears that the fighting was followed as a
hereditary profession in several families and localities from
where the hereditary forces were recruited.''' The Udaipur prasasti

also refers to hereditary soldiers as it records the word ‘hereditary
warrior.i
Srenihaddha or Guild Levies and Bhrit Forces
Next to Maulabala Bhoja places the Srenibaddha or guild
levies,® while Kautilya,® Agnipurana,* Kamandaka-nitisara® and
Nitivakyamrita' place ‘bhrita’ force third in sequence. Manasollasa
the contemporary work on polity speaks of ‘mania’, ‘bhrita’ and
‘mitra’ armies as the three best in order of merit and condemns
the guild-levy as untrustworthy. From this we may conclude
that Bhoja attached more importance to guild levies whereas they
were less preferred by the contemporary dynasties. These guilds
must have been corporations of soldiers.
Mitrabala
The ‘mitrabala’ was the fourth source to supplement the king’s
army. The epigraphic evidences prove that Bhoja himself had
settled, before proceeding to the south, accounts with putla-
bbaraja and entered into an alliance with Rajendra Choln and
Kalchuri Gahgeyadeva.'’
No reference of the rest two kinds of forces r.e. ‘amitrabala
and ‘atavibala’ is found in the contemporary records. It is,
therefore, possible that they ceased to be counted as separate
forces.
1 he Yuktikalpataru gives detailed description of the elephants
and horses with their kinds and availability alongwith the provi-
sion for their training. This shows that the cavalry and elephant
forces were well maintained by Bhoja. The above book enume-
rates four kinds of horses viz. the best, the average, the lower and

from Tajikistan and Tusara countries, while the Sindhu horses
were inferior in quality." But the Manasollasa refers to the Sindhu
horses trained by the Yavanas as the best.® This indicates that
the Indian horses trained by Indians were inferior in quality to
those trained by the Yavanas.^
Naval Force
Navy did not constitute a regular feature to the military
organisation during the period. But this force was not unknown
to the contemporary rulers. Bhoja refers to two kinds of boats,

viz ‘dfrgha (long) and ‘unnata (high). The former had ten varie-

ties, viz dirghika, tarani, lola, gatvarii, plavinT, dharini and veginl.
The latter’s kinds were urdhva, anurdhva, svarnamukhi, garbhini,
and raanthara.'' The other details of all these boats will be given
in chapter 8 under the sub heading of Boat making industry.
Mediaeval writers also refer to the use of boats in warfare.
Alberuni writes that on its return to Multan in 417 A.H.
1026 A.D., Mahamud’s army was destroyed by the Jats by means
of their large number of boats. He further speaks of sea-pirates
of Kachchha and Somanath, who used ships called VIra ® The
use of boats, in warfare continued till the later period. Yasasti-
lakachampu stales about the regular practices of Tirhut regiment
in naval manoeuvres ’ During the Sena period the Bengal fleet
proceeded on a conquering expedition along the Ganges.® In

Air Force
No direct reference of air forces is found in the contem-
Yorary records. Yet tve may get some idea of this force from the
writings of the period. In the Saraarahgana Sutradhara about
cne hundred slokas are devoted to the description of aeroplanes.
According to Bhoja these aeroplanes could move in all sides r.e.
in oblique direction, upwards, downwards, frontwise and side-
ways.® They could move in the heaven and also on the earth.*
These aerial-cars were shaped like elephants, birds, monkeys,
horses and chariots. T heir body was made of light wood, iron,
lead, copper and other metals.® The body of the planes was filled
w ith mercury and fire at the bottom.' It had two wings, which
were propelled by air and they carried passengers.’
Prof. Diskalkar thinks that the aeroplane was actually used
in warfare.® Dr. B P. Mazumdar, on the other hand, doubts
thiir tise.® \Vc agree with T. Ganapati Sastri who writes,
“although the flying machine etc mentioned in the work have
not been either seen or heard before, it is incorrect to say that
they are only the products of imagination and they were not
actually made and used. The things which once existed might,
in the long run, come to be considered as unreal on account of
their disuse and things involving much labour, time and money
may aho get out of use very easily.*”
It is controversial as to why the author has not described the

method of manufacturing the machines. In the words of Bhoja,
“in case these methods are revealed, then every one not initiated
in the art by the preceptor will try to construct the machines and
the attempt made by such a person may, instead of achieving
success, bring about troubles and difficulties. It may, therefore,
be presumed that the methods of constructing aeroplanes were
confined only to certain trustworthy persons of Bhoja who kept
them as top secret. Besides, Bhoja must have had a careful
watch on the engineers who were employed for this purpose.
Wages of Soldiers
The contemporary records reveal that the soldiers were paid
partly in cash and partly in kind. Rajatarahgani mentions that the
soldiers when out on duty, were paid an additional allowance.”
But this practice is not found anywhere else in the contemporary
records. The Chandella records speak of the provision of pension
etc., to the relations of the deceased soldiers." An inscription^ of
the Chandellas records the grant of two villages by way of
compensation for death on the battle field. Moreover, from the
earlier sources too, we hear of the provisions made for ^e wages
of soldiers. Kautilya® refers to the names of villages which were

enjoying exemption from land tax (ayudhiyapratihara) on condi-

tion of supplying a certain number of soldiers. According to
Kamandaka,® “a force which is given its wages without delay
will fight with greater enthusiasm than a force which is not
promptly paid.® Bhoja also, therefore, must have encouraged
soldiers by offering them prompt and direct remunerations.
Ambulance Corps
In fact no reference of the ambulance corps is found in the
contemporary records. The Rajatarangini,’ however, refers to
their use in the Kashmir armies. A similar use of the ambulance

corps may,' therefore, be presumed in Bhoja’s period as well.
Femaie Relatives and Slaves of kings
The rulers of northern India were often accompanied by
their wives, female relatives and slaves. Al-utbi informs that
the victory of Muslims against Hindus was owing to the revolt of
slaves in the household.^ But this practice, according to A.S.
Altekar, was not prevalent among the Hindu forces. He further
adds that it was composed of free men. Slaves could hardly have
found a place in it even for menial duties.^ So far as the wives
and other female relatives are concerned, they accompanied and
encouraged kings in their expeditions. The Bhoja-charita states
that Bhoja was persuaded by Kusumavati the wife of Munja,
when he launched an expedition against Tailapa II to avenge
the death of his uncle.^
Feudatories
The feudatories also played an important role in strengthening
the military position of Bhoja, who had several feudatory chiefs
under his vassalage. The prominent among them were the kings
ol Chamba, Dubkund, Sakambhari, Naddula, Medapata, Patan
with its vassal of Kachchha and Saurastra, Lata and Konkana*
etc., Dhandhuka orDandhu, the Paramara ruler of Abu also was
one of Bhoja’s feudatory chiefs.
These feudatory chiefs in the first instance assisted Bhoja in
his expeditions and if not they aticast remained faithful to him and
never caused any trouble.
Forts
The well known forts at Dhara and Mandu were under Bhoja
dominance. The fort of Gwalior was under the Chahamana
possession while that of Kalanjara under the Chandellas of
Jejakbhukti. These forts served the purpose of stationing troops
at different stages.

Adminisirdiion of Bhojix 123
Offensive Weapons
. Blioja most probably had a very rich artillary. But unfor-
tunately no traces of this department have been available either
through the epigraphic or the literary sources. The Yuktikalpa-
taru is the only source, which refers to the flourishing stage of this
organ. In the Samarahgana Sutradhara the reference has been
made to various kinds of machines the chief among them being
the elephant machine, door keeper machine, and flying machine
etc. Our ignorance with regard to these machines is owing
mainly to Bhoja’s unwillingness to disclose their secret to others.
He was of the opinion that passing on the knowledge of machine
construction to others would bring success to others and disaster
to him.i Besides, he certainly laid down certain qualifications
necessary for those constructing the machines."
Bhoja has divided all the war weapons into two categories
viz. mayika and amayika. The mayika weapons included boiled
oil, water wood, earthen pieces or pieces of stone ^ We do not
know if the mayika weapons were used in wars. The amayika
weapons referred to in the Yuktikalpataru are definitely found in
the contemporary records. Among them were swords, shields
bows and arrows, spears, a weapon shaped like half-moon,

narach, saktiya^i (unknown), parasu, chakra, sula and parigha

etc.*

154 ihoja ParamUra and his Times
The references of armoury are frequently found in the
contemporary records. Bhoja refers at least to three types of
armours, made respectively of wood, leather and “sakalatn”*
seems to have been used for metals for the economy of words.
War
Certain war instruments were also used in the period. The
epigraphic records mention among them the names of kettle

drums, jharjharas, Damarus (Kielhorn thinks that jharjharas and

damarus are names of certain kinds of drums (E. I. II. p. 191

fn. 2) and tabors. The Nagpur-stone inscription of the rulers

of Malwa V. S 1161, clearly refers to all these weapons in the
following way, “Bhoja’s army distinguished by the sharp sound
of roaring kettle drums, by the thunder of land jharjharas, by a
multitude of shrill damarus, and by tabors heard above the
tumult, roamed, filling the world with its noise over the whole
earth, thrown into confusion under the lootsteps of his rutty
elephants.®
The above description reveals the fact that the sounds produced
by the instruments used by one king differed from those used by
the other In this way they could easily be recognised. It is also
inferred that the above war-instruments were beaten atleast at the
time of launching an expedition or at the time of conquests.
The expeditions were launched after due consultations with
the astrologers employed in every court. This view is held by
Bhoja and Lakshmtdhara, the Gahadavala minister as also by
the author of the Manasollasa They prescribed that the king
should start on his expedition only after consultations with the
astrologers and watching the prognostics.®

Administration of lihoja 125
State Revenue and Expenditure
The revenue has been the most important organ of
administration throughout the ages. In this period too it was
considered as one of the strongest parts of the government machi-
nery. There was a sort of gradation among the kings in accor-
dance with their greatness and resources.^ The Sukranitisfira
introduces a mathematical basis of calculations which is most
peculiar for its definiteness and accuracy. It was according to
the state revenue that a king was styled as samanta, mandalika
raja, maharaja, svaraj, samraj, virat or sarvabhauma. According
to Venkatesvara this assertion is little more than pure political
speculation.^ We find certa n terms on coins and in inscriptions
which obviously point out to dlfTcrcnt degrees of kingly power.
They arc raja, mahanlja, mahe^vara, kshatrapa and mahaksha-
trapa.2 We shall now try to give a clear picture of the revenue
and expenditure of the stale of Bhoja. I'cr this we arc to depend
mainly upon the statements of the cpigraphic records, the infor-
mation supplied by the contemporary smriti-writers and the
accounts of the foreign travellers. The principal sources of revenue
to the Paramara government known to us were :
1. Share of the product of the fields.
2. House lax.
3. Rent in cash.*
4. Tools and other dues, Irom the market.
5. Ferry tools.
6. Imports on salt.”
Land revenue formed the most important and substantial part
of the state’s income. Almost all the land grants of the family
refer to the levy of land tax. But this tax was realised in share

of the produce. The Betma-plates of Bhoja’ and the Mandhata-
plales of Jayasiriiba of Dhara dated V. S. 1112- both refer to the
realisation of land tax in share of the produce. Alberuni also
writes that a person was bound to pay first the tax to the ruler
which attaches to the soil of pasture-ground.® We shall now
discuss the actual amount of the land tax levied by the govern-
ment of Bhoja. In the epigraphic records of Bhoja certain
technical terms have been used for the popular taxes. They are
sahiranya, bhiiga, bhoga and uparikara.'’ Unfortunatelv, the
modern scholarship has yet not succeeded in ascertaining the
exact meaning of these terms. Sanskrit dictionaries also do not
help us much in this regard. The smrili-lilcrature invariably uses
the term bhoga or its synonym ‘amsa’ far donating the land tax.®
In the Sanskrit literature the king is very often described as sub-
sisting on sasjha i.e. the 6th part of the produce of the land.
Alberuni. the contemporary foreign traveller also writes that a
king in his time had to receive one-sixth of the income in recogni-

tion of (he protection which he afforded to 'the subjects, their

property and their families. Altekar also considers it as almost
certain that the 'bhogakara* of the inscriptions must be standing
for the land tax.® The assertion of Altekar seems correct. The
empire was almost continuously engaged jn incessant warfare and
its opp-nents were also powerful, the military expenditure, there-
fore, must have been very high and consequently, taxation could
not have been light.’ A. S. Altekar® further presumes, on the
authority of Rajesvara Chola T’s inscriptions A. D. 1014 that this
one-sixth of the produce included all the miscellaneous dues like

‘uparikara’ or ‘bhogakara’.i The taxation, however, may have
varied according to the quality of land. Presumably, the less
fertile lands were charged a lower percentage. Contemporary
Chola inscriptions refer to 3 or 4 different classes of land, classi-
fied according to their quality. Sukra also lays down that the
government revenue demand should vary with nature of the
irrigations of the soil.-
Alberuni w-rites that Brahmans were exempted from all these
taxcs.= It is quite possible that in the ease of some special
tenures, the taxation was either very low or non-existent as is
proved by the dynastic land-grants where tlie references occur
about the free-rent lands given to Bri'ilimanas.-’ We sometimes,
find the holders of this tenure paying voluntary cesses for works
of public utility. An eleventh century record refers to a volun-
tary cess of ‘pana’ on all the rent-free lands at Tavargere for the
maintenance of a ‘pujari’ in a local temple.'- 'i hc public servants
and the military olTiccrs paid, if at all they had to pay any, only
a nominal tax for the lands granted to them.
As the land tax was realised in share, the question of periodi-
cal revision of land-tax did not arise. The remission of the land-

tax may have (’epended upon the production. If on any account

whatsoever, the yield was less, the .share of the Government also
would automatically diminish. This show-s that the land-tax varied
with the actual produce of the year.
Aho, as the land-tax was realised in share it is natural to
assume that it must have been collected after harvest of the crops

which usually were two i.c. Kharif (September) and ‘rabi’ (April).

It, therefore, goes without saying that the tax was paid atlcast in
tw'o instalments. Altckar writes on the authority of the Arthaia-
stra°, that the Government permitted the farmers to jiay tax in
atleast three instalments."
