128 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
Shoga
Bhoga is the next terra used for taxes. It is very frequently
found in the contemporary as well as earlier and later inscriptions.'
The term, however, is quite confusing. A.S. Altekar thinks that the
term ‘Bhogakara’ represents the petty taxes in kind that were to
be paid to the king every day in the form of betel-leaves, fruits
and vegetables etc. Although these taxes, according to Altekar,
were exacted by the king only when he was on tour, they
were enjoyed by the local officers as a part of their income.
Sometimes, the villagers had to pay some additional taxes for the
salaries of the district and village officers. This additional tax on
land may also have been included in the term 'Bhogakara’.^
There is evidence to show that in rare cases the land tax was
also realised in cash. The temple land usually was taxed on the
basis of cash income but the exaction was on a lower scale. A
record from Honawad in Dharwar district, dated 1054 A.D.,
refers to an allotment of ordinary dry land to a temple by king
Somesvara at the payment of half a ‘paija’ as the payment of a
matter.’ A.S. Altekar states that a gold ‘pana’ is equal to 3/4
tola and the silver ‘pana’ to about 6 tolas. He further adds that
a ‘matter' was equal to 4 or 5 acres of land.* If this assertion
is correct, we find that in case of gold ‘pana’ the land tax for

4 or 5 acres was 3/8 tola; in other cases as ‘pana’ being in silver

or copper it was still lower. This shows that the temple land
was either free of tax or this tax was very nominal-
Beside land tax, there were several other taxes, most of which
were realised in cash. The contemporary records show that m
this period the excise and octroi duties were also charged.
According to Altekar the term ‘bhutopatta pratyaya’ means a tax

(aya) on (prati) what has been taken in i.e. imported (upatta), and

what has been produced (bhula) in village. This would indicate

n tax upon articles (manufactured) and stored (sambluiia). He

further adds that the term ‘bhritopfilta-pratyaya’ is identical wHh

the point that the term ‘Sulka’ was u^cd for the excise
and octroi duties. Although the dynastic records of the Paramaras
fail to refer to the names of commodities on xxhich these taxes were
levied, we come to know from the contemporary Chola inscrip-
tions that they were icn^'rally levied on potters, shepherds, weav-
ers, oilmen. shopkeepers, stall-keepers, brewers tnd gardeners^
It is sad to note that the cpigrapliic records do not supply us v.'ilh
any information about the percentage of these taxes.

‘Vish{i’ or forced labour also formed one of the sources of

revenue. 1 his tax was levied on poor persons who were unable to
pay the taxes in ca^h. Manu f'rc.scribcs that arti.sans and labou-
rers etc., should be made to work f«>r one or two da>s in a month
for the state.'' This tax in this period was realised partly duiing

the tours of the king and partly by the village lundn.an on ('eca-

sions such as cleansing of the governnunt g'an.-ry, weighing or
measuring the land ttix paid in kind etc.
Miscellaneous taxes
There were .<cvcral other miscellaneous dues such as liousc
tax. which is mentioned in the Arthuna-inscription of the Paramara
Chamundaraja, dated V.S 1136. The inscription records that for
each house of the traders in the local ‘bazar’ was assigned a
‘dramma’." The existence of ferrytax which is recommended by
most of the Smriti writers, is proved by the detailed description
given in the Yuktikalpalaru. regarding the eonslruction of various

kinds of boats etc. for commercial purposes.^ Prof. K.D. Bajpai
also opines that by the beginning of the eleventh century the
shipping in India had become considerably developed on scicnfic
Iines.2 All this shows that some dues must have been charged on
the commodities transported by the ships. The actual proportion,
however, is not known.
The sales-tax was also levied during the period. An inscrip-
tion of the Paramiira dynasty tells us that on the ships of the
braziers was likewise imposed one ‘dramraa’ for the month, on

(eachVAvanivaka’ of the distillers four ‘rupakas’, on the gambling

(house) two ‘rupakas’ and on every house of the whole population

one ‘dramma’.^ Besides, the metals and wood made commodities
were also taxed. On each lagada* were assigned two ‘santas’, on
each ‘karsha’ of oil one ‘panaka’ and similarly on each load of
cattle-fodder one ‘vrisha vimsppaka’.'
The taxes were also exacted on cattle, pasture-lands and other
items. The Mandhata plates ofJayasirhha of Dhara dated V.S.
1112 clearly mention that the pasture-land was taxed in money.'
Alberuni states that a person had to pay some tax for his cattle ’
With regard to income tax, Alberuni writes that a person paid
to the ruler one-sixth of the income in recognition of the protec-

tion which the ruler afforded to the subjects, th;ir properly and

their families.® But, probably, people were not honest in paying
this tax, because writes Alberuni, they would always lie and cheat
in the declarations about their property.”

Administration of Bhoja 131
Besides the taxes, other sources of state revenue were fines,
^
the tributes from the trading business, = income from the govern-
ment properties such as waste lands, the land under cultivation
by government, heirlcss property, the property confiscated by it
for ofifcnces and forests etc.®
Regarding the revenue from mines and salt, the records arc
almost silent. Only records of Gahadwals and Cholas claim the
ownership of mines and salt.* The salt was manufactured by the
‘Silaharas’ on a large scale.
As regards the succession-tax, A.S. Allekar states that the
tax on persons dying without a son. proves that only a
fraction of their property passed to the state as a kind of succe-
ssion duty.
The other two taxes i.c. ‘hiranya’ and 'uparikara’ also added
to the enrichment of the state revenue.
Government Expenditure
No information is at hand to explain the expenditure of the
government. Kautilya enumerates 14 items but supplies no details.”
‘Sukra divides the income in six parts and lays down that one
part should be kept as reserve, as many as three should be assigned
to the army, and half a part should be reserved for each of the
four following items, viz. charity, privy purse, civil administration
and people.''* It is very likely that if ever the Paramaras’ budgets
arc discovered, they would disclose the similar allocation of
funds.

## CHAPTER VI
### RELIGIOUS CONDITION
The outstanding feature in the religious history of the period
under review is the tiiumphant revival of the Brahraanical religion.
Hinduism got the state patronage and it marked a distinct and
decisive advance One of the reasons for this ascendancy was the
waning position of Buddhism. The growth of the ‘Tantrika’
practices in Buddhism led to its downfall. Besides the catholicity
of the Brahamanical religion in this period was another impor-
tant factor. The re'igious reforms of Sankaracharya had done
much for the reviv. 1 of the Brahmana supremacy and under the
Rajputs, the Brahamanas regained the power which once Buddhism
had enjoyed Ramanuja, with his emphasis on the doctrine of
Bhakti or godly devotion, extended the possibilities of religious
experience.^ The doctrine of Buddha had now little hold on the
people and that too was limited to Bengal and Bihar. The rest of
the country was following the Brahmanical or Jain religion. The
Buddhist buildings of the Pala period in Bihar no longer exist.
Many Hindu and Jain Temples of the period have, however,
survived.^ Buddhism gradually merged itself into Hinduism and
the Buddha was counted among the ten incarnations of Vishnu.
Gradually, Siva and Buddha became identical with each other, as
in Java. In the modern Baline.se theology Buddha is regarded as
a younger brother of Siva. Later on, ‘Siva, Vishnu and Buddha
were all regarded as Identical and so were their ‘Saktis’.’
The Brahmanical religion could not assimilate Jainism as was
the case with Buddhism. During the period under survey Jainism
maintained its stronghold in Gujarat. Hinduism did not affect
1. A History of India, pp. Ill to 112, by J. C. Powell Price. ,
2. The Oxford History of India, p. 98. ,
3. The Struggle for Empire, Cisapt.. XVI, X). 402. ,,
Religious Condition 133
the fortunes of Jainism in Deccan too.^ The reason for this was
that Jainism was never subjugated to the contaminating influence
of the Tantric ideas. The other reason lies in the work and achive-
ments of a number of well-known Jain saints and writers, like
Samantabhadra Akalahkadeva, Vidyananda, Manikhanandin,
Prabhachandra, Jinascua, Gunachandra and Pampa.^
In spite of being the most popular religion of the period under
survey, Brahmanism could not recover its dominant position
osving to the destructive inroads of Islam. The effects of Islam
were permanent having in turn their future consequences ^ Let
us make here a critical survey of all the popular religions, viz.
Buddhism, Jainism and Hinduism.
Buddhism
As noted above, Buddinsm was losing its ground owing
to the Tantric innucncc, which brought a radical change in the
outlook and character of this religion. A new sect named
‘Vajrayana’ appeared in Buddhism and gradually it superseded
the Hinayana and Mahayana sects after the 10th C.A.D.
Vajrayana exerted its influence most in the eleventh and iw'clfth
centuries ^ Even then Buddhism could not gain wide popularity
and thus it was confined to a local religion. N. N. Das Gupta,
observes that Buddhism ceased to be a creative force in religion,
literature or aesthetics, and was merely a legacy of the past in

less capable hand-;.^ Similar view is expressed by C. V. Vaidya

who asserts that Buddhism vanisheJ except from Magadha.*^
This is also supported by the account of Albcruni, who writing
in the first half of the eleventh C A. D says that all traces of
Buddhism in Central Asia, Khurasan, Afghanistan and North-
Western India seem to have disappeared.'^ But Sachau, the
English editor of Albcruni’s work disagrees with this view. He

says that it is a remarkable fact that a man of the inquisitive mind
of Alberuni knew scarcely anything at all procuring any infor-
mation on the subject. He further adds that Alberuni’s notes
on Buddhism are very scanty, all derived from the book of one
Zurkan. Moreover, Alberuni was only acquainted with the
trinity of the Buddhistic system, as he refers to Buddha, Dharma
and Samgha. Alberuni calls Buddha as Buddhodana which is
a mistake for something like the son of Suddhodana.*
The available evidence leads us to believe that during the
period under review the power of Buddhism was waning and its
hold was confined to some parts of eastern India. But it does
not mean that it altogether disappeared from the land of Its
birth as is supposed by some scholars. Let us make a survey of
the regions where Buddhism was still alive.
Bengal and Bihar
During the period under review the Pala rulers patronised
Buddhism. V. A. Smith says that Palas were the only considera-
te princes who continued to profess and support Buddhism.®

Mahipala I, the ruler of Bengal (Gauda) had in his court a galaxy

of Buddhist scholars. The other celebrities also joined hands
with him to revive Buddhism from the 10th century onwards.®
Mahipala’s sou and successor Nayapala, contributed much to the
cause of Buddhism, but owing to political disturbances, it suffered
a setback. Buddhism again became more popular during the time

of Ramapala (A.D. 1077). He gave patronage to a number of

Buddhist scholars, teachers and artists. N. N. Das Gupta opines
that he was the last flickering of the lamp and after him Buddh-
ism steadily declined, consequently it was finally ousted from the
land by the Muslim invaders towards the close of the 12 th C. A.
D.® Throughout the period under survey, Bengal and Bihar
were the strongholds of Buddhism.

When Magadha was conquered by the Pain ruler Mahipala
I, the mastery over the great viharas such as NrUanda, Odanta-

puri (Udanqinpura, probably near the modern town of Bihar)

and Vikrama^ila (near Bliagalpur), naturally passed on to that

monarch. These viharas served various purposes. Besides
being Buddhist scats of learning, they were also the citadels of
strength of their doctrine and culture and through them the work
of propagating the cultural influence was embarked. Albcruni
refers to the Buddhist monasteries of Udandapurl in Magadha,
and states that the writing called ‘uhaikshuki’, was used in

Udunpur (Udaii^apurl) in Purvadc^a.'

The well known monastic universities of the age were at

Nfilandil (which was repaired by Mahipala in his llih regnal

year), Vikramasila and Somapuri.® Taranath Slates that the

Vikramasila university about this time had surpassed Nalanda
in fame and renown as its professional board kept watch over
Niilanda.^ The monastery of Somapuri also was in a flourishing
condition. It was located at Paharpur in north Bengal. The
great Alisa or Atisa Dipahkara resided there, where he translated
into Tibetan in collaboration with some others the Madhyamaka-
ratna Pradtpa of Bhavaviveka.^* The evidence of this university
is also atlc.stcd by a stone inscription found in the ruins of
Paharpur. The inscription has the characters of 11th C.A.D.
It records that the pillar on which the inscription is incised, was
raised up by Sri Dasavalagarbha for the good of animate beings®
Later on, the Somapuri viliara was set on fire sometime about
the middle of llth C.A.D. by the armies from South-east
Bengal.'^ About the same time, Rfimapala established a new

Mahavibara in north Bengal called Jagaddala. Among the
other less renowned Viharas of Bengal, were Deviloka in north
Bengal and Pandit Vihara in the Chittagong district of east Bengal
which were noted for being the important seats of Buddhist
learning and culture.
2, Kashmir
Kashmir was another vigorous centre of Buddhism. From
this place numerous missionaries went to Tibet and Central Asia.
In 1005, a ‘Sramana’ went to China with some manuscripts and
a twig of the sacred Bodhi tree of Bodh Gaya. He presented
.this twig to the Chinese emperor. The Statues of the ‘Great

Buddha’ (Brihad Buddha) set up by Lalitaditya at Pariharpur

and in the Jayendra Vihara’ at ‘Srinagar by Jayendra, the
maternal uncle of Pravarasena II, were the great objects of adora-
tion to the Buddhists even in the 11th C. A. D.^
3. Madhya Desa
The establishment of the Pala rule in Magadha, reclaimed
and renovated numerous holy places of Buddhists. An inscri-
ption of the reign of Mabipala I, at Saranatha dated A. D. 1026
records the restoration of many monuments by him. Between
A.D. 1000 and 1033 an influx of Chinese Buddhists took
pilgrimage to Bodhgaya where they built up several stupas etc."

'During the reign of Mahipala, the Kausambi (modern Kosam)

regained its importance as B^adilya, the follower of Mahayana,
hailing from Kausambi rebuilt the Mahavihara of Nalanda.^ The
name of Kosambamandala or the district of Kau^ambI is also
'found in an inscription dated A. D. 1035, over the gateway of
the fort of Kara.*
The other holy places of Buddhists, like SravastI and Sara-
nath were still prominent. In Kapihastu Damodara, one of
Mahipala’s Buddhist contemporaries, was born.® Afier the death

No trace of Buddhism is found in the Malwa region during
the period. The same seems to have been the ease with the
CImndcllas of Jcjakbhukti, for no contemporary king is referred
to as having directly supported the cause of Buddhism.^
South India
Owing to the rise of Jainism as well as Saivism and Vaishna-
vism in the .South, Buddhism lost its hold there. Some cpigraphic
and literary sources indicate that Buddhism lingered there in
some form even after A. D 1000, but it never became a forceful
religion in the south.
JAINISM
Gujarat
Jainism continued to be the religion of a strong minority
throughout the period under review. It was more progressive
in Gujarat owing to the popular and royal favour. From the very
beginning of their reign, the Chalukya kings of Gujarat patro-
nised this sect. The Jain temple of Mulabliastika, is said to have
been constructed by Mularaja, the very founder of the dynasty.
In the reign of Bhima, I, his minister Vimala of the Pragvata
family had creeled, at Abu. the most magnificent Jain temple of
Adimalha. This temple was completed in V.S. 1088 or about
A.D., 1031, i. c. within seven years of the demolition of

2. The Struggle for Empire, p. 421.

138 Bhoja Paramtra and his Times
Somanath by Mahmud of Ghazni.* Vimala'founded the town of
Chandravati also and built the temple of Rishabhadeva on the
Arbudachalam, for the promotion of Jainism.^ Vimala had the
approval of his master Bhima, for all these activities. Thus, it
seems probable that Jainism, in Gujarat, was in ascendancy. In
Malwa too, it was not discarded. Had it been the case Paramara
Bhoja would not have appointed Digambara Kulachandra, his
commander-in-chief as has been shown elsewhere.
During this period Jainism was well spread in some places
of Madhyapradesh such as —Ahar, Chanderi, Madanpur, Devagarh
and Vidarbha. The Chintamani parsvanatha temple at Khambat

(Cambay) was built about A. D. 1108 and repaired in A. D. 1225.

It records names of several devotees from Malwa, Sapadalaksha
and Chitrakuta, who endowed the temple from time to time ^
Jainism in the Deccan and South India
During the period under survey, Jainism was in full swing
in south India. The political ascendancy of the western Chalukyas
and Hoyasala, in the south paved the way to Jainism. Both the
dynasties, greatly patronised Jainism and their emergence into
power in the beginning of the 1 1th C.A.D. proved a great boon
for the propagation and glorification of the faith.* Several
temples were erected there by these dynasties.® There the samghas
‘ganas,’ ‘gachchhas,’ ‘balis* and sakhas of monks had grown
quite powerfully * The age produced a gal axy of Jain scholars
and preachers, whose literary and missionary activities helped
the advancement of the religion.
There are several spurious records at Lakshmesvara belong-
ing to the 10th or 11th C.A.D.—* purporting to record grants

ruling family, after whom the buildings were named - This

or patrons of Jainism.

clear that Jainism was flourishing in the south India. Rashid-
Ud-din on the authority of AIbcruni states that the people of

Konkana and Thana were Samanis (Monks) of Buddhists at the

beginning of eleventh C. A.D."* A.S, Altckar disagrees with this
view and asserts that the Muslims very often, mistook Buddhism
for Jainism He further adds that the above statements may be
taken to prove the prevalence of Jainism rather than Buddhism
in the Deccan during 10 th and eleventh C. A.D.'* It seems that
Jainism could not stand as state religion in South. According
to C.V. Vaidya, Jainism was yet a small religion spreading in
the South.”

Besides the Tirthahkaras, the worship of Ga!)c.sa and goddess

Mahalakshml was well popular in the Jain pantheon. There are

evidences of the worship of goddess Mahalakshmi along with

Much analogy is found in the Jainism and Hinduism, during
the period under review. The Jain temples were the replicas
of the Hindu temples. The worship of Mahavlra became very
popular and elaborate as that of Vishriu or Siva. Epigraphic
records show the pro\ision of ‘aiigabhoga’ and Tangabhoga’ to
Mahavlra like Hindu deities. The similarity is also seen in the
education system. The Jains pay homage to god GaneSa by
reciting the formula ‘Sri Ganesaya namah,” which is common in
the Hindu society. C. V Vaidya observes that the Hindus conti-
nued to leach their children the originally Jain formula i. e. “dm
namassiddhebhyah,’ even after the decline of Jainism.^ This
would indicate that during this period, both Hinduism and Jaini-
sm had much in common.
Hinduism
During the period under survey, Hinduism was the popular
religion of the masses. This religion is also known as the
‘Samskrit Pauranic’ religion. The contemporary epigraphic and
literary sources show that there were two main branches of
Hinduism, viz. ‘Saivism’ and ‘Vaishnavism.’ India, known to

Alberuni was in matters of religion ‘Vishnuitic’ (Vaishnava), not

‘Sivaite’ (Saiva). Vishnu or Narayana, is the first god in the

pantheon of his Hindu informants and literary authorities, whilst
‘Siva is only incidentally mentioned and that not always in a
favourable manner.^ But actually this was not the case. Saivism
also formed a dominant feature as will be discussed hereinafter
Whatever may have been the case, it is almost certain that Hindu-
ism was in ascendancy and most of the kings gave to it the royal
patronage. C.V. Vaidya on the authority of AI Ijerisi, states
that there were 52 sects, but all these were under one great reli-
gion i e. Hinduism, which flourished throughout the land.^ V|'e

VaisJmavistn
The doctrine of Vaishnavism underwent profound changes
diuing the period. The most important of these was the gradual
dominance of the pastoral life of Krishna and his dalliance with
thc‘Gopls’ or cowherd nirls.’ In the South, the then popular sect
called ‘Srivaisnava’ was the most cl.iboratecl form of Vaishnavism.

also support this view. Even then, it is sugges cd that he must
have b-’cn born some time a.boul A.D. 1037 and died about A.D.
1137.’ Ramanuja at first lived at Kanchipura and became the
follower of Yadavn prakasa the adva la philosopher. Later on,
he was dissatisfied with this feet and began to study the works of
alvars. Consequently, he became the .successor of Yamunacharya.
It is said that in the letter part of his life he was persecuted by
Saiva Chola monarch. This indicates that in the soutli too, the
popularity of Saivism was gaining ground.
Saivism
Saivism was the most prosperous sect of the age. Both
epigraphic and literary sources prove that it was patronised by
kings and people alike. The power and influence of “Saivism is
reflected in the numerous old temples dedicated to Siva, which

still exist in various parts of India.^ Majority of Gahadavala,®
Chedi,“ Chandella^ and Orissa kings® were styled as paratnama-

hesvara. Bharavaramma and Vaidyadeva of Assam ; Vijayasena

and Vallalasena of Bengal, Devapala Paramara and Bhartrivadha
Chahamana of western India also bore this religious epithet.

The kings of the Bhaiija or Mayura dynasty (9 th C. onward)

had the bull as their lanchana. Their family goddess seems to
have been the Stambe^varl, whose cult was wide spread in

Orissa.® The Chandellas of Bundel Khand {9th C. onwards) were

the keen devotees of Siva and Bhavani. The Kalanjara lithio
inscription contains a hymn of praise to Purari.’ Like the
Khadgas, the couchant bull formed the symbol of the Chandra
dynasty.® Their coins have the couchant bull on the obverse and
the trident on the reverse, a fact which clearly shows that the
ancient religion of the Buddhists was suspended and replaced by

Saivite objects. The Chahamanas of ‘Sakambharl’ (8th C. to

1193A.D)had the bull as their lafichana. The Bullion and

Silver coins of Somesvara and Prihviraja have on the two sides,

with regard to this line. The Malkapuram inscription states that
the Kalachuri king Yuvarajadeva of Dahala region made a
munificent grant of three lakhs of villages to Sadbhava Sambhu
who founded a monastery known as Golakimatha. As there is
the corresponding succession of the rajagurus and their royal
disciples the identification seems well-nigh certain.
The Saiva Sects
Before making a regional survey, it would be worthwhile to
discuss the popular sects of Saivism. Some times, Puranas divide

gaivism in three categories, viz. (!) Vedic (II) Tantric and

(III) mixed (Misra).' In the mixed (misra) school of Saivism,

the main deity Siva was worshipped along with four other gods.'
This school is, therefore, the same as the Smarta cult of the
‘panchadevopasana’. Vaidic Saiva school appears to be the same
LakullSa Pasupata whereas Tantric school was perhaps constituted
by Kalanana, Kapalika and other extreme sects. It may, however,
be noted that even LakulTsa Pasupata is originally Agamic but
it is described in the Puranas as vedic because it is comparatively
more orthodox and in the beginning it recognised the Vedic
institution of varna. The Saiva Siddhanfa school was Agamic.
The Malkapuram^ stone inscription of the time of the
Kakatlya king Rudradeva S.S. 1183 mentions four Schools of

Saivism (a) Saiva (b) Kalanana (c) Siva Sasana and (d) Pasupata.

Lists of these schools occur in the Vuniana’ and J^iva Puranas,^

other Agamic works.
A comparison of these lists would indicate that the four
important schools with their sub sects were prevalent in the

country :

—

(a) Saiva or more precisely Saivn-Siddhanta school,

(b) Kalanana also known ns Karuka.

(c) Pasupatn with a branch of the Lakuliita Pasupata, and

(d) Kapfilika with its associate cult of the Soma."

the prc-Rgvcdic period. All the Vratya sects had their own
distincti\e marks. We had, for instance, the S.iivas who had the
Lihga branded on both the arms, the Rudras who had the trident
branded on their forehead, the Ugras who had the ‘daraaru*

branded on both the arms, the Bhattas who had the Lings

respectively.* Also the appellations viz, Pasupata, Mahe^vara
and Mahavaratadhara are frequently applied to the followers of
more than one sect.
I. paSupata school

(A) iSrlkantlia and Founder : — §Tikantha is recorded as the

founder of this sect. A commentary on the ^aradatilaka begins

with a salutation to Srikan{ha, and refers to him as the first

teacher of the doctrine. Akin to other Saiva teachers, Srikantha
also is recognised as a god in the Saiva Pantheon Thus he is
mentioned as a deity in various positions in the Trika-School.
The Bihari inscription describes Yuvaraja II, who dedicated his
whole empire to his Saiva Siddhanta preceptor as always engaged
in the worship of Snkantha.
B. LAKULISA
R. G. Bhandarkar regards LakulTsa as the founder of the
Pasupata school ^ Bagchi suggests, “Lakullsa” was probably
Srikantha’s disciple and that these two were responsible for the
foundation of Pasupata religion.^ But Lakulisa does not seem
to be an immediate disciple of Srskantha, because the accounts
of Srikantha and Lakulisa available through literature and
inscriptions do not represent them as teacher and disciple.
Moreover, in the Agama quoted by Abhinavagupta, LakulTsa is
depicted as receivirg the doctrine from Svachhanda.* It would
therefore, be correct to suggest that the Pasupata school was
founded by Srikantha and from this sprang forth several
branches in the subsequent period. The branch started by
1 Pai, Religious sects in India among the Hindus, p. 69.
2. Vaisnavism etc. Coll. Works of Bhandarkar, IV, p. 1 72.

Religious Conditions 147
Lakulisa was called after him as LakuUsa Pasupata, while that
of iSrlkaplha is ‘Sivasasann.^
Pa,4upatas arc devoted to ^iva. According to Scriptures,
they worship Siva besmeared, with ashes and wearing Jatfi-
mukufa ” They believe in the eight forms of Siva which arc

(i) Sarv'U (ii) Bhava, (iii) I^ana (iv) Rudra (v) Ugra (vi) Bhima;

(vii) Pnsupati and (viii) Mahadeva. They arc mentioned in

Satapatha and Sahkhayana Brahmanas, Sutras etc. Their names
generally end in rasi and sometimes arc preceded by the titles
‘bhava’ or ‘panda*. The Satapatha and Kaustiki Brahmanas add '-

Asani to their list, thus making their number eight (inclusive of

Rudra). As regards the name Siva, occurring in the Vcdic Texts of

earlier strata, as one of the attributive epithets of several gods and
not of Rudra in particular, however, passed for a proper name in

the later vcdic literature (Cf. the Svetasvatara Upanishad). Siva is

one of the various names of (Rudra). A special mention of Siva,

in the Vedas, may be made of his identification with Agni and he

is also said to have given birth, by his contact with Pr^ni (the

Earth) and the Maruts (the winds). This identification is also

implied in his epithet of ‘Kapardin’. R. G. Bhandarkar points out
that the epithet Kapardin or the wearer of malted hair is probably
due to his being regarded as identical with Agni, the fumes of
which look like matted hair. As regards the later Vcdic period,
the Atharvaveda presupposes the rise of Rudra to the position of
the supreme god, for it assigns various such epithets to him as
have already been referred to above, either of which typify his

two aspects, ghora (terrific) and saumya (peaceful). Thus Rudra,

Sarva (arrow-wicldcr) Ugra and Bhima characterise his destructive

aspect, while Bhava, Pasupati, Mahadeva and Isfina, his beneficent
one. Such well known names of Puranic Siva as ‘Sambhu’ and
‘Sankara’, arc indicative of his beneficent nature, occur as so
many epithets of Rudra as early as ‘Satarudriya which also
contains epithets descriptive of the dark and fierce aspects of the

god. The beneficiery forms of Rudra (Siva) are known as Aghora,

or the milder (Saumya) forms of Saivism. The Samaraiigana

Srikantha, Ananta, Lakulisa and Goraksa were the Saiva
teachers who later on rose to the position of deities.
II. KSpoUka School
The epigraphio references prove the wide popularity of this
School. The Tilakawada C. P. inscription of the time of Bhoja
Paramara contains a reference to an ascetic who had observed.
Mahavrata.^ These Mahavratikas were identical with KapSlikas,
who are also associated with Sivasasana and Somasiddhanta.
The traces of Mahavratadhara are found in the contem-
porary inscriptions. The Tilakwada C. P. inscription describes
an ascetic Dinakara who was mahiivratadhara and was like ^iva
in the form’' of Kapalin. This sect is also referred to in the
literature. The Katha-Sarit sagara® Yasastilaka ChampQ* and
Nalachampu' all speak about this sect.
The 'Maha-'Vratins are identified with Kalaraukhas also. On
the authority of Siva Purapa, R O. Bhandarkar regards them as
identical.® The Nala Champu of Trivikrama Bhatta composed
in the 10th c. A. D , describes Kalamukhas as falling under the
class of mahavratikas.’
The Mahavratins were identified also with the KapSlikas.
The Tilakawada C. P. inscription® describes ' a mahavratin as
Saiikara in Kapali form. Moreover, as Kapalikas were associated

Obviously the sub-sects Kapfilika and ICrdrimukhas etc,
represent the extreme or ghora type of Saivisra and their
apparently unsocial and outlandish practices appear to be
reminiscent of the terrific aspect of Rudra-Siva and a very power-
ful sway that it exerted in the culture of India. When Siva
iconography rose to its height, these ghora forms proved to be a
bed-rock on which many a ghora-icon, the ghora or ugra-mvirtis
of Siva came into vogue.

The four-faced Mrilang Maliadcva (Mrtyuhjaya Mahfidcva)

temple at Khajuraho is a very valuable specimen of the Rudra-
Pasupatn form of $iva temple. It is situated close to the
Ramchandra or Chaturbhuja temple.
III. Soma Siddhilnta
The founder of this cult was Somasarma. The Purapas
state that Siva incarnated himself at Prabhasa as Somasarma

who had four disciples (i) Akshapada, the founder of Nyaya

School (ii) Kanada, the founder of Vaiseshika School (iii) Uluka,

a teacher of logic, someiimcs identified with Kanada and

tiv) Vat'^a.^ The Prabhiisa Palana inseription records that Soma

had constructed a golden temple of Somanafha at Prabhasa and
after originating his cult at the instance of Siva gave the place
to the Piisupatas -
During this period, Somasiddhanta was prevalent in Nepal
and Gujarat. The Umfi-Sahita-Chandra^ckhara form was
specially worshipped by the ascetics of this school.^
IV. Siddha School
The creed of cightyfour Siddhas had become quite influential
from the elcventh-twclfih century A. D From literature we learn
that the Siddhas were Siva-Sakti worshippers and hence the

150 Blioja Paramara and his Times
building of shrine for Rama by the Siddhartha Yogi is rather
striking.
V. Kaiananas
The Kalanana sect has not been mentioned in the inscriptions
of northern India. The sect was however very influential in the
south. It had several important centres under the Chalukyas.
Kalamukha ascetics also served as rajagurus of the Chalukyas.^
VI. The Saiva Siddhanta School
The inscriptions mention Guhavasi of Daruvana as the
preacher of Saiva Siddhanta doctrine. He is mentioned in
Linga-Vayu and Kurma Puranas as one of the twentyeight
incarnations of Siva.= Thus he seems to have been held as an
incarnation of Siva like LakulHa, the originator of Lakulisa
Pasupata doctrine.
The sect spread from the Punjab in the north to Tamil
' region in the south and influenced several kings of this period.
The-fifth ascetic in the line was Purandara who initiated
prince Avantivarman in the faith. The king dedicated his
kingdom to his spiritual preceptor as an honorarium where the
ascetic founded a monastery at Mattamayura town.®
The Prayaschitta Samuchchaya^ mentions Lambakarna as an
ascetic of Ranipatra monastery. He was the spiritual teacher of
the king Siyaka of Malwa. At the same time he has been
described as one who heard the whole of Saiva-siddhanta
philosophy and was like a Siva at Kailasa.
Thus, the ascetics of this branch spread front the Punjab

(Salt Range) to Deccan. They wielded considerable influence in

central India. Kings of Varroan dynasty of the Punjab, of
Chanderi Pratihara and Paramara dynasties in Central India were
their spiritual disciples.

Another line of Prabhava .^iva ran through Somasambhu
whose disciple Vamasambhu initiated a line of rajagurus of
the Kalachuri kings. 'This line continued upto the end of
Kalachuri dynasty in Dahalan. Vimaljsiva and his disciple
Viistusiva were probably the spiritual teachers of Kokalla II and
his son Gahgeyadeva. The name of the preceptor of Lakshmi-
karna is not traceable. Purushasiva was the prosperity of
Yasahakarna and Saktisiva was the real power of the king

Gayakarna. The two temples, one at Gurgi Masaun (12 miles

east from Pvewa town) and the other at Chandrchi also in the old

Rewa State, were possibly erected by one and the same person,
the abbot Prasantasiva of the Mattamayura sect of the ^ivas,
about the middle of the 10th century A. D.' The inscriptions of
these temples indicate that no less than seven Kalachuri kings,
namely, Lakshmikarna, Ya.sahkarna, Narasimha, Jayasimha,
Vijayasimha, Sahkaragapa and Trailokyavarmadcva, thcChandella
king who supplanted the Kalachuri dynasty arc styled as meditat-
ing upon the feet of the illustrious Vamadeva. But after the
fall of Kalachuri dynasty these ascetics had to migrate to diflcrent
places.
Thus, the line of nyagurus started from Vamasambhu in the

time of Yuvarajadeva II (C. 975 A. D ), and continued for more

than two hundred and fifty years. It ultimately came to an end
in C. 1225 A. H when the dynasty was supplanted by the
Chandclla ruler Trailokyavarmadcva.-

154 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
apparently having different forms, really has one form and it is
one monistic principle governing the threefold work of the universe,
the creation, the prejcrvation and the destruction. According to
Vishnudharmottara, the existence of the universe is guided by
threefold principle of energy or quality—Rajasa, Sattvika and
Tamasa Accordingly here Brahma aspect or former face repre-

sents the creative activity; Vaisnava one maintains and sustains

the universe, while the Saiva aspect simply destroys it (to create a

vacuum) for further creation. Let us have some idea of this

triad which is tabulated agewise hereunder :
—
Triad
Vedic Pauranic and Later Texts
1. Agni 1. Brahma.
2. Surya 2. Vishnu.
3. Vayu or Rudra 3. Siva.
In the Pauranic and later texts Brahma represents Agni. This
very truth is well-corroborated in the Samaraftgapa Sutradhara
by directing the image-maker to make the Pratima ‘antarita-
pratimiV to the flamed fire. The AparajitaprechS, a contemorary
work to Samarangana Sutradhara describes Trimurti of Mari,

Kara and Brahma, but also that of Candra (the moon) Arka

(the Sun) and Brahma (common) as well as Hari, the Sun (also

Viffiu) Kara and Hiranyagarbha. The former has not got its

place in sculpture, but the latter is fully depicted in it. The close
connection of Surya with Vishpu or Vishnu with Narayana is well
known. Accordingly a good many Trimurti sculpture depicts
this synchronism of six-armed composite image found at Madhia
in the old Panna State in Bundelkband region representing the
Trimurti of Surya, Siva and Brahma. The three-headed and
eight-armed standing Surya in Cidambaram temple also illust-
rates this. A somewhat similar composition, but a seated one,
is carved on the west face of the small shrine dedicated to the
sun-god in the southern corner of Limbhoji Mata’s temple at

Delmalt (northern Gujarat). Another eight-armed seated image

of this type is carved on the western side of the central Bhadra

(structure) of the Siva temple called Dula Deo at Khajuraho.

The worship of Siva with Sun J^akli (mother goddess), Ganesa

and Vishnu was performed in the MisJa-Pfi^upatn school.* This

development of the pented cult was the evolution of trinity com-

Brahma, Vibhnu and ,Siva formed the trinity ® Surya, Brahma

where.'

Garuda *

Pahchadeva ; —An inscription of Somavarman in the middle

of the e!e\enth C. A. D., invokes five deities Brahma, Gapapati,
Vishnu, Sakti and Siva.® But this pentad is not of the Smarts
variety as Surya has been replaced here by Brahma.
The evidence for the Smnrla group of the five deities comes
from the lingas of the I Ith C A, D., representing the four
sectarian deities, Vishnu, Sakti, Ganesa and Surya on four sides.
Similarly, the miniature shrines® representing these five sectarian
deities in sancta and around, furnish the testimony for the

prevalence of (his cult in early and late mediaeval periods.

It may, therefore, be concluded that the cult of the five
deities as envisaged by the Smartas came into vogue by the llth
C. A. D. and that it indicates the reapproachment of the Vedic
and Agamic tendencies The view that Pancadevopasand was
introduced by Samkaracharya does not seem to be right.
Inscriptions thus describe three Saiva movements. The
earliest movement was started by Srikantha who founded the
Pa^upata schools much before the Christian era. Soma a little
iater, started at Prabhasa two Schools, namely the School of logic
known as ‘naya-Soma’ and the Saiva devotional School called
‘Soma-Siddhanta.’ The association of School of logic with Saiva
devotional sects is perceived still later. The Pasupatas belonged
to the Nyaya while the Saivas were Vaiseshikas.
Lakulisa initiated the second movement which, in due course
became very influential. Inscriptions mention several important
centres of this school. From them sprang forth many other

branches, Ananta section of the Pancharthikas is specially

Giihavasi, just before this period, originated a very powerful
and vigorous movement which spread to the Punjab, Varfmasi,
Dahnla, Malvva, Central India and Rajputana. It sent its bran-
ches to Deccan, Tamil region and Andhra area.
Regionsise Surrey of ^alvism
We shall now try to present a clear picture of the regional
influence of Saivism.
Kashmir
In Kashmir .Saivism got a considerable support of the kings.
The efforts of scholars in this direction arc also praiseworthy.
Abhinavagupta“s successors wrote on the Pratyabhijria-hridaya
which is an important manual of .'5aiva doctrines,’
Assam
In Assam both the cults i. c. .Saiva and Vishjiu were prevalent
simultaneously. Ratnapala, a king of the llth C. professed
faith both in .Saiva and Vishpu cults.=
Bengal
Vijaynsena the founder of the Sena Kingdom of Bengal, was
a devotee of f5iva. He assumed the titles of Paramamahesvara
and Annishabha S'ankara and built a Siva temple. His son and
successor, Ballalascnn w.as also an ardent follower of Saivism.
But Lakshmisena, the son and successor of Balhlla became a con-
vert to Vaishnavism."'
Banaras and Kanauj
Govindachandra, the Gahadvfila king of Banaras and Kanauj,
was a ‘parama-raahciivara.’ He patronised the Buddhists also.
On the obverse of his gold and copper coins arc found the figures

of Tri^ula (Trident), the symbol of .^*iva.’

158 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
Gujarat
We may, commence our survey with the celebrated temple
of Somanatha on the seashore, in south-western Kathiawar, at
Somanatha-pattana, dote to the modern port of Veraval The
temple of Somanatha was built earlier than the 11th C. A. D.

It was probably as old as the maitrakas of Valabhi (the modern

Vala, near Bhavanagar). The great ‘Siva temple was built upon

fiftysix pillars of teak-wood coveted with lead. The glory of
its wealth and splendour is preserved in the records of Muslim
historians ’
It was sacked and desecrated by Mahmud of Gazni in 1026
A. D, and subsequently destroyed by a lieutenant of Mahmud,
but soon after, it was rebuilt of stone by Bhimadeva ll, the

Solanki King of Gujarat (A. D. 1022-6') - Siivism was paramo-

unt in Gujarat during the rule of Solanki Kings who were
great builders of temple dedicated to §iva.^ Their capital was at

Anhillapura (Anahilavada or Patan). The other important

places were Siddhapur and vadnagar, now included in Baroda
state, to the north of Ahmedabad. The first King Mularaja

(A D. 19't2-96) was an ardent devotee of Siva in the form of

Somanath or Somesvara. The temples of Mulesvara and the
Tripuresa Prasad appear to have been built by him in honour of
Somanatha.'*

Religious Conditions 159
Very few temples are however extant in Gujarat. The
remains of the temples of the Solankis and the Vaghels are
but fragments. Fortunately, the great temples of the Deccan,
Bundelkhand, Orissa and south India are still in existence. We
shall, in the course of our study, enumerate the more important
example which illustrates the progress and influence of the l§aiva
faith in those regions.
From Gujarat we may turn to Mount Abu which was a
flourishing centre of Saivism at an early age. The Abu region
was ruled by a branch of the Paramara dynasty of Malawa
from the end of the lOth C.A.D. to the end of llthC.A.D.
under the suzerainty of the Chalukyas of Gujarat. It may

be noted that Bhima I of Gujarat (A. D. 1022-64) brougut for

some time the Abu territory under his direct control in the
2nd quarter of the eleventh C.A.D., The Jain Vimal Saha
was deputed as the governor of Abu, who built the famous
marble temple of Adinath in 1031 A.D,
Among the lingas worshipped 'at Abu, Acale^vara was the
most famous An inscription of Yaso-dhavala, one of the
Paramara kings of Abu dated 1150 A.D. was found in the
temple of Acalesvara.
Mewar
Saivism was the prevailing religion in neighbouring Mewar.
The worship of Ekalihga Siva, the symbol of a national cult has
survived till today. The worship of Ekalinga is mentioned in an
inscription of Naravahana, dated 972, A.D. But the cult is
earlier than the 10th C.A.D. , as two inscriptions dated Saiiivat

1331 and 13:6 (1272 and 1285 A.D.) respectively, which give

the genealogy of the ruling dynasty place the cult of Ekalinga at
the beginning of the political history of Mewar.^
The evidence to be considered later will show that Saivism
represented a vigorous religious movement in a large part of

160 Dhoja Paramara and his Times
western and central India from Gujarat to Rewa in the 10th C.
and onwards. The expression of Saivism in western India dates
back to a much earlier period It was the prevailing religion
under the Valabhi kings even in the 6th C A. D.
Malwa
Malvva was a centre of Siivism. Its ancient capital Ujjayini
or Avanti was famous through the ages as the seat of the worship
of Mahakala Siva.
The Paramaras of Malwa, who ruled as independent monarchs
from about the middle of the lOih C. A. D. to the end of 13th
C. A. D. and whose dominions, in their widest limits, extended
from Gujarat to major parts of Madhya Pradesh and from
Khandesh to northern Indore, were devout worshippers of Siva
They were the great builders of Siva temples. The Paramara
king Bhoja, the most famous L.dian monarch of the 1 Ith C.A D.
built a number of temples dedicated to Kedare^vara, Rame^vara,

Somanath, Sundira li") and Kalanaln Rudra^; these temples do not

exist now because most of the religious edifices of Malwa were
destroyed by the Mahammadins.
Bhoja, I, the Paramara ruler of Malwa, was not only a
follower but also an exponent of Saivism.- One of his works, the
Tattvaprakasa deals with Saivism. In his Samarangana Satradhara
he has devoted a separate chapter for the treatment of lihgas,
the emblematical phallus of S-va. It introduces a novelty by its
description of the Lokapala lingas, like Agneya, Aindreya,
Yamya, Varuna, Vayanya etc etc.® Though traditional it is
unique in the sense that it gives the relative merits of each and
every material.
All the land-grants of Bhoja start with the benedictory verses
in the honour of God Siva His Ujjain-plates begin with

“Glory of the God whose hair is the sky and who bears on his

The Betma plates of Bhojadeva dated V. S. 1076 A. D. 1020

begin with “Om victorious is this Vyomakesa (l§iva) who for the

purpose of Creation bears on his head the digit of the Moon,
which appears like the shoot from which the world sprang.”^
Besides, in the Udaipur-prasasti^ Bhoja is mentioned as

‘Bhargabhakta’ (the worshipper of Siva). The Rajataranginl^

tells us that Bhoja constructed a pond in Kashmir close to
Kapale^vara Mahadeva temple.
The book Ganaratna-mahodadhi, praised Bhoja for witnes*
sing god Siva in His original form.® Thus, we come to know that
Bhoja was a great devotee of god ‘Siva’ as he, in his grants is
styled as Parama-Mahesvara. Not only Bhoja but almost all the
Paramara kings were devout worshippers of god Siva.®
Ujjain
Ujjain, as being under the dominance of Paramara Bhoja,
was naturally important centre of Saivism.'^ The Mahakala

162 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
temple of Ujjain was in great veneration. Bhoja used to live in the
vicinity of this temple.^ It is now needless to estimate the wide
popularity which this temple has attained for Saivism. Even
today the §iva-linga enshrined in this temple is counted among

one of Dvadasa Jyotir-lingas (the twelve ever-shining lingas).

Tripuri
The Kalachuris of Tripuri were iSaivas with few exceptions.^
The Karan Mandir is a Siva temple in the Chedi region. It is
ascribed by local tradition to Karnaraja, the powerful Haihaya
king who ruled in the 3rd quarter of llth C. A. D. Thus it will
be seen that the early monuments of the Chedi country are
mostly devoted to the cult of ^iva. Dr. A. N. Upadhya and
H. L. Jain^ opine that at the time when Somadeva wrote his
Yasastilaka, Saivism was predominent in central India as in the
South.
Bwidelkhand and Bagheikhand
From Malwa we may pass on to Bundelkband, which was an
important centre of Saivism during the rule of the Chandella
kings, in the 10th, llth and 12th centuries. The Chandella

kingdom included Khajuraho (now a village in Chhatarpur State

Madhya Pradesh), Kalanjar, (in Banda district U. P.) and

Mahoba (in Hammirpur district, U. P.). Dhanga was a devout

worshipper of Saivism.
Saivism was prevalent not only in Bundelkhand, but also in
other areas of central India. Jaso, the chief town of the tiny
State of that name in Bagheikhand, seems to have been an
important centre of Saivism in the middle ages.
Deccan
Saivism played a decisive role in the temple architecture of
the Deccan and the adjoining countries.* Saiva temples were

constructed or maintained, on a wide scale during the reign of
the Chalukyas of Kalyani as is shown by the various grants and'

subject of an endowment made for the benefit of the temple of
Suvarnaksi a form pf §iva, styled as Sankara Svayambhti-Suvarria-

Apart from the inscriptional evidence, there exists a large
number of surviving specimens of later Chalukyan ^aiva temples
ranging from the lOth to the end of I2th C. A. D. mostly in the
Dharwar district and the immediate neighbourhood.^ Somesvara

(A. D. 1043-1 C6S), the western Chalukya ruler of Kalyan was an

ardent Saiva.®
South-India
From Deccan we may turn to south India. 1 here the classic
land of Saivism was the Tamil country, which witnessed one of
the greatest epochs of the faith. Saivism is prevalent in south
even today and ^aivas are found in great numbers in the districts
of Tinnevelly, Madura, Trichinapoly, Tanjore, south and north
Arcot, Chingelput, Madras, Salen and Coimbatore.^
General Characteristics of the Religious Life
The Spirit of Toleration
Wide and sympathetic toleration was the general characteris-
tic of the age under survey. There was an inner movement of
synthesis. The Belur inscription® of Jayasimha Chalukya,
dated 1022 A. D., is extremely interesting in this regard. In the
inscription, the donor Akkadevi is described as practising the
religious observances prescribed by the rituals of Jina, Buddha,
Ananta i.e. Vishnu and Rudra. The Temple that she had
erected was for Tripurusha i.e. Vishnu, Brahma and Sankara.
1. E.I. XV[,pp.2,6.
2. Cousens (op. cit), p. 74 fif.
3. The Struggle for Empire, p. 443.
4. Tamilian Antiquary, No. 3, p. I, 1909.
5. LA. XVIII, p. 274.
164 B/iojfl Paramara and his Times
Thus she made a synthesis of not only Hindu cults but also of all
the main religious movements of the time viz. Buddhism, Jainism,
Vaishciavism aad Saivism. Another document belonging to the
same century begins with the laudation of Jina, followed immedia-
tely by that of "Vishnu The inscription informs us that at the
desire of the king the lord Nagavarma caused to be built a temple
for each of Jina, Vishnu, Isvara and the Saints * Kumarapala was
Saiva, but he is said to have been converted to Jainism.^ An
inscription in Kanarese language from Sravana Belgola records
that Paramara Bhoja worshipped at the feet of Jainacharya-
Prabhachandra 3 The Dubkund-inscription of the Kachchha-
paghata-Vikramaditya dated V. S. 1145 records that Jainacharya
Santisena defeated the Pandits of Bhoja’s court, who formerly
had defeated other Jaina-acharyas like Ambarasena * It seems as
if there was no cultural difference among the three religions and
that a man might follow anyone of them or make a combination,
suitable to his own temperament, of the acceptable elements of
any or all of them.
The spirit of toleration was not confined only to the religions
of the land, but was extended to Mahomadanism as well. The
liberal attitude of Hindus towards Muslims is incorporated in the
contemporary records. An inscription engraved on the grave of
Abdullah Shah Changal dated H. year 859-A. D. 1455 records
that Bhoja was converted to Mahommedanism and became
known as Abdullah.
The Guladaste-Abra a small Urdu booklet states that,
dazzled by the enchantments of Fakir Abdulla Shah, Bhoja was

l65Religious Condition
converted to Mahommedanism. But all the above statements
seem to be imaginary for there is no strong evidence to establish
that Bhoja ever embraced Mahommedanism. We may simply
infer that with regard to religion, the age marked the great
toleration, and there was no hatred even for the most anti-
religious followers of Mahommedanism.
Cessation of Sacrifices
The other significant feature of the religious life of the period
was the cessation of sacrifices. In spite of the prevalence of Vedic
religion, the age did not permit sacrifices which involved the
slaughter of sentient beings. Alberuni was informed that the
Vedic sacrifices were rarely performed and practically abandoned
because they presupposed a long life which was no longer
vouchsafed in the present age.^ Thus we hardly come across any
Hindu king of the period who boasted to have performed any
of Vedic sacrifices. These sacrifices were probably substituted by
Panchamahayajfias, the gospel preached by the Smritis. During
this period, Smarta Agnihotra was fairly common at least among
professional priests. Alberuni writes that the Brahmanas who
kept one fire were called Ishn’ns, and those who kept three fires
were called agnihotrins.^
Conception with Regard to God
The conception with regard to God among the Hindus was
one and the same Alberuni writes that the Hindus believed
that ‘God’ is one, eternal, without beginning and end, acting by
free-will, almighty, allwise, living, giving life, preserving, one who
in His sovereignty is unique, beyond all likeness and unlike-ness,
and that He does not resemble anything nor does anything
resemble Him’®
Worship by Brahmam Rewarding
The Brahmanas were the main exponents of the religious
performances during the period. It was a belief that the worship
1. Sachau II, p. 139.
2. Sachau I, p. 102.
3. Sachau, I, Chapt II, p. 27.
166 Shoja Paramara and his Times
performed by a Brahmana yielded more results. Bhoja writes
that the worship done by a Brahmana was more rewarding.i
Most of the religious matters were decided by special officer of
Government belonging of course to Brahmana castes. These were
known as Vinayasthitisthapakas, Dharraankusas, Dharmapra-
dhanas or Pandits. The last-mentioned officer was to be a
member of the ministry, who was to review the realm of social
and religious practices. Sukra and Bhoja both refer to this
officer as a member of the ministry.®
Daily Routine
The daily routine also got a radical change during this
period. The earlier Smriti writers like Manu and Yajfiavalkya
prescribe one bath and two prayers a day and place an emphasis
principally on the Panchamahayajfias. But Alberuni notes the
theoretical rule that a Brahmana should take three baths a day.
He further adds that in practice the evening prayers were recited
without a previous bath. He writes that evidently the rule about
the third bath was not as stringent as that relating to the first
and the second.® This shows that the number of prayers and
sandbyas, was also tending to increase to three.
Articles for Worship
A very good picture of the daily temple life, is found
in the contemporary records. Usually the daily worship was done
three times a day.'' Various articles were offered to gods at
thetimeofNaivedya. on each of these worships. Bhoja® gives
the list of some of these articles which were offered to the
deities, and were separately attached with each of the deities •
1. i ss., p. 22.5 v. 4.
2. Yuktikalpataru, p. 2, v. 13.
3. Sachau, 11, pp. 33,134.

Gandharva Fish, Flesh, etc.
Flowers and garlands were of course indispensable. The
contemporary records frequently mention grants made for flower
gardens which were usually attached to the temples. Rice,
Scented water, honey and costly clothes were the other articles
for worship On the occasion of worship musicians used to
play music. These items were known as *anga-bhoga’. Some
records of the period refer to the provision for ‘Tahga-bhoga”
of gods. By the term ‘Rahgabhoga’, A.S. Altekar means, the
periodical celebrations of Pauranic dramas, which may have-
been exhibited at the time of the annual fair at the temple.^
An inscription at the Rajarajesvara temple, belonging to the
beginning of the eleventh Century^, records a provision made
for the actors, who used to take part in a drama called Rajaraja.
The ceremonies like chariot procession etc. were performed
on the occasions of fairs. Alberuni refers to the populariy of these
festivals. He remarks that in Chaitra a festival was ceremonised
in which Vasudeva was swung to and fro in a swing.®
Animal Sacrifices
The animal sacrifices were fairly common in the northern
India. Alberuni writes that the worshippers of some deities like
DurgS, Mahadeva, Kshetrapala and Vinayaka used to kill
sheep, and buffalos with axes and offer them as naivedya.'^ But
we do not know whether these practices were common in the
1. The Rashtrakutas and Their Times, p. 291.
2. S. u. Nos. 4-5.
3. Sachau II, p. 178.
4. Ibid I, p. 120.
168 Bhoia Paramara and his Times
South. Al-masudi and A1 Idrisi do not refer to these practices
in the South. Possibly owing to the stronghold of Jainism, these
practices were exceptions in the Deccan.
Temple Building A meritorious Deed
The erection of the temple was known as the most meritori-
ous deed. Innumerable temples were constructed during this
period. Bboja alone is credited with the construction of several
temples as has already been mentioned. The kings, queens, and
their ministers and rich subjects delighted in building temples of
their favourable deities. Thus the whole country was covered
with temples.^
Religious Grants
The custom of issuing grants for religious purposes was widely
popular. Several grants were made during this period to defray
the temple expenses. The Kalvan inscription of Yasovarman, a
feudatory chief of Paramara Bhoja, records that certain places of
land with flower garden, 14 Baniya shops, oil mills and 14 drammas
were given to the illustrious Muni Suvratadeva in the temple of
Svetapada, to defray the expenses of worship such as Abhisheka,
Naivedya and Chaitrapavitraka as well as for the maintenance of
the Jain monks, who were called Rishis.® Thus temples soon
became recognised centres of public worship and were replicas of
Hinduism. It is note-worthy that the funds raised for temples,
images, their ornaments and daily worship rose very high dur/ng
the period. Al-utbi says that the kings of Hind, the chiefs of
that country and rich devotees used to amass their treasures and
precious jewels and send them time after time to idols, that they
might receive a reward for their good deeds and draw near to their
gods.®
We do not find any evidence from the Deccan belonging to
this period regarding the wealth unnecessarily boarded in the
temples in the form of ornaments etc. In northern India, temples
were the places, according to A. S. Altekar, that yielded the
1. History of Mediaeval Hindu India, II, p. 198.
2. E.I. XIX, pp. 69-75.

i.
Religious Condition 169
highest amount of plunder to Mahmud of Ghazni.^ But in Deccan
too, some temples were the centres of wcaltli as is proved by
the Rajarajcsvnra temple in which the Chola Kings and
their subjects furnished costly gold and jewel ornaments to the
deity -
Part of the charity that flowed into the temples was usually
utilised for the maintenance of schools and colleges. They also
discharged the duty of poor-relief by maintaining feeding houses
cic.^
Temple Organisation
The inscriptional evidences show that the organisation of the
staff of a temple was on sound fooling. The Sudi inscription of

the reign of Somesvara 1. Suka 976 (A.D. 1054) stales that the

posts in a temple were assigned according to the functions and
places.'*
In the South, the temple worship was performed according to
the castes. The Sudi inscription* records that in the temple of
Kfili at Conjeevnram the right hand castes worshipped, in one
map^apa, and the left hand castes in another.® F. W. Thomas
opines that the division seems to have been in existence long
before the 10th. C. A.D. and was indicated by the separation of
the castes in public worship and Rajaraju made the use of the

principle (or at least the name) to classify his armies. It is not

known whether in northern India too, this practice was prevalent.’
The Construction of Religious Ponds
The construction of religious ponds became the fashion of the
day. Albcruni states that in every place to which some particular
holiness was ascribed, the Hindus constructed ponds intended for

the ablutions. In this art they had attained success to a very high
degree. He adds that in Multan there was a pond in which the
Hindus worshipped by bathing themselves, if they were not pre-
vented. The pond became famous particularly for holiness either
because some important event had happened to them or because
there was some passage in the holy text or tradition which refer-
red to them.^
The gift of a piece of a land was regarded as the most pious
deed. An eleventh C. record shows that all wealth was really
produced from land = The grants were made particularly on
sacred occasions such as Samkrantis etc. Bhoja issued his Ujjain
plate dated A.D. 1021 on the 3rd day of the dark fortnight of the
month of Magha^ and Betma plates on the occasion of Magha
Sudi* 5. All this shows that the theory of charity being the most
effective means of securing religious merit, was in ascendancy in
this period. A verse in the Kalas Budruk plates, dated A.D. 1026,
says that neither learning nor we.alth produces so much merit as
charity.®
The alms-giving was fairly common. Alberuoi states that it
was obligatory with the Hindus to give as much alms every day
as possible. They did not let money become a year or even a
a month old, for this would be a draft on an unknown future, of
which a man does not know whether he reaches it or not.®
Idol Worship
The idol worship was the common factor with all the Hindus.
The Hindus regard idols as mere visible symbols of the divine.
But during this period a visible idol was regarded to be everything
and the greatest importance was attached to its safety. Alberuni
writes that the city of Thaneshar was highly venerated by the
Hindus. The idol of that place was called Cakrasvamin, i. e.

the owner of the cakra, a weapon of bronze, and it was nearly
of the size of a man. It was lying in the hippondromc in Ghazna,
together with the lord of Somanath, which was a representation
of the penis of Mabiideva, called Lihga.^
Al-beruni refers to next idol !§arada In Kashmir. According
to him, in inner Kashmir, about two or 3 days journey from
the Capital in the direction towards the mountains of Balor,
there was a wooden idol called Sarada which was much
venerated and frequented by pilgrims. But such idols were
erected only for uneducated low class people.^ Albcruni further
writes that the Hindus used to manufiicture the idol and monum-
ents in honour of certain much-venerated persons, prophets,
sages and angels destined to keep alive their memory, when they
were absent or dcad.^ But at the same time he states that the
construction of idols was not regarded as the most pious work.*
According to him such idols were erected only for uneducated low
class people of little understanding, that the Hindus never made
an idol of any supernatural being much less of god, and lastly to
show how the crowd was kept in thraldom by all kinds of
priestly tricks and deceits.® H.C. Rawlinson, on the authority of
Alberuni clearly writes that the educated Hindus abhorred
anthropomorphism.
Popular Deities
Let us now discuss the popular gods and goddesses of the
period. The deities and gods of the Pura-nas, soon became
popular gods of the masses. Different gods such as Siva, Vishnu
Aditya, Devi and GaijeSa were worshipped and even some trees
and animals,® The worship of Durga, Lakshmi, Bhattesvari.
Uma and Sarasvatl was in great veneration.’

The Goddess Sarasvaii
The association of Durga, Kali, Mahakall’ LaksmI,

MahalaksmI or Lalita etc, vvith Yantric riles is more popular;

The Aparajitaprchcha, however, has devoted a separate
chapter to the worship of Sarasvatl alongwith Bhairavl, the cult
deity of the Tantrikas and the ritual which has been propounded
there being called under the names, Mahavidya, Mahavlpa,
Bharall and Sarasvatl. YuttbeT, most cwionsly the mantres
of the worship as given in the ritual fully characterise the Sakta

mantras.i On one of ihe sculptures (lOih C. A.D.) hailing from

Khitching (Mayurbhanj, Orissa) is carved a handsome figure of

Sarasvatl like a railing pillar found at Bharhut. Paramara Bhoja
himself was a great devotee of this goddess. In her honour he
installed an image in his famous university at Dhara which be-
came known after her dame such as Bharatibhavana Varada-
sadana etc.
Lakshmi
All the forms of the goddess Lakshrai, whose worship is associated
with the cult of Vaishnavism, were in prevalence during the period.
In the Candikalpa, Mahalakshml is described as Sairabhamardinl

(Cp the destroyer aspect of the Sakti as represented in many of

the manifestations like MahishasuramardinI etc ). Her attributes

include the familiar weapons in the hands of Durga—Parasu,
kulisa, danda, sakti, asi, cafma, ghanta, sula, pasa etc. alongwith
winecup in her hands which number as many as eighteen In
the Visvakarma silpa, this goddess has been more curiously locali-

sed at Kollapur (western Deccan) as holding a pot and a club

in her right hands and a wood-apple in her left ones.

Apart from the Valshnavi Lakshmi, (Full of lotuses) the

worship of the Saivi Lakshmi or Simha-Vahinl Lakshmi was
also in vogue. The references to the Gaja Lakshmi form in which
the most conspicuous element is the presence of two elephants
pouring water on her are found in the art of Bharhut, Sanchi,
Bodhgaya, Amaravati and other places. In many medieval
temples, specially of Orissa, Gaja Laksmi was carved in the
1. Pratima Lakshana, pp. 217-18.
Religious Condition ^
center of the architrave over the doorway of the main structure,
whatever might have been the cult-amiiations of the shrines
as at Ellora where the goddess bathed by two elephants is
seated on a lotus in a lotus pond alongwith other celestial
attendants.^
The Samarahgana Sulradhura deals elaborately with the
canons of Lahshmi icon which will be discussed in detail in a
separate chapter on sculpture. The numismatic evidence e.g.,
the coins of the Kalachuris, Gahadavfilas and Chandcllas etc,
prove the wide-spread worship of LakshmT in her various forms.
The reverse type of the Kalachuri Gahgeyadeva coinage consists
of a rudely executed figure of goddess seated cross-legged.
The Chandella copper coinage represents the same pattern except
that it substitutes Hanuman for the goddess.- The obverse of the
eastern Chedi or Ratnapur coinage resembles that of Gangeyadeva
and the Chandcllas, but on the reverse an indistinct figure of a
rampant lion to the right lakes the place of the goddess. We may

cite the examples of the gold coins of prithvi Deva C^II) about

1 140 A.D., Jajalla Deva (711) about 1 1 60-7S A.D. Smith reckons

these coins as Rampant lion type.® The gold coinage of the

Chandellas of Jejakbhukti (Bundclkhand) and that of Kumara-

paladeva (about 1019-46 A.D.), the Tomara ruler of Ajmer and

Delhi contain, on the reverse, the figure of the seated goddess.^
The three principal gods i.e, Brahma, Vishnu and ^iva, in the
multitude of deities formed a trinity.'^ The architectural evidence
supports this view of trinity of gods. The triple shrined temple

at Kasara (Gujarat) now in ruins includes three shrines on its

east, out of which the western facing the porch being dedicated
to ^iva, the northern to Vishnu and the southern to Brahma.® The

other triple shrined temple is found at prabadi (Kathiawar) of

the same style as the above one and may belong to the same date

As the Vedic religion was considered supreme, so the land
recognised every worship from the agni worship of the Vedas
down to the worship of stones and trees, especially 5 deities of
modern Hinduism . Siva was the predominant and the favourite
deity with the rising kingly families, as has already been noted
under the heading Saivism.
Both Aryan and Non-Aryan civilisations had joined hands in
evolving the idolatry. Siva, Vishnu, and Aditya were Aryan deities
and Ganapati and Sakti or Bhagavatl, probably Non-Aryan
deities. To all these was added the worship of uncouth stones,
trees and serpen ts.=
There was no ill-feeling or animosity among people. All the
above forms of worship were phases of one religion. F. E.
Kielhorn,^ observes that the worship of god Siva and Vishnu was
performed in one temple. According to him this is proved by
the statement of the grant of Somavarmadeva aud Asatadeva
whose date according to Cunningham is between A. D. 1028 and
1031 also 1087. The plate records the grant of 15 bhtimi of land.
The same plate in lines 21-24, describes that two bhumasakba or
half of bhQmi were granted by the king as an agrahara, to a
temple of Vishnu in honour of gods Vishnu and Siva. Thus it
is not surprising if god Vishnu and Siva were worshipped in one
temple.
The idol-worship besides all its merits, had made the
Hindu religion more rigid and complex and consequently
brought disaster to the safety of the nation. This is a fact that
Hindus could have ousted the Muslims from Multan, had they
not been compelled to retire by the threats of the Muslim garrison
to break the famous idol of the Sun in that city, if it was besieged.*

A.S. Altckar agrees wtth this fact and corroborates that if the
philosophy of idol-worship were properly understood at the time,
this threat could not have deterred the Hindus and another idol
could have been installed in the proper religious manner to replace
the destroyed one.i
Pilgrimage
According to the descriptions of the puraijas the pilgrimage
to various important Tirihas or sacred places was quite prevalent
in the period. Alberuni writes that pilgrimages were not obliga-
tory to the Hindus, but were facultative and meritorious. A man
set off to wander to some holy region, to some much venerated
idol or to some holy river as the Ganges. He worshipped in
them, worshipped the idols, made presents to them recited many
hymns and prayers, fasted and gave alms to the Brahmanas, the
priests and others. He shaved the hair of his head and beard
and returned home.^
The importance of Benaras, as a sacred place of Hindus was
still predominant in the minds of the people. Alberuni states
that it was known as an asylum. He further adds that the
Hindus had some places which were venerated for reasons con-

nected whh their law and religion, c. g. Benaras (Varanasi). The

anchorites wandered and stayed there for ever as in Mekka.
They wanted to live there to the end of their lives that their
reward after death should be the belter for it.^
Besides Benaras, Alberuni writes that the places like Puskara,

Thaneshwar, Mahuta (Mathura) and Kashmir were known for

holy ponds. He further adds that the Hindus used to visit
Multan also before its Sun-idol temple was destroyed.* The
popularity of Prayaga, Varanasi and Gaya must have been still
greater, as the arrangements were made to provide fresh Ganges
water for the ablution of idols.®

176 Bho]a Paramara and his Times

During the period, the observation of vratas (fasts) was in

ascendancy. As the Pauranic religion was capturing the popular
imagination, the vratas powerfully appealed to the common mind.
These vratas were observed for the fulfilment of desires through
self denial and austerities. Alberuni writes that fasting was with
the Hindus, voluntary and superogatory. Fasting was abstain-
ing from food for a certain length of time. He has referred to
various common methods of fasting such as' : —

Upavasa, It means fasting ; not eating from one noon to the

other was called ekanakta

Kricchra ; A man took food in the day at noon and on

the following dav in the evening. On the 3rd day he ate
nothing except what was given to him without asking. On the 4th
day he fasted.
Paraka. A man took food at noon on 3 consecutive days.
Then he transferred his eating hour to the evening during 3 further
consecutive days without breaking fast.

CandrZyana : A man fasted on the day of full moon. On

the following day be took only a mouthful, on the 3rd day he
took double this quantity, on the 4th day he took threefold of it,
and going on thus till the day of new moon. On that day he
fasted, on the following days he again diminished his food by one
mouthful a day, till he again fasted on the day of full moon.
Masopavasa
A man uninterruptedly fasted all the days of a month with-
out ever breaking fast.
Thus from Alberuni, we come te know that the notion of
observing fasts, was deep-rooted in the minds of the people of
his time.2
Devadasis
A very regrettable feature of the temple atmosphere was its
vitiation by the association of dancing girls. The contemporary

2. S. a. II, p. 278 ff.

epigraphic records as well as the accounts of the foreign trave-

llers throw considerable light on (his raal-practice. An inscri-

ption of the RajarajcSvara temple^ belonging to the beginning of
the nth C. records an order of Rfijaraja, transferring as many as
400 dancing girls from the various quarters of his kingdom to the

Tanjore (emple and assigning to each of them one veli of land.

This practice persisted on and continued at least upto the time of

Marco Polo. He states (hat a person who desired to take td the

life of a sannyasin was tested by his capacity to stand the blandi-
shments sent to lure him. In this way, he tells us that these
dancing girls were not leading pure lives but had degenerated
into women of easy virtue. The reference of these dancing girls

is also found in (he Sudi inscription of the reign of Sorae^vara 1,

dated A. D. 1054, which refers to them among (he members of

organising staff of the temple.- Alberuni also while describing
about this unfortunate practice, writes that the association of
dancing girls with temples was obnoxious which was felt by the
learned men who were making efforts to abolish it.® According
to him, the kings maintained the institution of devadasis for the
benefit of their revenue even in the teeth of opposition by the
Brahmin priests. The kings made these dancing girls a source of
attraction to their subjects for meeting the expenditure of the
armies out of the revenues derived therefrom.'* It would thus
indicate that the girls were often purchased to be dedicated to the
temples.®
Kalhana® refers to the devadasis in the temples of Kashmir
and MerutuAga" in the Kumaravihara in Somanaih-pattana. This

practice of maintaining devadasis in temples, is as old as Kautilya;

but there was no attempt before the llth C. A. D. to pool
together all the dancing girls attached to all the temples in a

particular region, with a view to adding to the attraction of
religious festival, as noticed in the period under review. A 10th
century inscription from Rajaputana confirms the statement of
Alberuni. It records the express instructions of a chieftain to
his decendants that if the arrangement that he had made about
the services of dancing girls at different temples was interfered
with by ascetics and Brahamins, they should be at once stopped.*
It is, therefore, clear from this record that the opposition of
the majority of the Brahmanas and puritanical thinkers to the
association of the dancing girls with temples was so strong down
to the lOlh century that even kings wets afraid that their
arrangements may fail owing to the success of the puritanical
crusade. Obviously the Puranas, which recommend the employ-
ment of dancing girls in temples, represent the view of a minority
of the Brahmana community which in its zeal to provide pomp
and splendour at temple service, failed to realise the natural
effects of the new practice on the moral and spiritual atmosphere
of divine shrines. All the moral and religious leaders of the
community however, proved of no avail. The royal support
eventually succeeded in making the custom general and popular.

## CHAPTER VII
### SOCIAL CONDITION
Of the social condition during the period, we obtain ample
evidence in the contemporary literature, inscriptions and in the
work of Alberuni.
The essential features of the Indian social system, as preserved
in the Smriti tradition of the preceding centuries are faithfully
reflected in the commentaries and digests of this period. They deal
with the status, rights and duties of the four ‘varnas’ as laid down
in the Smritis. Most of the writers who have attempted to
describe the social conditions and institutions in ancient India,
have relied upon the evidence afforded by the Dharmasiistra
literature. Since the dates of many of the later Smriti writers are
very vaguely known, it would be necessary to check and correlate
their evidence with epigraphical facts and the accounts supplied
by the foreign travellers.
Four Varnas
The traditional theory relating to the professions to be
followed by the four ‘varnas’, was not scrupulously adhered to in
the period under review The Brahmanas, for example, did not
invariably confine their activities to studing, teaching, worshipping
and the performance of other priestly functions. Thus the circle of
the Brahmaiia community following strictly the rules of conduct
as laid down in the DharmaSastra gradually narrowed down.
The sacerdotal class came to regard not only the fine arts but
also some professions, which had been held peculiar even to the
Vai^yas in olden times, as fit.
Casie-System
The most striking feature of the Hindu society has been the
caste system since very early times. To trace the history of this
institution from its beginning is irrelevant here. We shall only
make attempts to ascertain its nature during our period.
180 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
Usually, in theory the number of castes was four, but Alberuni
states that there were sixteen castes, the four well known ones,
five semi-untouchables and seven untouchables.^ Alberuni here
seems to be confused, for he counts the untouchables as a separate
caste. But this was not actually the case. They were really
subcastes of the major Sudra caste, whose number may have been
more than 12. From the contemporary Smritis we know that
at the beginning of the 1 Ith C.A.D. ifumerous sub-castes existed
and their number increased more and more after the 11th C.A D.,
According to Kalhana, who flourished in Kashmir in the 12th
C. A. D. the number was 64P
Alberuni, being a contemporary traveller, throws abundant
light on the cas'e-system of his time. He writes, ’’the Hindus call
their castes, Varna’, i.e. colours, and from genealogical point of
view, they call them Jataka, /. e. births. The castes are from
the very beginning only 4.”^
Let us see the position of the castes, one by one.

Br3hma(ia

The Brahmanas, as usual, stood at the top of the social
hierarchy. Alberuni was struck by the power of the Brahmana
priesthood.^ He states, “the highest caste are the Brahmanas
of whom the books of the Hindus tell that they were created from
the head of Brahma. The Hindus consider them as the very
best of mankind.’’^ It seems that the Brahmanas had regained
their power. They were responsible for re-interpreting regulatory
canons of life as laid down by the earlier texts. The digests of
the 11th C.A.D, contributed to the conservation of the social
pattern.
Contemporary literature and inscriptions show that the
Brahmanas of our period were divided into numerous sub-sections
according to their localities. Alberuni refers to the fact that

2. i?.r, Sarga VIII, V. 2407.
