Social Condition 181

the Hindus give to every single man of (he four castes character-

istic names, according to their occupations and modes of life.^
He further writes, “a Brahmana is, in general called by this
name as long as he does his work staying at home. When he is
busy with the service of one fire, he is called ‘Ishtin’, if he serves
three fires, he is called ‘Agnihotrin’, if he besides, offers an
offering to the fire he is called ‘Dikshita’. And as it is with the
Brahmana, so is it also with the other castes . ”2 This motion
was an ancient one and was known to the earlier Dharmaiastras
and the nibandhas, which classify the Brahmanas according
to their vocations. Dcvala classifies the Brahmanas according to
the standard of knowledge of the Vedas.^ Prof. K.B Vyas and
Sankalia also assert that these Brahmanas derived their caste-
names from territories they inhabited. They further opine that
some of these Bnllimanas called Srlmfila, Meval and Udichyas,
migrated from northern India, especially from Rajputana and
Malwa, sometime between 1000 and 1300 A.D.
Profession
The Brahmapa community of the period under survey adher-
ed to a number of professions, besides those theoretically
permitted to it.'* They maintained their livelihood by following
varied types of professions. Some of these professions were
allowed by <he Dharmasastras.'^ A section, of course, followed
the scriptural duties. Our epigraphical records also make it
clear that some of them were maintaining the sacred fires and
were performing the various smarta-sacrifices, ascribed for
them. Alberuni narrates that these Brahmanas were called Ishtins.
Many others were discharging their scriptural duty of teaching
and conducting schools and colleges, where they used to impart
education without stipulating for any fees.® Jurists, astrologers.

182 Bhoja Paramdra and his times
mathematicians, poets and philosophers wese mostly members
of this class.* Administrative posts seem to have been largely
filled from among the Brahmanas. Government service was largely
manned from among the Brahmanas even during the Mauryan
period, such as is clear from the testimony of Megasthenese.*
The Smriti writers normally lay down that Brahmanas alone
should be appointed as a rule to the ministry and the judicial
posts.®
The ancient law-givers like Gautama,^ Manu® etc. allowed
the Brahmanas to take up arms in times of distress. Sukra* also
refers to this general practice, as in the past, so during the period
under discussion too, the Brahmanas engaged themselves as
soldiers. Inscriptions of the Candclla, Kalachuri and ChSlukya
also refer to Brahmanas as Military officers.'* The RajatarangapI
records the skill of Brahmanas as soldiers in the battlefield.
Trade, agriculture, banking and fighting etc., were permitted
to Brahmanas only in times of difilculties, by earlier writers on

Darmasastca, like Manu (X 86-116), Gautama fVII,8-14), 'Vasi^tha

(II, 31), Narada (Rpadana 61-63) and Yajnavalkya (111,40-42).

As time began to roll on, these became quite normal vocations
for the Brahmanas. Following the liberal views of Devala and
Lakshmidhara, Sukra has also permitted the practice of tilling the
lands by Brahmanas on certain conditions.® This is also support-
ed by AlberunF who writes that during his time, some of the
Brahmanas were trying their fortunes in the trades of clothes
and betel-nuts, while others preferred to trade indirectly by

employing a Vaiiya lo do the actual business for time. These
last-mentioned Dn'dimanas were following the maxims laid down
by Gautama-Dharma-sutra which permits Brahmanas to live upon
agriculture, trade etc., if they appoint agents to carry on business.^
Privileges
Exemption from taxation and the capital punishment arc two
of the main privileges claimed for the Brrihmanas by the Smritis
and the Purapas. But these privileges were meant only for the
Srotriya or the learned Brahmanas 'fhe contcmporar>' digests also
show the continuation of these privileges.!? Albcruni says that the
Brrihmanas were exempted from taxation.^ But whether ordinary
Brahmanas of this period enjoyed similar exemption is not certain.
The Ujjain plate of Paramara Bhojaiaja,* and some other contem-
porary records like the Honawad Inscriptions of Somesvara* and
numerous Chola inscriptions clearly show that even lands granted
to temples by the kings were not entirely exempted from
taxation. '1 his again would suggest that not all the Brahmanas
could have enjoyed complete cx:mplion from taxation Moreover,
there is no cpigraphical evidence to support such a general claim.
We may suggest here that unlike the earlier times, the learned
Brrihmapas were granted the land free from taxation, in lieu of
their public services and the service to the cause of education.*"'
Undoubtedly, it was a reflection of the Dharmasastra literature
which lays down that a ^rotriya or alcarned Brahmana was not to
be taxed.
The Capital punishment was not to be administered to a
Brrihmana even at the cost of committing murder. Albcruni writes
that if a Brahmapa killed a man, the former had only to fast,
pray and give alms,’ This shows that the Bnliimanas probably

were not given the extreme penalty of the law in northern India
in the eleventh century, Alberuni further adds that a Brahmana
was only banished and blinded for the offences that involved
Capital punishment for other castes.^ It may, therefore, be
suggested here that the Capital punishment on the Brahmapas
was probably an alternative and it was rarely inflicted on them.
But we are not sure whether the whole Brahmana community
enjoyed this privilege or it was restricted to only learned
Brahmanas as was the case with taxation.
Besides, Brahmana donees received grants from kings,
rich people and others. The Ujjain plate of Bhoja dated 1021
A.D. records that Bhoja granted a village named Viranaka to a
Brahmana Dhanapati Bhaita of the country of Radha Surasanga
Karnata, who had come into Malwa from Srivada, in the
country or division of Vellu-Vella 2 In his Betma plates,^ Bhoja
informs that this grantee in the inscription is Brahmapa or
Pandita Dalha, son of Bbatta Taljhasika of the Kaushika
gotra. Several other instances are found in the contemporary
inscriptions where Brahmana donees are referred to as receiving
the grants. From all these references we may fairly conclude that
the Brahmanas enjoyed privileges in the field of receiving grants
as well.
Ksliatiriyas
Let us now see the position of the Kshattriyas in the society
of the period. According to the Hindu law-givers, Kshattriyas
come next to the Brahmanas in importance. Alberuni states that
their degree was not much below that of the Brahmanas.* A.S.
Altekar opines that those among them who were actual rulers
enjoyed the highest status in the land.® This fact is further
corroborated by Alberuni who writes that it was probably these,
and not all the ordinary Kshattriyas, who enjoyed immunity from
Capital punishment. Since this privilege is mentioned by Albe-

runi, who was a close sludent of Indian literature, we raay
suggest that it was actually claimed by and yielded to the choice
part among the Kshaltriyas.
With regard to the privileges, it may be suggested that some-
times Kshaltriyas enjoyed more privileges than the BrAhmanas,
In the sphere of punishment, this was more clearly maikcd.
Moreover, Kshaltriyas did not usually become prey to the
Capital punishm nt.*
Contrary to the earlier times, during the period under survey,
not all the fighters were Kshaltriyas and not all the Kshaltriyas
were fighters.
The army also consisted of a number of non-Kshattriyas.
It would thus appear that Kshaltriyas had degenerated from
their ancestral military profession. Dr. Ghurjc writes that
Kshaltriyas had a shadowy existence from about ihc lithC.
onwards.^ It indicates that Kshaltriyas were shifting from their
duties prescribed by the Varnasramadharm.i canons. They were
thus degraded as they adopted the prohibited professions-
They took to trade and agriculture. Dudng the reign of Blioja,
a Kshattriya son named Memmaka, is referred to as cultivating
a field in Gwalior arca.^
Alberuni affirms that Kshaltriyas still were permitted to study
the Vedas. He furihcr writes, ’Mhe Kshattriya offers oblations
to the fire and acts according to the rules of the Purapas”.* If
shows that the Hindus were following the Puranic rather than
Vedic sacraments. But in the matter of religion too, Kshaltriyas
were gradually bring degraded to the rank of Vaisyas as they were
getting more and more dissociated from the orthodox Vedic
atmosphere.
Vaisya’i
In spile of being counted as a separate caste in the digests,

Vaisyas were losing their position among the traivarnikas (the

three high castes) much earlier than the period under survey, the

approximation of the Vaisyas to the Sudras began as early as
Manu' and Baudhayana Dharmasutra.“ H.C. Rawlinson observes
that during the 11th C. A.D., the Vaisya caste was disappearing
and there was a growing tendency to regard the Vaisyas as Sudras
and religious rites were confined to the two higher castes.’
Altekar and Ghurye rightly hold that the Vaisyas were levelled'
down to the position of the Sudras.^ Thus there was no very
great difference between the position of these two castes. Alberuni
did not find any difference betv/een Vaisyas and Sudras.®
According to him if men belonging to either of these castes
recited the Vedas, their tongues were to be cut off under the
rules.® He further adds," between the latter two classes there
is no very great distance. Much, however, as these classes differ
from each other, they live together in the same towns and villages
mixed together in the same houses and lodgings.’ The truth of
Alberuni’s statement invites much discussion, as in the middle of
the 20th C A.D., when laws make untouchability a penal offence
and intercaste marriages a perfectly legal affair, SQdras and
Vaisyas do not mix up together in the same houses and lodgings.
Unfortunately, we do not know whether the difference was deep*
rooted in the society or a slight one. But there is no room for any
doubt that the distinction was definitely there in the minds of the
people of the period under discussion
Sudras
The position of the Sudras in the society has now improved
at least from the days of Medhatithi. Birth alone ceased to be
the criterion. Harlta and Lakshmidhara are of the view that a
pure minded Sudra is better than a bad and .lotorious Brahmana,

can be a teacher in grammar and other sciences, can perform
rites allowed to all classes, and other sacraments without uttering
names of deities."*
The unanimous opinion of the Vcdic and Smriti writers that
the Sudras were not to be permitted to read the Vedas, is
supported by Albcruni’s statement.*’ It tlius seems that the rule
was aetually enforced in practice.
Status in Society
Long before the period under survey, the service of the twice-
born ceased to be the only profession of the Sudras. A number
of Smriti writers like Brhadyama,® Usanas and Dcvala” declare
that trade, crafts and industries were the ordinary and not the
exceptional vocations of this caste. Though they were forbidden
to assume the role of a Kshattriya, yet there arc plenty of
instances to prove that they fought as soldiers.*' The Military
career naturally brought the throne within the reach of
5udra, and we have already seen how two of the kings ruling
at the time of Yuan chwang were Sudras.® The theory that thet
Sudra could not own any properly was a dead letter long before.

UnioucI ability
The rot ion of untouchability was not a strange factor to the
people of the period under review. Its origin is ancient one.
The early Smriti writers know untouchables as Antyajas, but they
differ from each other in enumerating the subdivisions of these
Antjajas. Atri mentions them as seven, while Veda Vyasa counts
twelve including all the cow-flesh eaters.^
With regard to these Antyajas, Alberuni writes, “after the
iSudras follow the people called Antyajas, “who render various
kinds of services, who are not reckoned amongst any caste, but
only members of a certain craft or profession. There are eight
classjs of them, who freely intermarry with each other except the

fuller (washerman), shoe-maker and weaver, fur no others would

condescend to have anything to do with them These eight guilds
are the shoemaker, the juggler, the basketmaker, and shield-
maker, the sailor, the fisherman, the hunter of wild animals and
bit ds, and the weaver. These guilds live near the villages and
towns of the four castes but just outside them.® All these
correspond to Rajuka, Charmakara, Nata or Sailushika, Bumda,
Navika, Kaivaita, Bhillas and Kuviodaka respectively, who have
been regarded as chaijdalas and Antyajas in all early Smriti texts.^
Manu regards them as Madras and according to him they
belonged to the lowest caste.®
The later Smyitis, like Ahgiras,' Brihadyama,’ Atri,® and

Apastamba^ also pronounce most of these classes as untouchables.
Owing to their dirty professions, washermen, shoemakers, fisher-
nicn and chandulas were regarded as untouchables since early
times till quite recently, and jugglers and basket makers, probably
because these professions were the monopoly of the non-Aryan
aborigines in the beginning The Natas were not obviously the
members of this class, because wc learn from the inscription in
the Rajaraje^vara temple,- Tanjorc, that they enacted certain
drama at the time of annua! fair and in return they were paid
salaries. A perusal of Son.skrU litcrnlurc also shows that the
actors who enacted the dramas of Bhasa, Kalidasa, and Bhava-
bhuti were not regarded as untouchables. This indicates that
the natas were not counted as untouchables.
Let us now consider the case of the weaver, the sailor and
the hunter. Manu writes that the membens of the Margava and
Ayogava subcastes, who were suppo.scd to have sprung from
'Pratllonia’ unions, were assigned the professions of the sailor
and the hunter. It would explain why they were regarded as
untouchables.*
The Smriti literature docs not regard weavers as untouchables.
If we accept the account of Albcruni wc shall have to suppose
that the well-developed weaving industry of ancient India was a
monopoly in the hands of untouchables. But nowhere, is
reference to support this case. Brhadyama^ and Apastamba®
simply say that to deal with the red and blue cloths, is extremely
undesirable. A. S. Altckar thus opines that the information of
Alberuni only refers to a section of the class, engaged in product-
ing red and blue cloth which was losing in the estimation of
orthodoxy.® It suggests that the weavers of the period, were
divided into two sections. But wc arc not certain whether the

The Hadi, Domba (Doma), Chandalas and Badhatau

occup'ed the lowest status in the social helrarchy. According to
Alberuni' they were not reckoned amongst any caste or guild.
In spite of this, they were occupied with dirty work like cleansing
of the villages and with such other services Alberuni further
adds that they were considered as one sole-class and were
distinguished only by their occupations. In fact, they were
considered like illegitimate children, for according to the general
opinion they descend from a Sudra father and a Brahmana
mother, as the children of fornication, therefore, they are
degraded outcaste.-
The exact information about the Hadis, coming under the
untouchaTiles is not known. But it is certain that they kept
themselves free fiom everything unclean. Alberuni found
dombas playing on flutes and singing songs. They were regarded
as untouchables. This is proved by one example in the Rajata-
ranganl, where it is stated that King Chakravarman, went outside
the coiirthall, when he had to see a troop of Domba dancers.®
The term Badhatau of Alberuni, according to A. S. Altekar,
is an apabhrarnsa tfVadhaka or the executioner, who followed
the occupation of inflicting corporal punishment on criminals or
practised killing as a trade.‘ The Badhatau were the worst of
untouchables, who devoured flesh of dead 'animals, even of

A summary of the views, lends us to believe that the feeling
of uotouchability was deep-rooted in the society of the period. As
the lime rolled on, this incentive became more and more acute.
In one instance, we find a chandala sweeper refraining from
touching a foundling on the road, lest it should be polluted and
requesting a Sudra lady to pick and rear it up ’ It would simply
indicate that the position of the Anlyajas and the people lower to
them, has always been pitiable and they have been regarded as
untouchables in the community. But from this, we cannot
conclude that the caste system proved a hindrance on the way
of personal achievements.
Kayasthas
The emergence of the Kayastha caste in the fourfold caste
system, is to be noticed in the llih C.A.D. The Kayasthas were
originally officials of a king or a feudatoty chieftain. The earlier
Smritis like the Yajnavalkya- make their mention, as ofiicials and
not as ca4e. Since the time of Gupta period the Kayasthas are
referred to as officials in the cpigraphic records. During the
early Hindu period, they were officers in charge of records,
accounts and revenue departments, who also wrote documents,
helped judges and served as the ‘divans’ or ‘gumastas’. From
the days of Yajnvalkya to the times of Vijfxane^vara, very
frequent references are found to the rapacity of these Kayastha
officials. The RajatarahginI refers to the unscrupulous oppression
by the Kayasthas.
As a caste, Kayasthas appear first in the inscriptions of the
llth C.A D, Prior to this they were s’mply ranked as govern-
ment officials and not as a caste. In the Rewa inscription dated
K. S. 8C0/1048-49 A. D., the origin and pedigree of the Kayasthas
is given. During the period under survey, they, like the Brahma-
nas, came to be known as Ganda, Kayasthavam§a, Mathuranvaya
Kayastha Katariyanvaya, Srivastava and Nigama Kayastha,
1. i?.7’.,p. 77.
2. Kane : H.D.S. II, 175-76,
192 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
according to their original traits. The other subcastes of the
Kayasthas were Saksenas and Valabhyas or Valabhas,* The
latter wrote the charters of the Paramara king Bhoja^ as well as
VIjayapala, mandalika, a feudatory of the Paramara King
Jayasiihha.®
Provincial Barriers of Castes
The inscriptional evidences show that during this period,
Brahmanas of one province were freely going to settle perma-
nently, in another. The grantee of the Betma plates.^ is a
Brahmin named Pandita Delha, son of Bhatfa-Tafthaska of the
Kausika gotra with the three Pravaras Aghamarsharia, Vi^vamitra
and Kausika, and of the Madhyandina Sakha, who hailed from
Sthanisvara ar.d whose ancestors had come from the village
Visaiagrama. Se\eral other references are also found to show
that during the period a large number of Brahmana families
migrated from the following places and settled themselves in
Malwa.®
1.
Anandapura.* 2. Ahicchatra.'' 3. Chili cbakthana. 4. Sihanl-
^vara. 5. Visaiagrama etc.
It would thus appear that the provincial barriers of castes
had not arisen in this period. But the way to their formation
was being paved, for Alberuni notes that in his time it was
regarded as sinful for a Brahmana to cross the Sindhu or the
Chambal and enter the territories beyond them.® The time was
therefore, at hand when provincial barriers were to introduce,
further ramifications in the caste system

Social Condiiion 193
The System of Marriage
Every nation has particular forms of marriage arid especially
those who claim to have a religion and law, of divine origin. Here
references may be made to some historical evidences regarding the
marriage system of the period under survey.
Alberuni, with his wide experience in the north western India
during the first part of the eleventh century A.D. corroborates
many well known clauses of the Smnti law. According to him,
on the occasion of marriage, the Brahmanas performed the rites
of sacrifices and they as well as others received alms. The
implements of the wedding rejoicings were brought forward.^
We may thus conclude, that during the period the marriage was
not a social contract but was a religious performance and it was
totally based on religious grounds.
Svayamvara
The marriages were generally settled by the parents. Alberuni
informs us that among the Hindus, parents arranged the
marriage for their sons.^ The mode of marriage is beautifully
described in a passage occurring in the 6th chapter of Kalidasa’s
RaghuvaraSa. It forms part of the Svayamvara of Indumatl, the
daughter of the king of Vidarbha. According to it the kings of
Magadlia, Ariga, Malwu, Anupa and Surasena were successively
presented to Indumatl for her choice, and were rejected by her.
Aja, the son of Raghu became the chosen husband of Indumatl.
The father of Indumatl having helped Aja to alight from an
elephant, conducted him to the house, and seated him on the
throne, loaded him with diamonds, gave him the ‘argha’ and
‘madhuparka’, a pair of silken garments, having pot on which,
Aja went to Indumati, drawn as is the ocean’s wave to shore by
the influence of the lunar orb. Then the priest of the king having
poured ‘ghi’ and other things in Agni which he made a witness,
united the pair in wedlock. The bride of partridge-eyes cast grains
into the flames, from which a wreath of smoke arose encircling
her ears as with a garland pair. The royal pair mounted on a
1. Sachau, II, p. 154.
2. Ibid.
194 Bhoja ParamSra and his Times
golden seat were sprinkled with moistened grains by heads of
families and aged matrons. The rejected kings, hiding their wrath
under the guise of joy^resembled a tranquil lake beneath whose
surface alligators lurk. Bhojaraja accompanied Aja for three days
and then returned.

The elaborate ceremony of self-choice {Svayatnvara) of

Kshatttiya princesses referred to in the epics and Kavya literature,
is illustrated by the historical marriage of the western Chalukya

king Vikramadilya VI, and Vidyadhara with (Silahara) princess

Chandaladevi.' This fact is also supported by Dvyasraya-Kavya
which narrates that Mahendraraja, the king of Naduladesa

(Nadol) arranged a Svayatnvara’ assembly for the selection of a

bridegroom for his sister, DurlabhadevI, as has already been
discussed in connection with Bhoja’s war with the Chalukyas
of Gujarat.^
All the above descriptions point out to the systems of
marriage prevalent in the kingly families. Post-puberty marriages
were in vogue in Kshattriya circles down to the middle ages, and
hence ‘svayaihvara’ custom survived there till the 12th C, A. D.
Hence, it is referred to in Sanskrit poetry and drama, which
mostly deal with the life in royal and aristocratic families. When
society, writes A.S. Altekar, had conceded to grown-up brides,
the right to choose their own husbands, it was but natural that
love marriages, arranged by the parties themselves, should have
taken place with fair frequency,’
Age of Marriage
The marriages were generally settled at a very early age.
Alberuni throws a flood of light on the subject. According to
him the Hindu parents used to arrange marriages of their
sons, because they used to take place at a very early age.'* He
further informs that no Brahmana was allowed to marry a girl
1. Vikramnnkaderacharita,BeTga IX.
2. Dvyairaya, Sarga VII, pp. 547 If.
3. The Position oflVomen in Hindu civilization, p. 66,
4. Sachau II, p. 154.
Social Condition 195
above 12 years of age.i This system of early marriages was also
common in Deccan. The Nltivakyfimrta states that in Deccan
boys were usually 16, and girls not above 12, at their marriage.
^

Since the time of Dharma^astras, (300 BC—C 100 A.D.), pre-

puberty marriages were preferred to post-puberty ones. The
contemporary Smritis also speak of a number of curses upon the
guardians who failed to marry their female wards before they
obtained puberty.® We may, therefore, safely conclude that
during this period, pre-puberty marriages of girls were the order
of the day at least among the BrShmanas. Post-puberty marriages
were also known to the people but they were very few and were
exceptions among the ruling families. The marriage of Samyogita
with Chahamana Prithviraja, for example, took place when she
was quite grown-up, but such cases were only exceptions and not
the rule.
Child-marriages were not much prevalent among the
Kshattriyas for a long time. Heroines of most of the Sanskrit
dramas written during the period C. 300-1200 A.D., are grown-up
brides at the time of their marriage. This is so because they
mostly belong to Kshattriya circles. There are many historic
examples of grown-up marriages among the Rajputs during the
mediaeval period.
The analogy of ‘upanayana* was also utilised for lowering
the marriage age to this ridiculous extent. Parents anxious to
ensure a rapid progress of their sons in education were advised to
perform their ‘upanayana’ at the age of 5. Marriage was the
substitute for ‘upanayana’ in the case of girls, and so it could be
performed at the same early age.
It may, however, be pointed out that the extreme views
referred to above could not remain popular for a long time. We
learn from Alberuni* that during the 11th century A.D., the
1. Sachau, p. 131.
2. XI 28 XXX, 1.
3. E.I. Brhadyama HI. 22 Samvarta, /, 67, Yama /, 22, ^ankha
XV, 8, LaghuSstntapa 65,
4. Sachau 11 p. 131.
196 Bhoja Paramara and fils times
normal age of a Brahraana bride was 12. Parents in the lower
sections of society, where the pernicious custom of bride-
price prevailed to a great extent took advantage of the permission
to marry girls at the age of 5 or 6 for their own selfish ends.
Their example was later on followed by other classes. The custom
of very young marriages thus began to be more and more
common. The ramification of the caste system into hundreds of
sub-castes and the prohibition of inter-marriages among them
from about the 8th or the 9th C.A.D. further accentuated the
evil of the child-marriage. The selection of a suitable bride-
groom was becoming progressively more difScult, as the field of
choice was being further narrowed down by the rise of new-water-
tight sub-castes Parents did not like to take the risk of losing
a good bridegroom at hand by postponing the marriage to a
later date. Paradoxical though it may appear, it seems that the
‘Sati’-custom which became fairly popular at this time, helped
the cause of early marriages to some extent. If the father died
and mother followed him on his funeral pyre, there would be a
father-in-law at least to look after the young orphans, if they
were already married. The joint-family system prevailing in
society was also favourable for early marriages. Marriages could
be perforrned long before the husband became an earning member
because his parents, uncles or elder "brothers used to take care of
his wife and children till the time he began to earn. The
economic condition of the country was fairly satisfactory and it
did not, therefore, necessitate the postponement of marriage to
the time the husband became an earning member. Naturally,
therefore, society became more and more conscious of the advan-
tages of early marriages and oblivious of their drawbacks. It
further realised that child-marriages gave a good opportunity to
the bride to know gradually the natures and idiosyncrasies of the
different members of the joint-family of her husband, note almost
imperceptibly their likes and dislikes and entertain an affection
for him before the sex-instinct had been aroused. There was no
further chance of any scandal as was admittedly the case lU the
earlier era of post-puberty marriages. Of course the society
knew that under the system of child marriage there was the
danger of widowhood over-coming a bride before she came of
Social Condition i97
age. But such calamity was believed to be a decree of fate,
which could not be set at nought by any human effort. We
thus come to conclusion that the popularity of early marriages
went on increasing in mediaeval times.
Dowry-System
The dowry system did not stand as an impediment in
daughter’s marriage in ancient India. It was rarely practised

during this period. Albcruni writes (hat no gift was settled

between the parties. The man gave only a present to the wife as
he thought fit and a marriage gift in advance, which he had no
right to claim back, but the wife might give it back to him of her
own will.^ In rich and royal families some gifts in kind, cash or
gold were used to be given to sons-in-law at the time of marriage.
No Divorce. As marriage was not a social contract and was
only a religious performance, there prevailed no divorce during
this period. Alberunf writes that among the Hindus, the husband
and wife could only be separated by death as they had no
divorce.* There is no doubt that the later Dharraasastra literature

as a whole (C. 200-1200 A.D.) while liberally permitting the

husband to remarry during the life-time of the first wife, refused
the remedy of divorce to the wife even when completely forsaken
by the husband.
Some Restricted Laws of Hindus
Alberuni throws light also on certain restricted laws of
Hindus regarding the matrimonial relations. He says that among
Hindus, marriages were for bidden within certain degrees of

relationship. According to their (Hindus) marriage laws it was

better to marry a stranger than a relative. The more distant is
the relationship of a woman with regard to her husband the
better. It was absolutely forbidden to marry a related woman
both of the direct descending line viz. a grand daughter or great
grand daughter and of the direct ascending lines viz. a mother,
grand-mother or great grand-mother. It was also forbidden to
marry collateral relations, viz. a sister, a niece, a maternal or
1. Sachau 11, p. 155.
2. Ibid., p. 154.
198 ihoja Paramara and his Time's
paternal aunt and their daughters except unless the couple
relations, who wanted to marry each other, be removed from
each other by five consecutive generations. In that case, prohibition
could be waived, but not-with-standing, such a marriage was an
object of dislike to the society.
Adultery—An Offence.
Throughout the ages, adultery in India has been regarded
as a most henious sin by the Hindu society. Women in Hindu
Society never enjoyed the right of unchecked co-habitation. If
they tried, they were given proper punishment. Severe punishments
were administered by the society for adultery. Alberuni’s account
seems to indicate that the punishment for adultery was more
severe than that recommended in the Smriti law, for he says that
the adultress was driven out of her husband’s house and banished.^
No Inter-Caste Marriage
Let us now proceed to consider the attitude of the age under
survey, towards the question of intercaste marriage. These
marriages, if ‘anuloma’, have been permitted as legal by the most
of the Smriti writers, both old and new. The Nitivakyamrta,
which was composed in Deccan, about this period, concurs with
the Smritis.^
These inter-caste marriages could not have been numerous,
for Indian society usually preferred marriage with those equal
in rank. According to A.S, Altekar, they however did not exist
and could not serve the useful function of rendering the caste
system flexible to a great degree A Historical examples of a little
earlier period, show that these inter-caste marriages were actually
taking place. For instance, Rajasekhara seems to have married
a Kshatriya lady who was probably his only wife.
It was from about the 10th C. A.D. that inter-caste marriages

began to be unpopular. Alberuni observes (C. 1020 A.D.) that

1. Sachau II, p. 1&9.
2. XXXI, 28.
3. The Pa?trakulas and their Times, p. 336.
Social Co7idiiioil 199
the Brahmanas of his day no longcf availed themselves of the
permission to take wives from the three lower castes,^ Kalhana,
the 12th C.A.D. Briihmana historian of Kashmir, had to record

the marriage of a sister of king Sahgramaraja (1003-1028 A.D.)

with a Brahmana youth. The marriage evidently shocked him and
he did not fail to express his regret that the king should not have
realised how he was wringing his family into disgrace by such
an inter-caste union."
Inter-caste marriages were permitted down to the lOih C.A.D.
because the cultural difTcrence among the members of the different
twice-born castes were not many or far-reaching. They all
performed upanayana, and observed the various sacraments laid
down for them. In the Kathasaritsagara we oftern find the
daughter being asked by her father as to which of the suitors
belonging to the four castes met her approval. ^ When Brahmana
ASokadatta was married to a princess, the author of the above

book records his hearty approval of the union ; the marriage

between the princes and the Brahmapa youth appeared to mutual
advantage like the union of learning with modesty.'*
Alberuni, after laying down the theoretical rule that
‘anuloma’ inter-caste marriages were permitsiblc, adds that in his
time the Brahmanas did not avail themselves of this liberty and
were invariably marrying women of their caste only. It is, there’
fore, fair to conclude that the inter-caste ‘anuloma’ marriages
ceased to take place soon after this period. Kalhana, thus, was
the spokesman of the 12th C view and that soon thereafter the
inter-caste marriage became obsolete.
Only ‘anuloma’ marriages were prevalent during the period
under survey. Alberuni states that every man of a caste could
1. Sachau, II, pp. 155-56.
stFctit ii vm, iq.
200 Blioja Paramara and his Times
marry a woman of his own caste or are of the castes below his,
but nobody was allowed to marry a woman of a caste superior
to his own.^ Thus it seems clear that ‘pratiloma’ marriages were
getting outdated along with the inter-caste marriages.
Polygamy and Polyandry
The polygamous habit of Indian kings is proved by contem-
porary inscriptions and literature. Alberuni states that during
his period a man could marry from one to four wives. He was
not allowed to take more than four but if one of his wives died
he could take another to fill the legitimate gap." He further
adds, some Hindus think that the number of wives depends
upon the caste, that accordingly, a Brahmana may take four,
a Kshatlriya three, a Vai^ya two wives and a Sudra one.’ On
the contrary, there existed no system of polyandry as it was
strictly prohibited for a woman to marry more than one husband.
This is also pioved by the reference, as has already been discussed
that there existed no divorce.’
Family System
During the period under survey, the joint family system was
the order of the day. But causes of separation were not so rare
as is sometimes supposed. Some of our records which mention
the specific share of the co-shares of ‘agrahara’ villages, prove
that in many cases father and sons were living separately. In
the Torkhede inscription of Govinda III, separate shares are
assigned to two brothers showing that they were no longer
members of a joint family,’ Some of the above records do not
strictly belong to this period, but they can be utilised to indicate
the general state of affairs of this period as well.
The families of the period were mostly of maternal type. No
reference of paternal families, is found in any of the contem-
1. Sachau, 11, pp. 155-56.
2. Ibid.
3. Sachau II, p. 155.
4. Ibid.
5. I.A.VII, p. 303.
Social Cojtdiiion ’ 20t
porary records.. .Albcruni also speaks in favour of vagueness
of the maternal families as he states, “the child belongs to the
caste of the mother and not to that of father. Thus, for example
if a wife of a Brahmaija is a Brahmana, her child is also a
Brahmana, if she is a ^udra her child is a i^Odra.^ This would
indicate that the descent of the child born was traced out from
the side of the mother and not from that of the father.
General Poaition of Women
As regards the position of women in society, the Smrili
commentaries and digests of the period give us the impression
that women had no independent legal status. The theory of
perpetual tutelage alfcctcd their personal status. They were to
remain chaste not only in their conduct but also in their thought.
The wife must be guarded against the physical and mental unchas-
tity for the sake of her offspring. According to .^ukra, women
were to assist in the function of the males and also in agriculture
and store-keeping.' It was because of their dependent position
that the law-givers and digest-writers ordained that the superse-
ded and adulterous wives were to be maintained by their
husbands.2 Both Lakshmidhara and Vijriuncsvara^ recommend
that a superseded wife should receive from her husband a sura
equal to the expenses of her marriage or as such the new wife
received as a wedding gift. A woman could not dispose of her
husband’s property.* She had never to attend the court for
evidence except in matters relating to women.
, As in the ancient period® women continued to be respected,
Sukra states that they should be addressed as sisters and
Subbagu’." . The highest respect was shown for the mother.®

2. 5ukram(isnra (Ed. Cal). IV, 4, 26-27.

202 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
Rationalism has been at a discount with Hindu thinkers
during the last more than one thousand years. This circumstance
has been responsible for a very unfortunate change in society’s
angle of vision, vitally affecting the well-being of women. Down
to about 800 A D. the Smritis were emphatic in declaring that
women who were forcibly taken into captivity, or dishonoured,
should be admitted back to their families. Psudo-puritanical
no.ions distorted social vision in this matter soon after C- 1000
AD. Hindu society began to show a -surprising callousness to
women, who had the misi'ortnne of being carried away into
captivity for a very short time. An outsider like Alberuni could
not understand the stupidity of the Hindu community in refusing
men and women who had been captured and converted by force.
If one’s hand gets soiled, he argues, one should proceed to cleanse
and not to cut it. But Hindu society could not understand this
very reasonable proposition. From C. 1200 A.D. it refused to
follow the lead of Smyiti writers and declared that once a woman
was converted or taken into captivity, nothing would justify her
teadsttws'on to old family or TeUglon. This callous and
unreasonable attitude, in the words of Altekar has cost Hindu
society very dearly.* Had the women who had been forcibly
conveitcd or captured, been readmitted into Hindu society, its
population would certainly not have dwindled down to 73% of the
population of prepartiiioned India.
Law of Inheritance
With regard to the law of inheritance Alberuni® writes that

the chief rule of their (Hindus) law of inheritance was that the

women did not inherit except the daughter. She got the fourth
part of the share of a son, according to the passage in the book
‘Manu-Sturiti’. Alberuni seems to have been wrongly informed in
the matter of succession to property. But his statement is parti-
ally true with regard to the unmarried daughter, who according
to Manu, is entitled to get l/4th of the share of the deceased
father’s property. Alberuni® further elaborates that if the daughter
1. The Position of Women in Hindu civilisation, p. 359.
2. Sachau II, p. 164,
3. Ibid.
Social Condidoil 203
was not married, the money was spent on her till ihc time of her
marriage and her dowry was met with from her share in properly.
Afterwards she had no more claim on licr parental property. Both
JimOtavahana and Vijnanesvara preferred an unmarried daughter
as heir to a married one. Albcruni could not possibly know the

view of Gautama (28.22) and Yajnavalkya (If. 35), quoted in the

MUak?ara, prescribing that among the married daughters the
indigent one was to be preferred to one whowas well-placed.
On the other hand, our law- makers indicate a strong tendency
to advocate woman’s rights to property, Epigraphic records and
contemporary literature reveal that women .sometimes hid
important position in the state. Some inscriptions refer to the
estates being held independently by the Chahamana and
G.nhadvula queens. ^ Kalhuna refers to one Damara lady from
Nilusva who remained faithful to king Jayasimha at the lime of
Bhoja’s revolt. Thus some women owned, by their right impor-
tant estates even during the lift-time of their husbands.^
Their Political Status
Several instances arc at hand to show how queens and prince-
sses, sometimes exercised a commanding influence upon the admi-
nistration of slate. Besides, the wives of feudatories and even
some ordinory women arc known to have taken part in political
affairs. The enormous political influence of women in Kashmir,
northern India and Guj.arat arc well-recorded. For c.N:amplc, the
Sahi King Ananta’s queen Sur^nmaiT persuaded the king to abdi-
cate the throne in 1063 A.D. in favour of their son Kalasa who
was a licentious person. Moreover, sometimes even women porti-
cipated in military expeditions as has already been shown that
Kusumavali, the queen of deceased Muuja persuaded Bhoja in his
attack on Tailpa U, In Deccan too, royal ladies took part in war

204 Bhoja ParamSra and his Times
as is proved by the inscriptions of the 11th C.A.D. But no men-
tion of women governors, is found in the contemporary records.
Altekar also agrees with this fact.^
Education
The early marriage proved a bar in the way of higher educa-
tion of women. Even then, several women distinguished themselves
in higher learning and some of them rose to the.high ranic of
poetesses. Ballala Pandit^ and Merutunga^ state that poetess SIta
vas attached to the court of Bhoja. She studied, pot onlyihe
writings of Chanakya on morals and the principles of government,
but also the Raghuvamsa and the Kamasutra of Valsyayana along
with the three Vedas. Learning was usually acquired by the women
of high ranks Some of the sculptures ^df 'Bhuvanesvara and

Khajuraho depict women as writing love letters; We may suggest

that women of this age were well-educated and took an active
part in^ public life. Girls could read and write and understand
Sanskrit, they learned to play dance and paint portraits.* But it
seems that the provision for higher education meant only for the
girls belonging to the higher strata No reference regarding the
higher education being imparted to girls of the general class of this
period has been available so far.
During the first millenium of the Christian era there flourished
in Hindu society a few famous lady scholars and poetesseL Arnong
the authors from whose works, selections have been made by Hala

in IS anthology of Maharashtri poems (Gathasapta^ati) there are-

seven poetesses, their names being Reva, Roha, l^adhavl,
Anulakshml, Pahai, Vaddha- Vatu and Sasiprabha-®. Some Sanskrit
anthologies also have preserved the memory of a few distinguished '
poetesses, who apear to have composed poetry of a really high
order. Silabhattarika was famous for her easy and graceful style,'-

suited for a harmonious synthesis of sense and sound. DevT was
a well-known poetess of Gujarat, who continued to enchant her
readers on earth even when she had herself pone to heaven.
Vijjika was next to Kfilidasa in fame She sccm.s to have
attained a really high position among Sanskrit writers. Rajasekhara
a distinguished critic and poet compares her with the poddcsis
Sarasvatl herself. Nature was not very kind to this gifted lady
because it had chosen to give her a blue black complexion.
Rfijaickhara boldly declares that even masters of Sanskrit vcr.scs
were clearly in the wrong when they declared that Sarasvatr, the
goddess of learning was all fair in complexion.. Altckar writes
that it proves beyond all doubt that Vijitka was a celebrated
poetess. Rujasekhara's wife, a Kshattriya by caste, was a good
literary critic and poetess. Mamata, Morika and Subhndra arc
other poetesses referred to in Sanskrit anthologies.*
Unfortunately, we do not know anything about the status and
family circumstances of the above lady-poets, philosophers and
doctors. Most probably .some of them belonged to rich and
cultured families, which could make special arrangements for girls’
education even after their marriage, and some to Kshattriya circles,
where the custom of child marriage did not take root for a long
time even after it had become well-established in ihe rist of the
society. In the 9th C.A.D. higher education of women was con-
fined to royal oflicial, ricli and well-to-do families and to the class
of dancing girls.- It may be pointed out that most of the heroines
of Sanskrit dramas written in this period, who arc represented as
fairly educated, belonged to the class mentioned above.
Cultured and rich families were naturally few in society.
They had sufficient resources to enable them to employ special

teachers like Brhannala, Ganadasa and Haradatta for their daugh-
ter’s education. Ordinary families, however , could not afford this
and their daughters, who had to be married at the early age of 10
or 1 1, could hardly receive any education. Tt is hazardous to make
any statement about the exact extent of literacy among women at
about C.IOOOA D., for we have no data like modern census reports
to guide us. Literacy among men at this time was about 40 percent.
Tn the case of women it was probably not more than 10 percent.*
The cultivation of fine arts like music, dancing and painting
was encouraged among girls since very early times. Musical reci-
tation of same hymns was originally the special function of
Iadies.2 Heroines of Sanskrit dramas and poems like Priyadarsika,
Sakuntala and Kadambarl ivrilten during the first millenium of the
Christian era, are well-versed in most of these arts.’
Girls in ruling families used to receive some military and

administrative (raining also. In the Chalukya administration (C-

980-llfO A.D ) there were many queen Governors and officers.

These administrative duties required a good training on proper
lines. Inordinary Kashattriya families, ladies used to receive a
fairly good amount of military training. Women guards of kings,

referred to in dramas* belonged to this class; they were usually

expert in the use of bow and sword.
South Indian inscriptions of the mediaeval period disclose the
existence of many Kshattriya heroines defending their hearths and
and home in times of danger. Women from Karnatak seem to
have led the way in this matter. A heroine from Mysore is

It is quite a well-known fact that Rajput princesses were
adept in the use of swerd and spear. They could lead the
armies and direct government in hour of need. Rajput history
is full of such instances and they need not be enumerated
here.
Pardd. System
Slightly earlier than this period, we find some royal ladies
observing ‘parda’. An instance is found concerning the ladies of

king Mahabhava-Gupta 1 (C-950-975 A.D.) observing ‘farda’ in

Orissa.“ But this system, it appears, was exceptional. In north
and south India the ‘parda’ was usually unknown. We have
already seen how the Kashmir ladies of the royal family appeared
before the public without veil. It may, therefore, be well-presum-
ed that the common women-folk did not observe ‘pardfi’ at all.
They had normally to earn their living by working in the open
fields. Married women as referred to in the Kautilya’s Artha^astra
or in Medhatithi’s time, were busy with spinning and weaving and
often engaged themselves in mechanical arts in order to supple-
ment the income of their husbands.® Abu-^aid writes in this
connection that most princes in India allovVcd their wives to be
seen when they held their court. No veil concealed them from the
eyes of the visitors.^
Down the 10th C.A D. ‘parda’ was confined to a
very small section of the ruling classes. Abuzaid, an Arbian
traveller of the early lOth century, has noted that in most of the
courts in India queens appeared in public without any veils.® Even
in the Kathasaritasagara, written towards the end of the llth
C.A.D., there are hardly any traces of ‘parda’. In the story

of Arthalobha (III, 286) we find a lady participating in mercantile

Position of Widow in Society
The position ot widow in society
is one of the

;r—wSrme^ves often is

an indes to the attitude
of society towards woman as a
class.
The widow remarriages which were
known even to the 'Vedie
society began to be restricted from
about C. 1000 A.D. From
about 1100 A.D. they disappeared almost
completely from the
The Position of Women in Hindu Civilisation, p.
358.
210 Bhoja Paramira imd hh Times
society. This prohibition became operative only in the higher
sections of the Hindu society. In the lower strata re-marriages
continued to be current among them.
From C. 1000 A.D. the condition of the widow further
deteriorated. The custom of tonsure came into vogue. The
motives for its adoption may have been good but its consequences
were often disastrous. The custom of 'satT now began to appeal
more and more to all classes of society. As a consequence,
sometimes even unwilling widows were driven to follow it owing
to the force of public opinion. Sometimes, even child widows
were burnt, lest they should go astray and bring disgrace to their
families. Society had become quite callous.*
We thus come to know that during this period, the position
of widow was at a low ebb Alberuni describes in detail the
hard lot of the widow. According to him the widows preferred
to burn themselves because they were ill-treated as long as they
lived.= This is sufficient to prove that their position in society
was humiliating. The custom of tonsuring widows is however,
not referred to by any of the contemporary Muslim writers.
Nowhere in this period is found any reference of the widow

remarriage ; neither in the inscriptions nor in the contemporary

literature and nor in the accounts of the foreign travellers It seems
that the re-marriages were strictly foreign to them Alberuni
supports this view. He writes that if a wife lost her husband by
death she could not marry another man ’ We should not forget
that the question of widow-marriage was never a problem with
the lower classes, among whom it was and is even now fairly
common

77;e Custom of Sati

From about 700 A.D. fiery advocates began to come forward
to extol the custom of ‘Sati’ in increasing number. Angiras

argues that the only course which religion had prescribed for
a widow was that of ‘Sati’.^ Harita maintained, “the wife can
purify her husband from the deadliest of sins, if she burns herself
with his remains. The two will then happily reside in heaven for
three and a half crores of years.^ A passage interpolated in
Para^arasmriti observes that just as a snake-charmer drags out
a snake from a hole by force, in the same maimer the *Sati takes
out her husband from hell and enjoys Heaven with him for
three and a half crores of years.^ Even if the wife had led a

dissolute life, it would not matter ; her immolation even if not

voluntary, will ensure a permanent seat in heaven both to her
and her husband.*
The views advocated by these writers gradually began to
produce some effect on society. During the period 700-1100 A.D.
‘Satis’ became more frequent in northern India and quite common
in Kashmir. The history of Kashmir during this period teems
with the cases of Satis in royal families. Kalhana, the historian of
the province, was surprised to find that even notoriously unchaste
queens like Jayamati, the wife of king Uchchala, should be seen

the society, the court panegyrists would not have failed to
mention the eases of ‘Sail’ wlicn they were eulogising the kings
and qjicens of their royal ntrnilics. 'I hc Chedi king Gaiigeyadcva
is stated to have obtained salvation at Prayaga at the root of the
holy ‘vafp tree along widi his lOO wives in C 1020 A D. The
languigt of thi record, however, suggests that the old king and

his 10) queens simultaneously drowned themselves at the

confluence of Gahga and Yamuna,* According to Altckar,
it was not the ease of ‘Sati* custom. It, may, therefore, be
doubted whether the 'SatP custom had become common even in
the Rajputa royal families of northern India during the period
under survey, Kashatir was of course an exception,"
The ‘Satr custom was not so common in other parts of
India, as it was in the north. In the Deccan, widows were not
usually burnt ns is proved by the statement of Sulaiman, He
writes that sometimes when the corpse of a king was burnt,
his wives cast themselves upon tlic pyre and burnt themselves, but
it was for them to choose whether they would do so or not,®
The historical records frequently refer to widowed queens,
princesses and others performing acts of charity and piety. But
at the same time as we have already discussed we find instances of
not only the wives but also of other relatives burning themselves
after the death of their masters.
The statement of Albcruni shows that the wives of kings were
burnt whether they wished or not ^ A S. .Altckar disagrees with
this and says that as Albcruni’s account is based on the events in
the contemporary Kashmir, it cannot hold good for the Deccan.®
In the northern and central India also the custom during the
period seems to have been very much limited.
U.N. Ghoshal thinks that the ‘Sail’ rite was more widely
prevalent at this time than was warranted by the Smriti authori-

Prostitutes
With regard to harlotry, Alberuni informs us that the people

of his time thought no indecency in it. He further adds (hat in

reality the matter was not as people thought but it was rather in
the tendency that the Hindus were not very severe towards
punishing whoredom. The fault according to Alberuni lay with
the kings, not with the people.® This indicates that during this
period the prostitution was a commonly known feature of the
social organisation. But, however, after the 10th C. downwards
the system went out of fashion. Even then they were not
unknown. Merutunga refers to a Hetaera poetess of Gujarat, who
pleased the Paramara king Bhoja.* He also tells us the story of
Chauladev! or ChakuladevI of patjana, a courtesan, who remained
faithful to her lover during his long absence.® We also know
that the great grand-father of Kumarapala was bom of this
Chauladevi or ChakuladevI. It may, therefore, be suggested that
prostitution during the period was not so common as it became
in the later centuries.
In Kashmir it was more common as we learn from the
Rajataranginl of Bilhana.
Food And Drink
1and digests contain detailed accountselaborating the old Smriti rules related to prohibited food.
The obsereation of the contemporary foreign writers about the
food and drink differ in some respects from the canonical rules.
eruni gives us a list of forbidden food. According to him
five vegetables were forbidden to the Hindus by the religious
code. These were onions, garlic, a kind of gourd, the root of

n plant like the carrots called Krncn(?) and another vegetable

Ail over the northern India popular articles of diet were
rice, fruits, vegetables and milk. Wheat was largely produced
there, Jn Orissa rice cooked with curd and ghee was oOcred to
gods.* From milk various preparations were made. In Kashmir,

U.P. and Gujarat the pulses named ‘kulaitha’ t,?), ‘camV, Masur,

Mudga, ‘muj^ga’ etc. were produced.^
1-lesh‘Cating was not a common item of food among all

the ci.i.ssc.*;. Alberuni presents a list of lawful and forbidden meat

which is evidently derived from the Smfiii.s. He .states that
originally killing was forbidden to them. People, however, had

the desire for meat and would always (ling aside every order to

the contrary. Therefore, the rules mentioned herein apply in
p.irticular only to the Brahmanas, bceau.sc it forbids them to
give way to their lu>lb.^
Besides, Alberuni gives a list of lawful and unlawful animals
to be eaten. According to him, sheeps, goats, gazcllas

hares, rhinoceroses (ganda), buBalocs, fish, w'alcr and land birds

as sparrows, ring-doves, frnneolins, doves, peacocks and other
animals, w'crc not loathsome to man nor noxious.®
A summary, thus, shows that the customary items of food
were rice, wheat, pulses, vegetables and sweets with milk
preparations, such as curd, whey and coagulated milk. Meat
was also eaten, but owing to the growing influence of Vai§na-
vism and Jainism, bulk of the people particularly in Gujarat
gradually became vegetarian.'"’
Inter-Caste Dining
The ancient law’-givcrs like Gautama,’ Apastaraba® and

Baudhayana* allow freely iater-caste dinners. But the custom
had begun to decline in the period under survey, for a number
of later Smritis either restrict or condemn it. Angiras® prohibits
the dinner with a Siidra and permits one with a Kshattriya only
on days of religious festivity and with a Vaishya when in distress.'
Yama and Vyasa* declare that a Brahmana should beg cooked
food only in the houses of the members of his own caste.
Alberuni writes that a Brahmana was permanently expelled from
his caste, if he was found guilty of having eaten food of a
Sudra for a certain number of days.® He does not attest to the
cessation of inter dining among the three higher castes. But the
cessation of inter-caste marriages, and the difference in diet were
further making inter-dining impracticable.
With regard to Indian eating, Alberuni states certain preva-
lent customs and manners. He writes, “In a country the whole
spot in the house is prepared for people to eat upon it. It is
plastered with clay, where they, on the contrary, prepare a sepa-
rate table cloth for each person eating by pouring water over a
spot and plastering it with the dung of cows. The shape of the
BrShmana’s table cloth must be square. Those who have the
custom of preparing such table cloths give the following as the
cause of the custom.”
“The spot of eating is soiled. If the eating is finished the
spot is washed and plastered to become clean again. If, now
the soiled spot is not distinguished by a separate mark, you would
suppose also the other spots to be soiled since they are similar
and cannot be distinguished from each other.”®
At one place, Alberuni informs that the Hindus ate singly
one by one, on a table cloth of dung. They did not make use of
the remainder of a meal, and the Plates from which they had
eaten were thrown away if they were earthen.'

On the basis of the above description, we may conclude that
unlike today, the Hindus of the period under survey observed
purity in their eating. But it seems that Albcruni only marked
the customs of higher classes It is uncertain whether these
customs were prevalent also among the masses.
.As regards the drinking of wine, Alb’ruai states, "they

(Hindus) drink wine before having eaten anything, then they take

their meal.' But from some earlier Muslim travellers like Al-
Masudi and .M-Idrisi, we learn that the Brfihm.tnns were the total
abstainers from this practice. Similar may have been the case
in this period too, Bui it goes without saving that ail the other
castes were permitted to drink wine. Tliis is also supported by
Albcruni ^vho writes that a ’^udra’ was allowed to drink but
dared not sell it = We do not know whether there existed any
state shops for the sale of these intoxicated goods or they were
opened to private business. But this is almo.st certain that
drinking was widely prevalent. Had it not been the ease the
mention of wine vcssals would have not been made in the
contemporary records. The Yuktikalpalaru’ mentions vessels for
wine-drinking made for gold, silver, stones and glasses. This
clearly proves that drinking then was more common than is
generally supposed, and there must have been many taverns in
the cities. Men as well as women arc described to be taking part
in drinking bouts. Kremendra, an eleventh century writer, comp-
ares a lady with wine in her mouth to Rfihu devouring ilic moon.*
Dresses and Toilet
A detailed study of the dress and ornaments worn by Hindu
men and women of diflcrcnt classes age by age would be out of
place in this work. We propose to treat the subject here only
with the prescribed period in its broad outlines.

Dresses
The Hindu dress of the period under survey does not seem
to have required much of tailoring. Alberuni writes, ’’they

(Indians) use turbans for trousers. Those who want little dress

are content to dress in a rag of two fingers’ breadth, which they
bind over their loins with two cords, but those who like much
dress, wear trousers lined with so much cotton as would suffice
to make a number of panes and saddle-rungs. These trousers
have no visible openings, and they are so huge that the feet are
not visible. The string by which the trousers are fastened is at the

back.” ‘‘Their ‘sidar’ (a piece of dress covering the head and

the upper part of breast and neck) is similar to the trousers, being

also fastened at the back by buttons” ‘‘The lappots

of the ‘kurtakas’ (short shirts from the shoulders to the middle

of the body with sleeves, a female dress) have slashes both on

the right and left sides.
The above statement of Alberuni is supported by several
literary and sculptural evidences. The dress of women in the
Indus Valley Civilisation, as disclosed by its terracotas consisted
of a ‘sari’ fastened to a girdle and a fan-like bead dress. In the
Vedic literature we find frequent references of the under garment
Vasas Zntaram and the upper garment paridhBnam which were in
general use among the members of both the sexes. A waist band
was used round the lower garment.* In addition, on ceremonious

days, kings used to wear a gorgeous mantle {drapi). In one place

goddess Indrani is described as wearing a head-dress {ushnlsha) ^

A.S. Altekar conjectures that ‘ushnlsha, was something like
a ‘pagree’ and not merely a piece of cloth for covering the head

like modern {odhm)’* Goddess Lakshml, who appears seated on

numerous Gupta coins, is seen invariably wearing the upper
garment or ’shawl’ which completely covers her bosom, bust and

‘saris’. Fashionable ladies, however, did not like to conceal
from view their elaborate and artistic coiffure.
Of the various styles of wearing the ‘sari’ the two most
prevalent were ‘sakachehha' 'i.e., by passing a part of it in
between their legs and tucking it up behind at the waist and

the ‘vikachchha; i.e., round the legs without any portion of-it

being tucked up behind. The ‘vikachchha’ style continued to
be in vogue in U.P., Bihar, Bengal and Orissa throughout
ancient and medieval period such as is clear from the numerous
statues of Tara, Maya, Mahishasuramardini and female attendants
on Vishnu, that have been discovered in these provinces and are
now kept preserved in the Indian Museum, Calcutta,.
Unexpectedly enough, we find in Maharashtra, the Vikc-
hchha fashion of the Gangetic plain down to the 7th or 8 h
C.A.D., as is clear from the Ajanta frescoes.^
It is after about C.9C0 A.D. that we begin to get images
of goddesses in the Deccan wearing the ‘sarT in ‘sakachchha’
fashion. We may, therefore, presume that this fashion became
popular in the Deccan at about the end of the first millennium
of the Christian era The Deccan ladies seem to have borrowed
it from their sisters in Malwa and Central India.
The poet Rajasekhara supplies some further details about
the way in which the ladies of different provinces used to dress
themselves in the 10th C. A.D In Bengal the upper part of the
‘sari’ was taken over the head so as to cover the coiffure, [n
Uttar pradesh the upper garment or the upper part of the
‘sari’ was worn round the bust and used to reach down to the
knees. In Malabar the ‘sari’ hung down from high above the
navel, its knot being tied under the arm-pit. It is not very clear
how exactly this was done.®

Social Condition 221
Stitched Clothes
Let us now ascertain the role which stitched clothes played
in the dress of men and women. There is one view which
holds that the art of sewing was not known to the Hindus
before the advent of Muslims. It is however, altogether
untenable. The needle and the process of sewing arc both rcl'crrcd
to in the Rgveda* and Aitareya Brahmana.= The Buddha had
forbidden monks to do sewing work for nuns.^ Tne Aniarakosha
expressly mentions the la'dor* and the Chinese pilgrim l*tsing
informs us that shirts and trousers were quite common in
Kashmir and the Punjab during the 7th C.A.D. though they
were not in vogue in the plains.* The fact is that in the hot
plains of the northern India stitched clothes arc very inconvenient,
and so for the greater part of the year both men and women
could go on without them. That seems to be the reason \vhy
they arc so rarely referred to in literature.
The use of bodice can be seen below the breasts of the
statue of Chaiidi Yakshini at Bharhut on one of the coins of
Samudragupta where Lakshrai is seen wearing the bodice
precisely in this way. We can see both the knot and the two

end’s hanging (fown m fts conii'nuatjon." Kah'dasa afso refers to

the use of bodice.” A narrow breast-band was also used across
the bosom in the Deccan and Ceylon. This fashion did not
spread to northern India.®

222 Bhoja Paramnra and his Times
Jackets, blouses and frocks seem to have come into vogue in
the plains of northern India, when they were included in the
dominions of Scythians, Kushanas and other Central Asian
invaders. Uttar pradesh. Central India, Gujarat and Maharashtra
were under Scythian rule for different periods during the 1st
four centuries of the Christian era. This circumstance very
prabably facilitated the spread of the fashion of stitched
clothes in Hindu society. The tendency to imitate the dress of
rulers is not peculiar to the modern times. It existed in the
past as well.
Society, however, had a general prejudice against stitched
clothes, and women were for a long time, reluctant to take to
jackets, blouses and frocks. Only dancing girls are seen in
the sculptures of Bharhut, Bagh and Sarnath etc. wearing shirts
and jackets. These became common among a section of ladies
only in the Muslim period.'
The variety of fashion in wearing apparel is indicated in the
sculptures of the Vaital Deul temple, such as stripes set off
with zigzag lines, with triple lines, with oblique as well as
horizontal double wavy lines, with flowers and a scroll border.*
Still more varied are the types of coiffure of human figures.*
Among the foreign writer, Chauju Kua* observes that the people

of Nan-pi and Ku-lin- (Malabar) wrap their hair in silken turbans

at the time of the battle. Of the people of Hu-ha-la (Gujarat

the same writer says* that both men and women have double ear-
rings and wear close fitting clothes, with hoods on their heads.
A close examination of all the above statements makes it

clear that the turbans, trousers and kurtakas (short shirts) formed

the common dress both of men and women. So far as turban is
concerned, the practice differed in the south from the north
where down to the 11th C.A.D., no one could wear a turban

Socfal Condition 223
except the king and other government officials of high ranks.^
The Manasollilsa refers to the use of turbans by the ministers,
the provincial governors, the feudatory princes and the various
departmental olficcrs.®
The rich people usually dressed differently from what they do
now. They usually wore richly embroidered jackets and tightfit-
ting shirts which reached their knees, handsome belt and a patkfi’
which sometimes was very costly. Along with ‘dhoti’ they wore
an ‘uttarlya’ or upper garment. The M'lnasollasa also refers to
the ministers, the provincial governors, the feudatory chiefs and
princes and the various departmental officers wearing jackets
or ilhgika,* with long sleeves. Similarly the ladies of wealthier
classes wore saris’ with close fitting short-sleeved bodices and
petti-coats, all of which were richly ornamented. They also used
‘patkas’ of a shade to match their ‘.saris*. While on hunting,
a woman wore a short-skirt and a ‘choli’, which was perhaps
the usual outdoor dress.*
A detailed description of embroidery work, the uoolcn and
cotton clothes alongwith the silken ones in the Yuktikalpataru of
Bhoja is in itself a strong evidence to show the popularity of
fashion among the rich people.'^ But unfortunately we do not get
much information regarding the dress of the poor people except
that they wore barks of trees.®
Dress of the Monks
The dress of the priestly class and monks differed from that
of other people. They generally wore ‘dhoti’ and ‘uttarlya*.
Moreover, they appear to have preferred white dress to coloured
ones, as is evidenced by a picture of Hemachandra, wearing

224 Bhoja Paramnra and his Times
immaculate white dress. In the Manasollasa a ‘purohita’ is
described as wearing a white dress.*
Shoes
The shoes were not unknown to Indians. According to
Alberuni they used shoes, kept them tight till they began to put
them on. They were turned down from the calf before walking.’
The testimony of Alberuni is also attested to by the sculptural
remains of the period. In the Golike^vara temple of the time of
the Chalukya Vijayaditya III at Biccavol the Stirya has been
depicted as wearing top boots. He is not shown bare-footed as
in western Chalukya, Pallava or Chola sculptures ’ The Chinese
traveller Chau-ju-Kua states that the people of Gujarat wore red
leather shoes on their feet.'*
Coiffure
The styles of hair dressing were as numerous as graceful,
Bhoja refers to different kinds of hair-combs made of gold, silver,
copper, brass, iron, horns of deer and buffaloes and tusks of
elephants. He also refers to various types of mirrors.® One of
the sculptural remains from Khajuraho depicts a scene of a
damsel using the mirror to adjust her locks applying collyrium to
iheejcetc.* It shows an artistic knot of the hair beautified by
flower weather and gold plaques etc. . In the Gahadavala
sculptures in the eastern part of Rajasthan, much attention
appears to have been paid towards hair decoration. The ‘bhrama-
rakas’ or ringlets of hair are curled up to form little bunches. A
fine though broken piece is a lovely female head from Rajorgarh
in Bikaner with coiffure decked with flowers executed most
elegantly with great care for details.’

Ornaments
Ornaments were used by both men and
women alike. Men
used to Vic with women in the number
of ornaments to be worn

case of the ornaments on the forehead, which were generally used
by women alone. Alberuni states that the men wore articles
of the female such as ear-rings, arm-rings, golden seal-rings on the
ring finger as well as on the toes of feet i The Aparajitaprchha^
a contemporaneous work of Bhuvanadevacharya has treated very

1.
Hara. 2. Padaka. 3. Srivatsa. 4. Kaustubha. 5. Patra-

In the Manasollasa^ we find reference of some ornaments
such as head-ornaments, ear-rings, necklaces, bracelets, finger-
rings, waist-ornaments and leg-ornaments.
We can get an adequate idea of jewellery of the period
from the sculptures and paintings where it is abundantly carved.
As the contemporary djnasties have much in common, they
approximately represent the same pattern of ornamentation.
Accordingly, the sculptures of the Gahadavala, Chandela,
Kalachuri and the Paramara dynasties tally much with each other
in showing the list of ornaments.* The common ornaments

depicted in these sculptures are ; head-ornament, ear-rings,

necklace, ornaments for arms, bangles, waist-ornaments, thigh-
ornaments and the ornaments for ankles.
The ornaments were partly made of gold and partly of pearls.
The ‘sikhapaSa’ and ‘sikhajala’ etc. were used to adorn the head
and forehead. The necklaces of gold and precious stones were
used on the neck, which used to reach down to the chest. Besides
a guady ornament was used over the breasts. Shoulders were

adorned with ‘keyuras’ of which there were several types. Nor
was the portion just above the elbow neglected, 'llic car-rings
referred to as ‘Karnasobhana’ by Altekar’ and known as ‘kundala*
in the list of Aparfijita-Prchha arc also shown in the sculpture
of this period.
We do not find any reference of glnss-banglc-s in Sanskrit
literature before the lOth C.A.D, It is afterwards that these
bangles arc referred to.' In Bengal the number of bangles worn
on the hand was large.*"* They were often joined togctlicr so as to
form a composite ornament reaching almost the elbow.
Whether the same fashion was prevalent in the rest of the country
is not known. The fashion at Ajanta. however, prescribed only
a few banglc.s on the hands.'* It is very likely that this fashion was
followed by the sculptors of Paramarns, Gnhadnvalns. Kalachuris
and Chandclas as they depict only two bangles in each hand and
the same appears to be the ease with their fect-banglcs. But
Altckar writes that the number of feet bangles was large. Some
of them used to produce a gingling sound, when their wearers
were in motion ** The waist ornament or girdle i.s three-rounded.
On its centre hangs a chain in between the legs which reaches the
leg bangles, either front side of the girdle is linked up with the
thigh ornament.
The thigh ornament in the sculpture is fan-like. Bharata**
refers to it as ‘padapatra’ which was to be worn over thighs. It
seems to have been made of pearls, as may be gathered from a
painting of Mayadcvi in cave No. 2 at Ajanta."
Among the numerous ornaments described by the poets and

illustrated by sculptors and painters the nose-ring and toe-ring are
conspicuous by their absence. Even in the Amarakoia and
Natya^astra there are no words to denote these ornaments. It
appears that the nose ring was unknown to the Hindus before
the advent of Muslims. It was from about 13th C.A.D. that the
nose ring began to be depicted in the Hindu sculptures such as is
clear from the sculptures of Purl and Rajpii.ana of the post-
Mushm period. The Mural-paintings in the Padmanabhasvami
temple at Trivandrum of the 14, h C.A.D. also depict the nose-
ring. Both the positive and negative evidences thus show that
the nose-ring is not a Hindu ornament. It seems to have been
clearly borrowed from the Mohammadens. It is indeed a mystery
how this ornament of foreign origin should have come to he
regarded as the most important insignia of saubhagya !
Popular and Royal Amusements
The popular amusements of this period were more or less
imilar to those of the preceding centuries. Some of
the

Dramatic Performances
Dramatic performances were quite popular during the nerinH
The themes ofthe plays were usually drawn from thj court
life’The marriage of Karna, the king of Gujarat, for
example, receivedg neralappremationatthe stage.
Wc have already mentionedas to how
Damara staged a drama at the court of Dhara i Also

byMadana the preceptor of Arjunvarman, the

of Jayasimha took to flight and the
victorious Arjunavarman was still sealed on his war-elephant a

cluster of blossoms of a celestial tree (Parijatamanjari) fell on his

breast and on touching it was transformed into a beautiful maiden
This drama was acted for the first time at the spring-festival

gasanjotsava or Chaitrotsava) in the city of Dha-ra (modern

Inscription No. 67 at the Rfijarajcivara temple at Tanjore
records the provision made for the actors who took part in the
drama at the time of annual fair.* A number of Deccan records
also mention provision made for the ‘rahgabhoga’ of deities.®
The expression 'rahgabhoga’, according to A.S. Altckar, probably
refers to the provision for Puranic Dramas, that used to be perfor-
med at the annual fairs in D.*ccan till quite recently.® Such plays
were organised also on the days of festivals like Dashehara, Holi,
Ram-N.avami and GokulashlamJ, etc,
U.sually the venue of dramatic pcrformancc.s was a palace
or a temple.* Sometimes they were arranged in open air. Kalhana
points out such arrangements.® Both male and female participated
in the plays. 'I he theatre was patronised by the kings and
common people alike. Thus drama was neither localised in the
capital nor did it cater to the taste only of the court and fashion-
able people of towns. According to Mcrutuftga, the common
people shared the joys in such theatrical performances.
Dancing
Dancing and music were the other important sources of
amusement.'’’ In course of dramatic performances, a spectator
could see plenty of dancing girls and hear songs accompanied by
musical instruments. Dancers thronged in the temple court-yard
and the palace of the kings •In the plan of palace, Bhoja
included, inter alia, the construction of a dancing hall, a theatre
and a gymnasium.® The SamgUamakaranda, attributed to Narada,
perhaps, belongs to the 11th C.A.D. It deals with music and
dance in two separate parts.® In the Aparajita-pfcchu, another

The extant sculptures bear ample testimony to the popularity
of dancing On the Bhuvanesvara Lihgaiaja temple, several
figures in the dancing attitude have been elegantly carved out.®
We find a scene in the Rajarani temple depicting a damsel
sounding cymbals to make a peacock dance.® The temple of

Natesvara built by the Pala rulers in the 10th C.A.D. (remains

of which are now in Dacca Museum) depicts a scene of Nateivara

dancing on a bull.'* Ganesa in dancing pose has been depicted
in several temples, e.g., the temples of Somanatha, Navalakha in
Gumll, Tejaljapala temples at ‘^atruniaya and Abu of the 12th
and 13th centuries, the Rudramahalaya temple at Siddhpur, the
Sun temple at Modhera, the Vimala temple at Abu and the
temples of Bheraghat etc. Similarly, we find the figure of dancing
Siva in one of the niches of the early Chedi temples from
Chandrehi.® The figures of dancing damsels and ‘surasundaris’
can be seen in most of the sculptures of the age.®
Music
The evidences of music and musical instruments are abun-
dantly found in the contemporary literature and sculpture. The
Samaiangana-Sutradhara, while describing the plan of a palace
makes a provision for the construction of a music hall.’ The
Aparajitaprccha devotes two full chapters to music, musical
sounds, ‘Svaras’, ‘ragas’, songs and musical instruments etc.® The
Manasollasa® of Some^vara devotes 2500 verses to music and
musical instruments, and touches on new phases of music,
specially prabandhas.

The depiction of large number of figures in various
dancing poses and with musical instruments shows the general
inclination of the kings and the people towards these arts.
Chau-ju-Kua, the Chinese traveller, rightly describes, **at their

(Indian’s) banquets the whole company broke out into song, music

and dancing.
Games
Both the outdoor and indoor games were known to the
society of the period under survey. But their items were certainly
less than those of modern games. Both adults and children
took part in games. Duels, wrestling, fighting with a stick, polo
and something like hockey were played by sturdy young boys.
The game of polo was especially popular in the Chalukya domain.®
Playing with balls was a favourite pastime for children and girls.^
The swing was also enjoyed by young married® women, both at
