and others we know that dice and chess were also popular games.
U. N. Ghoshal opines that gambling was also popular.* Boys and
girls played the game of hide-and seek.*
Animal fights were very common. Hunting was the other
favourite game of the rulers. Somadeva* enumerates various games
and sports in his Manasollasa some of which are as follows
:
1, Military exercise 2. Literary exercise. 3. Elephant sports
and polo. 4. Cambatants duels. 5. Wrestler’s contests 6. Flight
of birds and beasts and pigeon flight and 7. Hunting etc.
Popular Customs and Manners
We have already dealt with some of the popular customs like
those of ‘sati’ and 'devadasis’ etc. Here we add to them a
categorical description of Alberuni depicting some other popular
manners and customs He writes, “They do not cut any of the
hair of the body, divide the moustache into single plates, let the
nails grow long, have red teeth in consequence of chewing areca
nuts with betel leaves and chalk, drink wine before having eaten
anything. In washing they begin with feet, and then wash the
face. They wash themselves before co-habiting with their wives.
They ride without a saddle, if they put on a saddle, they mount
on the horse from its right side. In travelling they like to have
somebody riding behind them. They wear a girdle called
‘yajnopavlta’ passing from the left shoulder to the right side of
the waist. In all consultations, and emergencies, they take the
advice of the women. When a child is born, people show
particular attention to the man, not to the woman. Of the two
children, they give preference to the younger, particularly, in
the eastern parts of the country. In shaking hands they grasp
the hand of a man from the convex side. In their meetings they

sit cross-lcggcd. They spit out and blow their noses without any
respect for the elder one’s presence and so forth.
Burial Ceremony
Aiberuni presents an elaborate picture of ceremonies of
the Hindus, performed after death. According to him the Hindus
preferred the burial ccrcmonj'. He writes, “they burn with as
much sandal and other wood as they can get. Part of the burned
bones arc brought to the Ganges.
The remainder of the ashes is thrown into some brook of
running water, on the spot where the body has been burned. They
raise a monument similar to mile-stone, plastered with gypsum.
Tlic bodies of children under three years arc not burned. Those
who cannot afford to burn their dead will either throw them
somewhere on the open field or into running water.’
We may thus suggest that in the period under survey the
masses preferred cremation to burial.
Some Orthodox Beliefs
The old belief in omens, portents, Astrology and dreams etc.
continued to have their firm hold on the minds of the people.
The complete ban against sea-voyage, imposed by some of the
canonical authorities of the earlier centuries w’as repeated during
this period.’ The sciences of Astronomy and Asiiology were in a
remarkably developed stage. Besides Astrology, there were a
number of other superstitions current in the society. It was
believed that if certain vows and conditions were observed, gods
could be compelled to do the needful. Catching a serpent alive
was regarded as a signal proof of chastity. Spells and enchant-
ments against serpent bites were current. The evidence is available
to show that their futility svas often realised.* Many women were
induced to administer herbs and medicines to their husbands,
which were supposed to be cfilcacious in keeping them under
1. 5'(7c/wu I, pp. 179-182.
2. Sflc/mn II, pp. 169-170,
3. The Struggle for Empire^ p. 492.
4. Yuktikalpataru, p.
bhoja ParamSra anti his Times
their control but which very often ruined their health and
hastened their death.^
Sometimes some loyal subjects used to take vow that they
would olTer their own heads, if their king was to be blessed with

a son. Sorab (No. 479) informs us that a little earlier than our

period i e in C. 991 A.D Katega took a vow to offer his head to
the Goddess Gundabbe of Hayve, if his king ^anti-varman was

blessed with a son ; a son was soon born and then Katega allowed

the royal soldiers to cut off his head, and of course went to
heaven.^
Suicide
To commit suicide in holy places, either by drowning or by
burning on auspicious days was also regarded as a pious deed.
We have ample evidence for this. Dhanga, the Chandela King
is known to have courted death after 1002 A.D., by allowing
himself to be drowned at Prayaga when he had grown very old.^
About the same year, the Sahiya King Jayapala sacrificed his life
on the fuheral pyre after his repeated defeat at the hands of
Sultan of Ghazna.^
Mahmud Ghazni, in his book kitab Jain-ul-Akbar com-'
posed in A H. 440/1049 A.D., quotes several such instances of
suicide.
It would be, however, unreasonable to .think that these
practices were universal. They, on the other hand, w'ere confined
to rryal classes, for there is not even a single instance to show an
ordinary man committing suicide.
Standard of Living
The people of the upper class maintained a high standard of

living. Yasahpala (C.A D.) describes, for example, in his drama

named Maharaja Parajaya as to how even the royal visitor of
king Kumarapala was taken to surprise on seeing the huge

2. E. C. VIII.

Social Con^iiioH' ^35
stores of gold, silver and jewels In the palatial mansion of a
wealthy merchant of his capital city.^ We find a similar example
in the Kathasaritasagara= in which the mansion of Madanamala,
a courtesan of Pratisthana, has been shown to contain seven
enclosures with a train of horses and elephants, with stores of
weapons and jewels, with numbers of attendants and bards and
with players of vocal and instrumental music. The Manasollasa^
provides us with another glimpse of the royal living. The king
and the governors are described as being served in a banquet from
gold and silver vessels respectively. When the king takes his seat
on the lion-throne in the tastefully decorated audience-hall
supported by the pillars of gold studded with jewels, be is fanned

by beautiful women ; the royal Jadies dressed in gorgeous robes

and adorned with various ornaments take their seats in front of
- the king’s right and left.
While describing the plan and construction of the king’s
residence, the author of the Manasollasa'* observes that it should

be from one to nine stories high ; it should be white-washed ; it

should have latticed passages in some parts and dark passages

illuminated by jewelled lamps in other parts ; it should have an

ivory fencing and pillars of gold or of sandal-wood as well as floor
of glass or crystal and walls of crystal mirror-like slabs. In the
same context we arc told that the king should change his residence
according to the seasons.
The court scenes and the scenes of royal processions of
the age under review can be seen also in the sculptures of the
Lihgaraja and the Konarka temples in Orissa, the Karidarya
Mahadeo temple at Khajuraho in Bundelkhand and the Kesava
at Belur in Mysore, as well as in the paintings of the Brihadl^vara
temple at Tanjore etc.
However, the life of the average householder, it seems, was
much the same as now. The dwelling house usually faced east,
had spaces on all sides and was enclosed by a compound wall

Gcncrcjl EstitnotB of Chctrocicr
The contemporary foreign travellers, on the whole, have given
a favourable estimate of the general character of Indians
Alberuni made a striking reference to the deep-rooted tendency
of Indian mind towards self-conceit and depreciation of
everything foreign as he writes. “The Hindus believe that there is
no country but theirs, no nation like theirs, no king like theirs
no religion like theirs. They are haughty, foolishf vain seS'
conceited and stolid They are by nature niggardly in commu-
n.cating that which they know, and they take the greatest possible
care to withhold it from men of another caste among their own
fo Their belij'’tir°''’ Accordingto their belief, there is no other country on earth but theirs no
other race of men but theirs, and no created beings beside them
have any knowledge of science whatsoever.’’^ The author concludes
y tracing the root of this evil to its source in the changed habits
01 the IndiaD sbout foreign travel.
Alberuni’s above remarks apply to a section of i .r-
society and not to entire Hindu society.
A summary of accounts of foreign travellers, literary
sources and epigraphic records, thus shows that the society of the
period was well organised having a synthesis of both old and
dynamic canonical rules and more or less, it was a replica of
ancient orthodoxical society. It retained the chastity which
guarded Hinduism against ‘Mlechchhas’ and Turushkas. These
features of Indian society have been noted by the foreigners as
well.

CHAPTER Vm
### ECONOMIC CONDITION
The sources of information wiih regard to the economic
condition of the period are very scanty. Neither indigenous nor
foreign evidences throw sufficient light on this aspect. The
accounts of Alberuni certainly give us some idea about the
prosperity of his time.^
The prosperity of a land depends primarily on agriculture,
natural products and industries and secondarily on trade and
commerce. We shall take up these one by one.
Land and AgrtcuJture
From ancient times in India the value of agriculture was
recognised. The State did not allow any owner to waste the land,
a valuable gift of nature. Manu* prescribes the imposition of a
fine on a cultivator, who does not cultivate his field at proper time
nor guards the crop from being eaten up by animals. Similarly,
Kautilya* ordains the confiscation of land from those who do not
care to cultivate them.
Agriculture which has been through all the ages the principal
occupation of Indians, was well cared for during this period.
The pressure on land increased possibly due to adoption of the
profession of a farmer by the people, who were previously busy in
earning their livelihood by trade and industry. The incessant
invasions of foreigners like Sultan Mahmud, adversely afifected
the trade and commerce of the country.^ Moreover, the disturbed
political condition added to the shrinkage of the volume of trade
about 1 1th C.A.D.
1. Sachau, p. 22.
2. Manu, VIII, p. 243.
3. Kautilya, II, 1.
4. Socio-Economic condition of northern India, p. 172.
238 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
Land and its Cultivation
Manu prescribes that agriculture was one of the pursuits of
the ‘Vaisya’ community. Some authorities mention this as a
pursuit of the ‘Sudras’.
In the period under review it seems probable that Brahmanas
and Kshattriyas were simply the owners of the land. The bulk of
the actual tillers of the soil may have come from the ‘Sudra’
community.
The prevailing system in the Deccan, during the period
appears to have been ‘Rayatawari. The zamindar class which
was assigned the royal revenues did exist only to a limited extent.
The mention of ‘gramapati’ alongwith ‘gramakuta’ in some of the
records shows that the former was the village-holder. But we do
net know whether the produce was to be divided out of the gross
or after the government dues had been paid. A. S. Altekar thinks
that in Deccan sometimes the owner received three shares and
the tiller two, sometimes the owner two and the tiller one and
sometimes the division was equal.* It is, therefore, difficult to say
as to what the actual proportion was. Dr. U. N. Ghoshal states
that the peasant proprietors, who paid revenues to the govern-
ment under various specific heads were left free in the possession
of their holdings.- All this shows that after paying the fixed land
revenue, a farmer was entitled to be the owner of the land. In
the south, the village communities maintained their corporate
organisation with full vigour.’
Types of Land
As today, different kinds of land were known in this period.
The gradation or classification was based on scientific principle.
Bhoja* enumerates as many as sixteen types of land. These were

‘balisvamini’ ‘bhogya’, ‘sita’ (arable), ‘apasrayavati’ (depended

on wells etc), ‘Kanta’, ‘khanimatyamradharini’, banikyaprasadhita’

(tradable), ‘dravyasarapanna’, ‘devamatrika ‘(depended on rains).

Economic Condition 239
‘dbanya, ‘hastivanopeta’ ‘mitraghatini’, ‘asrenipurusha’ and
‘sakyasamanta’ etc.
From the economic standpoint we may generally classify
lands under the following groups, viz.
1.

Cultivable land ; 2. Waste land ; 3. Habitable land ;

4. Pasture land ; 5. Garden and forest lands. The Amara-
kosa^ states twelve types of land which are :
I. ‘Urvara (fertile) ; 2. Oshara (barren) ; 3. 'maru’ (desert)

4, ‘aprahata’ (fallow) ; 5. ‘;§vadva!a’ (grassy) ; 6. ‘paiikila’

(muddy) ; 7. ‘jalaprayamanupam (watery or wet) ; 8. ‘kachehha’,

(contiguous to water) ; 9. ‘sarkara (land full of pebbles and

pieces of limestone) ; 10. ‘sarkaravati (sandy) ; II. ‘iiadimatrika’

(watered from a river for cultivation) and 12. ‘devamatrika’

(watered by rain\

The term ‘Kshetra’ is very often recorded in the inscriptions
of this period. 2 It generally means a field. But Basak® and
Pargiter^ interpret ‘kshetra’ as cultivable field. In the Amarakosa
also it is defined as a special type of land capable of producing
all kinds of crops.®
There were, however, two types of cultivable lands, viz the
dry land which required ample irrigation, and the wet land which
required less water. The latter type referred to in the grants as
‘vapa’ precedes ‘Kshetra’. Dr. Sircar interprets this terra as
cultivable land. This term here may signify ‘vapl’ which means
a small tank instead of ‘vapa’ in its derivative sense meaning the
field where seeds are sown.®
Other terms met with in inscriptions are ‘khila’’ ‘aprahata’.
Basak explains ‘khila’ as untilled land. This is also supported by
1. Amar. I, 5-6, pp. 70-71, I. 10-13, p. 72.
2. I A., 1910, PI. “A” p. 195 ; PI. “B” p. 200 ; PI. “C” p. 204 ;
E.I , XIX, p. 120
3. E.L, XVII, p. 348 and AMSJ. Vol. Ashutosh Mukerjee Silver
Jubliee Commemoration vol.
4. LA. 1910, p. 205, Text, p. 204.

240 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
Amarakosa.' According to Macdonell and Keith, the terms
‘khila*’ and 'khilya®’ seem to have the same meaning. Basak holds
that both ‘khila’ and ‘aprahata* are synonyms signifying untilled
land. But they are recorded in the plates. According to Monier-
Williams*, ‘aprahata’ means untilled waste land. Dr. Sircar
defines it as “unreclaimed jungle land’’. Thus it appears that
‘khila’ land is previously cultivated, but now left uncultivated, and
‘aprahata’ land is hitherto untouched by the plough. Both terms
can be taken together to mean ‘cultivable waste land’.
We have also reference to another type of land called ‘vastu’
or habitable land and from inscription* we know that plots of such
land were denoted for the purpose of building houses. Monier-
Williams explains the word as a site for a dwelling place. The
term is popularly used for building sites even today in India.
They are generally at a higher level than cultivated and cultivable
lands.
Also there was pasture-land for the grazing of cattle. Fleet
interprets* it as “the reeds grown by the cattle path’’. This may
indicate the pasture land of Narada* where the cowherds used to
tend their village-cattle.
Principal Crops

Since the time of Yajurveda’ (Vajasaneyi-Saiphita) if not

much earlier the principal crops of northern India were rice,

‘yava’ (barley), wheat and pulses like ‘mudga’, raasur and ‘masa’

and oil seeds such as sesamum. The Bhoja-prabandha also
refers to the production of ‘mudga’ i.e. the modern ‘rnuiiga’

(pulse). The contemporary epigraphic evidence shows that wheat

was cultivated in the Gwalior area.®

Irrigation
The period under survey, did not see any big
irrigational
project owing to the war-like activities of the
rulers. The
military strength always haunted the minds of
rulers. Con-
sequently, the lions-share of the states income was
utilized to
defray the high expenses of troops. Some traces of
small-scale
irrigational projects are certainly found in the
contemporary
records. They were carried out either by private persons
or by
Government.
The frequent mention of tanks, lakes and
wells in the con-
temporary records, both literary and epigraphic,
tends us to
believe that the excavation of all these was
regarded as most
meritorious. Merutuftga refers to the excavation of the famous

Karnasagar-lake by Knlachuri King Karna.i In Rajputana,
Lahini, the sister of Purnapala, the Paramara ruler of Abu, cons-
tructed tanks, probably at Vasantagacjha, in the Sirohi state, in
A.D. 104 '.2
A fragmentary stone inscription inscribed on a wall of modern
shrine at Patna, refers to a canal built by Siddharaja-Jayasimha,

the most renowned of the Gujarat Kings (A.D. 1094-1143).“

The site of the canal was selected two miles away from the
present town of Patna. The whole area it covered is known as
Sahasralingtalo. This was a big reservoir of water. Tradition
attributes the construction of this tank to one Durlabhraja,

(A. D. 1010-1022), a predecessor of Siddharaja, who is credited

with the restoration of the tank when it was dilapidated.^ If the
tradition contains anv grain of truth, it would be right to suggest
that the construction of canals was not unknown to the people of
this period.
According to Hirananda Sastri, Gujarat is known for its old-
stepped wells. Such stepped-wells were also known in the age of
KMidasa. For instance, one such type of well was found at Patna
which was built by Rani-Udayamatl, the queen of Bhimadeva I,
the contemporay Chalukya king of Mahmud of Ghazni, who ruled
till 1063 A.D.“ But it is doubtful whether these wells served the
irrigational purpose too. Most probably they were excavated
only for the religious purpose.
Venkayya* in his article on “Irrigation in Southern India in
Ancient Times, gives in a tabulated form the list of some irriga-
tional tanks of the Pallava, Chola and Vijayanagar dynasties.
We shall discuss here only the tanks of the Cholas as the other
two dynasties fall beyond our prescribed period. According to

Mr. Venkayya further gives a summary account of the elec-
.
tion, composition, functions and modus-operandi of the
committee of the village assembly for supervision of tanks which
each village in the Tamil country seems to have had in ancient
times. According to him, all endowments made in this connec-
tion were entrusted to the village committees and that they had
to invest money in the best possible way. We may suggest here
that they utilized the endowments in reclaiming waste land and
cultivating it in order to pay interest . on the endowment from
the produce. They had apparently to look after the cultivation
of lands granted to tanks. This income from both sources had to
be utilized to meet the charges for the annual or periodical
removal of silt in tanks apd for repairs, Fines to be credited tp
244 Bhoja ParamSra and his Times
the tank fund were to be levied by them. Endowments were also
made for water-sheds to supply drinking water to the thirsty.
Way-fares were generally entrusted to them.* The king does not
appear to have had any direct control over the upkeep of tanks.
He could, of course, enforce the obligations incurred by village
assemblies as trustees of endowments for them - It may also be
recorded that during periods of famine, even private individuals
undertook excavation of tanks as a relief measure.^
As in the south, the excavation of tanks, wells and lakes etc,
was popular, in the north also. Bhoja, for example, had excavated
Bhojapur Lake, the largest of the kind. It spread on the village
of Betwa to the extent of 250 square miles.* But no information
is at hand to afSrm its irrigational capacity. We may simply
conjecture from its wide extent that it may have served the irriga-
tional purpose too.

Land Revenue ;

Both the epigraphic and literary sources state that the land
revenue was realised in cash and kind both. Unfortunately, we
do not know as to what was its actual proportion. Generally,
three terms are found in the contemporary records to denote the
land revenue viz. ‘bhaga’, ‘uparikara’ and ‘hirapya’* The tax
‘bhaga’ was realized in kind which was levied in ordinary crops.®
The ‘uparikara’, according to Fleet, is the tax levied on culti-
vators who have no property right in this whole.* The ‘hirapya’
was the only tax which was realised in cash.® U.N. Ghoshal'
states that the ‘hiranya’ in literary sense, is a tax paid in the form

of gold or gold coins. According to Sircar,^ ‘hiranya’ is the
King’s share of crops, paid in cash.
Generally, the land revenue was one-sixth of the produce.
Alberuni states that the Indian farmers had to pay one-sixth of
their income to the king, in recognition of the protection which
he afforded to their property and their families ^ The proportion
of the land revenue in cash was probably assessed according to
the fertility of the soil, location of the land and also the use to
which the land was to be put.
Land Measurement :
The land measurements, referred to in the contemporary
records, are ^nivartanas^' ^phalakas'^' ^prastha',^ and ‘plough'.^ Of
these, the ‘nivartana’ seems to be widely used for land measure-
ments in the period The Kalvan-plates of Yasovarman record
that Ranaka-Arama, who was the chief of the Ganga family,
granted certain pieces of land to some ‘Jain-Acharya’ named
Ammadeva. The first piece of land measured 40 ‘nivartanas’,
the second 25, the 3rd 35 and the 4th 2 ‘nivartanas’.’ Let us
know the accurate measurement of a ‘nivartana’. Two types of
‘nivartana’ are referred to in the ‘Sukranitisara’, viz. the ‘nivar-
tana’ of Manu and the ‘nivartana’ of Prajapati. The Manu
‘nivartana’ is of 125 hands or 19200 ‘yavas®’ while the ‘nivartana’
of Prajapati measures 100 hands or 2400 ‘ahgulas.® The hand of
Prajapati is equal to 24 ‘ahgulas’, which, according to Sukra,
was more prevalent for land measurement. Sukra further adds
that the size of an ‘angula’ was like the lines of middle finger

Occupational Castes :
Beside the farmer, every village was peopled by the
carpenters^, goldsmiths and potters etc. They were maintained
ty the community through the assignment of a certain grain-share
from each farmer, in return for which the artisans were to supply
his needs during the year.^ This system has been very ancient in
India and continues even to the present day.
Markets and IVeights :
There were markets in the villages and cities which contained
the houses of traders’ associations.^ Various commodities were
sold in those markets The chief articles for sale were condy
sugar, jaggery, Bengal-madder, thread, cotton, cocoa-nuts, butter,

sesame oil, salt, areca-nuts, clothing fabrics, Jala (bunch of buds,)

lagada (bars of gold), silver or other metal, oil, cattle, fodder,

grains and barley etc.®
The articles for sale in the market were to be taxed either in
cash or in any share of the articles in accordance with their
quality usually determined by the Government. Also the shops,
houses of traders and their associations had to pay taxes which
varied according to the articles they contained. For example,
a shop of braziers had to pay one ‘rupaka’ for the month, a
house of distillers four ‘rupakas’, each gambling house two
‘rupakas’ and each traders’ association one ‘dramma’. A list of
some of the articles with the respective taxes imposed on them is
given helow on the basis of epigraphic records.'

Means of Exchange :
Usually the prices of articles during the period werebarter
ones. Nowhere in the contemporary records, we find mention
of prices being paid in money. The records of the closing year
of the 10th C.A.D. refer to the barter-prices. Two of the Chol-
inscriptions^ on the central shrine of Rajaraje§vara temple at Tan-
jore, one on the southern and other on the northern wall, enume-
rate a number of vilhges, that were assigned by king Rajaraja
before the 29th year of his reign i.e. before 1014 A.D. The taxa-
tion of about 29 villages is given in Paddy and is found to be 100
‘kalamas’ of paddy by the Advallan measure per ‘veil’. Since the
Village belonged to the same division, it is fair to conclude that
10 ‘kalanjus’ of gold were equal to 100 ‘kalams’ at paddy by
Advallan measure. This would indicate that under Rajaraja the
price of rice was 10 ‘kalamas’ per ‘kalanju’. A gold ‘kalanju’,
according to A. S. Altekar. was about a quarter of a ’tola’ in
weight and thus was equal to about Rs. 5 of loday.^

An inscription of the time of Rajaraja (ICOO A.D.) states that

four navis’ of paddy was the price of one ‘arakka’ of sweet ghce.^
Another inscription of about 50 years later observes that one
‘urakku’ of ghee was equal in value to one ’kurun’ of paddy. Two
other records belonging to the third quarter of the 10th C.A.D.
inform that one ‘nan’ of oil cost one ‘liiri’ i e. 32 ‘naris’ of paddy
i.e. 12i ‘naris’ of rice.' Oil is thus seen to be as costly as sweet
ghee. It is stated in two of the records of the time of Rajaraja'
that one 'nari of curds used to cost 3 ‘naris of paddy, i.e 6/5
naris’ of rice. The price of pulse at the end of the 10th C. A. D.
can be worked out from two inscriptions of Rajaraja which give

ratio between the pulse and the rice as 6:5; 5 ‘naris’ of pulse used

to cost 6 ‘naris’ of rice.^

Mines and Metals
A number of metals were known to the people of this period.
The SamaraAgana ‘Sutradhara contains the names of various
metals such as iron, brass, gold, silver and copper etc.^ Copper is
refered to by Periplus as an article of export from Bharoch and
since then northern India depended almost entirely on the produce
of the local-copper-mines down to the beginning of the 17th

Industries and Their Organization
Many industries were in highly advanced stage during the
period. Some of these are referred to in the Yuktikalpataru of
Bhoja, the lexicons like Vaijayanti, De^inamamala and the
commentary on Amarako^a by Ksirasvamin etc. But all these
sources speak of only small-scale-industries. Not even a single
evidence is at hand to show the existence of any large-scale-
industry. On the basis of all the available documents, we shall
make a positive attempt to have an inquiry into the flourishing
industries of the period.
Cloth Industries
The manufacture of taxtiles, which is a very old Indian
industry, appears to have been carried on during this period also.
In Malwa, according to Chau-ju-kua^ cotton cloths were such a
common product as to form the subject of the export-trade.®

Kautilya mentions Bengal, Kamarupa (Assam) Magadha,

Madura, Aparanta, Kalinga Kasi, Vatsa and Mahisha as the chief
centres of cloth manufacture.® During the time of Periplus, quite a
large quantity of cloth was expocted from Bharoch Damaka i e.
Dravida country.^ Periplus refers to Minanagar, Gujarat, Ujjain
Paithan and Toggara as the principal manufacturing centres of
cloths. Many of the above places are mentioned by Marco-Polo
and Tavernier® also as the chief centres of cloth industry.® As
the period under survey falls in between the above two periods,
we may safely infer that during this period also the cloth industry,
might have been equally flourishing. Moreover, the Yuktikalpa-
taru’ refers to varieties of cotton, woolen and silken cloths with
their distinctive qualities. The total absense of cloth mills shows

252 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
that the manufacture of cloth was the monopoly of a certain
particular class of people who probably were known as weavers.
Tanning
The contemporary records give no information regarding the
tanning industry during the period. The mention of shoes and
leather, however, clearly indicates its existence. Alberuni^ refers
to those engaged in the profession of tanning as shoe-makers who
belonged to a particuler caste. The Samarafigana Sutradhara*
and the Yuktikalpataru'* also refer to the manufacture of leather
shields and armours.
Glass Industry
The history of glass or ‘kacha’ industry in India goes back
atleast to the age of Susruta, who laid down that liquids and wine
should be served in glass vessels. Bhoja‘ refers to the cups and
combs made of ‘kacha’. 1 here is however, no reference of glass
mirrors in the contemporary records. The Yuktikalpataru gives
a list of different types of mirrors such as
:

BHAVYA : was one ‘vitasti’ in length.®

VIJAYA ; The length and breadth of this was 4 angulas.*

PAURUSA ; its length and breadth was half a ‘vtasti’ ; on

this fell the full length of a person.’
All these mirrors were made of different metals such as gold,
silver, iron and ‘lakh’ etc.® Thus it may be inferred that the
glass industry was in an advanced stage.

Economic Condithn 253
Metallurgy
The people of the period were acquainted with the metallurgy
also. Iron was used for variety of purposes such as erecting
temples, pillars and buildings. Besides, serveral other articles
like sword? and spears etc. were manufactured from this metal.^
Gold and Silver were used for manufacturing the vessels ^ The
Manasollasa mentions the golden lion throne.^ From Alberuni,
we hear that people of this time were well acquainted with the
purification of gold,* silver,® mercury and iron etc.® Stone-cutting
including stone-polishing is an Indian Industry going back to
prehistoric culture of the Sindhu Valley. Reference should also
be made to the superb stone terraces around the sacred tanks
which drew the enthusiastic admiration of so gifted a critic
as Alberuni.'^
The art of working metals which goes back to Vedic times
w as pursued during this period. The most fascinating remains
of the skill of iron-smiths of this period are the iron-beams,
all of un wrought iron in the temples of Bhuvane^vara, Puri and
Konark and above all, the celebrated iron pillar at Dhara, the
capital of the Paramara kings of Malwa.®
Mat Industry
No direct reference of mat industry is found in the contem-
porary records. Marco-polo, the writer of the 1 3th C.A.D.
refers to the working of this industry in the southern Gujarat and
northern maharashtra." He further adds that these mats in red
and blue leather were beautifully inlaid with figures of birds and
beasts and were skilfully embroidered with gold and silver. We
may thus infer that this industry also may have thrived during
the period.

254 Bhoja ParamUra and Ms Times
Ivory Work
The ivory work was also known to the people of this period.
The Yuktikalpataru states that several articles were prepared from
the tusks of the elephants such as bandies of knives and combs
etc.* Mysore being rich in elephants was known as the centre
of this industry.
Umbrellas
Different types of umbrellas were manufactured during the
period. Two of them referred to in tne Yuktikalpataru are
‘vi^eshya’ and ‘samanya’. The ‘vi&shya’ or especial types were
used by kings, princes and rich people whereas the ordinary
or ‘samanya’ types by common people.^ The especial type
was again divided into two categories i.e. ‘nirdanda’ and

‘sadanda* ; The ‘nirdanda’ umbrella could not be folded. The

king was advised to use an umbrella whose stick and neck
were to be made of pure wood, spokes of pure origin and
both the thread and the cloth were to be of scarlet colour.
The king also used another variant whose stick and neck were of
sandalwood, thread and cloth were to be pure while and this
umbrella was decorated with a golden pitcher at the top. At the
time of marriage or coronation, the stick of the umbrella was
to be of pure gold and the cloth was to be whiter. It was known
as the‘nirdanda’-type. The princes of royal blood were given
a different variant of umbrella. Its heights were to be one fourth
less than the length of umbrella of the king. The ‘pratapa’
type used by princes had bluish cloth and stick decorated with a
golden pitcher at the top. As for common people, their umbrellas
were to be half of tbe length of that used by princes The sticks
of these ordinary umbrellas were made of timber, ‘champaka’
‘panasa’, ‘sab’ , ‘sriphala’, sandal ‘vakula’, ‘neem’ and ‘vajra-

The epigraphic evidences also approve the manufacture of
umbrellas during the period. A copper-plate grant of Trilochana-
pala, the king of Gujarat, dated 5aka 972/C. 1050 A.D. refers to
the dedication of an umbrella resplendant with gold and jewels
to god Somanatha by Vatsaraja, the father of king Trilocha-
napala.®
Timber Work
The Yuktikalpataru refers to the furniture made of metal,
stone and wood, The verieties of wood used were ‘gambhari’,

‘panasa’, ‘chandana’ (Sandal) and bakula’^ etc. The ‘pithas’

called Subhasangaka, ‘jaraka’ were used during royal coronation
about the types of wood used for manufacturing the bed-stead.
As many as eight types of bed steads are referred to in the

Yuktikalpataru, viz' mahgala; ‘Vijaya’ Pusti ; ‘kshama, ‘tusti’,

sukhasana* ‘prachaihda’ and ‘sarvatobhadra.’ Each of them
differed from the other in size. These bed-steads were generally
made of 4 colours, viz. white, red, yellow and blue according
to 4 varnas viz. Brahmana, Kshattriya, Vaisya and Sudra respec-
tive!
Various other articles for daily use were manufactured from
wood such as armours,® combs® and cups^ etc. In the Rajatarah-

Bhoja prescribes some essentia! qualifications for carpenter.
According to him, he should be well-adeft in bamboo-work,
engraving, embroidering on stones and gold and in ‘silpa and
wood-work.® Thus we see that carpenliy was in a highly advanced
stage.
Sugar and Oil
The mention of sugar cane, sugar and sugar mills® in the
contemporary and some later records shows the manufacture of
the sugar during the period. As we have already seen that Bengal,
central India, Kashmir and Rajputana produced sugar cane, it
may be presumed that they may have been the leading centres of
the sugar manufacture.
Oil mills are also referred to in the contemporary records as
taila-ghanakas.'*
Salt
Almost all the dynastic records mention the manufacture of
salt. The contemporary inscriptions of Bengal® Rajputana,* the
Chandellas,® the Gahadvalas* and the KalachOrls of Tripnri’ refer
to the grant of salt pits and the right of manufacturing salt to
donees. But no reterence is made with regard to the large-scale
manufacture in northern India.

Weaving ;

Weaving is an ancient craft of India. In this period too, it
was very popular. Alberuni calls the followers of this craft as a

Arms : Swords, Spears etc.

As war was the option of kings, the age
was very rich in
manufacturing the war ammunitions. Several
varieties of war
weapons are described in contemporary
literature, inscriptions
and records of the foreign travellers.
Alberuni mentions the
manufacture of arrows, spear-points, swords and
knives in northern
r„dia.3 Sukra writes that the men adept in making light
machines,
arrows, cannon balls, swords, bows and
quivers were to be
regarded as useful servants.'' Utbi also refers to
the use of white
swords and blue spears by king Anandapala.®
A more detailed account with regard to
the manufacture of
swords, with their relative qualities and
manufacturing centres is
found in the Yuktikalpataru. The Chief
centres referred to in
te book were Benaras, Magadha,

blunt.

Thus according to Bhoja, swords manufactured at Benaras,
Saurastra, Rishika and Kalinga could stand the test of time,*
But the swords manufactured at Benaras were the best among all
whereas those of Anga were the worst.-
Bhoja writes, “the swords which have the following eight
qualities are known as good. These are well-shaped, made of
pure metal, beautiful at glance, of destructive capacity, producing
charming sound and of proportionate dimensions.”^ But the main
criterion of distinction between good and bad swords, according
to Bhoja, was the sound produced by them. The swords which
on being struck produced beautiful sounds were worshipped by
kings and one which was curved was to be thrown away.^
Besides swords, Bhoja gives a list of several other weapons
which were manufactured in his time. These were shields, bows,
arrows, armours, ‘shalva’ spear, axe and ‘chakra’ etc.®

The boat-making industry was in a most flourishing stage.

^
The Yuktikalpataru® is a Sanskrit work, which is
something
like a treatise on the art of ship-building in ancient
India, setting
forth many interesting details about the various sizes and
kinds
of ships, the materials out of which they were built and
the like.
It sums UP in a condensed form all the available information
and
knowledge about that truly ancient industry of India.
The ancient ship-builders had a good knowledge of the
materials as well as the varieties and properties of wood which
1. M//or, Vol. II, p. 478.
2 The manuscripts of the Yuktikalpataru are found in Calcutta
Sanskrit college library, Prof. Aufrecht has noticed it in his

No. cc. IXXI) The book is a compilation by Bhoja nrpati. It

treats of jewels, swords, horses, elephants, ornaments, flags,
umbrellas, seats, ministers, ships etc. and frequently quotes
the name of Bhoja, meaning probably Bhojarata of Dtara,
260 Bhoja Parathn'ra and his Times
went to tbe making of ships. R.K. MookeijU states that - accor-

ding to the ‘Vrksha-Ayurveda’ or the science of plant life (Botany)

there are four different kinds of wood. Bhpia likewise classifies
the wood into four categories according to 4 varnas, viz. Brah-
mana, Kshattriya, Vaishya and Sudra.^ There may also be

distinguished wood of the mixed (Dvijati) class in which are

blended properties of two separate classes.
According to Bhoja a ship built of the Kshattriya class of
wood brings wealth and happiness.* It is these ships that are to
be used as means of communication where it is difficult owing to
vast water. Ships, on the other hand, which are made of timbers
of different classes possessing contrary properties are of no good
and not at all comfortable. They do not last for a long time, they
soon rot in water, and they are liable to split at the slightest
shock and to sink down.^
Apart from pointing out the class of wood which is best for
ships, Bhoja laid down a very important direction for ship
builders in the nature of a warning which is worth noting. The
Yuktikalpataru further gives an elaborate classification of
ships themselves, based on their size. The primary division is

into two classes' (a) ordinary (Samanya) which were used in

Of these live varieties ‘Anhrdhva’, ‘Garbhini’ and ‘Manthara’
bring on misfortunes and ‘tJrddhva’ much gain or profit to
kings.
The Yuktikalpataru gives elaborate directions for decorating
furnishing ships as to make them quite comfortable to passengers.
Four kinds of metal are recommended for decorative purpose viz.
gold, silver, copper and the compound of all the three. Four
kinds of colours are recommended respectively for four kinds of
vessels. A vessel with four masts is to be painted red, that with
two masts is to be painted yellow and one-mast ship must be
painted blue. The prows of ships admit of great variety of fanciful
shapes or forms. These comprise the heads of lion, buffalo,
serpent, elephant, tiger, birds such as the duck, pea-hen or parrot,
the frog and m^n thus arguing a great development of the art
of carpenter or .sculptor. Other elements of decoration were
pearls and garlands of gold to be attached to and hung from the
beautifully shaped prow.
There are also given interesting details about the cabins of
ships. Three classes^ of ships are distinguished according to the
length of position of their cabins. There were firstly the ‘sarva-
mandira’ vessels which had the largest cabins extending from one
end of the ship to the other.® These ships were used for the trans-
port of royal treasure, horses and woraen.^ Secondly, there were
the ‘madhyamandira’ vessels®, which had their cabins just in the
middle part. These vessels were used in pleasure trips by kings
and they were also suited for the rainy season. Thirdly, ships

might have their cabins towards their prows in which case they
would be called ‘agramandira.^ These ships were used in the dry
season after the rain had ceased. They were eminently suited for
long voyages and also to be used in naval-warfare.= It was pro-
bably in these vessels that the first naval-fight recorded in Indian

literature was fought (the vessel in which Tugra, the Rishi king

sent his son).®

The construction of boats, during this period, seems to be
more scientific. Bhoja suggests, “the woods in the sea-going
vessels should not be joined together with iron because the magne-
tic iron in the sea can lead to the capsize of boats.”*
Like wood, the boats were also made of different other metals.
Bhoia states the manufacutte of boats from the metals such as
gold, silver, copper and brass.® 'the statement of Bhoja is also
attested to by the epigraphic records. It is said that it wa.s
because of the use of HOO war-boats that Sultan Mahmud could
defeat the Jats in 1026 A.D.® In Bengal during the days of
Devapala, there were officers in charge of ferries called Tarika and
Tarapat.’ The inscriptioi s of Rajendra-Chola record his supre-
macy over several islands such as Ceylone, Laccadvipa, Malaya
and Nicobar etc. They also speak of his ships which even used to
advance up to Indonesia, China and Korea.® Moreover, the des-
criptions of contemporary Arab-travellers also throw light on
the working of ship-making industry in India. These travellers
write that the ships of India were better as they were joined toge-

ther with ropes whereas those of Mediterranean with irons.
Besides, the Arab-writcrs also mention two-storeyed ships of
Indians consisting of separate rooms such as kitchen, bathroom,
sleeping-room and the room for commercial goods ^ Prof. K.D.
Bajpai opines that it was probably owing to the use cf iron that
the Mediterranean ships were often capsized. He further adds
that because of this they adopted Indian technique.^ These state-
ments go a long way to prove the highly advanced stale of
boat-making industry during this period.
Direct evidences from Indian Sculpture, Painting and Coins
The conclusions pointed out by these literary evidences are
further supported by other kinds of evidences mainly monumental
in their character. They are derived from old Indian art, sculpture
and painting and also from Indian coins. The earliest of them are
found among the Sanchi sculptures belonging to an age as old as
the 2nd century B.C. Among the temples of Jagannath at Puri
there is a well-preserved representation of royal barge shown in
relief on stone, which is said to have been once a part of the
black-pagoda of Karnataka belonging to the 12th C.A D. The
sculpture shows in splendid relief a stately barge propelled by
skilled oarsmen with all their might and, one almost hears the
very splash of their oars, the water through which it cuts its way
is thrown mto ripples and waves indicated by a few simple and
yet masterly touches, and the entire scene is one of dash and
hurry indicative of the desperate speed of a flight or escape from
danger.
The beauty of the cabin and the simplicity of its design
are particularly noticeable. The seat within is quite an inno-
vation, probably meant to be effective against sea-sickness while ,
an equally ingenious idea is that of the chain which hangs
from the rope and is grasped by the hand by the master of the
vessel to steady himself on the rolling waters. It is difficult to
ascertain what particular scene from our Shastras is here repre-
sented. Dr. R. K. Mookerji concludes that the interpretation
1. Sulaiman-Nadvi, (Arab Aur Bharat ka Sambandha) (Hindi)
Trans, by Rama Chandra Varma, Prayaga, 1930, p. 72-73,
2. Bharatiya VyapUra ka liihasa, p. 178.
i66 Bhoja Paramara and his Time^
probably of the surrounding sculptures, was that they represented
Sri Krishna being hurriedly carried away beyond the destructive
reach of King Kaihsa.^ It should also be remembered that the
vessel herein represented is that of the ‘Madhyamandira’ type as
defined in the Yuktikalpataru.
The representations of ships and boats in the Ajania paintings
are rightly interpreted hy Griffiths as only a “Vivid testimony to
the ancient foreign trade of India.” There is an oar behind, and
under the awning are a number of jars, while two small platforms
project fore and after.^ The vessel is of the ‘Agramandir’ type as
defined in the Yuktikalpataru.
Means of transport
Our information regarding the means of transport during the
period is veiy scanty. However, we may draw some inferences
from literature and the earlier monuments.
The roads of the period, opines Dr. Moti Chandra," were not
so even as they are today. Journey was not safe owing to fre-
quent occurrences of robberies Yet the tradesmen travelled
throughout the country in pursuit of their business and other
people for earning livelihood. Bilhapa,^ for instance, narrates in

the Vikramahkadevacharita (1080-88 A.D) as to bow he started

country-wide journey from Kashmir and visited several important
places like Mahapath, Mathura, Kannauj, Prayag, Banaras, Dhara

Anahilavad (Gujarat) and Somanatb. He voyaged on a ship from

Berawal to Tonawar and then turned towards the north after visi-
ting Rameshvaram.
The works like KutfanTmatam of Damodaragupta and
Subhasitaratnabhandagara® also lay emphasis on the country-
wide travels.
From ancient times, bullock carts have been the principal
means of transport in India. In the Paintings from Bharhut,

Mathura, Amaraoli, Sanchi and Ajanta etc., we find the descrip-
tions of these carts. One of the portraits from Bharhut^ depicts
a scene of bullock cart whose shape exactly resembles the modern
‘saggar’. On another occasion- is seen a rectangular bullock cart

having two wheels. Its vertical rear (plfhak) is made of wood.

The bulls are shown sitting nearby. Among the Mathura pain-
tings^, we come acrossa portrait of an ordinary bullock cart proba-

bly for carting light loads; the bulls and the driver are seen sitting

on the ground. At another place'* is the depiction of ahorse-
driven open vehicle whose driver is seen sitting on the yoke with
three persons inside.
The portraits from Araaraoti® show that in southern India,
in the early centuries, a light bullock cart was used for carting as
well as journey purposes. The bullock carts depicted on the
Bauddh Stupa of Goli® are shown richly decorated. They are rect-
angular and their sides appear to be knitted with cane. The roof
is also well decorated and a curtain being hung on its open side,
has been thrown up on the roof. The driver is seen on the yoke.
Also one oF the wall paintings on the i7th cave at Ajanta depict
an open cart. The portraits from Sanchi show that merchants
used bulls for transport.® But we do not know as to whether the
horses also served the same purpose. However, the portraits of
well decorated horses from Sanchi, opines Dr Moti Chandra,
prove that they were used for short journeys. As for elephants,
they too appear to have been used only for riding and not for
transport. Caravan system was also adopted for transporting

used both for riding and transporting purposes from ancient
times.==
We may thus conclude from the above details that no impor-
tant change took place in the means of transport for a very long
time. Although we hardly come across a sculptural evidence to
show as to which means were adopted for transport after the 7th
C A.D., we may surmise that they might not have differed much
from those of ancient times ^
Trade and Commerce
During the period, the Kadambas, ‘Silaharas and Cholas
contributed much to the cause of trade. Among these, Cholas

deserve special mention. Rajaraja (985-ini4) and Rajendra Chola

(1014-1044) were the most powerful kings of dynasty, and they

extended their power both on land and seas. The inscriptions
of Rajendra Chola credit him with the mastery of 12000 Islands,
such as Ceylone, Malaya, Nicobar etc His commercial ships
voyaged upto Indonesia and China.® The .kings of the Chola and
Pandya dynasties also constructed several big roads which facili-
tated the trade of the period.'
Like the rulers, the guilds also contributed much to the
expansion of the trade and commerce. In one of the inscriptions
of Rajendra Chola, is found the reference of a guild. The techni-
cal word used for this corporation is ‘Banajja*. This ‘sarngha’
comprised the people of different classes. Its assemblies were
held at different places for commercial matters.* Likewise, the
three copper-plates from Kotteyam and Kochin refer to Anju-
varam and Manigramara trade associations.®

Generally every guild had an executive, the strength of which
varied with its membership and activities." The reference of
these guilds is also found in the literary works like Yajnavalkya-

Soma-

1070, states that the king of Pratisthana named

other motives, the sea-voyages had their commercial purpose as

Let us now have an inquiry into the commercial relations of
India with other countries during the period.
Western Countries
The commercial relations of India continued with western
countries throughout the period.^ This is attested to by the
accounts of Arab travellers like MusudT, Ista-Khari and Alberuni
etc. Many of them prepared maps of Arab, India and the
countries falling in between these two Alberuni has given
in detail the description of Indian seas in his India which he
completed about A.D. 1030. According to him Indian sea
coast was extended from Tiz, the capital of Makran to south-east

upto Debal, Loharani (Karachi), Kachch, Soraanatha, Khambat,

Broach, Sandhan (Daman), Subara and Thana, After Thana

ships used to voyage to Simal via Jimur, Vallabha, Kanji and
then reached Rameshvara in Cholamandala.®
He further slates that this route was most dangerous as the
sea-pirates of Somanatha and Kachcha used to cause havoc
to tradesmen.^ Malabar also was, in those days, the key of
Hind, whose productions such as rubies, aromatics, grasses and
pearls were carried to Irak, Khurasan, Syria, Rome and Europe.
It had a great amount of foreign trade. Large ships, called in
the language of China ‘Junks’ brought various sorts of choice-
merchandise and cloths from China and Ma-Chin and the coun-
tries of Sindh.'*
In the 12th C.A.D., AHdrisi found Debal to be a station for
the vessels of China, and ships laden with the productions of

Uman. Baruch (Broach) was port for the vessels coming from

China, as also for those of Sindh.^ He also mentions the cotton

fabrics of Coromandal, the pepper and Cardamomes of Malabar,

and the lemons of Mansura on the ‘Mehram’ (Indus).^ According

to Abul-Feda, the merchants of Java could understand the
language of the natives of Madagascar.^ From the accounts of
of these travellers, we come to know about some of the important
seaports of India such as Debala in Sindh, Tej, Kalyana, Thana
and Bharoach on the western coasts. These were great commer-
cial centres. Besides, Banchi on Malabar coast and Pakar on the
Kolama and Koromandal coasts were the other important
seaports of this period.
Arab Dominion
By this time, the sway of Arabs extended probably all
over the western and middle Asia. Bagdad, Basara and Sikandria
were the chief industrial centres of Arabs. Kochaka, on the
Black-sea in Asia and Tarabajan in Russia were the most impor-
tant sea ports for the meeting of Muslim and Christian traders.
The foreign trade of the Arabs developed to a very great extent.
Their ships voyaged through the Mediterranean and the Red-sea.
The history 01 Indian commercial relations with the Arabs goes
back to the early centuries. With the advent of Islam the
maritime activities in Arab registered an unexpectedly great adva-
ncement. It was due to the growing relations with India that
several Indian words of maritime technology were adopted by

Arabs; for example the Arabian words ‘Var’ (shore) ‘doniz’

‘batzed’, ‘huri’ (a sma'I boat) and ’banai’ are taken from the

Sanskrit words bar’, ‘dongi’, Bera’, horit’ and ‘banika’ respec-
tively.® The Arabs attached great importance to India. Once
when Hazarat Omar wanted to know something about India
from a businessman, the latter replied “Her rivers are pearl,
mountains are ruby and trees are essence.’’
By the 7th C.A.D. the Arabs extended their raids upto the

western seacoasts of India for occupying Broach (Berigaza) and

India office”.
2. Reinaud’s, Abid-Fedda, ch. XXII,

Economic Condiiion 273

Thana. After becoming the ruler of Sindh (in 107 H), Zunaid-

bin-Abdul defeated king Jayasena in a naval warfare and occupied
Bhroach and Mandala.i Previously, these raids over the western
sea-coasts of India were nominal ones, but soon after the Arabs
launched a very forceful attack which ended in kingdom of
Valabhi. Alberuni tells that the man responsible for this catas-
trophe was Vallabhi’s own traitor who invited the Arabs to send
a squadron from Mansura against Valabhi .
2
This fact is evidenced
by the history of Arab as well. Under the comraandership of
Mulk, the Arabs launched a naval attack on Gujarat in H. 159,
and reached Barbud. According to Motichandra. the word
‘Barbud’ is the corrupt form of ‘Valabhi’. However, it is but certain
that their squadrons were comparatively more poweiful.^ But the
influence of the Arabs during the 8th and the 9th C.A D. was
confined only upto the sea-coasts of Sindh, Gujarat and Konkana.
The eastern sea-coasts were safe from these invasions and it was
from there that the Indians sailed their ships ceaselessly up to
China.* Suleman® tells us that the commercial relations between
Arab and China had slowed down for several reasons. Firstly , be-
cause the danger of sea-pirates increased day by day and secondly
because their ships were usually wrecked in the way and their
repair took months which left them with no alternative but to
dispose off all their goods there at considerably low rates. What-
ever may have been the reason, infers Motichandra, the commer-
cial relations of the Arabs were more, during the 9th C.A D., with
India, Malaya and Sindh than those with China. These relations
became still closer after the Arabs occupied Sindh. Thus the
Chinese trade was adversely affected. But by the end of the
10th C.A.D. Canton and Tsuan Chu regained that place as the
chief centres of Chinese foreign trade. Thus China re-established

direct commercial relations with Arab, Malaya, Faking, Siam,
western Sumatra and Boreo.i Though we do not know as to what
happened to the Indian trade with China during this period, it is
quite likely that alongwith the Arabs, Java, India had to confine,
for sometime, her trade with Malaya, Siam, Sumatra and Java.
After the post of Obulla was destroyed in the 256 H, Basra
became the centre of Arbian trade with India. The custom and
other duties from this port became a great source of Caliph’s
income. Yak-ut tells that the king of the Kaisa Island was held
in high esteem by the Indian kings as he possessed many ships.

Kazvini (H, 6161 states that Kaisa was a centre of the

Indian trade and also a port for Indian ships. Even the finest
of the Indian goods were carried there. Abu-zum-aid Saire-Fi

(9th C.A.D.) tells us as to why the Arabian ships did not go to

Egypt through the Red sea and returned to India from Zadda.
He states, “The Indian sea is full of pearls, her mountains and
forests have the mines of gold and gems, her animals have ivory in
their mouth, she produces cedar, cane 'zad’, camphor, cloves,
nutmeg, ‘bukkum, sandal and all the varieties of perfumed mate-

rials ; her birds are parrots and peacocks and musk is found even

in the excreta of her land.’’-
This highly developed stage of the Arab-trade added much
to the revenue of India in the form of imported duties.
Moreover, ir may also have inspired Indians to produce commo-
dities which were imported to Indian markets.
South Eastern Cowitries
From the accounts of Arab-travellers, inscriptions and leg-

ends (Kathasahitya) we hear about the highly developed comme-

rcial relations between Indian and the south-eastern countries.
The Arab travellers write that several sea ports such as Srivijaya
and Katah or Keda became the chief parts of foreign trade
among India, China and Arabs.’ Keda in those days was under
the dominance of the Salendtas of Suvarpadvlpa ^

The Arab travellers further tell us that from Keda port the
spices, sandal wood, tusks or elephants, cocoanuts and camphor
were imported and exported.
The inscriptions of the Cholas record the struggle for
supremacy between them and the Salcndras. An inscription of
Rajendra Chola from Tanjorc states his conquests over seas.
First of all, he got hold over the eastern coasts like Kalihga and
extended upto Bengal and thence after he conquered the south-

eastern provinces like Pandai (Pcnai,cast of Sumatra) Malaiyur

(modem Jambi) Mairudingam (Middle of Malaya), Ilangi-shoram

(probably the Isthumus of Ligor or Johorcs), Mappalam (west of

kras) Morvalimbagam), in the middle of Ligorc, Ballaipandura

(Panduranga or phanaranga). Tallaiteakkola (Takkala or

TakuwapaVport), Madamalingam (bay of Banadan, cast of

Malaya and in the middle of Sridharmaraja), Ilamuridesam (in

the north Sumatra) Nakkavaram (Nicobar) and Katah (modern

Keda) These statements of the inscriptions arc supported by the

legends (Kathasahitya) which also speak about several Islands

such as interpretation that India’s foreign trade with that south
eastern Islands developed to a considerable extent. During this
period, several Indian states remained in Indonesia and Indo-
china 1 Beside the Cholas, the people of Bengal and KaliAga also
extended their commercial relations with these south eastern
countries. Several canonical books written in Bengali script are
found in the Horivuzi monastery of Japan, These books, according
to prof. K.D. Bajapai, belong to the 11th C.A.D.^

Kambuja : (Cambodia)

During the period Kambuja also played an important role
in the propagation of Hindu culture. They adopted the Hindu
worship of gods and goddesses. Suryavarraa II, is referred to as
the performer of kotihoma, Laksahoma, Mahuhoma and such

other sacrifices. The theme of the conception of the temples of

Champa :

The Hindu kingdoms were in ascendancy in Campa. Some
political disturbances were caused by Anamese in the 11th C.A.D.,
but they were ultimately crushed by Hari-Varma. The chaos how-
ever, could not be pacified altogether and it finally extinguished the
Hindu colonies from there by the beginning of the 15th C A.D.,*
But the Hindu colonies were established in Burma and Siam etc.
Surarnadvipa
During the period the colonies of the Salendra kings
continued to exist in Sumatra, Java, Malaya and nearby
places. Arab-writers have referred to them as Maharahas. Albe-
runi writes that Java was known as Suvarnadvipa because its
islands were rich in gold.^ Prof. R.K. Mookerjee* confesses that
Indian businessmen of Java used to go abroad as far as the
Madagascar—situated in the south east of Africa. With the 11th
C. the wars for supremacy started between the Cholas and
Salendras. In 1030 A.D. only Java and some other islands, could
remain under the possession of the Salendras. After the death of
Rajendra-Chola in A. D. 1044 the Salendras may have reclaimed
their supremacy there.®
China
There existed during the period, economic, political and diplo-
matic relations between India and China. From the Chinese travel-
lers we come to know about the highly developed Indian foreign
trade with China even after the lOth Cent. A. D. Chu-Ku-Fai

(C. 1178 A. D.) states that in tlic trade of costly goods no race

could compete with the Arabs (Ta-Chi). Next to them were the

people of Java (SIuvpo) and the people of Palcmbeng (San-pho-

tst) respectively.^ Motichnndra presumes that the writer has

probably included the Indian traders among those of Java and
Polcmbcng.=

It is stated in the Ping-Chu*Ko-Tan (A. D. 1122) that the

ships named Ki-Ya-tu used to voyage continuously in the Chinese

seas. Sri Hearth idcniirvcs these ships with (he Katur ships

used in the Malabar coast. These ships of Kalikat (Calicut)

measured 60 to 65 hands and both their extremities were sharp-
edged.^ It is also stated in the Ping Chu-Ko-Tan that the bigger
ships which vo3'aged In the Kalihgn sea-coast could accomodate
several hundred people while the smaller ones had an accomoda-
tion for one hundred or few more persons only. These traders
selected from amongst themselves a head who was assisted by his
deputy in carrying on his business. It was his duty to prepare a
comprehensive list of goods of a fellow-traveller if he died in
the way. Moreover, he was empowered to indict mild punishment
like caning to a traveller held guilty of offending the rules."* These
tradesmen voyaged only when their ships were large enough and
the travellers were great in number because of the fear of the sea-
pirates. Moreover, asking for presents had beeomc a custom, and
consequently, a large quantity of goods had to be carried for this
purpose also. The traders, therefore, did not find it safe and
convenient to voyage in smaller ships ^ The Chinese manuscripts

tell us that the journey from China to Kanclii (Kanjivaram) a

town of southern India took 8 months. 1200 years before,
this distance was covered in about a year. Moreover, a large
quantity of Chincsc-silk, manakon, tea and pots were imported
in India.*

Export
We may gather from the accounts of the foreign travellers
like Periplus, Al-Idrisi, Marcopolo, Ibn-Batuta, Al-Masudi,
Bukhari, Abu-Zaid-Sairafi, Chau-ju Kua, Huddu-e-AIam, Musair-
bin-Muhalhil, Ibnul Fakih and Suleman, that India exported a
number of articles to foreign countries.
We come to know from Huddu-e-Alam> that India exported

to Arab gold and aloes (agar) of Kamrup, conch and ivory of

Orissa, Chilli of Malabar, shoes of Khambhat, turban-cloth of
Raevind, gems, linen, turbans and medicinal roofs of Kannauj and
musk of Nepal.

According to Musair-bin-MuhalhiF (331 H) the Gazair pots

of India were sold in Arab like China pots and other important
Indian articles sold there were teak, cane, timber wood, crystalline
sugar, benjoin cassia, aloes and camphor. According to Ibnul-

Fakih® (330 H) India and Sindh exported cosmetics, gems, dia-

mond, aloes, amber, cloves, sambuhkulanjan, cinnamon, cocoanut,
myrobalan, copperas, bakkam, cane, sandal, teak-wood and
black peppers to foreign countries. The Arabs used to
purchase Indian rhinoceros horns for sale in China where they
were used for manufacturing costly suit cases. Betel nuts were
also exported from India.
‘

Indian cotton'cloths were so line that even a full piece of cloth

(thana) could easily pass through a ring. Masudi and Bukhari®

admired the shoes of Khambhat and also the cloth of Thana which
were manufactured there, or were brought there for sale from
other parts of India. It appears, India exported printed cloth to
Egypt, samples of several such cloths have been found there. In

Import ;

—
According to Nadvi there was a great demand of gold coins
of Sindh in India, in the beginning of the 10th C.A.D. Emerald
rings were brought for sale in tastefully decorated bandoliers.
There was demand of coral and ‘dahanj’ and also of Egyptian

wine. Silken cloth, fur, ‘postin’ (a garment made of leather and

covered with wool) and swords were imported from Rumania,

rose-water from Persia and ‘dcpal’ and ‘dates’ from Basra. Arabian

horses were in demand in Cbolamandal.* Also, silk earthen pots

referred to by Periplus® and Marco-Polo* it is reasonable to infer
that with some exception they may have been imported in this
period too.
Currency
7he currency of the period consisted of coins of various
denominations, in gold, silver and copper. ‘Dramma’, ‘Suvarna’,
'gadyan* named, ‘Kalanju’ and ‘Ka^u’ were the principal coins.
The reason for the absence of these coins may be the inhuman
plunder of accumulated gold and silver in cities like Kanauj
Mathura and Somnatha by Sultan-Mahmud. This may have
adversely affected the currency system in northern India. Gold
had gone out of currency. Let us have an inquiry into the coins
which have been handed down to this period.
Dramma
According to Prof. K. D. Bajpai, various kinds of coins
were issued by different dynasties between 10th and 12th centuries.
But the popular name for all these coins, referred to in the
contemporary inscriptions and literature is ‘Dramma’.® The
same author writes that these ‘Drammas’ were of various
types. As they were issued by different dynasties the ‘Drammas’

bore different names such as ‘Panchayika Dramma; ‘Adivaraha

Dramma’, ‘Vigrahapala Dramma’ and ‘Kbataboddica Dramma’.®
‘Dramma’ is the Sankritised form of the Greek term ‘drachme’.
The silver coins of the Indo-Bactrian kings, weighing about
65 grains, were known by that name and we may presume that the
weight of the ‘drammas’ mentioned in the record was more or less

the same.i A.S. Altekar writes that the name ‘Dramma’ was given
to both silver and golden coins in the northern provinces, and the
silver ‘Dramma’ was about one third bigger than this coin for its
four anna silver piece weighed about 48 grains.^
Let us divide all the available coins of the period into two
parts i.e. the coins of northern India, and the coins of southern
India.
Northern-Indian-coius
The only available coins of the period from northern India
is ‘Dramma’, which is in gold. The credit of resurrecting this
gold coin goes to Kalachuri Gahgeyadeva, the ruler of Tripurl.s
His well-known coins exist in three metals, viz, gold, silver and
copper, and in four denominations namely, the ‘Dramma’,

(drachma), half Dramma, quarter Dramma, and one eighth

Dramma. The gold Dramma weighing about 62 grains is by far
the commonest. Smith found his specimen at Kachiva in Paragana
Rath of Hammirapur District, U. P."* The design of Gaageyadeva’s
model coin is very simple The obverse is wholly occupied by the
Raja’s name in bold characters not differing very much from
modern Nagari. The reverse type is a rudely executed figure of a
goddess seated cross-legged.®
The coinage of the Chandella dynasty of Bundelkhand is a
copy of the coinage of Gangeyadeva. The gold coins are exactly
the same as Gangeya’s except for the names. The copper coins
substitute ‘Hanuman’ for ‘Laksmi’. Both copper and gold
pieces follow the same scale of weights and are ‘Drammas’ or

'
He opines that these kings should issue an altogether new device
of sheathed dagger on their copper-coin. According to him the
coins have the goddess device of the coins of Gafigeyadeva, which
had been very popular in northern India and it was initiated by

the Chandellas, Gaha<)avalas and the Tomaras. The Mahakosala

Kalachuris have also followed the same tradition and copied this
device on their copper coins.’
The mint-masters of these kings, according to Mr. Gupta
are notorious for their crude art. The crudeness with which they
had engraved the Gajasardula had baffled the numismatists like
Cunningham, Hoernle and Rapson.® It is no wonder if
Mahamahopadhyaya Mirashi could not identify the crudely
executed LakshmI.

286 Bhoja Paramnra and bis Times
General Economic Condition of the People
In the above pages we have furnished sufficient evidence
regarding the developed condition of agriculture, industry and
trade in India during the period From these it is not unreason-
able to infer that a high level of economic prosperity prevailed
generally among the people concerned with those vocations. It is
indeed quite natural to think that this level varied. The opulence
of the northern Indian cities and the exceptional magnilicance of
their temples in the first half of the eleventh century are proved
by the accounts of the contemporary Muslim chroniclers.
If these accounts help us, though indirectly to realise the
wealth of certain geographical regions of northern India in the
first half of the eleventh century. A.D.', other foreign notices give
us- glimpses into the economic prosperity, by means of trade and
commerce, of the coastal regions of western and southern India in
the 12th and 13th Centuries.
The above account refers to the economic condition of the
people before the Muslim invasions began on a serious scale.
1. The Struggle for Empire, p. 527.
2. Ibid. p. 583. if.
CHAPTER IX
LITERATURE AND LEARNING
The era under review marked great development in the field
' of literature and learning. This was possible due to the bene-
volent contributions of the contemporary kings to the cause.
Several of the kings were themselves writers of repute. Scholars
and poets were patronised by the royal courts. The contributions
of Paramara Bhoja to the cultural activities of the age deserve
special notice. He was a great votary of the goddess of learning
and in reality the very pivot of the literary movement of the day.
He himself was a polymath and as many as 38 works are attribut-
ed to his pen.^ Merutunga tells us that Bhoja composed 104
poems.“ His bounty to men of letters was immeasurable. Thus
he took great interest in literary activities, patronised men of
letters and had a galaxy of erudite scholars in his court. It was
an age of scholastic elaboration and systamatic analysis of techni-
cal skill. Learning of commentaries, manuals and submanuals
was a significant feature of the day. Practically, all branches of
literature are well represented during this period and their volume
is also immense.®
The whole nation had a unity of culture, Prakrit Apabhr-
amsa, and languages closely allied to Sanskrit were understood
by the people. Sanskrit was the language of the cultured. It was
spoken and understood among the educated throughout the
country and was most prevalent to the east of Banaras.,"* But by
the end of the 10th century A.D. a decline of Sanskrit litera-
ture in poetry, drama and other branches was apparent. The
literature lost touch with the common men. It began to cater
mainly to the needs of the pandits and the courts. Thus it gradu-
1. Catalogus Catalogorum, pt. I., p. 488.
2. Pc. pp. 60-64.
3. The Struggle for Empire, p. 298.
4. Gujarat and its Literature, p. 55,
288 Bhoja ParamSra and his Times
ally tended to become stereotyped according to rules of poetics
and dramaturgy etc. There was an ever widening breach between
the languages of drama and those in every day use.
The political regionalism is reflected in the literature of the
period In spite of favourable efforts made by the kings the
cultural activity did not attain a national character It was
mainly due to the narrow outlook of the kings who lacked in
vigour, inspiration and originality. The kings never cared for the
quality of the work. They remained contented with their own
eulogies. It is mainly due to this that the whole range of literature
lacks originality. It is more initiative, artificial and laboured
instead of being natural and spontaneous. All this shows that the
creative age was over by the end of the 10th century A.D.
In spite of these shortcomings, many kings encouraged literary
pursuits. Bhoja the Paramara ruler of Dhara, himself an author,
was always surrounded by a crowd of scholars.^ Kalhana tells us
that both Kalasa and Bhoja were very learned and were friends of
the poets.^ Ain-I-Akbari states that as many as .‘00 sages, the
most erudite of the age, graced Bhoja’s court.® In the Udaipur
Prasasti. Bhoja is glorified with the title of ‘Kaviraja’®. This
patronising of scholars continued even in the succeeding centuries.
For instance, Visaladeva, the Tomar ruler of Delhi, not only pro-
duced but also patronised poetry. Kalhana, who wrote in C. 1149
A.D. the Rajatarafiginl, was the son of a minister of the Srinagar
court. The Gitagovinda of Jayadeva was composed, shortly
before the conquest of Bengal by Muslims. The Rajas'ekhara
graced the courts of two of the pratihara kings of Kanauj®.
Now let us make an inquiry into the main characteristics of
the literature of this period. The first thing which is most striking
was the large number of royal authors and patrons of learning.
Bhoja, Yasahpala, Somesvara, Kula^ekhara, Apararka, Ravi-

varma, Prnhladanadeva, Vigraliaraja (Visfiladcva) and Ballfila-

scna figure prominently among those who composed poetry,
drama, poetics and Dharma^astra literature. Another important
feature is the growing number of polymaths such as Ksemendra,
Bhoja, Hemachandra, and Ramachnndra, There were also ency-
clopaedic works like ‘Sfngaraprakasa, Manasollasa, Krlyakalpa-
taru and Chaturvargachintamaiiii, etc. The contribution of the
Jain monks, especially to the Kavya literature is considerable.
The regional survey indicates that the bulk of literary contri-
.bution comes from Kashmir, Malw.'i, Gujarat, Bengal and the
South occupying the next place. We shall now make a survey of
the different branches of literature.
\' Kavya
As already remarked, the poetry of this period lacks in
originality. Dr. Mchcndalc remarks, that it is artificial, unimagina-
tive and stereotyped but lofty, cultivated and exclusive ^ It was
apparently composed for an urban and sophisticated audience
and was out of touch with the common life and realities. Tendency
towards uniformity also rendered poetry of this period, a
monotonous reading. Whatever the shortcomings may have been
it is clear that almost every branch of poetry was enriched during
this period. We shall now deal with each branch of poetry one
by one.

(a) Mahaknvya

Ksemendra, the eleventh century polymath and courtpoct of
king Ananta of Kashmir, gives us the epitomes of the two great
epics in bis Bharatamafljarl. His Padya-Kadambarl is the
metrical rendering which describes the Ten incarnations of Vish-
nu. The life of the Buddha is told in the 9th canto wherein the
Buddha and Krishna legends have been mingled together.
Ksemendra was the son of Parusendra, and grandson of Sindhu.
His literary activity extended to the period of Kala^a, the son and
successor of Ananta - K5emcndra’s contribution to poetry,
poetics, and story literature is commendable indeed.
All sorts of poems such as devotional, didactic and historical

as well as anthologies were composed during this period.* Sambhu

wrote in the 11th. C.A.D. His Anyoktimuktalatasataka; is a

collection of 108 stanzas. The earliest anthology of this period is
the Subhasitavall of Vandyaghatlya Sarvananda.^
We hardly find any sober history in Sanskrit literature of the
period, even then there are some works which provide us with

—
Atula’s Miishika vamia
This is a work of 11th C. A. D. which narrates in 15 canto's
the line of kings ruled over the Mushika kingdom.
Rajendra Karnapura of Sambhu :
This is another historical work of 11th C.A.D. Sambhu
wrote this work in praise of his patron king Harsha.
SomapslavilSsa of Jalhapa :
Jalhaija wrote this woik to eulogize the king of Rajpurl.
11-Drama
A few legendary plays and court comedies were written
during this period. The Kashmiri polymath Ksmendra is the only
writer of this period. He speaks of a drama, ‘Chitrabharata’,
by himself, which has not survived.^ This along with ‘Lalitaratna-
mala’ is known only in the name.^
H. G. Rawlinson writes that the Rajput courts were great
centres of literature. The drama in particular, flourished and
found liberal patrons in many of the Rajas, some of whom were
themselves authors of plays.® The dramas were also staged during
this period in the royal courts. We have already referred to the
drama initiated by Pamara which was played in the courts of
Dhara.

292 Bhoja Pcramara and his Times
II-Grammar
The period after A D. 1000 marks the stage of deterioration
in the study of grammar. Even then, there are found some valu-
able works on grammar, belonging to this period. Among these
the ‘Sabdanusasana of Bhoja, otherwise known as Sarasvatlkan-
thabharana, is a popular and very successful compendium of all
grammatical rules in Sanskrit prevalent in his time. V.A. Rama-
svami Sastri considers it as a brilliant success among the initiators
of Panini.i The work is the substance of the author’s comparative
study of the unique trio of the grammarians, the commentators
and the initiators of Panini. It has eight adhyayas. It embraces
the Vaidika Prakaranas. In order to make his system easier,
Bhoja has quoted Paniniyan ‘Sutras’ at several places. Many of
his own ‘sutras’ contain the substance of the ‘sutras’ of Papini,
Katyayana etc.
The subjectwise arrangement of the ‘sutras’ into 'Adhyayas’
and ‘Padas, is as follows : —
AdhySya P3da Subject
1 1 SamjSas
ii 2. Paribhasas

VI i-iv) Sandhi, aluk-Samasa
