Prakriyas
Thus Bhoja has made the study of Paniniyan-'sutras’ much
easier.
A more commendable attempt of Bhoja is his justification
of such usages as would require grammatical sanction especially
1. Sarawoitkanjhabharana, Introduction, pp. XVIIl to XXII.
Literature and Learning 293
when they arc rejected as unscientific and incorrect by the
‘Bha§yakara’ and the ‘Vrittikara’, but which w'crc accepted by
great poets and other writers in Sanskiit.
The formation of words in alternate sense like in the

sense of n ’T{rW is explained by Bhoja in the opposite sense, of

deviations and departures from the ‘Bha§ya
and other texts arc the results of his over-anxiety of and care for
making his system up-to-date by incorporating all changes of
words, both phonetic and semantic from the times of those
‘Acharyas’ to his day. In this respect Bhoja has done a signal
service to the cause of Sanskrit language and its grammatical
literature.
Ill-— Poetics and Dramaturgy
Rrijanaka-Mahimabhalla, the son pupil of Mahakavi-Syaraala,
wrote Alamkara-Sarvasva. He flourished sometime between 1020
and 1060 A. D. The Alankara portion of the Agnipurapa which
belongs to this period , apparently follows a tradition different in
many respects from the orthodox systems.- Bhoja further develops
the definition of poetry given in the Agnipurfina by expressly adding
‘Rasa’ among its essential characteristics. In his SarasvatT-kap|.hfv-
bharana, Bhoja follows Agnipurana, Upama, Akshepa and Sama-
sokti etc. for figures both of Sabda and ‘artha’. He enumerates six
rltis — Vaidarahhi, Pdncliuli, Gaudtya, Avantiku, Lupka and Milgadhi.
He raises all the six Pramanas of Jainism to 'figures of speech’
and looks upon even ‘Gunas’ and ‘rasas’ as ‘alahkaras’. Though
he speaks of 8 rasas his way of treatment shows that he looked
upon ‘Srihgara’ as the only ‘rasa’. This he has expressly stated in
his ‘Srihgurapraka^a’ which has 36 ‘Prakasas’ and is the largest
known work on poetics. Us extracts have been quoted by Hcraa-
chandra and Saradatanaya, The first eight chapters deal with the
grammatical technicalities and the theory of ‘Vrtti,’ the next two
with the ‘mahakavya’ and the twelfth with the ‘drama’. The last
twentyfour chapters are devoted to ‘rasas’.®
1. Sarasvatlkaijthubliarana, vv.-2-226.
2. The Struggle for Empire, p. 321.
3. Ibid.
294 bhoja ParamSra and his fimei
Another work on poetics belonging to this period is Ksemen-
dra’s Auchityavichara-charcha. It contains ‘Karikas’ with his own
‘Vrtti’ and illnstrations called from various works. But this work
is not of much importance in the Alahkara-sastra.
No work on dramaturgy is found belonging to this period.
Metrics
Ksemendra of Kashmir who lived in the middle of the llth
C.A.D. is said to have composed a work on Sanskrit metres.^ No
other work of the period dealing with this topic is known to us.
Politics and Practical Life
Works on Politics or Dharmasastra were also composed.
Bhoja’s known works on this subject are Rajamartanda,
Yuktikalpataru, Vyavaharasamuccaya and Ratnavall.^ But all
these works deal little with the subject.
Medicine
Several treatises on medicine such as Bhoja’s Rajamrgahka,
Carucarya and Ayurvedasarvasva were composed. In Bhesajakal-
pasangraha is given the reference of Bhoja in context with
Charaka.’
The other contemporary well-known writer on medicine was

Chakrapanidatta (llth. C.A.D) the son of Narayana of Lodh-

ravali family of Bengal. He wrote Ayurvedadipika and BhanumatJ,
commentaries respectively on Charaka and Susruta. These are
important in the history of Indian medicine. He also wrote
Sabdachandrika, a vocabulary of vegetables and mineral sub-
stances and Dravyagunasaiiigraha, a work on dietetics.
Mathematics and Astronomy
The only known treatise written on Mathematics is Trisati of
Sridhara who is said to have been an eleventh century writer.^
But his exact time is not known.
1. The Struggle for Empire, p. 326.
2. Govt. Oriental Sanskrit Manuscript Library, Madras, Vol. 23,

Liicroturc and Learning 295
Various works were written on Astronomy. But after Bba-
skaracharya, no scholar contributed a thing of much importance.

To Bhoja arc ascribed many works on Astronomy; five of

them arc known to us. llicsc arc Rajamrgahka-Karapda,
Yidvajjana-Vallabha-PraSvnmjiiana, Adityapratfipasiddanta and
Bhuja-balanibandha. We shall deal in detail with each of these
works in connection with Bhoja’s contribution to literature.
The other eleventh century writer on Astronomy was
Satananda. To him is ascribed the Bhasvatl. Two chapters of
Siddhantasiromapi, viz. the Grahagapita and the Gola arc of
great importance in Astronomy. He is also said to have written
KarapakulQhaln a little later,* Besides, several treatises were
written on various other subjects.
Dharmainstra and Politics
Bhoja is said to have written Purtamurionda, Chdtiakya
RdjanltiiUstra, VyavahUra-Somuccaya^ CliUnicaryd, Vividlia-VidyS-

VkdracaUtrn and Siddhnntasdrapaddhati. His views (of Dhdresvara

or Dhojadeva) have been cited in various works on Dharmasastra,

It is not known whether his work on the subject was a commen-
tary or an independent digest.
The other writers on Dharmasastra, who flourished between
A.D. 1000 and 1100 were Jitendriya and Balaka of Bengal. They
arc known only through their references in the works of Jimutava-
hana, Raghunandana and Sulopani.
Jitendriya, wrote an extensive work on Dharmasastra. This
was completely eclipsed by the treatise of Jimutcavahana. Prof,
Kane thinks, with hesitation, Balarupa to be identical with
Bfilaka^ of cast Bengal. He wrote on several branches of Dharraa-
^astra such as Vyavahura and Prayaschittu. Halayudha, the
well known jurist is also said to have belonged to this period. He
is frequently quoted by Lakshmidhara, Chandesvara, Hariniitha,
Raghunandana MitramiSra and others. He was also a Bengali-
writcr. He wrote Brahraasarvasva and other works. He was the
1. The Struggle for Empire, p. 328.
2. KHDSl.V. 205.
Bhoja Paramara and his times
‘Dharmadhyaksha’ ofLakshmanasena. Thus we come to know
that the age was very rich in Dharma^astra literature.
Several works on ‘Yoga’, religion, music. Lexicography and
technical sciences were also composed. Bhoja wrote Yogasutra-
Vrtti on ‘yoga’ in which all kinds of oil and medicine are dealt
with. It contains 560 verses. He wrote a treatise on music But
its name is not known. His works on technical sciences deseive
notice. They deal with every extant technique. Among them
rank the Samaranganasutradhara, !§alihotronnaya and Yuktikal-
pataru.
As for Lexicography, Bhoja made successful attemps to write
Sanskrit ‘kosas’. Two of his works, namely the Namamalika and
gabdanusasanaare known as Sanskrit lexicons. On religion, Bhoja
wrote Tattvaprakasa,! Sivatattvaratnakalika^ and Siddhantasan-
graha, which deal with Saivism. We also find a commentary
named Aghorasivacharya’ on Tattvaprakasa.
Philosophy
The period marked a great philosophical movement in the
history of Hindu mediaeval India. It naturally influenced litera-
ture. There flourished philosophers who vrrote books on philoso-
phical aspect of literature. Srivatsa, for instance, wrote Lilavati,
which is a commentary on Prasastapada’s Padarthadharma-
Sangraha.^
Yadava Prakasa was another eleventh century philosopher.
He lived at Kanchl and was, for sometime, the teacher of
Ramanuja. He wrote an independent commentary on Advaita

learning. He holds that Brahman is changed into ‘Chit’ (spirit),

‘Achit’ (matter) and I^vara (God). According to Ramanuja, the

RfiraSnuja was born in A.D. 1017. He learnt ‘Vedanta' from

that almost all the branches of

Treatises on all branches of literature embracing
science,
technology, dharraasastra, scholastic texts,
grammar, poetics and
lexicons were composed during this period.
1 he net result of
all these is that from Blioja's time a new
form of poetry became
predominant, where emotion was less
important lluan ingenious
play on words, where fantastic
conceits predominated over poetic
images, the sound became as
important as the sense, and the
technique of poetry as important as its
matter, flic commentaries
stifled the thought of the original

science usurped the place of appeal to

the contribution of the age to
Sanskrit literature was immense
and multiform.
Apart from Sanskrit which was a notional language
spoken
throughout India, several other dialects

„,riod Rujalekhara. who nourished a litlle

limes. AS he was a widely travelled poet,

1. Bhojarnja, Chapt, VII.
Btioja ParamUra and his Timei
east to Banaras, spoke Sanskrit well but Prakrit badly.i A Gauda
could not speak Sanskrit properly. He, therefore, should have
either given up the attempt or improved his Prakrit. The Latas
hated Sanskrit but spoke Prakrit, fluently.^ The people of
Saurashtra and Travana spoke Sanskrit but mixed with Apabh-
ram^a to their speech. Kashmirians were good poets but their
recital sounded like a mouthful of ‘guduchi’^
The above description reveals the fact that at least Sanskrit,
Prakrit and Apabhrarhsa were spoken in different parts of India
during the 10th century A D. The same may have been the case
in this period too. We shall now make a survey of the popular
languages widely spoken all over India during this period.
1. Pa/i— The Bodhivaihsa or Mahabodhivamsa is one of the
popular works of the period. This is a chronicle written by
Upatissa whose date is assigned by some scholars to the 1st half
of the 11th. C.A.D. No other great work of this period in Pali
language is known to us.
2. Prakrits—Out of the important works in Prakrits is
Dhanesvara’s Surasundarl-charya written in AD. '038 in the
town of Chandravati. It is a big romantic work containing 4,000
‘gathas’ which are divided into 16 ‘parichedas’. The main story
narrates, in an essay style, the love affairs of a Vidyadhara chief.
Several other stones are also included within this framework.
4. Apabhramsa
The important works in Apabhrarhsa written during this period
reveal that they were originated by the Jainas. Most of them
cither reproduce the life-sketches of the Jaina teachers and heroes
or simply narrate the tale-preaching practices of the Jain religion.
We shall deal with some of the important Jain works hereinafter.

(a) The KathSkoia : It is written in poor Sanskrit with

Prakrit verses by Srichandra. The work was composed either in

(b) The PSrsvapttriina of PadmakTrti: This Is another

Apabhram^a work which was probably in the lOth or lUh C.A D.
It describes the eighteen sandhis and the life of the twentythird
TirthaPkara.

(c) Sudarsanacharita : This was written in A.D. 1043, by

Nayanandl, who was the pupil of the Manikyanandi of
Kundakunda line of teachers. The work narrates, in twelve
sandhis, the life of a Jain hero.
A review of the above works shows that the Apabhramsa
continued to exist throughout the period, 'Jhc birth of these
vernaculars or new Indo-Aryan speeches, which gradually develop-
ed in their own respective areas, was the most important cultural
event of this period,
4. Bengali
The Aryan languages of the eastern India comprise the
the three Bihari speeches, viz. Bhojapuri, Maithili and MagadhT.
Ihc latter two arc very close to each other. Some people regard
them as dialects of the same language. It is also said that Oriya,
Bengali and Assamese originated from the Magadhl-Prakrit.
These languages had become fully established by A.D. 1000.
5. Kannada
As there was a literary activity in the Tclgu country, the
Kannada literature remained undeveloped throughout the period.
Some old-talcs and songs of the tribal legends may be attributed
to the period between A.D. 1000-1300.
5. Kannada
As there was a little literary activity in the Tclgu country,
the Kannada literature remained undeveloped throughout the
period. Some old-ialcs and songs of the tribal legends may be
attributed to the period between A.D. 1000-1300.
6. Gujarati
Preservation of neuter and some other traits in modern
Gujarati suggest an unbroken linguistic tradition. Also parts of
3^6 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
the west Rajasthnna and west Gujarat were known between the
8th and the lUh centuries, as “Gujarat” or ‘Gurjaratra’.' It
would, therefore, be more appropriate to call the language of that
area by the name ‘old Gujarati’ instead of old western Rajasthani’
as coined by the late I.P. Tessition,^
educational system
The extensive literature of India is the visible product of a
rational system of education, which had no parallel in the history
of the ancient world. In India, the importance of education was
realised from very early times, and utmost emphasis was laid
upon the acquisition of knowledge. The educational institutions
were multiform and several in number. In their simplest form
they were the gatherings of one or more students in the house of
a teacher. The students were brought up by the teachers as
members of their family. They too looked upon the teacher and
his wife as their father and mother. As regards tuition fees, the
practice varied from case to case. In some cases no fees was
charged except something voluntarily presented at the end. In
other cases the sons of rich men paid a lump-sum amount to
teachers as honorarium at the commencement of their study,
while the poor students performed menial services in the teachers’
houses in lieu of paying fees.’
A new form of educational system was introduced during
this period. The system of education as outlined in the Dharma-
sastras and Nibandhas fails to convey an adequate idea of
imparting instructions to students of different categories in the
eleventh century. A.S. Aliekar writes that the teacher did not
figure among the grain-sharing servants of the community.* It
shows that the community at large did not regard the primary
teacher as essentially necessary for the ordinary villager. It is very

Literature and Learning 301
probable Ibat only the children of the Brahmans and the trading
cla'^scs eared to be literate. The arrangements for educating them
could not obviously be made at the cost of the whole community
by assigning the village teacher a grain share from every villager.
The highly skilled architects of huge temples, the sculpturers
of beautiful images, the Jewellers of excellent diadems and
ornaments, the learned physicians, and chemists excited the admi-
ration of Albcruni.^ These skilled men must have acquired their
technical knowledge through education.
Metallurgy and Chemistry were also taught. There was an
extensive literature on ‘Vilstuvidya, dealing with the principles of
Indian architecture. Atleast two important works on the subject,
the ‘Samarrihgana-sutradhara’ and the 'Isana-Siva-guru-Paddhati',
were composed respectively in the northern and southern India
during the lllh C.A.D, These arc written in difficult Sanskrit.
The architects therefore must have had a good knowledge of
Sanskrit.
We have already seen that the number of ‘Sudras’ swelled in
the period. Most of them earned their livelihood through the
professions which were monopolised by the 'Vaisyas' in the earlier
periods. The belter class of Sudras, and especially persons
engaged in trade and industries, may be presumed to have
an elementary knowledge of reading, writing and arithmetic.
Primary Education
The records of this period arc almost completely silent about
the methods of primary education. Bhojaprabandha,^ however,
giver a meagre information in this regard. Usually, the education
started at the early age of five. The initiation of a boy into
1. When Albcruni failed to learn the science of Alchemy and
preparation of a compound with gold, he observed “the
adepts in this art try to keep it concealed and shirk back
from intercourse with those who do not belong to them.
Therefore, 1 have not been able to learn from the Hindus,
which methods they follow in this science and what element

they principally used.’’ (Ed. Sachau Vol I. p, 187).

2. Bhojaprabandha, p. 6.
302 Bhoja Paramnra and his Times
reading and writing tock place on an auspicious day.^ The young
pupil first saluted the deities like Hari, Lakshmt, Sarasmti, Vidyn
of his family and the Siitrakara as of that particular ‘Vidya’ and
the ‘Vidya’ of his choice.®
Mediaeval digests prescribed disciplined life for the students
who would receive Brahmanical education. The system of begging
for daily food, rules of cleanliness, observance of ‘sandhya’,

‘upasana’ (twilight prayers), tending of fire, restraint of sense

organs and other rules for the Brahraacharin continued to be
observed and are mentioned by Lakshmidhara ®
As regards the higher education, several educational institu-
tions were set up for the cultural development of the people. It
is believed that Bhoja founded at Dhara his famous university
called the temple of Sarasvati’ the ‘Sarada sadana’ or ‘Bharati-
bhavana’, where lived a host of scholars who must have helped
Bhoja in his literary activity. This ‘Bhojasala at Dhara, was a
meeting place of great poets, of scholars proficient in the three
Vedas, of critics and accomplished men possessing great apprecia-
tion and taste coming from all quarters * This university building
was later on converted by Muslims into a mosque, which is now
kno,vn as ‘Kamal-maula’ mosque at Dhara. The tradition is
strongly corroborated by the discovery of two charts on the walls
of that building, which contain alphabets and rules of grammar.'
They are very important, inasmuch as they give us an idea of tfie
method of teaching that was pursued in those days. The first
is sketched out by the windings of a single serpent. On its body

The second chart is formed by the intertwining of two serpents.

Through the strenuous cflorls of all these institutions, opines

India."*
Beside Malwa, the other known educational centres were at

Kashmir, )'aranasi and Kmmuj. Albcruni refers to all these

CENTRES. He states that the towns of Varanasi and Kashmir
were the high schools of Hindu sciences and the country all around
Kanauj, called Aryavarta, was also known for its learning
centre.''
Monastic Estahlishmcnir
During this period, there were several notable monasteries
imparting education. A.S, Altckar suggests that the arrangements
for the higher education were made in three places, viz. ‘Maths’
which were associated with temples endowed by the state or
private charity, ‘Agraliiiras' which consisted of the villages granted
to Brahmana settlements, and the especial educational institutions
conducted by private individuals or village communities with the

Many original books and commentaries were written in the
monasteries. At Sarnath, a devoult MahSyana-Buddliist lady
named Mamka compiled a copy of the ‘Ashtasharika’ in a
monastery known as ‘Saddharma-chakra-pravartana-Vihara’ in
K 5810/10'82 A.D, The heads of these ‘Viharas’ tried to impose
a rigid control over students and teachers. Merutunga quotes an
adage to the effect th tt for one who holds office in the state for
three months or functions as an abbot of a monastery for three
days, for him hell is certain. =>
Matha
We do not find any direct evidence of the existence of
‘Mathas’. But on the ground of some earlier and later records,
we may conjecture that similar may have been the case in our
period too. An inscription, dated 975 A.D. records the grant
of 50 ‘matters’^ of land for the ‘matha’, where students were
fed and taught.® The other inscription at Jatiga-Rameivara
hill in Chitaldurg district, dated C. 1064 A.D. records 50
‘matters of land to the Rameivara temple for defraying the
expenses of temple worship, and for imparting education.®
The Chinese travellers also tell us that the Buddhist monasteries
used to attend to the t'-aining not only of the monks, but also of
the children of the laity.’ Indirect help to the cause of education
was given by some of the temples, which used to give free food

306 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
informs us that when the college hall built in A.D. 945, crumb-
led down it was rebuilt by a local feudatory in the next century.1
From Chittarajadeva’s grant, dated A.D. 1024,= we learn
that there existed scores of 'agrahara’ villages in the period.
These villages were given to Brahamana donees, who were engaged
in the six scriptural duties such as learning, teaching etc A S.
Altekar conjectures that these villages must have been maintaini-
ng educational institutions.^
Sometimes, ordinary villages also had their own schools and

colleges; one institution for Sanskrit education existed at Belur

in BIjapur district in C. 1022 A.D.‘ another during C. 975-10-
75.® and the next one at Yewoor in A.D. 1077® and so on. The
literary activity of the Jains also was remarkable. They seem to
have taken an active part in the education of the masses. That
before the beginning of the alphabet proper, the children should
be required to pay homage to ‘Gape^a’ by reciting the formula
'Sriganesaya ramalj’, is natural in Hindu society. A.S Altekar
writes that in the Deccan even today it should be followed by the
Jain formula ,om namassiddhebbyab’.’ C.V. Vaidya points out
that the Jain teachers of this age had so completely controlled the
mass education, that the Hindus continued to teach their
children this original Jain formula even after the decline of
Jainism.'
Thus a summary of the above statements reveals the fact
that all the educational institutions were financed partly by the
state and partly by the private charity. The ‘agrahara’ village
institutions were indirectly financed by the stale. The state
alienated the village revenues to Brahmapas, who being freed from

literature and Learning 307
the anxiety of their maintenance, could devote their energies to
the cause of education. The ‘Mahfijans* of Bclur had granted in
1022 A.D., 12 ‘matters’ for the purpose of feeding and clothing
the local siudcntsd These institutions were the centres of higner
education. They employed the staff, provided lodging and
fcoding to the students and arranged the salaries for teachers.
These facilities roust have tended learned men to adopt the
teaching profession. Also, ns there were arrangements for feeding
houses, even the common man could afford to get his children
admitted in the institutions. This inexpensive and easy process
of education must have removed the illiteracy of the country to a
large extent. As in the south, these institutions may have existed
in the north as well for the purpose of imparting education.
In the last reference should be made to the Muslim impact
on education. The destructive fury of the early Muslim con"
querors gave an irreparable blow to the indigenous learning in
the territories brought within their sway. Albcruni writes about
the disastrous consequences of the invasion of Sultan Mahmud of
Ghazni. He states “Hindus” Science have retired far away from
those parts of the country that have been conquered by us and
fled to places which our hand cannot yet reach, to Kashmir,
Banaras and other places”^
Courses of Study
We shall now consider the popular subjects for studies during
the period. The extensive literature, as had already been discussed,
tends us to believe that higher education meant Sanskrit

education. ‘Veda’, ‘Vyakarana’ (Grammar) Jyoti?a’ (astronomy

and astrology), ‘Sahitya’ (literature) ‘Mlmamsa’ Dharma^astra’,

Puraija’ and Nyaya’ (logic) were the main subjects which were

widely studied. According to Manasollasa’ various branches of
literature e.g. the ‘Vedas’, logic, the ‘Dharmasastras’, bcllcs-lctter
and grammar were widely studied. Besides the princes were also
instructed in military science,* but it appears from the reference
1. /./(., X, p. 129.
2. Sachau, I, p. 22.
3. Manasollasa III 1283-1304,
308 Bhoja ParamSra and his Times
made in the context of princely education that the study of these
subjects was meant only for the princes and the rich and not for
the masses.
Proficiency in the ‘DarmaSastra’, thinks A.S. Altekar, must
have been regarded as a passport to government service in the
judicial branch.' It may thus be presumed that the study of this
subject was more popular than that of the ‘Vedas’. A record of
some earlier period shows that the term ‘vedic study’ did not mean
only the cramming up of the vedic ‘Mantras’. In some centres
the meaning was also studied as the title ‘Vedarthada’ implies.®
The Voluminous works on Astrology and Astronomy as shown
previously, make us believe that these subjects were studied widely.
We have already seen that even the royal courts maintained
Astrologers. Mathematics was in a very advanced stage. Alberuni
writes, ‘‘The Hindus do not use the letters of their alphabet for
numerical notation, as we use the Arabic letters in the order of
Hebrew alphabet. As in different parts of India the letters have
different shapes, the numeral signs too, which are ‘anka’ differ.
The signs which we use are derived from the finest forms of the
Hindu signs- Signs and figures are of no use if people do not
know what they mean, but the people of Kashmir mark the single
leaves of their books with figures which look enke drawings
or like the Chinese characters, the meaning of which can only be
learned by a very long practice. However, they do not use them
when reckoning in the sand. In Arithmetic all nations agree that

all the orders of numbers (e.g. one, ten, hundred, thousand) stand

in a certain relation to the ten, that each order is the tenth past
of the following and the tenfold of the preceding”. He further
says, “I have found that no nation goes beyond the thousand....
those, however, who go beyond the thousand in their numeral
system are the Hindus. They extend the names of the orders of
numbers until the 18th order for religious reasons, the mathema-
ticians being assisted by the grammarians with all kinds of
etymologies. The 18th order is called ‘Parardha’. i e. the half
of heaven or more accurately the half of that which is above
For if the Hindus construct periods of time out of Kalpas, the

On numeral notation, Alberuni writes, “the Hindus use the
numeral sipns .Arithmetic tn the same way as we do. Possibly,
they arc ahead of iis in this subject. They compose their books
in ‘Slokns’. If, now, they wish in the astronomical hand books,
to express some numbers of the various orders, they express them
by words used to devote certain numbers either in our order

alone or at the same lime in two orders (eg a word meaning

either 23 or both 20 or 200). l-or each numbers they have appro-

priated quite a great quantity of words’’.^
Writing Materials
From Alberuni we come to know various kinds of writing
materials used in those days. He accounts “The Hindus are not
in the habit of writing on hides like the Greeks in ancient times.
Muslims, too, used in the early times of Islam to write on hides.
It was in China that paper was first manufactured. Chinese
prisoners introduced the f.ibrication of paper in Samarkand, and
thereafter it was made in various places’’.®
Alberuni categorically describes the writing materials used in
different parts of India. He writes, “The Hindus have in the
south of their country a slender tree like the date and cocoanut

palms bearing edible fruits and leases of the length of one yard (?)

and as broad as three lingers one put beside the other. They

call ihese leaves ‘tari’ (Tala or Tar-Borassus ilabcliformis), and

write on them.
1. Sachau, I, p. 174.
2. Ibid,, I, p. 177.
3. Ibid., I, p. 170.
310 Bhoja Paramaro and his Times
In central and northern India people use the bark of the
Tuz-tree a kind of which is used as a cover for bows. It is called
‘bhuija’. They take a piece one yard long and as broad as the
out-stretched fingers of the hand or somewhat less, and prepare
it in various ways. They oil and polish it so as to make it hard
and smooth and then they write on it. The proper order of the
single leaves is marked by numbers Their letters and whatever
else they have to write they write on the bark of the Tuz-tree.”‘
We may thus conclude that usually the palm-leaves of difiisrent
trees and the paper were used by Indians for writing-purpose.
People were well adept in the art of book-binding as well.
Alberuni states, “The Hindus bind a book of these leaves together
by a cord on which they are arranged, the cord going through all
the leaves by a hole in the middle of each The proper order
of the single leaves is marked by numbers. The whole book is
wrapped up in a piece of cloth and fastened between two tablets

of the same size. Such a book is called ‘puthi’ (Pushta,

pushtaka)”*

We actually do not know whether the modern type of
ink was used for writing purpose. But from Alberunis’ writings
we certainly get an idea that some sort of ink was definitely
known to the people. He writes, “they use black tablets for the
children in the schools and write upon them along the long side,
not the broad side. But writing with a white material was from
the left to the right’’. One would think that the author of
the following verses had meant the Hindus. “How many a writer
uses paper as black whilst his pen writes on it with white colour
as charcoal. By writing he places a bright day in a dark night,
weaving like a weaver but without adding a woof.’’® This
description of Alberuni snows that paper was also used for
writing purposes though rarely. The black and white tablets
referred to by him, may have represented modern black-ink tablets
and white chalk respectively.

The above statement of Alberuni is also attested to by the
epigraphic records of the period. Almost all of them are found
written in one script re. ‘Devanagari’. The alphabet of the
Betma plates of Bhojadeva dated, V S. 1076, observes D.B
Diskalkar, “is ‘Nagari’ of the eleventh century A. D., prevalent
in Malwa as is known from other records of Bhoja e.g. Banswara
plates etc”. He further gives some of the odd features of the
alphabetical arrangements made in the record. In fact writes he
There is nothing to distinguish between the letters a" and u

The alphabet ir is wrongly replaced by n (atTraiTiPiT 1. 23) Similarly

tr is wrongly replaced by "t in siraaaT (]. j6) and in marsit (1.25).

A consonant following ‘r’ is generally doubled, eg. in tmmr

(1 1.) Conjunct consonant is generally expressed in this inscrip-

tion by the full letter added below the first consonant (e g Tra

11.8; 14 and 15; dra 1.5; bhra 11.8.9; gra 1.9 e‘c), and not by a

stroke turned to the left as we generally find. The letter ‘Kri’

in the two cases where it ocurs (ll.l and I6)is written imperfectly.

The left portion of the letter Ka being omitted. The document
is drawn in Sanskrit prose mixed with verse ^ Also the inscriptions
of the Ujjain plates" and Banswara plates^ of Bhojadeva are written
in Devanagari script. Likewise the inscriptions of the Kalvari

plates of Yasovarman" and the Arthuna (in Rajputana) of Arjuna

Varman" etc are all written in the same script.
Another characteristic of the lettering is visible in Alberuni’s
account. He remarks,... “ihe Hindu alphabet is explained
firstly, by the fact that they express every letter by a separate
sign, if It is followed by a vowel or a dipthong or a 'hamza

(visarga), or a small extension of the sound beyond the measure

of the vowel, and secondly, by the fact that they have consonants

which arc not found together in any language”.^ This proves
that the lettering of the age was well planned and the opportune

economising of time and space in lettering by indications or signs
was the special characteristic feature. This corroborates that the
lettering of ilu- crti wa.s most scientific.
The contemporary records show that during this period there
existed a unity of the langnoge also. In this connection Alberuni

states. '•Hindus like other pc -(pie. boast of the enormous range

of their hinguagc. whilst in reality, it is a defect”,- Whatever
may have been the reality, it is certain that there existed much
unity of hugnage, ATicniai farther writes “the language is
divided into a neglected s'ernacultir one, only in use among the
cornm.m-pcoplc, and a classical one, only in use among the upper
and educated chssc.s, which is much oaltivatcd and subject to
the rules of gra.mmutical inllcctions and etymology and to all the
niceties of grammar a.nd rhetoric”.^ Albcruni’s statement, thus
show.s that lite language of the educated differed from that of the
uncduc.ucd ns the former was based on more grammatical
rules.
The language of the period was well-bound by the prescribed
grammatical rules sucli as. noun, verb etc. Alberuni remarks
“the Hindus magnify the nouns of their language by giving them
the feminine gender, as the Arab.s magnify them by the diminu-
ti\c form.”^
A quite diiYercnt fashion w.is prevalent regarding the title of
a book. Alberuni writes that the Hindus used to write the title
of a book at the end of it and not at the beginning.^ This i.s also
testified by the works of Bhoja each of which contains its title
at the end such as . . . “Here ends the Rajamartapda of Bhoja
the treatise on Astronomy”," and so on.

314 Bhoja ParamSra and his Times
It has been a nice and novel practice of the Indian writers
throughout the ages that they begin their books with some
benedictory verses in favour of their favourite deify. During
this period too, this practice was widely common Alberuni
writes ‘The Hindus begin their books with ‘om’ the
word of creation as we begin with in the name of God. The figure

of the work ‘om’ is ‘2’ This figure does not consist of letters ; it

is simply an image invented to represent this word which people
use believing that it will bring them a blessing meaning there-
by a confession of the unity of God. Similar to this is the
manner in which the Jews write the name of God, viz., by three
‘Hebrew Gods’. In the Thora the word is written ‘UHUH’
and pronounced ‘Adonai’. Sometimes, they also say ‘Yah’.
The word ‘Adonai’, which they pronounce is not expressed in
writing”.'
A survey of all the literary activities of the age tends us
to believe that the era tinder review, was very prosperous in the
field of literature. Specially Sanskrit made a considerable
progress.
CONTRIBUTION OF BHOJA TO INDIAN LITERATURE AND
LEARNING
Bhoja’s contribution to the literature and learning is rich and
varied. He promoted and accentuated the literature of the period
at different stages. Himselt a versatile writer, on various subjects,
he can be ranked among the great polymaths like Ksemendra of
Kashmir and Hemachandra of Gujarat. Being a devout worshipper

of the goddess of learning (Sarasvafi), Bhoja gave his whole-

hearted patronage to the men of letters. His mastery over Sanskrit
and Prakrit was unchallenged. The age of creative art is said to
have been closed with Bhavabhuti and that of scholarship with
Raja^ekhara. The age of commentary and compilation began with
this encyclopaedic author.
A study of all the works of Bhoja would reveal the unity of
authorship of at least some of the works which reflect an equal
sweep and richness in the style of the royal author. Eightjfoiir
I. Sachau. I, p. 173.

K. M. Munshi* and D.C. Ganguly® doubt about Bhoja’s
authorship of all the works listed above. According to them,
some of these works might have been written by a group of lite-
rary men under his guidance. On the other hand, prof. Ayyangar’
surmises that Bhoja himself had written all these works.
He writes, ‘it is stated that one man, and that a king who was
engaged in many vsars, could not have found time to write so
many works Hindu scholars can easily understand how this
is easily possible. In India from boyhood scholars are trained
to carry all books they read in their memory, so that when they
in their turn begin to write works, they need not spread on their
tables all the books that they have learned on the subjects.
Drawing from the Inexhaustible stores of memory, they can
write new books especially on the ‘Sastras’ at a rate so fast that
the forefinger cannot understand their speed of composition.”
Sri Kashinath Krishna Lele clarified doubts of the scholars in a
beautiful manner. *
' According to him, it is not beyond human power and
wisdom to be able to write as many books in one’s life-time as
Bhoja could. No doubt, Bhoja was the master of a rich and
a well extended empire. But none of the Sanskrit scholars of the
past, has ever raised the doubt that Bhoja exercised his power to
get any of these volumes written by the learned ‘pundits’
patronised by him.
Kalhana has written in his ‘Rajataraftgim’, the following
‘sloka’ in praise of two kings, Kshitipati of Kashmir and Bhoja
of Malwa.

The other details have already been discussed elsewhere.' Bhoja
composed the SriAgaramanjari, a unique prose work, in which a
hetara instructs her daughter in twelve attitudes towards her
clients. The great work on architecture, the Samararlganasutra-
dhara deals with the art of constructing cities, palaces and
mansions. The Yuktikalpataru is a treatise on the ‘Dharma^astra.
It also contains sufficient matter related to the SilpaSastra. All
these works are treated as authoritative and have been quoted
by several authors.
The Rajamartanda also known as Bhojavrlti, is a commenta-
ry on Patanjali’s Yoga^utra. In the very introduction of it, Bhoja
compares himself with Patanjali-Muni.® TTie book ends with
?ffr Trspii^o^iniTErrqf
II
It clearly proves that Bhoja, the Dharadhipatl himself wrote
this work. It contains 1421 verses in all and deals with the
astronomy.
The other astronomical work of Bhoja is Rajamriganka.’
The starting year of the book seems to be §aka 964, as borne

out by a verse. Unfortunately only H hand-written ‘Patras’ (or

28 pages) have been discovered so far.*

Vidvajjanavallabha' is the next astronoinical work of Bhoja.
It comprises of 17 'adhyayas’ such as ‘SubhaSubha-adhyaya’,
Satrusamanadhyaya, Gamanagamanadhyaya, and ‘Prositadhyaya
etc.
Bujabalanibandha* also deals with the astronomy. It contains

Sarasvatlkunjhnbharana, is a work on ‘Alamkara’. It contains
8316 verses and 5 ‘Parichchedas’, The work describes the merits
and demerits of Kfwya. Sabdalariikara, Arthalamkfira, Ubhayala-
mkara and kinds of Ragas etc.* Bhoja’s two works ‘Sarasvatl-
'katjthabharana’ on grammar and on poetics, remarks Mr. Josyer,
‘rank him among the great kings of the science of grammar and

the science of poetice.”=(a)

As has already been noted the SrihgfiraprakilSa of Bhoja
is a work on poetics. It comprises 36 ‘Prakadas’, such as Prakr-
tyadi, Pratipadika, Prakrtyudi^abda and Kriyadyarthachatus-
taya etc.° In this work, the main importance is attached to
‘iSrihgara-rasa.’
Dr. C.P, Ramaswami Iyer writes ’ in the 'Srihgfira Praka^a’,
the aim of Bhojaraja has been comprehensive. His discriptions
and analysis of the means of poetics arc original, and he takes
the line that ‘SriAgara Rasa is the substratum of all literary
beauty. But his conception of ‘^hrihgara’ is not that of love as
understood in western poetry, but is essentially transcendental in
character. Srihgaraprakasa is in essence an encyclopaedia of
Indian thought and Indian locution.*
Mr. Josyer in the very prologue of the Srihgaraprakasa’ has
truly remarked, “this is Bhojaraja’s magnum opus.” He further
adds, “only in India could a work of this kind remain unpub-
lished for nearly a thousand years. Elsewhere writings are rushed
to print before the ink is dry on the paper.®

' The lafe Prof. S.K. Kuppuswami observes, “Sringara
Prakasa’ is a hitherto unpublished work on Alarhkara of an
encyclopaedic character. In this monmoth work king Bhoja endea-
vours to bring under literature and literary ' appreciation every-
thing of importance in Indian thought as embodied in Sanskrit
writings both shastraic and non-shastraic and the method which
Bhoja’s massive mind adopts for this purpose is eclecticism of an
all comprehending type.'
In the words of Dr. V. Raghavan Bhoja’s Srihgaraprakasa
in 36 chapters, running to 190? pages in foolscape size in manus-
cript, is one of the biggest works in Sanskrit-literature, and the
biggest in the whole field of Alankara ^astra.- Besides Bhoja’s
eontnbuticn to Sanskrit poetics, a thousand things of interest with
reference to almost all branches of Sanskrit learning be scattered
in the 1908 pages of this magnum opus of king Bhoja.°
Dr. F.O. Schrader of Kiel, Germany, wrote, “'Glancing of
!5ringara Prakasa, I find that Ibis in spite of all defects is a very
useful publication which will be helpful for the textual criticism of
the classical Kavyas and dramas as well as on many other
occasions.^
Dr. Ganganath Jha was lyrical while appreciating this work '
The Charuchaiyii® deals with the daily routine of a man. The
Siddhantasarapaddfaati is written in poetry mixed with prose
containing 1384 verses.® The Samariihgana-Sutradhara,’ as has
.already been pointed out, is a work on Architecture. It comprises
83 adhyayas and 7000 verses. The total number of verses in the
'Sfuktikalpataru is 2016.®

The ChampQramfiyana has been published with Ramachandra
Budhendra’s commentary. The first five ‘kandas’ are said to have
been written by king Bhoja while the sixth one i. e. 'Yuddha-

(war) kapda’ is attributed to Laksmanasuri, Various commen-

taries were written on the above work. The prominent among
them arc the Sahitya-manju?^ of Ramachandra Budhendra, the
commentary written by Karunakarat and Vidvat-Kautuhala, a
commentary, probably written on Lankakapda, by Kamc^vara-
SQri.=
In the words of C.P. Ramaswamy Tycr, “It should be super-
fluous on the part of any student of Sanskrit to comment on
Maharaja Bhojadeva, whose marvellous gifts of compression,
verbal felicity, and depth of meaning have been illustrated
in his Champu Ramayapa, which is one of the musts for all
Sanskrit students.”^ Mr. Josyer writes, “Bhoja’s ‘Champu
Ramayapa.”*
The two ‘Prakrita-Kavyas’ named Kurmasatakas were found
engraved on a stone pillar at Dhar in November, 1903. Each
of them contains 109 Arya metres.® Both the parts begin with
“Om Namah-Sivaya”. In between the beginning of the second
and the end of the first book is mentioned the name of Maharaja-
Paramc^vara Bhoja.® The total number of lines contained in
both the books added together is 83. Barring only some of the
words lying between the lines 26 and 38, the contents of both the
books arc still intact and clearly legible.

were the contemporaries of Bhoja. Some of the prominent poets
and authors like Kalidasa, Subandhu, Mayura etc. are assigned
to the court of Bhoja. But this is not correct.
Ksildasa
Kalidasa, the great Dramatist and poet of Sanskrit litera-
ture, flourished in the Gupta period. Sri Kashinath Krishna
Lele writes that it is probable that some Kalidasa graced the
court of Bhoja. But it is not certain who this Kalidasa was and
how many books he wrote. According to Lele, he may have been
the namesake of the great poet Kalidasa.* Padmagupta, the
author of the Navasahasankacbarita has also been referred to as
Kalidasa. Moreover, in the Suktimuktavali and Haravall, there
is quoted a verse of RajaSekhara, which tells us that before his
time i. e. V.S. 957 —A.D. 900, there flourished three Kalidasas.*

Subandhu :

He wrote a book named ‘Vasavadatta’. He flourished before
Bapabhatta, as is proved by the evidence of the Harshacharita
where BSna has quoted Subandhu.

Mayura :

In the commentary on Bbaktamara written by Manatunga-
charya.’ and the Prabandha-Chintamani, Mayura is referred to
as the contemporary of Paramara king Bhoja. It is interesting to
note that at one place Merutuhga refers to him as Bapa’s brother-
in-law. Moreover, in the Jain-works MayQra is referred to as
Bapa’s father-in-law. Besides, a verse of Raja§ekhara quoted in
the Sarngadharapaddhati, tells us that Bapa and Mayura were
contemporary poets in the court of Harsha.* There are several

other instances to show that Mayura was Bana’s contemporary

Poet M3gha
Both Ballala and Mcrutunga associate the great poet Magha
with the court of Bhoja. Jaina Prabhachandra also describes in his
Prabhavakacharita* that Magha, the writer of SiSupalabadha was
the grand-son of Suprabbadeva, who was the Prime Minister of
King Varmalata. The king Varmaliita reigned sometime in the
7lh century A.D. as is clear from his own inscription which dates

back to V. S 682 (A.D. 625).= This shows that the poet Magha

flourished much before the time of Bhoja.
Bnnabhafia
Born in a Brahman family. Banabhatta belonged to the
Vatsyayana descent. Certain scholars place him in the time of
Bhoja But there are strong evidences to prove his presence in

the court of Harsha (A.D 605-45). In honour of his patron

king, Baga sketched out the life history of Harsha by the name
the ‘Harshacharita’. Thus the poet Banabhatfa was attached to
the court of Harsha and not to that of Bhoja.
It is quite possible that the poets named Bana and Magha
have flourished during Bhoja’s time also. But they were definitely
different from the great poets Bana and Magha, the writers of
‘KadarabarP and ‘§i§upalavadha’ repectively.
Like the above poets and authors, Vasudeva, Raja^ekhara,
Bhavabhmi, Dandi, Mallinatha, Manatuhga, Bhaskara
Bhatta,
Vararuchi etc. flourished long before Bhoja’s time.
Dr. D. C.
Ganguly mentions that the list of poets and authors
given by Meru-
tuhga, as Bhoja’s contemporaries, is incredible.*

him many of these erudite scholars flourished long before Bhoja’s

ous from the chronological point of view.i But Ganguly is firm
in his judgement that large number of poets flourished under
Bhoja’s benevolent patronage.* Prof. H.C. Ray also holds the
same view and asserts that Bhoja got the assistance of the nume-
rous scholars who flourished under his patronage.*
We shall now deal with the poets and scholars who actually
were Bhoja’s contemporaries.
Dhanapala
Among the literary men attached to Bhoja’s court, Dhanapala
is the earliest known who flourished at the end of the 10th and
beginning of the 1 1th C.A.D.*
Dhanpala was the son of Sarvadeva and grandson of Devarji
who was born in Madhyade§a, in a Brahmana family belonging to
Kasyapa-gotra. Sarvadeva himself was a learned man and was

the resident of Visala (Ujjain). He had good relations with Jains

which reflected in his younger brother Sobhana, who later on
became a Jain. But Dhanapala previously had hatred for Jains,
and owing to this he shifted his residence from Ujjain to Dhara.
It is said that Vakpati-Munja conferred on him the epithet of
‘Sarasvati.
Dhanapala in favour of his younger sister, Sundarl or Avanti

Sundari, wrote a Sanskrit-Ko^a named ‘Paiya-lachhi’ (PrSkrta

Lakshmi) in V.S. 1029 (A.D. 972).'

Merutunga tells us that subsequently through bis younger
brother Sobhana’s influence Dhanapala followed Jainism.
Later on, he gained the favourable notice of Bhoja and rose to be
one of his principal court poets. Dhanapala also preached the

With regard to the composition of the Tilakamailjari, the

siderable time. This long absence tended Bhoja to enquire into
the matter. At this Dhanapala replied that he was busy in comp-
leting the Tilakamanjarl. Bhoja, then curiously went through the
book. The book left a desired effect on Bhoja, which prompted
him to ask the poet to male him the hero of the tale changing the
name of the country from Vinatfi to Avanti. But Dhanapala
refused to comply with the king’s request. This infuriated Bhoja
which resulted in the burning of the book It is stated that his
daughtcr-Balapanditu had crammed up half of the stanzas of the
book and she reproduced it in the original form. She also com-
posed the next part, in a new form and added it to the book.^
We along with D.C. Ganguly, find the story unreliable, for there
is no authentic evidence in support of this story.”
Anyway, Bhoja was so much impressed by Dhanapala’s
brilliance that he made him his patron poet. The Ain-e-Akbari

tells us that of the 500 poets of Bhoja’s court, Bararuj (Vararuchi)

was the foremost and the next was Dhanapala; so far, four books

have been di.scovered from his pen.®
The first of these works was composed in Sam 1029 —A.D, 972,
when Dhanapala was residing at Dhara. In conclusion the poet

tohhana
He was the younger brother of Dhanapala and lived in Ujjain.
His only known composition is the Caturvim^ikastuti.i
DSmodara
It is said that on Bhoja’s command, he re-edited the drama
‘Hanuman-nataka’. He also composed the Abdaprabodha

Sliii (poetess)

According to Mcrutuhga Sitfv was an eminent poetess who
flourished during the reign of Bhoja ~ Mcruturtga narrates that
she was at first employed as a cook at Dhfira and that during this
period in her career a pilgrim slaying in her house died from

taking ‘Kanguni’ oil (aniseed). This so alarmed her lest she should

be charged with his murder, that she tried to commit suicide by
taking a dose of the same emetic. But, instead of dying, she found
herself thereby endowed with a brilliant intellect, and thereupon
she set to work to study the three Vedas, Raghuvamsa, the
Kamasutra of Vatsyayana and Cunakya's treatise on polity. Hav-
ing thus become accomplished in various arts, she went to the
court of Bhoja with her daughter Vijaya where she gave proof of
her literary-merit and was highly appreciated by the king. Dr.
D C, Ganguly opines that Merutunga’s report appears to be cloth-
ed with fiction.®
Padmagupta, the court poet of Sindhuraja, has referred to the

poetess SIta in the context of king Upendra (Krsiiiaraja the first

king) of the Paramara dynasty.* Ganguly thinks that the two

ladies would seem to be identical.® Prof. H.C. Ray' also
holds the same view and states that Buhlcr’^ actually mentions

the poetess Sita in the times of Upendra, who wrote a ‘pra^asti’ on

Uvafa
Uvata was the son of Vajrata a resident of Anandapura, the
modern Vadnagar in Gujarat. He settled in Avanti, in the 1st
half of the 11th C. A. D. and devoted his time to literary studies.
He composed his book ‘Mantrabhasya’ a commentary on the
Vajasaneyi Samhita during the reign of Bhoja.^ He has written
a commentary on ^gveda-pratiiakhya, and he is known as the
author of Nigamabhasya, a commentary on Yajurveda.* This
commentary he composed while he was residing at Ujjain in the
time of Bhoja, as is proved by the second copy of the same
commentary.'
Madana
Madana who was also known as Bala-Sarasvatl-Madana, com-

posed a ‘Natika’ (drama) named Parijatamanjarl or Vijaya^ri.

The first two acts of this drama are inscribed in the Bhoja-
palhaiala of Bhoja at Dhara which later on was converted by
Muslims into Kamal mosque. This drama was first played at
Dhara. The book praises Paramara king Arjuna Varma in his
war against Jayasirhha, the king of Gujarat. This shows that
Madana was a patron poet of Arjunavarma, who was alive in

V.S. 1267 (A.D. 1210).' It is thus clear that Madana flourished

much later than Bhoja’s time.

A critical survey of all the achievements of Bhoja thus
reveals that through educational institutions, donations, patro-
nage and above all by his own creations he added much to the
enrichment of Sanskrit literature, Ilis clforts in the domain of
literature arc unchallenged. The grammatical rules engraved by
him on the wails of University at Dharu, ucrc popular in his
times from Kashmir in the north to Maharashtra in the south.

They arc still taught in Bengal and some of the ’sutras’ (formulae)

arc learnt by heart even today in every indigenous vernacular
school of Malwa, Gujarat and other parts of India under the
names of'Siddha'. Every child’s first lesson begins with ‘Siddha’,

but in the mouth of unlettered Pandyas (Padagogucs) and their

ruder pupils the text has been so corrupted that it can now be

hardly recognised as Sanskrit,’ So long (here e.xists the Sanskrit

language, the name of Bhoja will be commemorated by the band
of scholars
Col. James writes "While Hindu literature survives, the name
of Bhoja Paramara and the nine gems cannot pcrirh.”=
Mr. Josycr remarks, "Maharaja Bliojadcva was an erudite
and enlightened sovereign of Malwa in central India over nine
centuries ago. He wrote numerous works in Sanskrit, and has
enjoyed almost mythical fame ns a patron of men of letters.’’^
Dr. Sir C. P. Ramnswamy Iyer observes, "Maharcija Bhoja-
deva was one of the most celebrated patrons of rrt that human
history has witnessed.*
Architecture And Art
Architecture is regarded as the principal visible and material
record of man’s intellectual evolution. Each cultural movement
1. For example -Original form ...fWt ^ror: cT^r
Corrupt form
II
2. Annals and Antiquities of Rajasthan, p. 1 10.
3. Prologue, Srihgnra prakah, p. 1.
4. Foreword, ^rihgarapraka^a (Josyer edition) Mysore, 1955.
^3 ^ Bhoja Paramnra and his Times
contributed to the art of building. According to Percy Brown,
in India ‘ Man’s ideals have found expression in numerous noble
monuments, showing that few countries possess a richer architec-
tural heritage”.^ From this we may reconstruct much of the past
and visualize the social and political conditions of the country as
phase succeeded phase and one period merged into another.
Like the French, Gothic and Italian renaissance, which reveal a
condition of pastime, energy and scholarship, the architecture of
India also reveals its spiritual content. The fundamental purpose
of the building art was to represent in concrete form the prevail-
ing religious consciousness of the people. It is mind materialised
in terms of rock, brick or stone.
The architecture of the period under survey had some
especiality. The chief architectural characteristics were the
emphasized treatment of its wall surfaces, the scheme of sculp-
ture covering the whole exterior part of the building with the
richness of its decorative effect and its subject matter. Moreover,
in it the transcendent life itself is plastically represented. Carved
in rich or low relief are depicted all the glorious gods of the age-
old mythology. The finished quality of the materials employed,
the high standard of their manipulation, and the stability of
construction as a whole is astonishing.
The Indian architecture, as a whole, may be divided into
two parts, viz. the reiigious and the secular. The first part
comprises the temples, stupas, chaityas and other religious build-
ings, whereas under the secular come the forts, palaces and
ordinary houses. In order of sequence, we shall deal first with
the religious buildings.

After the close of the Gupta period (600 A.D), architecture

is covered by productions of two successive periods, namely, the

early post-Gupta period (chiefly comprising the cave-temples

executed between the 6th to 8th C. at Aihole, pattadakala
Badami, Mahabalipuram, Elora and Elephanta and the late post-
Gupta period covering the structural temples, executed between
1. Indian Architecture, Hindu and Buddhist, p. 60.
raitire and Learning 33 1
the Sth and the 13jh century, comprising three branches of Pala

arcltitccturc, Orissan (Kalihga) and Cliandclla architecture.

Religions Ruitdings
In the words of Benjamin Rowland, the art of India “is the
example of prolongation of religious art".* Ihc period under
survey is noted fur its temple architecture. Innumerable temples
were erected by the kings .and private persons, rich and poor
alike. Unfortunately, very few remains arc now extant partly
because of the time ravage and mainly because of the cruel hands
of invaders like the llunas and Mahmud of Ghazni. On the
b.asis of the remains that escaped the onslaughts of the invaders,
we shall dc.a! with them here.
Let us first have a look into the common features which are
usually seen in the mcdi.acsal temples. In short, c.ach temple of
the period consisted of the following parts

(a) The I’inu'ina :~^The Sanctuary as a whole is known as

the Vimfina.

(b) The Sikhara The upper and pyramidal or tapering

portion of the temple is called the ^ikhara, meaning tower
or spire.

(c) The Garhhagfiha : — Inside the Vimana is a small and

generally dark chamber or cclla for the reception of the divine
symbol.
This cclla is the ‘gnrbhagriha’ or “Womb house" and was
entered by a doorway on its inner and usually eastern side.

(d) M(>ii{hpa :~In front of the doorway was a pillared hall,

or ‘niaodaptt’, actually a pavilion for the assembly of those
paying their devotions to the divine sjmbol in the cclla. Some
of the earlier temples indicate that the 'mandapa’ was a detached
building, isolated from the sanctuary by a definite open space,
as in the ‘‘Shore" tcmp'c at Mamailapuram, built about
700 A-D.=
1. Pelican History of Art, The Art and Architecture of India

{Buddhist, Hindu, Jain), p. 150.

2. Indian Architecture by Percy Brown, p, 62.
i32 shoja Paramnra and his fimes

(e) Antarala As the centuries rolled on, it became a

custom to unite the two buildings, thus forming an intermediate
chamber, or vestibule, and called ‘antarala’.

(f) Ardhamandapa ; —Leading up to the main hall, or

raandapa, is a porch or ‘ardhamaijdapa’, while there may be a
transept on each side of this.

(g) Mahamandapa The central hall of the temple is

known as the ‘mahamandapa’. The Sikhara or tower figures in
all the types of temple-designs in the country. This spire evolved
from the peaked or domed huts of eastern and central India. It
is quite evident that such huts, whether bamboo or wood, were
the prototype of the Nagara temple with the ‘^ikhara'.i On the
other hand, the ^ikhara of the northern Indian type of temple
develops out of the Buddhist stupa gradually becoming elongated
from the semi-globular mound, through the various crudal
changes.^ As Buddhism merged into Hinduism, so the symbol of
the chaitya eventually merged into the Sikhara from the
ceremonial umbrella through the stupa to the tower.
The main difference between the temples of the Jains and the
Hindus is that the former preferred enclosed compartments
instead of open columned halls. The device on the finial of the
Sikhara distinguished the temples of Siva and Visnu, the
three-pronged trident signifying a Siva temple inside the cella of
which will be a linga or phallic emblem and outside on the east,

infront of the main entrance an effigy of the sacred bull (Nandi).

On the other hand, above the spire of the Visnu temple will be
a discor wheel, and inside the sanctuary a statue of the deity.
Other determining symbols are the dedication stone, carved
above the doorway of the sanctum on the centre of the lintel,
corresponding to the keystone of an arch. Moreover, around
the exterior walls of the sanctuary three principal images are often
carved, one in the centre of each side. The figure at the back and
west generally had some connection with the particular deity
- 1. Dr. Ramprasad in Rupam, No. 17, Calcutta, Jan. 1924.
Longhurst in “The Story of the Stupa", Colombo, 1936.
Literature and Learning 333
enshrined within. The various compartments are usually co-
ordinated axially. They are all built on one alignment running
cast and west with the entrance to the east. The edifice normally
faces east so that the early morning rays of the sun may penetrate
into the holy of the holies. This system of orientation,
however,
is not so strictly adhered to in the temples
of the Jains, which
sometimes face the north.
The Brahmanical architects digested some portions of the
Buddhist architecture into their Hindu conceptions. The Chaitya

hall was adapted to vaulted roof as in the Telika-mandir (11th C)

at Gwalior and Vaital Deul.^ In the south, Hindu shrines having
the “Chaitya” roof with iis apsidal end are not so rare. It is
known as the “elephantback” or ‘Gajaprishthakrita’ and is
illustrated in the Chola temples of Sumangalara Manimangalam.

(11th C.A.D.)

The temple architecture, as a whole, was guided and controll-
ed by certain fundamental principles and
some comprehensive
technical codes. This shows the uniformity of the style. This
co-ordination was brought about by two indigenous institutions
e g the ‘§reni’ or guilds and the ‘§ilpas»
or canons of art. At
first the craft guilds were maintained by the
apprentices. The
young learners were attached domestically as well as
profes-
Lnally to their master’s establishment. Later on, each craft was
specialised and, like a family property was handed down from
father to son The character of the building necessitated
the local
guild to settle down near the side. In this way any large architec-
tural undertaking became an art centre from which a local school
and style were derived as in the group of temples at Khajuraho

(C. 950-1050 A.D.)

Several treatises on ‘§ilpa-saatra’ were written during this
period. The Samarangapa Sutradhara is a great contemporary
work on the subject. It is in two volumes and deals with
architecture 2 It deals with the three artistic expressions, namely
sculpture, painting and architecture. The portion relating to the
1. Indian Architecture, pp. 63-64, PI. V, fig. 7-
2. S.S. Yol, I. Preface by T. Ganapati Sastri.
334 Bhoja ParamUra and his Times

building art. the ‘vaStu-sastra’ or ‘rules of architecture (Vastu)

contains detailed directions for the proper construction of every
kind of building, religious and secular. The book also includes
an imposing array to technical rules of proportion and recondite
descriptions with a fund of astronomical calculations and
religious precepts. The ‘Silpis’ thus committed to their memory
the language of the work or workshop.
It is regretting that a very few names of these master
mansions have survived. One of the reasons for this was the
constructional methods of the Hindus, which were most simple,
and had little inventi\eness, or any serious attempt to solve
structural problems of the equilibrium of forces in action by
means of the arch, vault and other mechanical devices. This
is why the Hindu masonry is described as of the ' dry-order”.
The Indian builders knew the architecture as a fine or liberal art.
They prepared their building material not on the site of the
structure, but in the quarry itself.
The classification of types of temple architecture in the later
Hindu period is an enormously intricate problem. It can be only
briefly outlined for, no very strict geographical or stylistic division
is possible. “The temples, opines Benjamin Rowland, ‘‘belong-
ing to one traditional type borrow elements from another fype.>
In the most ancient surviving sources mentioning temple
architecture we find that the sanctuaries are classified as Nagara,
Dravida, and Besara. Geographically these types are assigned
respectively to northern Indian from the Himalayas to the
Vindhyas, to southern Indian from the Krishna river to Cape-
Comorin, and to central Indian from the Vindhyas to the
Krishna-river. Although actually there exist no differences in these
types to the orders and the smallest details of ornament,^ still for
purposes of clarity, we may differentiate them by their most
salient feature, namely, the treatment of the spire or super-
structure.
A 'brief survey would thus reveal that the regional develbp-
ment is the chief characteristic feature of the period under review.
1 . The art and Architecture of India, p. 156.
Literature and Learning 335
Generally, three styles were popular during this period \\hich

were (a) The Nagara (b) The Besara and (c) The Dravida

The Nngara
The Nagara also known as Indo-Aryan style is the earliest
form. The temples of this type are still extant in several poets
of northern India. The most striking feature of the medieval
’lindu-teraples of the northern or Indo-Ar3'an type is the loftily
ibbed curcilinear or building spire. It is canical and convex in
’orm. It is surmounted by a large cushion-shaped block of

stone (amalaka) and above this is a Vase-shaped cap or i pennacle

or ‘Kala^a’. A characteristic group of temples built in this style,
dating from A.D. 750 to 1200 is at Bhuvanesvara in Orissa. The
majestic ‘LiAga-raja’ temple has a spire of 180 feet high rising
imposingly above the surrounding buildings At Konark, on the
neighbouring coast, is the famous black Pagoda, now in ruins.
But Mediaeval Hindu architecture finds its highest expression
in the Jain temples which crown the summits of Mt. Abu, in
southern Rajaputana, and of Girnar and Satrunjaya in Kathiawar.
Of the temples at Dilwara or Devalvada at the former place, the
two most important are those of Vimal shah and Tejapala, whose
halls of pure white marble, now beautifully mellowed by time,
dated from 1032 to 1232. They have often been described, and
only a passing reference is necessary to what Fergusson calls,
“the lace-like delicacy of the fairy forms into which the patient
chisel of the Hindu has carved the white marble.^ The principal
feature is the octagonal do ne of the vestibule, around which is
a columned peristyle.
The Nagara-temple is always convex and curvilinear. The
planning and the elevation are the two distinct features of this
style. In plan the temple is always a square with a number of
graduated projections in the middle of each side. These
projections give it a cruciform shape with a number of re-entrant

angles on each side. In elevation, it exhibits a tower (^ikhara),

gradually inclining inwards in a convex curve. The projections
in the plan are also carried upwards to the top of the 'Sikhara*.
1, Architectural Antiquities of Northern Gujarat^ pp. 39-40.
336 Bhoja Paramnrd and his Times
Thus there is a strong emphasis on vertical lines in elevation.
On account of it and the vigorous and unbroken outline of the
tower, it is also known as the ‘Rekha-^ikhara’. The Nagara
style exhibits distinct varieties and ramifications in different
localities, but the cruciform plan and the curvilinear tower are the
two common and fundamental features, wherever it is situated
and whatever its local stamp might be.
Let us have a regional survey of some of the important
Nagara style temples. The Brahme^vara temple, now lost, was
built by Kolavati, mother of king Uddyotakesari, in the
18th regnal year of the latter. The date of this temple may be
placed about the middle of the 11th C A.D.' The temple
resembles the phase of transition like the Siddhesvara and
Kedaresvara. Situated within an enelosuie and with four

subsidiary shrines (Chaturmarakutt) at the four corners, it makes

a regular ‘Panchayatana’ group. It is more finished in its
appearance.
CENTRAL INDIA
Khajurnho-Group
One of the groups of the Nagara style temples is found in
the central India, which had been the home of early 'Sikhara’
temples. This group includes the temples of Khajuraho ranging
from A.D. 850 to 1050 A.D. During this period the Chandellas
of Jejakabhukti were predominant in this region.^ S. K. Sarasvati
suggests that none of the temples at Khajuraho can be dated
prior to the second half of the eleventh Century A D Accoiding
to him, the first half of the eleventh century was a critical age for
the chandella dynasty, partly, owing to the rise of Kalachuris
and mainly because of Mahmud’s advance upto Kalanjara.
Thus the temples that now stand at Khajuraho very probably
belong to this period.^ O. C. Ganguly opines that the magnifi-
cent group of temples at Khajuraho was constructed during
C. 950 to 1050 A.D., the most flourishing period of Chandella
1. J.R.AS.E.L., XIII, pp, 63-13; The Struggle for Empire,
p. 211.
2. H.I.EA., II. p. 141 ; H I.L.A., p. 104 ; Br. LA, p. 133.
3. The Struggle for Empire, pp. 564-65.
Literature and Learning 337
dynasty, the time of Dhahga and Vidyadhara.^ Sri Krishnadeva
has established a new theory regarding the date of Khajuraho
temples. He points out that closer scrutiny reveals that the
earliest temple cannot be much later than 850 and the 1 test
may go beyond 1100, He further adds that there is no doubt
that there was an older tradition of architecture in granite at
Khajuraho and the transition from granite to sandstone must
have been accomplished gradually.^ However there is no
unanimity of views in this regard.
The forms of the Chandelia-architecture temple belonging to
the Nagara order with a curvilinear tower are anticipated. In

Orissa (Kalinga) as well as in western India, (Rajputana, Lata.,

(Gujarat) the universal Nagara type developed in distinct varieties.

According to the unpublished text of the Aparajita-priccha, the
Nagara type is the native of the Madhyadesa - the middle land
country, and may not have come from Orissa though there are
practically no surviving remains of this type in northern India
except the magnificent group at Khajuraho
The ‘^ikhara’ of Chandella temples are never curvilinear near
the shoulder as typically developed in the main shrine at Bhuva-
nesvara At Khajuraho, the outline of the ‘sikhara’ stops at the

top, making a straight horizontal line (in the words of O.C. Ganguly

typicaly illustrated in the smaller subsidiary shrines at the corner.®
Sometimes the central vertical projection on all sides of the ‘^ikh-
ara’ overstops the limit of the horizontal termination and tries
to reach and touch the ‘amalaka’ crown.^ Sometimes this feature
has occurred at Orissa, but it is never a characteristic feature.
The great temples at Khajuraho of the Chandella kings, carry
on the traditions of the Gupta art.
The other Nagara style temples are Vi^vanatha temple at
Maribagh, the temples at Amarakaptaka and Sohagpur. These
are identical in form and design with the temples at Khajuraho.
1. 1 he Art of Chandellas, p. 15.
2. Ancient India Bulletin of the Archaeological Survey of India
No. 15, 1959, p. 49.
3. The Art of Chandellas, p. 16, plate, 38.
4. Ibid^ plates 38 and 52,
338 Bhoja Paramdra and his Times
It is believed that these temples belong to the transitional phase,
which may roughly be placed between the 9th and the 11th centu-
ries A. D.
We shall deal here with few prominent temples of central
Indian Nagara style. Among these the Kandariya Mahadeva and
Visvanatha temples deserve special mention.

Kandariya-mahndeva at Khajuraho 'Old Chhatarpiir state)

This is the lergest and the loftiest temple of Khajuraho. It is
about 100 ft each in length and height and 66 ft in Width, exclu-
ding the gagti. According to Sri Krishandeva “It is the most
evolved and finished achievement of the central Indian building
style and one of the sublimest creations of Indian architecture,”^
It is decorated with graded and ascending series of smaller replicas
of itself totalling 84. It has a grand lofty ‘Sikhara’ and intricately
ornamented pile bus in unified in theme and design. The plan of
the iemple consists of ‘ardhamandapa’, ‘mapdapa’, ‘raahamah’
dapa’ with lateral transepts, ‘antarala’ and ‘garbhagriha’ enclosed
by an inner amnulatory with transepts on the sides and the rear.
Each constituent element of the plan, the elaboration of design
recesses and ornamentation distinguished this temple from others,
which further, has the largest number of projections and are
rhythmically carried up on the elevation.®
The Kandariya is the only temple of Khajuraho, where the
‘gagti’ shows projections on the transepts. Again, it has the loftiest
‘adhisfhana’ with the most numerous and elegantly-ornament-
ed mouldings, which includes two rows of processional friezes
teeming with elephants and horses, warriors and hunters, acrobats
and musicians, dancers and devotees, and miscellaneous scenes
including erotic couples. Its niches over the ‘Kumbha’ and ‘Kalasa’
mouldings of the ‘adhisthana’ contain couples. The largest
number of sculptures of alluring beauty appear on the three
bnnds of its ‘Jangha’ and represent an animated array of gods and
godesses, ‘mithunas’ and ‘apsaras’ on projections and ‘Sardulas’
I. Ancient India Bulletin of the Archaeological Survey of In lia
No. 15, 1959, pp. 56-57,
2 Ibid, p, 57
Literature and Learning 339

and ‘nagis’ in recc;<?05, the la<?t forming a special feature that this

temple shires with the latest temple at the place, the Duladeo.^
The interior of the Kandariya temple is more spacious and
gorgeous. It his a lavish wealth of carvings and sculptures on the
pillars and architraves, brackets and ceilings and on the wallniches
and faces of the sanctum. The doorway of the sanctum with seven
‘Sakhas’ or vertical components is more elaborate than other local
temples which have only five.
Visvanntha-Tempk An early phase in the transition is

represented by the Visvanatha temple at Maribagh (old-Rewa

state,)".

This Saiva temple enshrining a ‘lihga’ is Sandhara-prasada of
the ‘panchayatana variety and is among the finest examples of the
developed Khajuraho style. Architecturally, it comes midway
between the Lakhmana and the Kandariya, and its importance
lies in the fact that it anticipates the Kandariya, which marks the
culmination of the central building style- Thus, the basement
mouldings of this temple closely resemble those of the Kandariya,
with this dincrcncc that the latter shows a few additional mouldings
in the lower one-third The two temples also agree in the general
arrangement and disposition of sculptures. Even the 'Sikharas’ of
the temples arc essentially similar in design, though that of
Visvanatha is appreciably simpler, showing only one ‘Urah-SriAga’
and two rows of ‘Karna’-‘^rihgas’ on each sidc,“
Malwa
The Paramiiras of Malwa, who ruled as independent monarchs,
were the great builders of ^iva temples. The Paramara king Bhoja,
built a number of temples dedicated to Kediiresvara, Ramc^vara,

Somanatha, Sundira (?) and Kalanala Rudra.^ Buhler takes Kala,

Anala and Rudra separately. But A. N. Upadhya and H.L. Jain
1. Ibid, pp. 56-57.
2. Journal of the Department of letters, Calcutta, XXIX,
Article No. 8.

340 Bhoja ParaniHra and his Times
opine that the reference seems to be to Kalagni-Rudra, one of the
terrible forms of Siva, mentioned by Somadeva in the Ya^astiJaka,
Book I, p. 151.*
Bhojapur Temple
One of the most significant specimens of the Paramara Tem-
ples of Malvva, is the Siva Temple known as Bhojesvara temple
at Bhojpur. It is situated 20 miles south of the modern city of
Bhopal. The exact date being unknown, the temple is presumed
to have been erected in the latter part of the 1 1th century or in
the 12th century. In plan the building is a simple square. It
differs from others of its class in this respect that it has no re-
entrant angles Its exterior diamension is 66 feet. Four massive
pillars support an incomplete dome inside the building Each of
the pillars is 40 feet high and is divided into 3 sections. The lower
two are octagonal and are surmounted by a 24 faced section. The
whole of the pillar is tapering in appearance. The dome is carved
with rich designs. There are two sculptured figures on either side
of the doorway, which is richly decorated above but plain below.
There are three balconies on three sides, each supported by massive
brackets and four decorated pillars. Inside the building lies a
colossal ‘lingam’ seven and a half feet high and seventeen feet
eight inches in circumference, on a platform made of superimposed
blocks of sand stone, twentyone and a half feet square The
temple, though incomplete, is singularly beautiful.®
Another temple belonging to the same age is a Jain temple
which lies close to the above shrine but whose name is not known.
In shape it is rectangular. The two figures of Par^vanatha, one
of the Jain Ttrthaftkaras, are carved on it. It also contains
colossal statue of Mahavira, the Jain Tirthahkara which is twenty-
feet high.®
Vdayesvara or NlJakanthesvara Temple
The UdayeSvara temple was built by the Paramara king Udaya-
ditya at Udayapur temple in the old Gwalior state. It resembles the
1. YaSastilaka and Indian Culture, Journaled, by A.N. Upadhye
and Dr. H. L. Jain, p. 471.
2. Imperial Gazetteer of India, Vol VIII, p. 121.
3. Ibid. H. P., p. 270.
.tiicraiurc and Learning 341
‘Nagara’ temple style of Dekkan.* The sanctum and the ‘marn^apa’
rise on a high ‘adlii^thana’ or socle. The farmer is surmounted
by a ‘Sikhara’ and the latter by a pyramidal roof both in Dakha-
nese expression " the exterior of the sanctum is in foliated star-
shaped plan, the points of the star, meaning the chases between
the central bands on the four faces, being obtained on the principal
of rotating squares,^ The central bands arc parallel to the square of
the ‘garbha-griha’. The ‘bada’ or the cubical section has a plinth
nearly as high as the ‘Jahgha* above. The socle as well as the
plinth consists each of a series of bold mouldings those of the
former spreading towards the bottom as if to hold the ground
in a firm grip. The plinth mouldings rise perpendicularly. In
shape and treatment these mouldings have a general likeness to
those of the Khajuraho temples. But with these the affinity with
the typical central Indian temple ends as the design on the whole
follows typical Dakhanese Version.'* The chases in the ‘Jahgha’
arc in the shape of faceted pilasers as in the Ambarnatha. They
are angular, because of the star-shaped plan Every chase accom-
odates a sculpture on each of its two sides. The chases are termin-
ated by capital shape mouldings above which a cornice, with a
deep recess intervening serves as the ‘baranda’ or transition from
the cube to the ‘^ikhar’a®
The ‘^ikhara’ rises with a slight curvature above the unbroken
vertical spines, in continuation of the central band below, with their
richly traccried ornamentation holding it in shape. The vertical as
well as the horizontal themes, as seen on the perpendicular cube,
are repeated on the ‘Sikhar’® The former finds eloquent expression

3. Fid, 31.

in the firm contours of the spines on the four quarters transcen-
ding the limits of the ‘bisama’ and in the arrays of ‘anga'-Sikharas’
in continuation of the chases in the lower section, rising in succes-
sion towards the peak. The latter theme is reproduced in the
division of the sub quarters of the anga Sikharas into successive
tiers by deep horizontal passages in between. The principal
scheme of the composition, according to Stella Kramrisch' is,
a great correspondence of the structure and super-structure
Likewise the edges of the ‘bisama, repeating the five-pointed
star in each of the sub-quarters, and in the rims of the amal-
aka-‘sila’ responding in its cups to the angles of the star-shaped
temple.” The ‘mandapa’ roof, pyramidal in shape, is composed
likewise of miniature repjtitions of itself in successive tiers conver-
ging to the pinnacle. The mighty gable over the antarala, rising
again in successive levels, serves as an effective bond between the
super structures over the sanctum and the mapdapa, which are of
two divergent shapes. Every part is carved in a texture of exqui-
site delicacy and variety which emphasises its lineaments in the
most effective manner. The entire monument represents a unified
design. The temples at Arang and Rahilya and that of Savarl-

Narayana near Kharod (all in central India) exhibit each certain

of the characteristic features of the ‘Dakhanese temple but not in
so complete a degree as the Nilakanthesvara at Udayapura.®
Rajpuiana
The curved ceilings at Khajuraho linkup Chandefia architec-

ture with the Jaina marble shrines at Mt. Abu (Rajputana).^ For

in the temples of Vimala shah (1032 A D.) we come across analo-

gous curved ceilings. According to A. N. Upadhya and H. L.
Jain, this famous marble temple of Adinatha was built by the Jain,
VimalShah in 1031 A. D.‘ It is difficult to say if the builders
ofRajputana borrowed the idea from temple of Khajuraho or
