vice- versa. It may be pointed out that such ceilings occur at Soma-

natha-pattana (Kathiawad) first destroyed by Mahmud of Ghazni

in 1025 A. D. Besides the Adinatha temple, Vimal Shah erected
the other three Jain temples at Mt. Abu. These temples are more
restrained without but marvellously decorated within and thus
show a similar activity. Vimal Shah was an officer of the Chalukya
king Bhima of Gujarat.^
The Parsvanatha temple at Kumbharia in Rajputana, also
belongs to the Nagara style. It probably belongs to the 11th
C. A. D.=

The date of the Chaiishat Yogini temple at Mitauli, (near

Padhauli), near Gwalior in Madhya Pradesh, is ascribed to the

1 Ith C, A. D. It is 120 feet in diam ter, has 65 peripheral chapels
and a circular central shrine with a mandapa in front.“ Circular
yogini temples arc also found at Ranipur Jharial in the old Patna

State,* (Orissa), at Dudhai in Lalitpur district (U. P.)® and in the

old Kalnhandi State.® All these temples seem to belong to the
early mediaeval period."
Uttar Pradesh
Reference may be made in this connection to a few brick
temples in Uttar Pradesh which offer certain interesting analogies
to the circular temples at Chandrchi and Gurgi Masaun. The
temple at Parauli in the Kanpur district® is circular in plan of

appearance are found at Kuran in the Fatehpur district* (U.P ),

where, however, the interior of the ‘garbha-griha is of a square
plan. As usual in brick architecture each of the temples is covered
by a double dome constructed on the carbel principle. The clear
cut definition of the different faces and deep-cut traceries covering
entire surface from the base to summit shows a good appearance

well preserved condition. According to S. K. Sarasvati these
monuments indicate a new direction in the development of the
north Indian temple style.- Another temple of an external circular
shape, though square internally, is found at Tinduli in the

not unlike those of the group mentioned above In this predilec-
tion for a circular plan, whether in the exterior or in the interior,
or in both as in the cases of the temples at Chandrehi and Gurgi
Masaun, central India and the Gangetic region may be found to
have interesting links with each other.
Orissa-Group
The Orissan local type temples do not come before the close

of the 11th C.A.D. The Kalinga school (a sub-class of the

Nagara type) is built as under three distinct religious and political

inspirations.* The earlier phase—mainly Saivite in its inspiration,
is concentrated in a group of ’iSiva temples at Bhuvanes'vara, of
which the earliest are Satrughnesvara, Disiresvara and Parasura-
mesvara, statable in the last part of the 7th C.A.D.
In the Orissa group of temples the Indo-Aryan style prevails.
This group architecture has two main features, the ‘Vimana’ or
Bara Dewl, i-e. the towered sanctuary or the sanctum or cella and
the ‘Jagamohan’. Both are square in plan, the former is a cube or

Gujarat and Kathiawar
Like other regional expressions of the Nagara style the typical
Solanki temple of Gujarat and Kathiawar appears to have received
its form by the close of the 10th or the beginning of the 11th
C.A.D.=
The Indo-Aryan temples of Gujarat are remarkable for their
richness and delicacy.^ Although some of these shrines and also
those of Kathiawar and Kachchh were begun in the 10th c., the
majority of the structures may be dated between A.D. 1025, the
year of Mahmud of Ghazni’s iconoclastic raid on Somanath, and
the final conquest of this whole region, by the Delhi sultan in
A D. 1298.'*

The sun temple at Modhera (Gujarat) is more elaborate in

design and enrichment. The grouping of pillars supporting the
dome is in an octagon.* This temple cannot be later than
A.D. 1026 because in its back wall, it bears an inscription dated

V.S. 1083 (A.D. 1026), which may tentatively be taken as the

date of the temple.
Mention may also be made of two other monuments, apparent-
ly of the 11th C.A.D. one is the Navalakha temple at Chumuli.’
and the other also going by the same name, at Sejakpur,* both in
Kathisvar.
Sindhu-Ganga Valleys
The Nagara style was also known in the regions of the

One cf the finest monuments of the Nagara design in this

(Bankura District, West Bengal)^ Aecording to Coomaraswamy

this was built lOth C.A.D.® Dikshit thinks this date to be a
century or two too early. According to him from general
architectonic shape and ornamental style a date in the 11th.
C.A D. may not appear to be unreasonable.*

The Besara Style (Deccan)

The scope of the Besara style was largely restricted to western
India and the Deccan. “ Some of the tc.Kfs ascribe the Besara style
to the country between the Vjndhyas and the river Krishna.® In
this region or more properly, in the region for some time under
the royal dynasty of Chalukyas, a .separate style of temple
architecture was recognised by the archaeologists as the Chalukyan
style. This, however, is a hybrid style, borrowing elements and
features both from the Nagara and the Drnvida styles. Thus
having regard to the evidence of monuments, the three styles of
the ‘Silpa le.xts’ can be resolved ultimately into two, viz. the
‘Nagara’ and the Dravida.’

The Dravida Style (South)f

The sphere of the Dravida style, according to the ‘Silpasastra-
karas' extended in the country lying between the river Krishna
and Cape Kanya-Kumarl.® In other words its limit extended to
the south, Tnis style is the storeyed form of the Gupta temple.

34§ Moja Paramura and his finies
The rock-cut ‘rathas’ at Mamallapuratn supply an interesting stage
in the development of the Dravida style.^
During the period under survey, some outstanding examples
of this style are found only in the Chola country. The Cholas
built many temples and that at Tanjore early in the Ilth c is a
good example of the southern type with its more massive ‘Sikhara’
and more ornate decorations.
Generally speaking, the style is mainly indebted to two

Chola monarchs i e. Rajaraja the Great (A.D. 985-1014) and his

son Rajendra Chola (A.D. 1012-1044).^ It is to these monarchs

that we owe two supreme creations of the Dravida temple style.
The first of these was built at Tanjore, the seat of Chola
sovereignty, by Rajaraja the Great and is known as the Brihades-
vara. According to the temple records, the construction was
begun sometimes A.D. 1010.’ The second was created about
A.D. 1025 by Rajendra Chola, in the new capital city of Gangai-
konda Cholapuram, which this monarch founded in commemora-
tion of his extensive conquests.* In A.D. 1025 he constructed

another monument at the same place, which was built w.«;hin a

quarter of a century. Percy Brown suggests that the buildings
attributed to the Cholas of this period are not many, nor are they
large and imply a local rather than imperial development.’
Let us discuss some of the salient features which distinguish
each of the three styles from one another.
The profile of the Nagara temple is always convex and
curvilinear with an emphasis on the continuous verticality of the
spire. The efi'ect of the Dravida type of temple is that of a tower
ascending in a series of horizontal terraces. In the architecture of
Dravidian India the term ‘Sikhara’ is applied only to the crowning
member of the edifice, a conical cap also designated as the
‘stupika’ or miniature ‘stupa’. This crowning member is repeated
on the corners of successive levels of structure. In the Dravida

SECULAR ARCHITECTURE

350 Bhoja Paramnra and his Times
followed the scheme of defe ,cs that was prescribed for the forts by
the works like the Maaasara'^ aal the Sattarangaija-Sutradhara’
on the Hindu science of architecture.
The Samarahgana-Sutradhara defines a capital as that city,
where the king resides * The second or the Summer capital is
referred to as ‘Pattana’.* The scheme of town fortification consisted
of five principal parts namely, moats and ramparts, parapets,
towers and turrets, different types of town gateways and lastly the
king’s highways together with other important roads.®
An ideal town was to have 3 or 4 principal roads from east to
west, and north to south. The two king’s highways also ran in
the same direction crossing at right angles at the centre of the city
and each of the two was to be 36 feet wide in a town of the first
grade.® Roads connected with the ‘janapada’ and city
meant for communications were known as ‘Maharathyas’
and ‘Yanamargas’. The former’s width varied from 18 to
12 feet according to the gradation of the town and 6 feet width
was prescribed for all ‘Yanamargas’. The ‘Samarangapa-
Stitradhara’ on both sides of eight ‘Yanamargas’ of a city,
and which may be known as modern footpath, the width of
which ranged gradually from to 3 feet.’ As the two “GhantS-
margas” ran close to the parapets, they extended on all the four
sides of the town. In order to separate one sector from the other
and to facilitate the enjoyment of sunl ight and fresh air by city
dwellers, the construction of lanes and byelanes constituted an

essential part of townplanning. A big street was to be of half

the width of a king’s highway (while lanes and byelanes were the

one fourth (i. c, 9 ft) in width. Every city was to have an cfTicicnt

system of public drainage and drains were always to be covered
cither with stone-pieces or wooden planks,^ The profession and
caste of a particular person determined his place of residence in a
city, which the Samarahgana-Sutradhara refers to by the expres-
sion ‘Jativarnfidhivasa’. According to it, Ghee and fruit-sellers

resided exclusively in the north-cast (Aisanya) of the city.- Mcru-

luhga refers to the “Vegetable market^ The four principal castes
of tnc Hindu society namely, Brnhmanas Kshatriyas, Vaisyas and
Sudras dwelt respectively in the northern, eastern, southern and
western portions of a city.* Security officers, both of the
police and the military, physicians and the *Vai.syas’ who sold
articles of daily use, were allowed to reside in all directions of a
city.® The central part of the capital constituted the palace area
and was reserved for royal residence.*
Since among Hindus fiowers constitute an essentia! clement of
god-worship, temples and pleasure gardens or flowergardens within
a city had importance for each other and this point is stressed by
the Samarnhgaoa-SQtradhnra."

There was also a provision to lay down gardens and all
around the city 3 moats or trenches must be dug, filled up with
water which facilitated flower, trees on their banks. Assembly
halls, parks, pleasure-flower-gardens, fountains and artificial lakes

(Kasaral were the chief characteristics of a good town. The Sama-

rangana-Sutradhara^ and the Yuktikalpataru^ throw significant
light on this point.
Palatial Buildings
The principal buildings were also regularly oriented having
their sides towards the cordinal points : while each city was divided
into wards for protective purposes All the walls of both houses
and public buildings were constructed with a pronounced batter
or slope, but it is in the substance and preparation of these edifices
that the artificers showed exceptional knowledge Buildings were
entirely composed largely of wood, the roofs being flat and built
of stout beams covered with planking finished with a top-dressing
of beaten earth, openings being generally spanned by wooden lin-
tels but several instances of the corbelled arch formed by oversai-
ling courses of brick have come to light.®
At least three-sized i. e. small, middle and big, palaces have
been referred to in the Samarahgana-Sutradhara ® These palaces
were built mainly for gods, king, Brahamana and the rest of the
varnas. Normally, the book refers to 3 kinds of palaces, namely

the ‘Ruchaka’ (?), ‘Dravida’ and Meru’ each of which had their

own sabdivisions. The Ruchaka palaces were of ten types, viz,
Vairaja Kailasa, Puspaka, Mamika, Trivishtapa and Chitrakuta
etc ® Among them some were studded with gems and gold.'
The Dravida palace was to be constructed on the 5 hands
extended ground. The thickness of its walls was to be 2 angulas

(?) and the height not more than 7 hands.’'

2. Ibid. i

Literature and Learning 353
The second list comprises sixteen kinds of palaces, such as

‘Mcru’ (Prastidaraja) ‘Kailasa’ (Harapriya). ‘Sarvatobhadra’,

Vimanchchhanda’, ‘Nandana’, Svastika’, Muktakona’, ‘Srivatsa’,
'Hariisa, ‘Ruchaka’, ‘Vardhamana*, ‘Garuda’, *Gaja’ ‘Mrigaraja’,
‘Padma’ and ‘Valabhi.’ Among these much importance is attach*
cd to the ‘Mcru’ palace, which is referred to as ‘Prasadaraja’

(the king of the palaces). The area covered by the ‘Mcru’ palace

was to be between 33 and 50 hands and the dimension of the
‘^ikhara’, onc-lcnth of the whole structure. The ‘Sikhara’ was to
be again divided into two parts. The length and breadth of the
entrance path was to be l/6th of the ‘Sikhara’. Inside the
‘Mcrupalacc’ was the provision for a pool, whose area was l/160th
part of the whole Mcru-palacc,
The next category of palaces refered to in the Samaratigapa-
Svliradhara, is the ‘town-Mcni-Palaccs’. These were of 20 types
such as, ‘Merit’, ‘Mandara, ‘Kailasa’, Kumbha, ‘Mrigaraja and
Vimanachchanda’ etc. These palaces, more or less, resemble the
synonymous names as given in the above list. The ‘Town-Meru*

Palacc had 100 (MudrS), 4 gates and 16 storeys with peculiar

Sikharas). The Mandara Palace was of 12 storeys and had 8

roofs.
Among another list of palaces, come the names, such as
Uttama etc. which were built within the courtyard. These were
of 36 types with 6 divisions. Each of them w'as divided into 3
parts, i. c. larger, middle and smaller. The larger part was of 3J
hands, the middle of 3 hands and the smaller of 2| hands.
Ordinary Houses
The Samarahgana-Sutradhrira also deals in detail with the
construction of ordinary houses. According to it these houses were
generally well-roofed. He roof was called ‘Harmya’, which was
approached by ladder made of wood, or stone or bricks. The wooden
staircase was called ‘Ni^reni’. In each house was the provision for
windows and ventilators for supplying fresh air and light. The
bigger window was called ‘Gavanha’ while the smaller one ‘Aval-
okanaka’. The middle left part of the ‘Sfila’ ^yas as ‘PuskarinT’

354 Bhoja ParamSra and his Time)

but in case it was covered it w as called ‘garbhagriha’. The bigger
middle part of the house known as ‘Upasthana’ had the provision
for the accomodation of several persons. Its smaller size was
known as ‘Upasthanaka’. It was also known as ‘Prasada’ and
its srpaller size as ‘Prasadika. The bigger ‘Prasadika’ was known as
‘Valabhi’ but when it was in front of the house, it was called
‘Alimpa.’ The ‘§ala’ smaller than ‘valabhi’ was called ‘Valabha’
The doors of each house were known as ‘Kapata’ but if they were
decorated they were called ‘Torana’ which were of two kinds viz,
‘Manitorana’ and ‘Puspatorana’. The former was studded with
gems while the latter had the carvings of flowers etc. The ‘Tha-
kar’ in front of the ‘Torana’, was known as ‘Simhatorana’. The
land of the inner-part of the house was called ‘griha’ while the
surrounding ground ‘Samyamana’ and front part ‘Parisara. The
Portico was palled the ‘Astalaka.’ In every house was the provi-
sion for latrine and urinal which were called ‘Varchaska’ or
‘A?thalaka’.
The book also refers to atleast 2 types of houses i. e. the ‘Alia’
and the ‘Attalaka’. The ‘AUa’ house was big and lofty, while the
‘Attalaka’ was comparatively small. There was a provision for
eaves and drains which were attached to each house.
The dwelling house generally faced east, had spaces on all
sides and was enclosed by a compound wall which was decorated
with the engravings of flower plants, groves etc.^ Every house had
a courtyard and a staircase. It was generally two-storeyed and
was built upon a good foundation with a terrace.® Often, there
was no arrangement inside the house for the supply of water
which had therefore to be fetched from a common well probably
dug by the king or some other rich person, desirous of acquiring
merit for the next world. There was a small reservoir for collecting
rain water inside the house. The houses were usually made of
bricks’ and morter with a tiled roof and were provided with eaves,*

LUsraiure and Learning 355
to carry the rain water to a drain which emptied it into the street.^
This style is still in vogue.
The usual furniture consisted of the ‘charpai’- in the bed-room
while cancscats, grass mats and curtains completed the equipment
of the sitting room.’
Art
Let us discuss the important organs of Art, i. c. the sculpture,
iconography, and painting.
Sculpture
The plasticity of the fully rounded and modelled form had

l)ccn the significant charactci istic of classical Indian sculpture and

painting. But during the period under survey, his impression was
not registered everywhere. It was mostly visible in central India,
Gujarata, Rajputana and certain Himalayan-tracts.
The pivot of early medieval sculptors is the human figure,
both male and female in the form of gods and goddesses and their
attendants. In elasticity and transparency in illumination and
depth of feeling and experience, early medieval period can bear
no comparison with that of the classical age."*
Like the architecture, the sculpture also had regional develop-
ments. Let us have an inquiry into these regional schooh of
sculpture.
According to Kramrisch,® during the early centuries of the
second millenium, traditional sculpture was controlled by a
conscious formalization. With heightened awareness, the
regional schools expressed themselves in characteristic idioms.
In the whole country it was a descent from the metaphysical to
the psychological level, and in western India particularly, from
the fullness of sculptural content to a linearized recording of

2 . Ibid,
3. DesinamamSid by Hcmachandra, II V. 7 IV, Vii, IV 129, II V,
8. V. vv. 2 and 19.
4 . The Struggle for Empira, p. 640.
5. The Art 'of India by Stella Kramrisch, pp. 44-45,
356 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
tremors-lying just beneath the surface. Their translucent shapes
are seen in the marble sculptures of sout i Rajasthan.
EASTERN INDIA
Bihar and Bengal
Throughout thi whole history of Art in the east and west
sculpture has stood in relation of a twin brother to Architec-
ture, embraced by one integrated principle of plasticity creating in
the same breath structural as well as imitative or symbolical
forms*
Generally speaking east Indian sculptures are carved out

of black chloriti (Kashtipathar), either of fine or of coarse grain.

The pivot is the human-figure in the full bloom of youth, radiant
and vibrant. It is combining in both spiritual and mundane
suggestiveness frankly physical and sensuous."
Theendofthe 10th and the beginning of the 11th century
transform the vigour and the strength of the bodily form into one
of conscious gracefulness and elegant mannerism.’ Understanda-
bly, a blender bodily type comes to be favoured. The deep
broad outlook of the 10th century becomes somewhat thin and
circumscribed and the elegance of the physiognomical form
gradually becomes more evident.
In Bengal and Bihar under the Pala regime flourishei a
school which produced steadly hieratic figures of Mahayana : nd
goddesses i.i black corboniferous shale.* In this school, the
volumes are outlined by meticulous curves composed of orna-
ments and draperies. Their details are as polished as the metal
smooth surfaced stretched over the withheld sensuousness of their
Bengali countemance. The nature of the black stone of the
Bengali images enhai ced the idiom of this regio i. It said all
that it was meant to say in this, the last phase of its continuous
evolution through past centuries.’ A magnificent figure
1. Orissan Sculpture and Architecture by O.C. Ganguly, p. 20.
2. The Struggle for Empira, p. 644.
3. Ibid. p. 646, fig. 96.
4. Ibid. p. 48
.
5. Stella Kramrisch, The Ari of India, p. 44,
Literature and Leartimg ^57
of Surya, the Sun-god in the Indian Museum south Kensington
is typical of the art of the period.
Orissa
From the lOth century onwards the sculptural art of Udaya-
giri, Ratnagiri, Jaipur and Cfaandur is a part of contemporary
art of Bauddhagaya and BhuvaneSvara, Puri and Konark and
may be viewed together.
Typically orissan sculptural art can best be studied at centres
like Bhuvanesvara, Bauddhgaya, Puri and Konark. Together
they cover a period of five hundred years from 8th to 13th
centuries. Medieval trends in Orissa do not seem to make
themselves felt earlier than the 11th C.A.D. Certain reliefs of
the Lihgaraja and Bhuvanesvara tcmpls and those stored in
the Konark museum side by side witli figures arc inspired by the
classical tradition.
The theme of Bengali images also applies to Orissan
sculptures. The colossal statues on the sun temple of Konark
hold in limbs that look as though they had been turned on
potter’s wheel their joyous attitude in space, high upon the roof
of the temple. They transport their being into the volume of
their emotions. The imagis of the Sun Temple of Konark, in
the words of Kramrisch, belong to eternity in the world of
art.^
The reliefs on the walls of the temples at Konark and
Bhuvanesvara are erotic and arc based on the ‘Kama^astra’.
They illustrate the view of sex mysticism which runs through
the Hindu religions and is especially prominent in the worship of
Krishpa, sexual congress is symbolic of the union of the individual
and the world-soul.
'I he statuary sculpture of the Lihgaraja temple and the
Rajarani temple at BhuvaneSvaraa, is one of the strong features
that firat attract the attention of the visitor. The large number
of statuttes on its pages carved in high reliefs almos t in the
round are well proportioned.®
1. Setella Kramrisch, The Art of India.
2. Orissan Sculpture and Architecture by O.C. Ganguly.
358 f f « < ,Bhoja ParamSra and his Times
Ganga Yamuna Valley
Specimens of sculpture belonging to the 9th and the three
succeeding centuries are extremely rare, so far as the Punjab
plains and Ganga Yamuna Valley are concerned. This is mainly
due to devastation wrought by Muslim iconoclasts from the days
of Mahmud of Ghazni.
Central India
Occupying geographically an intermediate position between
the east and the west, central India extending from the borders of

Rajputana and Gujarat in the west (roughly from Ujjayini and

Mandasor) to Allahabad in the east, holds an intermediate position

artistically too, in the history of medieval sculpture.^ Here products,
from the 10th to the 13th centuries turned out under the aegies and
Patronage of the Chandellas of Jejakbhukti and the Paramaras of
Dhara reflect an admixture of both east Indian tradition of Bihar
and Bengal and that of Rajputana and Gujarat, where the medi-
eval trends found their most congenial home. Garhwal, Mahoba
and Khajuraho preserve the most important relics of the
Chandellas. The ParamSra tradition is stored at Dhara and
Mandsor in Indore and Gwalior. Finally and psychologically
they are different, though common denominator is admissible.
Throughout in central India of the Chandellas and the
Paramaras and in MahSkosala of the Haihayas, classical volume
has become ampler, but grows increasingly stereotyped which
means a thinning down of the inner vision and consequent and
corresponding loss of plastic sensitivity.^
Chandeila
Decorative ornamentations at Khajuraho, indeed in Chanc ella
art in general, show an amalgamation of classical and medieval
grammar and composition existing side by side, often in
one relief, sometime separately and sometime integrated into one
complete pattern. Though the sculptural art of Khajuraho lacks
1. The Struggle fov Empire, p. 655.
2. Ibid p. 656.
Liicraiurc and Learning 359
classical Gupta art in sublimity, depth of feeling and expression
of inner experience of the artists it pulsates with human vitality,
which is amazing. Tlic Khajuraho sculptures arc completely
liberated from their wall-surface and stand out almost fully in the
round as enchanting lyrics of modelled beauty.*
Mr. Krishnadeva* divides the sculptures at Khajuraho into
five categories. The first category comprises cult images generally
standing in sama-bhanga having a large prabhavall. The back slab
is decorated with figures of attendant gods and goddesses.
But owing to the strict adherence of canonical formulae they
reveal a thin aesthetic vision The second group consists of
parivara, and uvarana devatiis numerous gods and goddesses.
These occur in the niches or against the walls of the temple and
arc executed either in the round or in high or medium relief, and
arc more forma! The remaining of gods and goddesses arc less
formal and more free and stand in a lively ‘Tribhahga’ pose and
arc distinguishable from human figures only by their peculiar

head-dress, (Jala, Kirlfa or Karanda-mukuta), long mala and aign

of diamond in the chc.st
The Third category consists of the figures of ‘apsaras’ or ‘sura-

sundarls : These arc executed on the 'jangha’ in the minor niches

of the facades, on the pillar or ceiling brackets or recess between
the pilasters in the interior. These arc portrayed to express com-
mon human moods, emotions and activities etc.
The fourth category consists of secular sculptures, which
comprises miscellaneous themes including domestic scenes, teacher
and disciples, dancers and musicians and erotic couples or groups
some of which express intense absorption and rapture and trans-
cend the physical to the spiritual plane.
The fifth or the last category consists of sculptures of animals
including the ‘^ardiila’, primarily represented as the rampant
horned lion with an armed human rider on the back and a
counter-player, attacking it from behind. Numerous varieties
1. Ancient Indian Bulletin of Archaeological Survey of India, No.
15, 1959, p. 64.
2. Ibid. p. 63.
366 Bhoja Baramara and A/s times
of this basic type are known with head of elephant, man, parrot,
boar etc. The sardula is normally figured in the recess of the
Jangha, but also appears on the sukanasika and in the interior.
Like the apsaras, this is a most typical and popular sculptural
theme of Khajuraho and is invested with a deep symbolism.
Beside Khajuraho, there were other centres in Bundelkhand

where the (medieval) grip was equally, if not more, potent as for

example at Chandpur in Jhansi.
We now come to the system of seulptural embellishments at
Khajuraho arranged in triple-bands of plastic decorations which
are used to decorate the ‘waist’ of the temples very like the triple-
girdles which go round to deck the waists of beautiful damsels in
Indian plastic art. This is not met in the Kalinga temples.^
The age-old plastic unity between sculpture and architecture
is once again demonstrated in a vivid manner ir the monuments of
Chandellas at Khajuraho. Almost all the temples at the site are
adorned by these bands of sculptural girdle, sometimes, in triple
strings. O.C. Ganguly writes that the Chandella architects have
made new and distinguished contribution to Indian plastic art.®
Thmgh the plasticity of the fully rounded modelled form is
replaced by sharp edges and pointed angles, with a stress on hoi i-
zontals, verticles and diagonals, opines Krishnadeva,® the art of
Khajuraho surpases even the medieval school of Orissa in reveal-
ing the sensuous charms, ecstatic joy, passion and admiring physi-
cal beauty of human body. Coquettish languor and frankly erotic
suggestion form the Key notes which distinguish the Khajuraho
art from the contemporary schools of art.
The Visvanatha temple has indeed the most proportionate

figures with admirable poise and balance; illustrated by ‘apsarasas’

playing flute.® These attained their maturity in the Kandariya
Mahadeva temple. There are bracket figures on the pillars and
1. O. C. Ganguly, the Art of Chandellas, p. 16.
2. Ibid , Orissan Sculpture and Architecture, p. 17.
3. Ancient Indian Bulletin of the Archaeological Survey of India,
No. 15, 1959, the sculptures of Khajuraho, p. 64.
4. Ibid., p. 65.
literature and Learning iei
sometimes nearer the ceilings, depicting a damsel softly filling the
flute with sweet musical notes, lost in a reverie of a love-lorn
thought, playing the ball, using the mirror to adjust her locks,
applying the collyrium to the eye or looking back wistfully on the
pretext of pulling out the thorn and so forth in addition to the
several ‘mithuna’ and other amorous scenes. According to C.
Sivaramamurti this is to speak the least, brilliant proclamation of
artistic achievement by the Chandclla sculptor.^ The emotional
expression of lovers on the Khajuraho temples still remains unsur-
passed.
=
Paramara
Paramara art was prolific in its sculptural output, but hardly
did it ever reach a high tide of aesthetic achievement. Though
bound geographically to what was known as Western Malwa, its
products, really belong to the contemporary Rajputana idiom. The
greatest builder and lover of art of the dynasty was Raja Bhoja

(C.A.D. 1000—1055). The image of the Sarasvati’ Mandir (temple)

a sort of a university of learning at Dhara, shows Paramara sculp-
ture at its best. Other specimens can be seen at Mandu, at the
local museum at Dhar, on the walls of the Nllakantha or Udaye-
svara temple at Udayapur within the enclosures of the Mahakala
temple at Ujjain and at other places including Indore.
Largely and vigorously conceived and modelled in ample
dimensions, the figures arc informed by classical value of form.
They ate free from jerky movements and intense flexions and do
not seem to feel he weight of heavy roundness which charact-
erises their youthful body. The face, also fully and vigorously
modelled, wears an expression of blankness and is lighted up nei-
ther by any pleasure of the senses nor by any inner experience,
urge or inspiration. Despite youthfulncss and vigours round mode-
lling, plastically hangs in suspense arc stilled into stagnation, which
goes 'along with a pair rigid and heavy less equally lifeless. Medi-
evalism touches ornamental devices and jewelleries. The latter
1. Indian Sculpture, by C. Sivaramamurti, p. 105.
2. Tourist Guide, M. P. issued on behalf of the Tourist Depth
Ministry and Communication, New Delhi, pp. 10-11.
3. The Struggle for Empire, p. 658 fig. 17.
Blioja ParatnSra and his Times
are sharply cut and are just laid on the body with which they have
no organic relation. Classical decorative ornamentations,are also
flattered and linearised as far as possible, while specifically nor-

thern patterns of sharp and flat contract in light and darkness (as

in chess board and diamond patterns) are common.

Under some Tantric influence, Bhoja at the beginning of the
1 1th C. expanded the simple canon for depicting ‘mithunas’ into an
elaborate rule for presenting figures. engaged in reproductive acti-
vity. In his Samarangana-Sutradhara he writes
...“Ratikridapara naryo nayakastu yadrichchhaya i
Kinchit pratanubhir gatraih karyah suratalalasah ii’’^
It means that the woman should be depicted as fully enga-
ged in sexual intercourse and the lover, on the other hand, if so
desired, may be shown, as desirous of' sex play with slightly slum
bodies Some such new canon must have inspired the sculptors of
Rajarani temple of BI uvanesvara in the 10th as well as the Kand-
ariya temple at Khajuraho in the 11th C.A.D. The doorframe

discovered at Betwa, district Satan (C. ICOO A.D.) and kept in the

Patna-Museum, has got the representations of “man-woman” in
embrace of mithunas.
We are not oblivious of the fact that there were hundreds and
thousands of people in northern India during the period, who were
inspired by the ideals, compassion, liberality, truth, purity, gentle
ness, peace, bliss, stainliness and self-control as preached in Dhar-
masastras. But, they worked for their individual salvation without
making any earnest effort to improve the moral tone of their con-
temporary society.
VINDHYA REGION
Haihayas
The Haihayas of Tripurl ruled in the area of the Rewa state
in Bundelkhand. They rose to the importance in the time of Karna
in the beginning of the eleventh century. Their sway extended
from Allahabad to Jabalpur. Under them fiourished the temple

architecture at places like Bheraghat (near Jabalpur), Satna,

traditions. The eastern Chalukya art resembles the wastem
tradition except in case of the figure of Surya. In the former
Surya has top-boots while in the latter he is bare-footed. Brahma
is youthful and without a beard as in Pallava-Chola figures.
Vishnu carries ‘Gada’ and ‘Sankha’ in southern. Natesa as at
Pattadakala has four arms but dances in ‘chatura mode combin-
ing northern and southern predictions. Alingana Chandra-
sekhara has a peculiar charm.^
The Sun temple at Modhera of the time of Chalukya Bhima I

(A D. 1000-1063)® resembles the style of the Jain temple of

Rishabhadeva at Mt. Abu, erected by Vimalasa in A D. 1032.’
To the north of the entrance, among the larger reliefs on the
wall is the figure of Kalabhairava as it is locally known.
It is standing male figure having three faces and three arms
one left and two right, there seems to be also three legs two

left (one behind the other) and one right The figure of ‘agni’

is represented with three legs, and four right and three left hands.
In his upper right hand is a curved dagger, and the other hands are
broken off. Down to the right of this figure a ‘makara’ seems to
be represented.*

sented with only two arms.^ The other superhuman figures on the

Punjab Hill States
These states were not isolated from the main current of north-
Indian life and culture. Chamba, Kangra, Kulu and Kumaon
have preserved sculptures of images and decorative patterns in relief
that originally formed parts of temples some of which arc still
extant, as for example at Brahraor and Chatrarhi in Chamba and
Masrur in Kangra.
Kashmir, Nepal and Tibet

In medieval sculpture of Kashmir (9th to 12th centuries), too,

contemporary cast Indian tradition stamps its strong impress, in
form and treatment as well as in physiognomy. There is evidence to
show images from Bengal were imported into Kashmir during these
centuries. The elegant bronze group of Padmapuri accompanied
by his two ‘Saktis’, with an inscription of the reign of queen Didda

(A.D. 980-1003), is an instance in point. But even at this stage

suggestion of Hellenistic drapery remains patent,
Deccan
By about the end of the 8th century all creative inspiration and
elTort in the Deccan sculptures had entered a stalemate after cease-
less activity of very deep and abiding significance for about 3 centu-
ries, Yet temples were built and decorated with sculptures and
images made for the purpose of worship.
South India
South India, on the other hand, carries on the classical tradi-
tion as transmitted by the Pallava idiom, in a purer form, so under

conviction and more vital manner; and this is done through centu-

ries. The inheritance skill and creative urge of the artists and cra-
ftsmen exploited the patronage of the Cholas of Gafigai-Konda-

puram (C. 850-1 100) and the Pandyas of Madura (1 100-1300) etc.

366 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
All Chola sculptures of the 10th and 1 Ith centuries are end-
owed with a tough vitality and are modelled vigorously, but have
the surface as if in a stage of animated flexibility. Plastically they
have relation with contemporary Deccanese sculptures, but reach
throughout successive centuries.

Iconography :

The affinity of iconography with the temple architecture is like
body and soul. There is no Hindu temple without a principal
deity installed in it. Usually the outer walls of the super structure
of the temple are adorned by the minor images such as in the
temples of Khajuraho and Bhuvanesvara. Apart from temples, the
iconography has its independent nature.
As the age under survey was prolific in temple architecture, the
iconography, naturally rose to its height. The texts like the Samara-
hganasutradhara, the Aparajitaprccha and the Manasollasa deal in
detail with the icons of different cults such a Saiva, Vaispava BrShma,
Qapapatya, Saura Sakta and Jain. Various illustrations of the
substances, materials Mudralogy, ornament, drapery, weapons and
aestheticism in context with the construction ol different cult-images
are found in these texts
The Samaranganasutradhara* classifies the icons according to
the substances used therein. Different types of materials are refe-

rred to in the books as ;

Tlic Aparajitaprechu devotes twelve chapters to the art of

From the above texts we come to know the various types of
clay and also of metals such as gold, silver, copper and iron were
used for the construction of images. The Mansollasa® contains
various references of metal cast inages. The Aparfijita precha'^
under the heading Ratnja images deals with the ivory construction.
According to Gopinatha Rao® precious stones were also used for
this purpose.

368 Bhoja ParamUra and his Times
Alekhya
‘Alekhya’ was the most popular media of divine represen-
tations such as is proved by the illustrations given in the works of
the period.
Mudralogy
The contemporary texts direct the Indian artists to express in
art certain symbolic representations of particular gods or goddes-
ses and also their various moods. Coomarsvvamy^ observes,
“such notions must have been elaborated and codified at a very
date, and later on the art of silent communication by means of
signs, which is in effect a deaf and dumb language and just like
the American-Indian hand-language, was regularly regarded as one
of the sixty-four arts, which every educated should have knowledge
of”. J. N. Banerjee also expresses the same view.- The Samara-
nganasutradhara has devoted three chapters to Mudralogy accor-
ding to which Mudras can be classified under three heads
PatSkadi 64 hands poses of hands.’ 2. Rivagata, eic. sihanas-
Poses of the body.^ 3. Vaisriava c/c,-standing postures, posfures
of the legs.®
The Aparajitaprccha speaks of Dhruva devas, or the principal

types pf Vishnu-iraages, viz. ‘Sthanaka’ (standing), Asana (seated!

and 'Sayana (reclining).

Weapons :

The Aparajitaprccha® deals with the 26 weapons which were
to be taken by different gods and goddesses. The Samarapgapa
sutradhara for example, expounds the weapons of Kartikeya, the

war-god, some of which are.-Khadga’ (sword) Mudgara’ (club)

‘Kheta’ (shield) ’Dhanus’ (bow) etc.

Literature and Learning 369
Drapery
As regards the drapery, J. N. Banerjee writes, “In some late
medieval figures great care is bestowed by the artist on the carving
of the garment, thus the ‘Sari’ which is shown round the body of

the figure of Parvati, one of the ‘ParSvadevatas’ (deities shown in

the side niches) of the Lihgaraja temple at Bhuvanesvara is an

example of the highest skill in carving.*
Ornaments
The Hindu artists from the early times seem to be very atten-
tive in displaying ornaments. The Aparajitaprccha gives the list
of sixteen ornaments such as necklace, bracelets, bangles, waist
ornaments, car-ring, armlets, leg ornaments etc, which are fre-
quently visible in the sculptures of the Paramaras, the Chandellas
and the Gahadvalas etc.
Aestheticism
For an artistic perfection of an image it is essential that it
should arouse in us the ac.sthctic experience. According to the
Samaranganasutradhara, it is the supreme achievement of the
imagemaker or the sculptor to sec to this side of aestheticism in
art- the bhava-Vyakti to give a distinct and unique character.**
Cult Images
The cult images of the period are based on the canonical
injunctions and resemble in uniformity of standard of quality

;

though they reached their climax in each region at different periods.

In Bengal and Bihai it is reached in the 9th and 10th century ;

in Orissa in the 12th and 13th; in the central India in the 10th; in

Gujarat in the 1 1th and in the far-south in the 10th centuries. The
Deccan ceases to be a sculptural province after the eighth century.®
Trimiirti
The Composite images combining the features of two or more

gods such as Hari-Hara-Hiranyagarbha, Brahma— Visnu— and Siva,
Surya, combining the features of Brahma, Visnu and Siva are of
great interest. For example, six armed composite images found
at Madhia in the old Panna state in Bundelkhand region represents
‘Surya-Siva-Brahma.’ The three-headed and eight-armed standing
Surya in Cidambaram temple also illustrates this. Another eight-
armed, seated image of this type is carved on the western side of

the central Bhadra (structure) of the ‘Siva’ temple called Dula*Deo

at Khajuraho.
Pahclmiirti
This form represents the five principal deities of the Hindu
pantheon i. e. the Brahma, Visnu Surya, or Siva, Ganesa and the
Parvati which are worshipped by the Smarta people. According
to Dr. J.N Banerjee’ this form is largely depicted in the mediaeval
sculptures. A Panchayatana Siva liftga originally found in a part
of Bihar, now in the Indian Museum, shows the four cult deities,
Umapati, Vi§pu, Parbati and Surya on the four sides of the central
Saiva emblem, all of which taken together symbolise the five
Brahmanical Hindu cults.
Chaturmurlis
The Aparajitaprccha describes the ‘Chaturmurtis’ of Hari

(Visnu), the Sun, Kara and Hiranyagarbha. The fine, specimen of

this is found in the Limboji Mata’s temple at Delmal (northern

Gujarat). Burgess remarks, in one figure the four dignities, Visnu

Siva and Brahma with Surya, appear blended or shall we rather
say, it represents'a vaisnava Trimurti with Surya Narayaqa as the
central figure, seated on his Vahana ‘Garuda’.^
Dvimurti
There are several sculptural representations of Dvimurtis, like
Harardha or Hari-Hara and the Saiva dvimurtis, such as Hara-
Gaurl, UmamaheSvara, ardhanarisvara etc. The fine example of
Martanda Bhairava dvimurti is found in the collection of the V.R.

"Qrahmil
The Samaranganashtradhara besides the traditional features
refers to him as having a pot-belly and clotted hair and long
moustaches or a beard, The Aparajitaprccha.^ also describes
Brahma as having beard This form of Brahma is
frequently referred to in the mediaeval sculptures. Brahma Padm-
asana with all attributes is depicted in the iSiva temple Aihole
seated on Swan and surmounted by Ri?is, Aihole stone : ‘sthanaka’

and bearded with usual emblems, Madras Museum ; Chalukya

Hosala-school ; seated on Visvapadma in a Lalitakscpa pose in

Rajasahi Museum.
Vaisnava-cuIt-DeUics
Various forms of Vishnu icons arc found in the sculptures.
Samarahgapa sutradhara lays down that the image of Visnu should
be represented Vaidurya-SaAkasa’ i c. having the colours of the

‘vaiduryamani’, and wearing yellow garments (Pitambara).

Two, four and eight armed ‘sthanakamurtis’, of Visnu
belonging to the medieval period arc found in the northern and
eastern India.
Yogesvara
Visnu in Yogesvara mudra is found in the twelfth century

stone image from Sarisadcha, 24 paraganas, Bengal (Now exhibit

No 2592 in the Indian Museum). Dr. Benerjee suggests that it

illustrates a good many traits of Yogesvara Visnu.^
Laksmlnarayatm
Vispu as Narayana in the company of goddess Laksmi, gener-
ally in the left with other attributes is known Laksmi Narayana.

Rao has given its illustration in a piece of sculpture found in the
Kappe-Chenigarayas temple at Belur ^
Dhruva-Bears

When Visnu is depicted in Yogasana with his ‘natural’ (front)

hands in the ‘Dhyana mudra’, he is lavishly decorated with orna-
ments, and sometimes accompanied by both or one of his consorts

(Sri and Pusti or Sarasvati according to north Indian custom or

Sriand Bhu according to south Indian conception). According-

to Rao these images fall under both the classes, Yoga and Bhoga.2
Vira varieties of Visnu images of early and medieval period have
not yet been found ® The ‘bhoga’ variety is numerously found
such as a black chlorite Visnu of the early medieval period is

fouud in the Chaitanpur (Burdwan district, Bengal) and now in

the collection of Indian Museum, Calcutta) ‘

In the early phase of Pala sculpture we find the images of
Vispu on Garuda from Laksbmapakali carrying his consorts
Lakshami and Sarasvati.
The images of Viinu as sitting in ‘Yogasana’ pose are found
in the Kalesvara temple at Bagali in the Biliary district of Madras
belonging to the later Chalukyas'®
Dasdvataras
The zoromorphic representation of Varaha in the Varaha
temple at Khajuraho is a noteworthy iconographic carving of the
medieval period. It is almost comparable in importance to the
Gupta sculpture of the same time from Eran, in central India.'
The zoomorphic form of Varaha from Billari recalls similar repre-
sentation from Khajuraho and elsewhere. Yoga arasimha , and

Saiva-Icons. The 5aiva icons in their various forms arc nnmer-
ously found in several centres like Bhuvanesvara, Khajuraho,
Ellora, Rame^vara etc. Generally two types of ^iva icons are
found, vi^, Lihga and the human form.
Linga-i-cons This emblem is associated with Mahesvara.
According to the Aparajita-precha, Siva and ^akti arc typified
into this cosmic emblem. The Samarai^gana-Sutradhara^ devotes
a full chapter to the substances of which the Lihgas are made such

as Mrt (earth), Lauha (of all metals), Ratnas (gems of various

kinds), Daru, (timber) and Sila (Stone lihgas). The book also

describes the threefold critcrian for the classification of the lihgas,
i. c. the measurements in relation to both the temple, prasada and
the worshipper, the materials, and the potency.**
The Mahakala temple at Ujjain enshrines in it the Tihga’ of
Siva which is the fine specimen of the period. It is one of the
important twelve lihgas of Siva. The temple emblem of Siva is
enshrined in the Brhadlsvara temple. This motif was very popular
in the south in early and late medieval periods but not so popular
in the north. We however find one specimen which is now
in the collection of Rajputana, in which Brahma and Visnu are
depicted as attendants of Siva*'.
Human form of &iva Icons : —The Saraarahgana-Sutradhara

classified thses figures into two types viz, the mild (Sauraya) and

Various representations of Siva-icons in mild and fierce poses
are found in the contemporary literature and sculpture.
The facades of the Kandariya and Vi^vanatha temples at
Khajuraho have various forms of Siva figures and those of the
Duladeo temple carry the figures of Nandisvara, four-armed stand-
ing Siva carrying the Varada, Trisula, Serpent and water vessel,
and standing Siva-Parvatl.
According to Aparajitaprccha^ the Mahasadasiva aspect
represents the Dvadasakalasampiirana Sadashiva. Dr. Haridasa
Mitra“ writes that this form is remarkable for its representation
in some of the mediaeval reliefs of Bengal. Dr Banerjee writes
that the Sena Kings of Bengal were devout worshippers of this
aspect of Siva.’
ArdhanUrVsvara Miirtis : —This aspect represents Siva in the
half male and half female form. It shows the combination of

Parvat! and Paramesvara ; Siva and Sakti. The north niche of the

sanctum of the Visvanatha temple at Khajuraho contains the
figure of Siva in Ardhanarisvara form.
Chandrasekhara Murti :—Many Indian sculptures depict this
form. The Umamahesvara form of Siva is found in the Chaunsath
Yogini temple at Bheraghat near Jabalpur.’ According to S.K.
Sarasvati this temple might have belonged to the 9th or lOth
century A.D.’

Gahgadhara : —In this form Siva is seen standing with the right

leg planted vertically on the earth and the left slightly bent with
all attributes. On his Jata is located Ganga. The specimens

of this form hail from stone Kailasa temple Ellora, Bronza, Siva

beautiful specimen of this form.^
Kalyana-Sunda’-a : —It depicts the marriage of Siva with
Parvatl, Dr. Banerjca states that the relief of Elephanta is the
fine example of this form where Siva is seen holding the hand of
Parvatl in the centre surrounded by various gods and goddesses.-
Other specimens arc the Bronze Tiruvorriyur stone panel, two

stones from Ratnapur (Bilaspur) and Madura,

Anugraha-Murtis : —In this form Siva is shown as bestowing
grace on his votaries like Ravana, Visnu, Arjuna etc. The

Ravai.ianugraha is depicted in Kailasa (Ellora panel) and the

Candesanugraha in the Gaingaikopdacalapuram sculpture of the
first quarter of the eleventh century A,D.“
Nfia-Murtis Nrta or dancing of Siva murtis have a special
significance in iconography. In each temple of importance a
separate place is allotted to Nataraja. The examples of this form

are—Nataraja Bronze, Madras Museum ; Stone panel, Ellora and

Badami, stones Kailasanatha templet Conjeevaram and the figure of
Nartesvara dancing on a bull from Sankarabandha now in the
Dacca Museum, The Bhadra niches of the sanctum of the Kan-
dariya and Muladeva temples at Khajuraho exihibit the figures of
Siva in Nataraja form.
Dakshina-Murtis It is so called because Siva is seen seated
facing south when he taught the sages, Yoga and Jnana, Rao
observes that this form is remarkable for its peacefulness.^ This
form is found in four aspects i.c. as a teacher of Yoga, of

Vina ; of Jnana and as an expounder of other Sastras (Vyakhyana-

murtis). These forms are found in the Kailasanathasvamin

temple at Conjeevaram ; Siva temple at Nanjangodu etc. Banerjea

Nandi listens to its master in rapt attention;^ with its head raised

The AsSnta or Ugra-Murtis-Samhnra. Etc.: — In this group
Siva is shown in his destructive form Various representations of
this are found in the sculptures.
KSmantaka-Murti This form shows the figure of Man-
matha as having fallen down at mere glance of Siva. According
to Dr Banerjea, the Gangaikondacolapuram temple relief illustra-
tes this form. ..the serene contemplative pose of the god seems to
show that the sculptor has intended here to present the god just
before he had burnt Kama to ashes by his angry look.^ The
Tripurantaka and Andhakantaka forms of Siva-icons are found in
the bhadra niches of the sanctum of the Kandariya and Ouladeva
temples at Khajuraho. The Virabhadra, Jalandhara and the
Mallari Siva forms of Siva-icons are found in the Madras Museum,
the nine niches of the facades of the Visvanatha temple at

Khajuraho ; Kailasa temple Ellora (Stone panels) and in the

Mahakala temple at Ujjai n respectively.
Kahkula and Bhiksalana Miirtis : —Both these forms represent
him as begging in severed Kapala for the expiation of the sin of
Brahmahatya. According to Rao, these icons are found almost
in all famous Siva temples of southern India.
The Ekadasa-Rudras are very well depicted in the sculptures
at Khajuraho Elsewhere, these icons are rarely found.
Mahesa or Mahndeva This is the fully manifested form of
supreme Siva, who is the cause of creation, protection and destruc-
tion. In this form, he is shown as having three eyes, four arms,
Karandamukta and wearing all the female ornaments. By his
side stands Sakti. Some of the specimens of this form hail from

Chitorgarh, in Udaipur ; Marwar, Belgam Tidytivy district, stone

from Siva temple at Melchcri, near Kaveri-pakkam.
^iva-Ganas—Nandi In north India temples, he is represented
as a bull, squatted on the raised pedestal facing the entrance door

of practically every Saiva shrine ; such as at Khajuraho and

Bhuvanesvara.
The Candesa or Capdanaiha and Ksctrapala are the other
gaoas of Siva. The former is very commonly depicted in the
south Indian temples.

Ganesa : — Aparajitaprcchai gives the account of this god

with all the traits such as elephant head, four arms with svadanta

(his own tooth) in the right, Parasu in the left, lotus and Modaka

in others, three eyes, one tooth, himself Mahacanda, thread of a
serpent, head as red as Sindhura body like Kumkuma, seated on
the mouse. According to the book, such a figure is bestower of
Siddhis and fulfillcr of all desires.
The finest examples of Gancia are found from Kitching

(Mayurbhanj, Orissa). There we fine standing images with all

attributes Dr. Bancrjca remarks, “...and apparently grotesque
iconic type could be converted into an elegant piece of sculpture,
' by the anonymous artist of a corner of Orissa.”- The same
locality provides us with another eight-armed Nrta-Ganapati-icon.
The icon of the same god in dancing pose is found from
Bheraghat.

Kartiikeya : — The Samaraftganasutradhara® elaborates that

his figure is to be represented like the morning Sun, clothed in red
body in fry-red colour, youthful, tending towards ehildhood, look-
ing beautiful, adorned with variegated ornaments like a garment of
the Muktamani. cither six heads or one with crowns and one with
his §akti. In the town places he should have twelve hands in
Khejaka, six and in the villages only two and so forth.
The Aparajitaprccha^ states that his mount is jackal and other

attributes arc six heads, twelve arms, three eyes and a youthful
appearance.
The figures of Karttikeya are found in the upper rows of the
facades of the ViSvanatha and the Kandariya Mahadeva temples
at Khajuraho.
Dev'i-p:atimn . —In consonance with the conception and princi-
ple of Trinity, the three goddesses, i e, Laksmt. Sarasvati and
Durga are the supermost, others being their manifestations,
Ambika, Uma, Durga, Kali ete. are associated with Sakta cult.
The Visvanatha and Kandariya Mahadeva temples of Khajuraho
contain in the niches beginning with the south east in Pradakshina
order the figures of Chamunda, IndranT, Varahl, Vaisnavi,
Kumarlj Mahesvari and Brahmanl.
Laksmi The icons of LaksmI in the sculptures are found
in three forms i.e. Mahalaksmi, Laksmi and §ri or Gaja-Laksmi.
Her iconic attributes are— welldressed, decked with various

ornaments, fully developed breasts, narrow-waist ; heavy

buttocks ; 'Padmastha’ ‘Padmakara’, being bathed by two

elephants ; Sithhavahini etc. . According to the SamarSngana

Sutradhara* Sri, a form of Laksmi, the spouse of Visnu should be
represented in the first stage of youth, face looking like the full-
moon complexion white, the lips red like ‘bimba and smiling’ in

white garment ; most handsome, adorned with Divya ornaments,

left hand on the waist, right holding the lotus flower, bathed by
two elephants, pouring out water over her head from jars.
Dr. Banerjee suggests that this motif is very common in many
medieval temples especially of Orissa, where Gajal.aksmi was
carved in the centre of the architrave over the doorway of the
main structure, whatever might have been the cult affiliations of
the shrines. Separate niches in medieval temples were also
assigned to the goddess, where she was elaborately depicted.^
Sivaramamurti remarks that there is practically no medieval

Hindu temple without the gateway presenting ^ri or Lakshmi on
the lintel.^
SarasvaiJ The Aparajitaprccha has devoted a full chapter

to the worship of this deity. Her main attributes are ; the eight

petalied Lotus, aksamalii, vIna, Pustaka and a ‘Kamandalu’ with
her blue neck, white arms, white body and adorned with crescent
moon and being called under names Mahavidya, Maha-
Bharatl and SarasvatT etc. A sculpture hailing from Kitching

(Mayurbhanj Orissa) of the 10th century A. D. and the images of

Sarasvati found from the Brhadesvara temple at Tanjorc and
collections of Dacca museum arc five specimens. Dr. Banerjee
regards the features of the latter as typifying one of the fully
developed hieratic forms of Sarasvati-.
In Samvat 1019-1033 A.D. by the orders of Bhoja the
famous sculptor Manaihala, executed an image of Sarasvati now
preserved in the British Museum which has all the characteristics
of Mediaeval sculptures. The goddess is in the ‘abhanga’ pose, with
four hands, the front pair being now imperfect, half of each
having disappeared. She wears a crown and her ear-rings hang
down to her shoulders while round her neck is a pearl necklace,

and a pearl —embroidered band encircles her breast; her waist is

also decorated all round. She is attended by live subordinate figures,
2 above and 3 below. On the left below, are a r§i and a dwarf and
on the right probably Parvaii on the lion. On the left above is a
flying female-figure with a garland in her hand, the other figure is
distinct. The Devi is in meditative mood, and her face is lovely
and serene. O.C. Ganguly^ remarks that the image is chef-d-
oeuvre of rare beauty, in its exquisite serenity of pose, m its
entrancing and balancing rhythm, in the elegance and suavity of
its aquiline features, and in the general restraint, in the treatment
of the anatomy, which is almost free from any exaggeration. To
us, the emotions of its static conception, almost alternating
betsveen a “rajasika' and a ‘sattvika’ is most appealing”.^ D.C.

Ganguly* opines that the image is of a great value for more than
one reason. In it we find the union of characteristic features of

both the north and south Indian sculptures Its ‘Urumala’ (thigh

ornament) and caronet are Dravidian in style. The ornaments of

its arms remind us of the early images of Bengal and Orissa.*
Sri Sivaramamurti” remarks, “it is perhaps one of the most
marvellous creations of the sculptor of the Paramara realm
patronised by Bhoja. It closely resembles sculptures of the
Pratihara school, especially from the western area, approaching in
several respects the idiom of Gujarat”.
The general affiliation of the figure with ‘sole sculpture’ of
the period and partial resemblance to the contemporary Pala and
Orissan sculptures show that Indian art has been essentially one
throughout the country, while a companion, with the figures shows
steady, continuous evolution of Indian art throughout the ages.^
An inscription on the pedestal, dated A.D. 1035, as translated by
K.N. Dikshit, runs as follows : Om, the Vidyadhiraja of the town
Bhoja, the moon among Kings having first made the mother
goddess of speech great in fruit, created the auspicious image.
This was made by Manathala, the son of the craftsman Sahira,
written by ‘Sivadeva’ in the Samvat year 1091”.

Durga : —This deity is also known by the names, Mahakali,

Kali or Bhadrakall. Durga panel in the rock cut temple of
Varaksvamin in Mahabalipuram is a good illustration of her
eight-armed form, standing erect on the head of a buffalo. Some
medieval reliefs from Bihar are now in the collection of Indian
Museum. Banerjee remarks that these icons show the development
of Durga motif in a characteristic manner. lu the temple of
Konark Narasiihha has been depicted as presenting himself before
the image of Durga.* The Brahmor image also follows the

inconographic concept of the Chalukya period (i.e. 7th C) i.e. the

goddess puts her right foot on the head of the killed buffalo demon
after having run her trident into its neck, while with her left hand

she catches the bufTalo’s tail and lifts its whole body up almost
vertically.^
Saura-PraUmds : — The image of SQrya is installed in the sun
temple at Konark which was built by Narasimha, the eastern
GaAga king," The sun temple at Madhera also provides us with
a fine specimen of the image of Sun,

Jain :-Imagcs. Among the Jain images the Iwentyfour Tirth-

ahkaras, along with their ^iisana dcvas, the yaksas and the Sasana
devikas, the yaksinis are delineated. The Jain temples of Khajuraho
enshrine images of Jain deities on the niches and the doorways
of the Jain temples invariably represent Chakresvarl, Yakshi on the
latata bimba, while the doorjambs and niches contain Vidyadevis
and SSsanadevatas, besides figures of Jinas. The sixteen auspicious
symbols such as Airavata, the noblest bull, the noblest lion, Sri-
devi etc, seem in the dream by Vardhamana’s mother are represe-
nted on the architrave above the doorway of almost all the Jain
temples except the Parivanatha.
Besides numerous gods and goddesses of the Brahmanical and
Jain pantheons, difierent forms of lower deities like Vidyadharas,
Gandharvas, Nagas, Gapas, Bhutas and Apsaras arc also represe-
nted on the exterior as well as the interior of the temples at Khaj-
uraho. Like the dikpalas, navagrahas and the river goddesses
they have no sectarian affiliations and they appear promiscuously
on the temples of all sects.
The chlorite figures in the Lingaraja temple especially those of
the par^vadevatas around the vimana with their delicately flowing
folds of drapery arc few but excellently executed. So are also the
floral devices, intricate traceries and scroll work. Large statuettes,
flying nymphs, niigas and niiganis, groups of musicians playing on
various instruments and the attractive dikpatis are all full of life.
Remarkable for detail arc the scenes from the two Hindu epics
carved on the frontal projection of the Jagamohan and on its
pidas.®

Painting.
The literature of the period provides us with many details
regarding the art of painting. We shall deal here with some of
them.
The Samarahgana sutradhara* devotes .'everal chapters to the
painting, which deal especially with the ‘rasas’ and ‘rasadrstis’ the
dancing poses of hands etc. and with the popular technique and
conventions. It elaborates that the types of painting emerge from

the types of back-ground such as the board (Patta) or the (Pata)

cloth or the wall (Kudya) all of which require the ‘vartis’ outlines,

mouldings Ithe Kratu-bandhas), the proportion of the portrait etc.

(Lekhamana) the colouring substance shading and delineations etc.

It also describes nine positions of legs and feet and innumerable
poses or ‘mudras’ of the hands. The book also prescribes eight-
fold parts as essential limbs of painting such as : —1. Vartika-Paint

brush ; 2. Bhumibandhanas—Canvas (background) ; 3 Lekhya-

Dravdng ; 4. Rekhakarma— Delineation and Articulation of form ;

5. Varnakarma- colours ; 6. Vartana— display of light and shade ;
7. Corrupt ; 8. Corrupt.
The book also contains details regarding several measure-

ments such as ‘Apdaka-Pramanam, Kaya-pramanam (body dimens-

ions), and Manotpatti etc. in addition to plaster and ointments

under the chapter ‘Lepyakarma’ and varieties of brushes with
their use in the pictorial art under the chapter Vilekha-Laksalaksa-
nam it gives fivefold brushes.

As regards the aestheticism in painting, Bhoja writes, ‘‘now

(after I have described the canons of painting in detail), I shall

describe the Rasas and the Rasa-drstis, because the Bhava-vya-

ktih— the manifestation of sentiments in pictorial images (citra) is

dependent on the ‘rasas’ and 'rasadr?tis’ (tadayatta). Then follows

the exposition of eleven ‘rasas’ and eighteen rasa-dr§tis suited
to in pictures and images. He further adds that the art of painting
is based on the art of Natya or dance and musich
Aparajitaprcchd The book deals with the pictures like
natural scenes, especially the animal kingdom, the birds and beasts
the plants, the trees, leaves and flowers etc. It expounds six styles
of painting i. c. Nagara, Dravida, Vesara, Kalihga, Yamuna and
Vyantara. It gives certain valuable illustrations of pictorial art
and deals with the topics such as the patias, kan takas, the vartane
and the manifold motifs of the animal kingdom and the celestial
one.”

Abhilasitartha-Cintamani (MBnowliasa) The book is ascribed

to Chalukya king Somesvaradeva. It deals with both the religious
and secular topics. The prakarana III gives various details regar-
ding the picture drawing and painting under the heading ‘Alekhya*
karma.’ Various details, regarding the painting and the painter,
Vajralepa, brushes, pure and mixed colours, pictorial painting, the
divine and human both in all its limbs and sub-limbs as well as
the anatomical descriptive of the whole picture and several tech-
nical terms are found in the book. The author describes himself
as Citra-Vidya-Viranchi, the creator or master of the art of

drawing and painting; It also explains five types of painting i c.

Viddhacitra Aviddhacitra, Bhavacitra, Rasacitra and dhulicitra.
According to the Manasollasa® “Vartika” is a sort of colour
stump. The book deals with various types of colours such as
white made of conch-shell, red prepared from red-lead or alaktaka

juice, i. e. lac or from red-chalk-gairika ; green-brown (Haritala)

i. e. sulphurated arsenic and black from Kajjala (soot). The
colouring substances are r-gold, silver copper mica, deep-coloured
brush, red- lead, vermilion tin, yellow lac, indigo etc. The blue
colour is essential for preparing secondary colours ^ The gpld
seems to be the hobby of the painters for delineating the
details of costumes and making the painting attractive and
brightening.® The paint- brush termed as ‘Lekhani’ is to be prep-

ared by the hair of calf and bound up by the aid of Laksa (lac) on

the lip. This Lekhani is of three kinds—Sthula, Madhya and
Suksma. The first is to be used in the application of paint plaster

or ointment (^iw^ the second in drawing out the outline and the

third in finishing up the minutest of lines (!i?h>i). Somadeva

also prescribes certain essential qualifications for a painter such
as fully initiated in the visualization of the theme and experts
at the line, adept in the execution, skilled at designs of patras,
dexterious at colours and masters of delineations of light and
shade.®
There are also descriptions of pictorial art in the Sundarl-

Kaha, the Magadhi’ Prakrita Jain legends (A. D 1038) which show

that even such pictures were drawn in which the presence of human
figure was not always necessary. The TrisasjhiSalaka purusa-
caritra of Hemchandra states that each palace had a picture-hall
containing the wall-paintings. Moreover, the work of painting was
allocated among the painters who had their own assemblies. The
Kathasaritsagara narrates that once a painter drew a picture on a
smooth pillar and then a sculptor turned it into an image by car-
ving. The same book at several places, suggests to hand the screen
inside the house. It shows that there was a fashion more popular
to hand the screen rather than wall-paintings. Rajasekhara, in the
1 . ^ tnr i
2. BliSrata-ki-Chitrakals, p. 37.
Literature and Learning 385
prologue of his drama Ba]a-Bharata or Pracan^a-Papdava, com-
pares nature at dusk to an old picture dulled by smoke ^
In the TilakamanjarT,” we find the descriptions of painted
halls in the palace and also of the moving ‘Citra^ala’ of the bed-

room, the’ 5ayana-Chitrasa|ika ; The same book states that the

‘dantavalabhika’ is painted with pictures on ivory. The ‘ahiga’
or the court-yard is decorated with ‘rangoli’ patterns according to
the drama Vrishabhanuja. Of the types of Chitrasala there is a
mention of the general ones for public in the heart of the city,
those of the palace and the harem.®
Sri Sivaramamurti^ remarks, “if today most of the Chitrasalas
are lost, we yet have literature to supplement our knowledge by
this graphic description. Ajanta, Bagh, the walls of perambula-
tory passage around the central cell of the Brhadi^vara temple at
Tanjore are to be studied only in this context. He further adds
that the attempt of our ancients in the pictorial world was at reali-
stic treatment. But at the same time the canons regarding the
three worlds and the forms of divine ones prescribed by the seers
had to be strictly followed.”^
From about the 8th century A. D. a new tendency began to
develop in the art of painting. The gliding and modelling lines of
Ajanta were replaced by sharp and thin lines, modulated curves by
sharp and pointed angles and roundly modelled surfaces by flat
coloured spaces. The burden of plastic-modelling, writes Kramrisb,
was shifted to the lines, distorting the wave form in a manner that
both recalled and belied its past history.® These features according
to S. K. Saraswati are called ‘Northern’ because they were prev-
alent in north India.’

386 Bhoja Paramnra and his Times
But the commencement of new tradition does not mean the
total exhaustion of the classical tradition. Its examples, though
rare, are found in the Brhadi^vara temple at Tanjore and in the
M. S S. illustrations of Bihar and Bengal, Nepal and Tibet (10th

to 13th century A D.) W. G. Archer writes that until the 10th,

century A. D. this cultured style of Ajanta remained the accepted
norm of painting as is proved by the rock-cut Hindu-temple of

Kailasa and the Jain-cave temples at Ellora, (lOth century) where

dancing girls were still depicted with moulded limbs with strident
lithness but lacking the languid grace.’ According to Kramrisch,
the classical tradition in its thinner and variously accentuated
context survived unto the 11th century in south India and conti-
nued in paintings of illuminated manuscripts for one or two cent-
uries in eastern India.^
The northern or mediaeval tradition thus became influencive
from about the 11th century, in some of the miniatures of eastern

India (Nepal, Bihar and Bengal). In the south its extents can be

seen in the earlier layer-paintings at Tirumaruttikunram and the
second layer of paintings at Tirumalaipuram. In Uttar Pradesh it
is visible in the Pauchatantra paintings on the ceiling of a mandapa
of a Vishnu temple at Madanpur in Lalitpur district. In book
illustrations, it was extensively in use in west Indian, mainly
Gujrati miniatures, and in limited range in east Indian M.S.S.
illustrations and copper-plate drawings. Outside India, it is felt in
Javanese sculpture of Panataram temples and also in the contem-
porary Murals in Pagan, Burma and Siam.
South India : —The specimens of painting from south are
found in the walls of the Vijayalaya Cholesvara temple at Nartta-

malai in the old Pudukkottal state (c. 1 100 A. D ) of the Brihadi-

svara temple at Tanjore (c. 1 100 A. D.) on the brick-built walls

affixed in front of the cave (c. 1100 A. D.) and so forth.

Vijaynlayacholisvara Temple In the temple large scale paint-
ed figures of Mahakala. Devi and Siva Nataraja are still visible
along with other minor figures. These figures represent both the
1 . Indian Painting, Introduction and notes by W. G. Archer p. 7.
2. Op. at. pp. 45-47.
Literature and Learning 387
earlier and the later Chola styles. The figure of Mahakala here
resembles the I ripurantakala of BfhadlSvara temple at Tanjorc.
The later Chola style is seen in the form and shape of hair decora-
tion, Karanda-mukuta, costumes of the Dcvl. Outlines of the
figures are red and the colours extensively used are terre-verte
yellow and red of varied tones, green, black, white, high-lighte in
white are used in figure tips. Here along with the Chola forms
and physiognomy the classical modelling of colour though
in lessened form, is apparent. The mediaeval trend is found in
the fragment of two rows of flying figures whose faces and busts,
flattened out on the nose's surface, arc shown in three quarter
profile, and noses, eyes and mouths arc drawn in sharp pointed

angular lines (in the second recess, to the right from the entrance).

Brihadihara-Tcmple : —There arc two layers of wall paintings
the lower one is earlier. The secncs here represent ‘Siva’ along
with ‘ganas’ ‘gandharvas’, ‘apsaras’ etc. The paintings arc
executed in the surface of lime. The outlines arc drawn in light
red or brown, later on deepened by black or reddish browns.
Pigments used for contours, back grounds and decorations arc
yellow ochre, red ochre, tcrrc-vcrtc, brown earth, white, black and
lapis lazuli-blue. The figures look flatter and thinner different
from classical tradition. In the frescoes of this temple a mixture
of lime and sand was used for preparing the ground.*
The seated figures show the classical tradition as they have
broadly modelled lines, ample curves and colour tones and graceful
bhahgas etc., while the standing figures look stiflf and idealistic.
The medieval factor is seen here in the treatment of face invari-
ably in three quarter profile, the fish shaped eyes drawn sharply to
pointed angles in both directions, long beak-like sharply pointed
nose and pointedly angular chins and fully plastic lips drawn in
double curves. The two modes and visions 'classical’ and
medieval’ commingle in these paintings But according to
S. K. Sarasvatl, the south adhered more tenaciously to the former
than the latter."

388 Bhoja Paramnra and his Times
Laksmisvara Mandapa The other specimens of the wall
paintings are found in the first layer on the brick-walls of the
outmost chamber on the second storey of the Lakshmisvara
mandapa at Tirumalai. The figures shown here are in full or
three quarter faces with eyes wide-open look out intently towards
a definite point, the hands are held in anjali-pose. The classical
tradition here is visible in the body-shaping colour modelling
while the medieval one is evident in the sharp lines and angles,
beaklike pointed noses, upline curves, and flat treatment of orna-
ments. The colours used are white, ochre, terre-verte, gray and
red. The black-black ground seems to have been special feature
at Tirumalai. The paintings on the outside of the brick fecade
also belong to the same category.
The second paintings on the ceilings of the outer-most cham-
ber of the Lakshmisavara mandapa and on the walls of rockcut
caves exhibit thinned volume and the further sharpened lines and
the bhaftgas without plastic flexibility.
The portrait-statues ofTirumala Nayaka of Madurai, the
famous bronzes representing Krishnadevaraya and bis queens.
King Achyutarayas bronze portrait, the portrait statue of Vishpu-
vardhana Hoyasala from Halebid, that of Rajaraja from Tanjore
and Kanchipuram, portraits of three generations of kings Simha-
vishnu from Mahabalipuram, the portrait in relief, of king
Gandaraditya set up by his sorrowing queen SembianmadevT, the
grand aunt of Rajaraja, are some of the well known specimens.
These portraits according to Sivaramamurti represent viddhachitra
for the drama of RajaSekhara style the Viddasalabhanjika as
based on a portrait statue of a princess.i Somadeva also refers
to ths Viddha type of portraits.^ The term Viddha also occurs in
the Tilakamafijari.

LUeraturc and Learning 389
These Viddha portraits were also used to bring lovers together
as is proved by several stories of the Kathasarilsagara where the
marriages of princes and noblemen arc described as arranged with
famous beauties of the day by exchange of their painting carried
hither and thither by land or sea. Sculptural versions of pictures
of both princes and princesses illustrating a Jataka story is found
at Barabudur.
It is the literary reference that helps us to understand the por-
trait statues of an ancient India in their proper perspective. The
remark that Tejabprda cried when he saw the portrait of his
mother carved of wood, writes Sivaramamurti, need not surprise
us if we have no misapprehension about the ability of ancient
Indian artists in portrait work.*
West India
The northern tradition or the medieval conception did not
make its headway in the western India before the 12th century
A.D.2 From about A.D. 800 onwards, opines Krarnrisch, "there
is a change in the physiognomy of Western, Dcccani and Mid-
Indian painting." The treatment of faces shows complete disrup-
tion of the classical tradition. The outline of what was once a
three quarter profile now cuts across the eye, which henceforward
and until the end of the Idth century lies outside the face, on the
flat colour of the painted-ground. The nearer eye, wide-open
and seen as it were the front, travels across the entire face
extending as far as the car. The tones of classical painting
were consumed in the luminosity of a flat colour area. The figures
were now limned in a daring tracery of clovc-faiicd patterns,
speckled on to its red or golden radiance. This new style of
Indian painting was first applied to illuminated Palm-leaf and
later to paper manuscripts. It was prolific in Gujarat, and to the
north of it in Uttar Pradesh and Mid-India from whence it spread
to the cast.®
Gujarat
In Gujarat the art flourished under the aegis of the Chalukyas
who were great patrons of Jainism. All the Gujarati paintings
1. Memoirs of Arch. Survey of India, No. 73, p. 92.
2. The Struggle for Empire, p. 687.
3* Krarnrisch, The arl of India, pp. 47-48,
390 bhoja Paramhra and his fimei
are M. S. S. illustrations the miniature executed on palm-leaf.
They are found in various styles. One of these according to
S. K. Sarasvati,* is relatively simple without any decorations,
Jewellery here is simply suggested and not actually worked out.
The line strokes ate relatively thick, the curves are full and steady
and the drawing sure and unfaltering. The profiles and three-
quarters are given preference to human figures. Eyes, noses,
chins, beards etc. are drawn in sharp-pointed angles. The eyes
are drawn in two parabolic curves with sharp-pointed sides, set
on the same level, the eye-lashes being drawn in sharp and exten-
ded bow-like curves. Both eyes are shown. The earliest examples
of this class are a couple of paintings in a palm-leaf M.S. dated in
A.D. 1127.®
The next Gujarati style came into being from about the middle
of the 12th century A. D The lines here are sharp, broken and
somewhat desiccated. The eyes are separately on different levels,
the corners of the eyes meet at the nose. Earliest examples of this

class can be seen in a M.S. (No. 1155) dated 1161, A D belonging

to the Viravijayaji Sri Jain Svetambara Jnana Mandir Bhandar at
Chavi near Baroda.

Malwa :

Several Jain-book illustrations of the Western style are found

in the fort of Mandu (23 miles from Dbar). Sbree Ramabhai,

Nawab of Ahmedabad has taken the notices of at least 60 book
illustrations from Mandu and one of these is found in the Jain-
Kalpadruma which he has published. Besides, several copper-
plates of Paramara Bhoja and his ancestors and successors reveal
the same style bearing the figure of ‘Garuda’ of this style. More-
over, some wall-paintings of the time of Bhoja’s nephew, Udaya-

ditya (1059-80) are found from Belur, These contain the faces of

males. Here the nose and the eyes are drawn in sharp-pointed
angles, the stiff-limbs etc. show the same pattern. It is therefore
reasonable to infer that the western style was popular in Malwa
as well.*
1. The Struggle for Empire, pp. 688-689.
2. SantinUtha Temple Bhandar, Cambay, M S. No. 62.
3. Bhdratki a chitrakala. pp. 44-45.

(jcncrally, the glowing colours of the west Indian paintings

are red, yellow, blue and golden. Technically, they arc examples
of Perfect craftsmanship and reveal the inner meaning and psycho-
logy of Jainism to which the majority of paintings owe their origin
and inspiration.^
Eastern India Bengal, Bihar, Nepal and Tibet
No specimen of cast Indian painting earlier than the Pala rule
has been found so far. The remnants arc, however, illuminations
on Palm-leaves and paper of MSS. and on wooden covers of
MSS But in Bengal arc found three drawings on copper-plates,
which may be said to belong to about the 12lh and 1 3th centuries
and arc Brahmanical in inspiration. These miniatures, according
to S K. Saraswati, arc mural paintings and arc largely guided by
the earlier traditions of Bagh and Ajanta.2
The colours used in these paintings arc orpiment yellow, while,
indigo blue, Indian-ink-black or Kajjala, Cinnabar red but not
Indian red, and green. But highlights are given in white while the
outline is drawn either in black or red.
The main divinity is relatively larger and is shown in the centre
flanked on two sides by lesser divinities. Vacant spaces are
filled by flying semi-divine beings with vegetal and ornamental
decorations.^
The plastically modelled masses of the cast-Indian miniature
paintings resemble the classical tradition. The fine specimens of
this arc the illuminations of two prajnanaparamita MSS. executed
in the 5th and 6th regnal years of king Mahipala^ the Gandavyuha
MS. in the possession of Roerich and another Prajnaparamita

MS. dated in the Nepalese era 191 (A.D. 1071).“ j he colour

modelling shows the thinning down of the plastically modelled
treatment. The line however, is still vital. But in certain illumi-
nations they rc.scmblc the medieval tradition as the lines are

.
