weak, brisk, faltering, broken and sharp such as in the Cambridge
MS. No. Add. 1643 dated 1015 A.D. and the 13th century copper
plate drawings from Sundarbans and Chittangong.*
The lines of the east Indian tradition though have the same
predilection lor brisk and extended curves of the western one,
are not unoften broken and are drawn without any warmth of
feeling or emotion and show sensitiveness and tempered lyricism.
The colours here are applied in thinner and subdued tones. The
reason for this was that the leisured romance was no longer in
tune with contemporary conditions.^
East Indian manuscript illuminations refer to the regnal years
of the Pala kings but those from Nepal are invariably dated in the
Nepalese era. The Nepalese paintings comprise MSS. illumina-
tions, prabhats, painted banners and painted wooden MSS.
covers. Dated and illuminated MSS are known from the 11th
century onward on the dated banners from the sixteenth. Some
painted Nepalese banners, ascribed to dates onwards from
A.D. 900 were discovered at Tun-Huang, which resemble the
eastern style but inferior version. The same is the case with the

earlier (11th century A.D.) MSS. illuminations where the line

has a largeness, a simple vivacito, and something of an emotional
'' quality. But towards the 12th succeeding centuries there is a
progressive desication of colour-modelling capacity of the line
which became crisp, dry and brittle, curves shorter and clipped.
Sharp and hectic western lines and painted angles also made their
intrusion. The wooden cover for example, of about A.D. 1200
belongs to this phase.’
The remnants of Pala manuscript illuminations are preserved
in the Government library Nepal, in the library of Rajagnru
Hemaraja, in the collections of Avanindra Thakur, Ajitaghosh
and of Sri Jalan, in art gallery collection of Benaras and in the
Royal Asiatic Society of Calcutta. Outside India the examples

of these are found from Boslan (America) Oxford University

(.England), Detroit Institute (America) etc,*

The Prda style lost its importance in Bengal and Bihara in
about I3th century due to political disturbances. It, however,
thrived in Nepal upto J6th century A.D."
DANCE MUSIC AND DRAMA
The relation of Dance, Music and Drama with other fine
arts such as painting and sculpture is like the twin-sister- A
favourite theme in sculpture is the disposition of limbs, in
difTerent dance postures known as ‘sthfinas’, ‘karpas’ and ‘angha-
ras’. The groups of these form never-ending pleasing motifs of
architecture. ‘Siva’ as the lord of dance has been represented in
different ‘sthanas’, like the ‘chatura’, ‘lalitatilaka’, ‘lalita’,
‘katisama’, ‘filK^ha’ ‘bhujahgatrasitalca’ and so forth. It is in con-
nection with the glorious Sandhjfi nrila of ‘Siva that the Natya-
sabha or Nulya-mandapa is made special feature to receive the
bronze image of Najaraja a feature in every south Indian Temple.
Sarasvati as the presiding deity over fine-arts which includes
dance is represented in fine dance moods in many a sculpture from
the Hoyasala temples in Mysore.
General rejoicing is sugnested by dance and music. Among the
most marvellous carvings from Mt. Abu arc many dance figures
such as dancing apsaras. The beautiful sculptural representations
of dancing are the Bhutesvara-yakshT listening to the parrot in
lovely posture and the pictures presented by Kalidasa of maids
dancing before Siva at the time of evening worship. These are
marvellously caved in the holy temple of Mahakula at Ujjain.
In painting and sculpurc, the dance of Siva with his form
encircled by his numerous hands, is more than vividly represented.
The Chola-painting from the Brhadl^vara temple at Tanjore depict
the dancers in the presence of Siva danciug to the accompaniment
of music. There arc numerous sculptural remains of Siva’s dance
in the ‘chatura’ and ‘lalita’ pose at Ellora, from central India,
Uttara-Pradesh, Orissa and Bengal.
1. BhUrata-kl-cfiUrakalU, p. 40.
2- Ibid.
394 ihoja Paramara and his TimdS
Wherever dance is shown in sculpture to the accompaniment
of music, writes, Sivaramamurti,’ it is always ‘nritta’ or pure
dance, as the soul of 'nritta’ or suggestive dance is ‘abhinaya’ or
gesture, and music has only a secondary place and even has no
place sometimes as it is said in the Natyaiastra®
“where the song is expressed by gesticulation there should
be no music”.

To understand the unbroken traditions of Bharaia Na}ya and

the great suggestive significance of it, it is sculpture and painting
that aid most. The mastery of iconography depends on ‘citra’
which is its turn on dancing.® Bharata-Natyasastra mentions as
many as 108 modes of dancing. All these forms of dancing are
depicted on either side of a ‘gopura’ in the Nafaraja temple at
Chidambaram.
Music And Musical Inslcuments. It is archaeology that
presents us a faithful picture of the glory of music in India. Music
as one of the fine arts was a great hobby, and there was practi-
cally no ‘nagaraka’ who was not acquainted with this one of the
foremost branches of the ‘vaiharikaiilpas’ or arts for amusements
as the Ramayana styles them.^ Some of the musical instruments of
ancient India which were in the early centuries of the Christian
era have now disappeared, though in one form or other they still
linger in distant places like Burma, Siam, Malaya and Java. The
harp-shaped ‘vina’ which now survives only in Burma, occurs in
sculptures The time scale was connected as much by the tapering
of hands as by the drum and sounding of the cymbols. This was
very popular and there are many early sculptures representing it.
In fact in the early centres ‘karatala’ was more popular than
‘kamsyatala’. The glory of arobu-mrdanga, a musical instrument
now having its existence in India especially in south under the name
‘jalataranga’ but mentioned as ‘ambumrdanga’ is stated in a media-

val Inscription. There arc several sculptural representations of
music and musical instruments of the time. The delightful motifs

and the bracket figures on the pillars and sometimes nearer the
ceilings, depicting a damsel softly filling the flute with sweet
musical notes, arc some of the most, significant features of the
mediaeval sculpture, especially of the Chandellas, These are
beautifully carved in the Khajuraho group of temples.
Architectural Activities Of Bhoja
The architectural activities of Bhoja deserve special notice.
The Udaipur PrasastI glorifies him with the construction of

several temples dedicated to Kednresvara, Ramesvara (?)

Kalanala and Rudra ^ Mcrutunga tells us that Bhoja rebuilt
the city of Dhara.2 A Hoyasala grant from Bclur dated 1117 A.D.
records that Dhara was made prosperous by Bhoja.^ Unfortunately
all of his temples have been destroyed by the hands of time,
renovators and iconoclastic Mussalmans. But D.C. Ganguly'*
doubts about Bhoja’s participation In the building of the great
temple of Somanaih in Kathiawar or of Ramesvara in southern
India.*^ Beside these two temples there were several others,
dedicated to the same gods in diflerent provinces of India. At
Bclur in the Bombay state, there is an old temple of Ramesvara ®
Similarly, a temple of Somanath is to be found in Deolalas in

the Rewa state (Madhya Pradesh)'

The most famous of Bhoj’s temples was the one built at
Dhara dedicated to Sarasvatl styled Sarasvatl-Sadana or Bharati

Pillars. Bhoja erected several pillars. An iron pillar had
recently been found at Dhara. prcbably, Kulachandra Bhoja’s
general after returning from Anahilvar, set up this iron pillar to
celebrate his victory over the Chalukyas of Gujarat. ‘ The pillar
when entire, was of a total length of 43 feet 4 inches and an
average width of 10 inches each way. The largest portion measures
24 feet 3 inches, and is square in section through out, the second
1 1 feet and 7 inches of which 8 feet 6 inches is square and 3 feet
1 inches of octagonal section, and the S^d piece, 7 feet 6 inches in
length, and with the ception of a circular collar at the end, about

8 inches deep is of octagonal section throughout (there are) a

number of small holes at intervals in its sides, varying in depth
from 1 to 3 inches and in diameter about 1 inches. They run up
each of the four sides of the square shaft and the coresponding
faces of the Octagon. ..The holes in all probability were made as
the mass of the Uphill was built up in order to hold the ends of
crobars or levers which the workmen could better handle and
roll over the great heavy columns bit by bit of semimolten metal
which was added and welded on to the white hot strump of the
shaft. The crobars were removed and shifted from place to

The pillar was probably topped by a ‘garuda’ the figure of
which was engraved on the seals of Bhoja and other Paramaras.
Three pieces of the pillar have been recovered and perhaps there
still lies somewhere concealed a fourth piece and the total length
of the original piece was probably 50 feet.^
The Muhammadan rulers of Malawa in the l5th century
destroyed all the temples of Dhara and used the material for new
structures of their town, like the Lat Masjid outside which lies
one of the portions of the iron column. In its neighbourhood
is the mosqne of Kamalamaula “where not only pillars and
building-stones from Hindu temples have been utilized, but a
large portion of the flooring of the praying chamber is paved
with black marble slabs, formerly covered with Sanskrit inscrip-
tions, and even the lining of the Mihrub itself was found to
consist of similar slabs in this case with the engraed faces merely
turned inwards and the inscriptions thereon unharmed.® The
slabs were found to contain two long inscriptions and several

were allowed by the convention of society in those days and were
not taken literally as the modern man would do. Of the fragments
“all that can be said at present is that in all probability, they
belong to odes to Bhoja of vast dimentions” and probably contai-

ned strings of verses in praise of the bow (Kodapda) of

Bhoja.'
There are two Sanskrit grammatical inscriptions in the
‘Nagari’ characters, on two stone-pillars in the mosque near the
t6mb of Maulana-Kamaluddin at Dhar. The mosque is still
popularly called ‘Bhojaraja-ki-Nisal’ or Raja Bhoja’s College.
There is ample evidence to show that the mosque was built not
only with the materials of the temple of Sarasvati discovered in
1902, but on the very side of it. The existence of numerous
mutilated large Nagari-inscriptions on the pavement and of
a part of the plinth of the original temple with a “Gomukha”
on the north-east corner proves the fact beyond doubt.
Moreover, the image of Sarasvati recording its installation in

the temple of Raja-Bhoja himself in Sarfivat 1091 (A.D 1035)

exists in the British Museum and its facsimile has been published
in Rupam.2
The two pillars bearing the incriptions are among those that
support the dome of the prayer hall. The one near the pulpit
containing the Sanskrit alphabet faces the east. The other, at a
distance from the pulpit to the south, contains the Sanskrit verbal
terminations facing the south with two Sanskrit verses incribed
over it containing the names of the two successors of Raja
Bhoja.
The pillars being of gray lime-stone unsuited for engraving
have undergone partial decay in some places. The surface has
been cut through by lines which have deformed some of the letters.
The right corner of the base of one of the inscriptions has altoge-
ther disappeared Most of the other numerous Nagari-inscriptions
lying in this building were engraved on durable black-stone-slabs.

He built up Jumma-Masjid. But when Sultan Gujarati conquered

Blit when the streams that (lowed into the valley were counted

it was found that they were only 359. The engineers cast about
for means to bring up the streams to the mystic number 365 that

were wanted by the sage; then a chief of the Gonds who then

(as now) liwid in this bit of the Dnndnka forests pointed out

another stream nearby, which tvns fed by 6 hill springs. The
chief name of Kalia and the grateful engineers called the river
Kaliasot. But the Knliasot which could increase the drainage
area of the lake about 500 square miles, flowed outside the well
enclosed valley, which was to be the bed of the proposed lake. To
utilise the water of the Knliasot ths engineers built near Bhopal
20 miles off a magnificent c\»clopcan dam constructed exactly like
the other two, but immensely broad for its length and height.
Thus was created a storage tank from which the surplus water
could feed the lake during the 3 dry months that succeeded the
rains. From here the river ran at right angles to its former course
round the hills into the Betwa valley and became almost valuable
feeder to the construct of the great lake, because it carried the
surplus waters of the great lake. "The greater dam was so cons-
tructed as to turn the Kaliasot in to the Betwa and from the top
of the Bund the old bend of the Kaliasot can even now be seen.
The bund itself is now so covered with .lungle as to be invisible
unless when carefully scrutinised.
Thus was created the Bhojapur-kakc, one unbroken sheet cl
water except where Inlands added to its beauty. It ttas in
places 100 ft. deep and on all sides surrounded by hills. It was
provided with the water-weir by means of "a cutting through the
solid rocks of one of the lower hills on the east-side." It is at the
blunt apex of trianglnr valley opening from near the great dams
and is probably 2 miles from it in a direct line. Its position so
far from the dame offers another proof of the practical ability of
the Hindu engineers of the time for any error in the level would
have quietly destroyed the dam which, though stone-faced on both
sides was filled in by earth and could not long have with-stood an
1. Ibid.
402 Bboja PararnSra and his Times
overflow. These are signs on its rocky and unbroken sides
which show that high water mark was within 6 feet of its top.
The water weir is now buried in impenetrable jungle and was
accidently discovered by Col. Kincaid The village of DIpa

(from Sanskrit DIpa, island standing on a small hill and now a

station on the Bhopal state Railway testified to the fact that it was
in Bhoja’s time an island, 2 miles in length spread on a hill
standing on the borders of the Bhojpur-lake Below the water-
weir was Bhojpur where more than one group of large flat
stones mark the sites of Bhoja’s boat house. The people of the

locality still believe that (hey were used as a boat house by Raja

Bhoja, who every morning used to sail across the lake to visit
the fort or temples. In order to obtain the materials for the
construction of their mosques, the Muhnmadans demolished many
Hindu temples. In . that ruined city are the remains of a temple
celebrated for its gigantic polished quartzite Lingam. Not far
from the northern end of the lake was built Bhojpal, Bhoja's-pal

(Bund) on which nestles the ancient fort to the west of modern

city. The fort is on the brick of the storage tank already described
and is filled with remains of Jaina temples and the fine sculptures
that characterise them. Beyond the western shore away on the
mountains rose the powerfully built fort of Gonar defended
by a deep dike cut out of the solid rock. The vast watery plains
cooled the atmosphere for several miles and held up the water so
that the river Betwa was never in floods.'
Regrettably enouch, this wonderful piece of ancient Hindu
engineering was destroyed fay Shah-Hussain, the greatest of the
Mandu-Sultans, a little more than 400 years after it was built. He
coveted the fertile land which formed the silt-covered bed of the
lake for agricultural purposes and ordered the smaller of the
dames to be broken. It required an array of labourers to work
for 3 months to destroy it and 3 years elapsed before the lake was
emptied of its waters. At the same time it took 30 years before
the bed became dry enough for human habitation. Skab-Hussain
however, did not liie to see the consummation of his hopes.
Then numerous little towns arose where the lake existed. The
Bhopi'l-railway-station runs where Bhoja’s pleasure boats sailed
1. Imperial Gazetteer of India Vol. VHI, 121,

in the breeze and India’s sons pass across the bed of the lake on
which sailed their forefathers nine centuries ago. Ignorant
of the history of the site, notwithstanding the silent testimony of
the great bund, the ruined temples and the large stone pillars of
Bhoja’s boat house part, which they arc handled by the Railway-
cngincs'snorting with a speed unknown to Bhoja’s boats which
once silently slid along. Human eyes stare unintclligently on
the jungle which has grown all round the verdant hills and stand
primarily looking on and laughing at the mutations of human
ambitions.
The glory of the lake still survives in the well known sayings :
“Tal to Bhopal tal.”
Dusaren Talaiya hain.”
“The lake is Bhopal lake, others are just ponds.”
The site of the lake justifies its reputation. As one climbs the
hill which stands in the centre and upon which Bhoja started to
build a temple of ^’iva, one can observe the huge bowl which
once held the expanse of water. V. A. Smith writes “the great
Bhojapur lake, to the south-east of Bhopal, formed by massive
embankments, closing the outlet in a ciiclc hills was the noblest
monument and continued to testify to the skill ol his engineers
untill the 15th century, when the dam was cut by order of a
Muhammedan King, and the water drained ofT.^

404 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
every-day in water brought from ‘Papa-^odhana-Tirtha’ in
Kashmir. He was enabled to fulfil this vow by the good officers
of a certain padmaraja, who excavated a tank for this purpose
at Kapatesvara, with heaps of gold that Bhoja sent. Kapatesvara
observes D.C. Ganguly, is the modern Kather.^ Padmaraja, a
betel-seller and a favourite of Ananta, the contemporary king of
Kashmir, brought Bhoja’s vow to fruition by the regular despatch
of a large number of Jars filled with the holy water from the
‘Tirtha’. The remains of the enclosure which the emperor
constructed in order to collect the waters of the sacred spring,
are still extant. “The latter now rises”, says Stein, “in a circular
tank of at least 60 yards dia-meter, which is enclosed by a solid
stone wall, and by steps-leading to the water From the
formation of the ground, it is evident that this tank had been
formed by closing artificially the gully in which the spring rises
on the hill sides.
Local tradition runs that a king of the Deccan, once being
disfigured by the growth of a number of horns on his head, took

his bath in that ‘kunda’ (tank) and was relieved of his trouble

whereupon he records his gratitude by surrounding it with a wall.
Sir A. Stein thinks that there is a good deal of truth in this story
although in those early ages, there was less facility for the
conveyance of anything from one country to another. The
Muhammadan historians- relate that Ganges water was brought
regularly to Gujarat for the baths of Somanath. D.C. Ganguly*
rightly suggests that a great king like Bhoja could make tempo-
rary arrangements for bringing water regularly to him from that
particular tank in Kashmir.

court of Chitor. He had named the Statue of Siva after his name
‘Bhoja Svamideva.^ As has already been noted, Bhoja and the

Bhoja raised the fortress of Mandir or Mandapadurga and

In addition he built bathing-ghats and temples at Ujjayini.* The

Thus we .see that Bhoja was a great builder, perhaps the

of Bhoja was (he transfer of his kingdom from Ujjayini to

Prof. H.C. Ray opines that there is little evidence available to
corroborate the extensive building operations which are ascribed
to Bhoja in the Udaipur Prasasti but as Buhler has remarked, ‘‘it
is very probable that a prince so fond of display as he must have
adorned his capital and perhaps even foreign-sacred places with
architectural monuments”. But of these, thanks no doubt largely
to the iconoclastic zeal of the early Turkish conquerors, very few
specimens have survived to our times.^ D.C. Ganguly- states that
he founded a city named Bhojapura and built a large number of
temples in honour of god Siva. All these attainments of Bhoja in
different spheres of life establish his claim to be regarded as one of
the greatest kings of mediaeval India.

CHAPTBR X
A GESBRAL ESTIMATE OB BIJOJA'S CHARACTER
Bhoja’s vcr.^utiiiiy and achicvemcnis arc, in a way, unique. He
\sasun eminent author, and a seasoned statesman. His achieve-
ments at diflcrcni states as a Ivinf?, as an ardent lijihtcr, and as a
distinguished scholar were characterised by rare courage and
decisive energy. Acknowledged as one of the talented writers he
has to hjs credit a %‘ast and varied Ijiicraturc, A men of high
character, Bhoja applied his scholarship and statesmanship to
the l>ctlcrment of his subjects and to the enhancement of the glory
of his^ faith. Bhoja ims acquired the training and culture prescribed
for a Kshntriya ruler.
Malwa v.-ns not merely the home of wealth and valour under
Bhoja, out it was an abode ot Dharma also. Bhoja was secular
and benevolent king of high order. Throughout his life he stood
firm in the faith ofhi.s forefathcr.s. He was a great devotee of
Siva made pilnrimagcs to dilTerent religious places all over India,
gave liberal donations and thus enriched ail the religions of the
a'"?. If giving of grants was generosity, his generosity had the
magnificence, for he preferred to scatter individual gifts rather than
set up institution.a! organisation. He contributed much to the cause
of religion bv erecting various temples.
The Literary taste of Bhoja was unbound He gave the kind
patronage to the scholars, poets and the learned. He himself was a
versatile literary figure, a polymath and a pioneer of the literary
tradition of polymaths like Kshemendra of Ka.shmir and Hema-
chandra of Gujarat. He wrote books and ver.ses on various subjects.
Verse 18 of liic Udaipur-Prasasti alludes to his extensive knowle-
dge and be.stow-s on him the lilie, ‘Kaviraja’, the king of poets’.^
He presided over learned assemblies like Sri Harsha, Mihir Bhoja
and Mahipfila. The Bhojaslu'da at Dhara was a university of all
India importance.^
1. Udaipur Prasasti, V. 18. E.l. I. p. 235.
2. A' /. vni p. 96 ff.
468 Bhoja ParamUra and his Times
Bhoja has been the universal standard for comparing anyone
who is liberal towards Literature. The RajataraftginI says, “He

(Kshitipati) and king Bhoja, famous for their great liberality,

were at the same moment both poets themselves and friends of
poets”.*
Abul-Fazl in the Ain-e-Akbari mentions that Bhoja conquered
several countries. He was known for his keen sense of Justice and
for benevolence. His court was crowded with 400 selected scho-
lars.^ Dhara at this time was the centre of political, intellectual
and cultural activities. It was known for its skilful musicians and
learned scholars.^ Merutuftga’ and Ballala-Pandit^ both refer that
poets and scholars from different parts of India used to come to
attend the court of Dhara with a hope to receive rewards for their
works. In one place, Bhoja orders his premier “Let him, who is

a fool be outside my city, even (though) a Brahmana, let him (who

is) wise remain within my city, even (though) a potter”.® Several

men well-versed in letters migrated to Dhara, settled permanently
there and sought their livelihood for Bhoja’s liberal donations to the
cause of literature.* Bhoja, with the help of institutions and liberal
bounties, boosted up the morality and intellect of the masses He
cannot be omitted from any literary history of Gujarat. From
A.D. 940 to 1050, Malwa and the mainland of Gujarat formed one
Integral kingdom and its greatest cultural and literary centre was
Dhara. Further, the literary activities of Hemchandra were mainly
inspired by Bhoja.® In the Udaipur-prasasti be is praised for his
kind head and heart." Kalhana in his RajataranginI*" and

A General Estimate of lihoja's Character 409
Mammata^ in his “Kavyaprakasa” praises Bhoja for his liberality
and benevolence.
Bhoja, the greatest scholar of his age, steeped in the immense
philosophical literature produced during two milicniums before his
time, and the greatest warrior of the century who had tested
catastrophic reverses of fortune since his boyhood, always arrived
with him the thought that life and its enjoyments were uncertain
as the waves of the Sea. He would frequently repeat to himself the
following stanza.'
*]f men but saw the hand of death impending over their

heads;

Even food would give no joy, much less the deeds that are not
a right."
The remarks and quouuions embodied in the copper plate
grant, show how the sense of transitoriness of ail things had burnt
itself deep into the soul of Bhoja.^
Mcrutuhga narrates a number of anecdoles relating to Bhoja
from which this extraordinary historical figure emerges clearly.
Above and beyond the conquests he achieved, apart from the rich
magnificence with which he aderned shrines, rivers and itomes of
learning, appears the sage comparable only to the royal sages
Janaka-vidchi aid Marcus-Aurclius.^ To him virtuous deeds were
the only justification for life. As he got up every day he asked
himself "what good can f do today". One of his sayings indicates

the spirit of a Karma) ogin.

“z\s men arc always between the teeth of death, what they
give and what they enjoy may alone be said to be their own, about
the rest tlicrc is a doubt, The sun Avill set in the western sky and
take away with him part of life,

4i6 Bhoja Paramara and ids Times
Men ask me daily—what’s the news today ? Are you quite hale
and glad and welt ? How can wc keep the body’s health. Is not
our life departing day by day ? Perform today the duty of tomo-
rrow, the afternoons’ before the noon.
For death would not wait to see if you have done the duty of
the day or not.
Are life’s disasters now destroyed ? Is the rush of illness quite
arrested, that all these men are lost in mirth” ?'
Power, glory and wealth which came to him plentifully had no
permanent value for him. In one of the verses he says : —
“If I have not given my wealth to supplicants before the
bright Sun sets in the west.
Can any one man that is living assure me to whom this my
wealth belongs on the morrow.”®
On his bracelets were engraved certain memorable sentences‘

Uncertain by nature is human prosperty; the time of possession

is the time for bestowal. Misfortune is certain to reach you quite
soon.
Too late to discover a chance for well-doing.”®
Filled with these thoughts Bhoja would go daily to the pavilion
of distribution and bestow gold on petitioners waiting at his gate
and summoned one after another by his attendants. This habit of
giving away his wealth to those who sought, became a vice and

A General Estimate of Bhoja's Character
made wide holes in the royal treasury. His minister Rohaka was
alarmed but could not summon enough courage to protest to the
king in person, against the extravagance of the royal gifts.^
The most pompous and richly adorned dress of the princes
could neither dazzle Bhoja’s eyes nor could deviate him from his
daily duties. He was easily accessible to the poorest of his subjects.
On one such occasion in winter season, when he was walking in
front of a temple he heard a certain man reciting stanzas to the
following effect.
“The fire in my belly pinched with hunger, it blows and
parches my lips ”
The king summoned the distressed the next day and gave him
a large present for this sally of wit.^
Nuiherous passages in the inscriptions as well as the Praban-
dhas, may lead the modern man to conclude that Bhoja was extra-
ordinarily vain and was pleased with silly flattery. But the praises
of the king by his courtiers and others were not intended to be
understood literally , nor were they so. Indeed, even today the con-
ventions of certain religious sects require that when two men talked

together, each should call the other a God (deva) and himself a

slave (dasa). Like the English words “thanks” these are but dead

locutions, mechanically repeated and meaning nothing. Such speci-
mens of conventional politeness ought to be considered along with
the words used when, as already quoted, the poorest of the poor
unhesitatingly scolded kings of their evil deeds. These references
are suflicient to show the actual relations between the kings and
their subjects.®
Bhoja had the outlook of a philosopher. As heard with indul-

gence Dhanapala, was a fanatic Jain; who in a trechant way con-

demned Vedic-sacrifices and ridiculed §iv?, his guardian-god. Even
the cow did not escape the poet’s pointed shaft. But the emperor
smiled, followed his own way and cherished him as a friend”,^
There is no reason to doubt the authenticity of these stories.
They show that according to the ideas of the time, the royal throne

412 Bhoja ParamSra and his Times
was not isolated by the atmosphere of divinity and kings behave
like ordinary men to all their subjects.
Bhoja’s Death
On the whole, Bhoja was a beloved king of his subjects. His '
death was a great national loss. The friends and foes alike bemo- '
aned his death by means of a number of elegies. After his death
in C.A.D. 1054, the second empire of Gurjaradesa began to break
up. The Udaipur Prasasti* says

“When the devotee of Bharga (Siva) whose brilliance resembled

the sun 'i.e. Bhoja) had gone to the mansion of gods, the earth like

Dhara, was filled with dense darkness of his foe (and) his hereditary

warriors became infirm.”
Bilhana,= though attached to a rival court, mourned the death
of Bhoja in these touching words
“Bhoja was the lord of the earth and not, indeed, comparable

to vulgar kings, woe is me, “Dhara, cried to him (i.e. Bilhapa)

through the voice of the pigeons nesting on the lofty towers of

her sates” Why dids’t thou not come into his (Bhoja’s) presence.”

An anonymous eulogy says
“King, when the cloud of your hand had begun its auspicious

ascent in the ten quarters of the heavens; And was raining the

nectar flood of gold, with the splendour of the trembling golden
bracelet flickering like lightening, the river of fame became swollen,
all Virtues were refreshed like the earth.
The wide lake of petitioners was filled and the forest fire of
the poverty of the learned was extinguished,
Like the wishing tree, having frightened away by his munificent
gifts all poverty on the earth.
Like an incarnate Brhaspati, having swiftly put together vari-

ous compositions;

1. E.I. I, p. 238, V. 21.
2. Vikiamahkadevachania, ...XVIII, vv-96.
3. PC. pp. 76-77
A General Estimate of Bhoja's Character .413
In Radheyavadha like Arjuna, summoned speedily by the

hands of immortals;

Whose hearts were long ago made to wish for him by his glory
for his presence, and soon to heaven he went.’
On his death, tells us Ballala Pandit, a verse was recited by

one Kalidasa, a poet;

“To day (hath) Dhara no foundation, Sarasvati no support,

the scholars (are) all scattered, king Bhoja gone to heaven”. ^

If we sum up the career of Bhoja as a whole, we come to the
truth that he left such deep imprints on the sands of time that he
became the one great Indian emperor remembered by prince and-
peasant alike even uptoday.
Even while he lived, his name passed into history as an ideal
king. He had a splendour of character which has left an ineffece-
able tradition in India. Of all the great kings who flourished in
India, it is only his name that remains from one end to the other
as a synonym of learning and generosity, a distinction unique in
the history of the world.
Most of magnificent of all the kings of India, Bhoja will live in
history even as the Udaipur prasasti styles him ..
“The illustrious Bhoja, the poet king
He achieved, he gave, he knew.
And what he did, none but he could,
What greater praise can mortal ever covet
Bhoja's ability, his heroism and his unstained charity moved
even a cook to seek his audience and sing.'*
“His valour has ruined the race of his enemies. His glory has
filled the wide vessel of earth. His bounty has reached all the wants
of petitioners. As each does extend to the shores of the great sea.
His faith, it does mount to the feet of the husband of Parvatl, dau-
ghter of the lord of the mountain.

414 Bhoja Paramara and his Times
CONCLUSION
Our task of presenting a picutre of the political condition in
general and the cultural history in particular of the time of Bhoja
has now come to a close, and only a few lines are necessary by way
of epilogue.
Bhoja on the whole, occupies an important place among the
ablest rulers of India. He is remembered not only for his political
achievements but also for his scholastic and cultural performance
through which he enriched the Indian literature and learning and
brought a dynamic era of cultural reforms.
Bhoja was the first and the last in his dynasty to have ruled
over a vast kingdom. He subdued several rulers of northern and
southern India. He fought successfully against the lords of
Kamata, Lata, Toggala and Konkana, Jaya-Sirhh the Chalukya-
ruler of Kalyani, became the first prey of Bhoja’s war mercenaries.
The Kalachuri Karpa and Bhima the western Chalukya ruler of
Gujarat were the other great rivals who chal.'enged the power of
Bhoja But it was to Bhoja’s credit that they could not do any
considerable harm to his shining career till he was alive.
Bhoja struck a terror in to the hearts of Mahmud of Ghazni,
who was badly engaged in laying waste the prosperity of the entire
nation. Apart from indulging his foes in incessant wars, Bhoja also
showed high rank of diplomacy by maintaining cordial relations
through alliances with certain powerful chiefs such as the Chola
monarch Rajendra I and the Kalachuri King Gangeya Neither
Vakpati-Munja nor his father Sindhuraja had left for him a conso-
lidated empire. When Munja met his end, the empire of Malwa had
almost collapsed and his successors Sindhuraja retained no more
than a precarious hold over the feudatories of outlying parts. With
Malwa alone as his patrimony, Bhoja built up a great empire.
Through his chivalrous deeds, Bhoja extended the Paramara domi-
nion three times to its former size. His imperial sway extended
from Chamba and Thaneswer in the north to the Krishna and

Tungabhadra in the south; and from Dwaraka to Kanauj. The

different parts of the old Guijaradesa had, for the time being, been
brought together. Of all the Paramara rulers of Malwa, it was
Bhoja alone who could put several rulers to submission. The
saying of Alberuni and the Udaipur Pra^asti that Bhoja was the
A General Estimate of Bhoja^s Character 415
most powerful king among all the powerful monarchs of the time,
is not merely an eulogy but indicates a bare fact.
Bhoja ascended the throne amidst the chaos and political dis-
turbances. The contemporary rulers were fighting among them-
selves for supremacy. None of them liked to remain under the
dominance of the other. The hunger for power and prestige had
coveted the eyes of every ruler of this period. Thus the national
unity had become a thing of the remote past. These were the inter-
nal loop-holes which paved the way to the foreigners to penetrate
the pious heart of mother India. At this critical juncture, arose a
shining star in the horizon of the Paramara family in the form of
Bhoja, who attempted on one hand, to bring the entire nation into
one whole and on the other, saved the country for sometime from
the foreigner’s devastation. His towering personality made his way
easier to rule gloriously for about fiftyfive years. With his death,
faded away the harmonising flame of India. The safety of the
natio T hanged first into the weak hands of his successors and later
on, into the most quarrelsome hands of Gahadvala and the Chau-
hana rulers, Prithviraja was the last flickering lamp of the Hindu
Kings after whom the entire nation fell prey to ths foreign rule.
With the emergence of Bhoja, commences the advanced era of
constructive reforms in a most conspicuous form. He laid down
in a comprehensive manner the political concepts and the rules
for the construction of navigating vessels such as the boats and
ships etc. It was owing to his contributions in this regard, that
the naval welfare and navigation became within an easy approach
of the later kings.
Although in the field of art and architecture, the age of Bhoja
did not mark any especiality, at least the old Indian styles conti-
nued to exist in their original forms. As has already been noticed
in connection with art and architecture of the period, Bhoja, in
person, had built up several temples, lakes, tanks, forts and insti-
tutional buildings etc.
The Society though it continued in the old Smriti lines and the
social organisation remained the same as in the preceding centuries
Bhoja added to them certain liberal notions. During his regime
the attempt was made to see that the qualities of a man and not
the heredity be accounted for. Any one belonging to one caste
4I6 Bhoja Paramara and Ms Times
could freely enjoy the privileges of the higher castes provided he
possessed the required merits.
Religious tolerance was one of the important characteristic
features of the time of Bhoja. In matters of religion every man
was free to follow his own way. A man possessing extraordinary
abilities was not only adorned by the laity but also by the mona-
rchs, even if he belonged to anti-sects. Bhoja himself was an
ardent follower of Saivism, yet he ptitroniscd the exponents of other
sects also The various bounties and preferences such as grants,
donations and liberal patronage etc, were offered to different sects
also without any consideration of their distinctive nature.
The most remarkable feature of the first half of the eleventh
century was the literary activities which were pursued by the dis-
tinguished men of letters and the poet-kings alike. No branch of
literature remained un-devcloped. Various dialects got their firm
footing in the soil of the nation and Sanskrit language in particular
became more polished Beside patronising scholars, Bhoja him-
self wrote a set of books on important subjects such a Astronomy,
Astrology, Polity, Dharmasastra, Poetry, Metrics, Lexicography,
Music and Grammar. Bhoja has often been quoted by later autho-
rities on various subjects. Kalhana, Bilhapa and similar top-rank-
ing scholars owe much to Bhoja’s contribution to learning. Apart
from his own writing he accentuated the cause of literature and
learning at different stages. By patronising the men of letters,
giving them liberal donations and by establishing various educa-
tional instituti ns at different places, Bhoja proved to be a boon
for Sanskrit literature.
